# Security Remediation Decision Log

This log is the single source of truth for **why** every non-trivial decision in the security
remediation was made. Code comments state only *what* a change defends against and carry a stable
`SEC-NN` identifier; the reasoning lives here, keyed by that identifier. Source code, configuration
files and dependency manifests therefore contain no rationale, and nothing here is duplicated into
them.

**Who reads this.** A security reviewer working through the change set before it ships. Every
judgment call is a row, so the review never has to reconstruct intent from a diff.

**What lives elsewhere.** [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) carries operational secret handling, the
verification commands, the deferred follow-ons and the residual-risk register.
[`traceability-matrix.md`](traceability-matrix.md) maps each finding to its files and each file back
to its findings. This log records reasoning. Neither of those files is restated here.

A decision is non-trivial if a competent engineer could reasonably have chosen differently. Any
deviation from a literal reading of the requirements gets its own row. An unexplained deviation is a
defect.

**Columns.** *Decision* is what was chosen. *Alternatives considered* names the options rejected.
*Rationale* says why the choice won. *Risk / residual gap* states what the choice leaves open — an
empty cell would mean the decision carries no residual, which is rarely true.

**Entry keys.** Rows carry one flat sequence, `DL-01` onward, assigned in the order the decisions
were taken. The sequence is flat on purpose: the implementing work used several colliding local
labels, so no local label is a key here. Where one appears inside a cell it is provenance, not an
address. Section headings carry the `SEC-NN` finding identifiers, and that is how a code marker
resolves.

---

## Where to start

- **One finding's reasoning** — the finding index immediately below.
- **The accepted dependency advisories** — section 2.2, the suppression register.
- **What was deliberately left undone** — sections 32, 33 and 34.
- **Why this log departs from its own brief** — section 26.
- **The two user-rule conflicts and how they were resolved** — section 37.
- **What a later boundary review withdrew, and what it cost** — section 38.
- **What the frontend and client-contract review corrected** — section 40.
- **What the security assessment of the delivered work corrected** — section 41.
- **How this document scores against the prose rule** — section 40.8.

### Finding index

Every `SEC-NN` marker in the repository resolves through this list. Sections in **bold** carry that
finding's primary reasoning; the rest refine it. Two sections serve every finding at once and are
left out of the lists below: 40.5 and 40.6 record the wording of the code markers themselves, and
40.8 records the prose verdict for this document.

## 1. Dependency manifest decisions

`backend/requirements.txt`

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-01** — Keep the pydantic pin as the bare `pydantic==1.10.26` and declare `python-dotenv==1.2.1` as a nineteenth requirement, so the prescribed coordinate stays exact and the parser `env_file` needs is present. **Amends the earlier bare-pin-only decision recorded here; see DL-61 for the audit-gate half.** | (a) `pydantic[dotenv]==1.10.26`, the extra alone, which installs the same package under a coordinate the manifest contract does not carry. (b) The bare pin with no dotenv package at all, the earlier decision. (c) The bare pin plus removal of `env_file` from `backend/app/core/config.py`. | Three contracts have to hold at once, and only this spelling holds all three. The manifest contract prescribes the bare coordinate, and QA raised the extras spelling as a defect twice, which rules out option (a). The `config.py` contract freezes `env_file = ".env"`, and the provisioning and template contracts both state that the `.env` written next to the process is the file the application loads — the template's own acceptance step is that a filled copy constructs `Settings()`. Option (c) deletes a frozen line and leaves the template describing a mechanism that no longer exists. Declaring the parser is one added line against a broken primary instruction. | The nineteenth line deviates from the eighteen-line manifest contract and from its prohibition list, which is why this row exists. `python-dotenv` carries `PYSEC-2026-2270`, whose fix release 1.2.2 requires Python 3.10 or newer and cannot be installed here, so the closure gains a fifteenth suppressed advisory — register row 15, accepted on reachability grounds. Exported variables still win over file values, so a stale `.env` can be shadowed silently by an exported one. |
| **DL-02** — Keep the version line itself at `1.10.26` and stay on pydantic 1.x. | Migrate to pydantic 2.x. | Version 2 moves `BaseSettings` out of `pydantic` and renames `from_orm()`; seven call sites would have to be rewritten. `1.10.26` is the highest 1.x release. | The 1.x line will eventually stop receiving fixes. A pydantic 2 migration is a separate project, not a security fix. |
| **DL-03** — Pin `bcrypt==4.3.0`, explicitly, below 5.0.0. | (a) Let `passlib[bcrypt]` resolve bcrypt freely. (b) Pin `bcrypt==4.0.1`. (c) Express the ceiling as `bcrypt<5.0.0`. | Under bcrypt 5.0.0 the construction at `security.py:13` raises inside passlib's own capability probe, which breaks all password hashing at import time — an availability outage, not a warning. 4.0.1 also works and avoids one trapped warning, but 4.3.0 is more recently maintained. A range specifier would break the manifest's exact-pin discipline. | bcrypt 4.3.0 accepts an over-length password **silently**, truncating at 72 **bytes**. The library supplies no protection, so the request-schema validator must bound the *encoded byte length*, not the character count. A character-count maximum alone would leave non-ASCII passwords truncating undetected. |
| **DL-04** — Omit `python-multipart` entirely. | Pin it at the highest version installable on Python 3.9. | The package carried six advisories whose fix releases all require Python 3.10 or newer, and the backend contains no `Form()`, `File()`, `UploadFile` or `OAuth2PasswordRequestForm` usage — both authentication endpoints take JSON request models. Removing it closed six otherwise-unclosable advisories at zero functional cost. | A future endpoint that needs form or upload handling must re-add the package and re-open the advisory question. Anyone reading a FastAPI manifest will reasonably expect this package, which is why the absence is recorded here rather than left to inference. |
| **DL-05** — Omit `pytest-asyncio`. | Add it so `backend/tests/test_tasks.py`'s `@pytest.mark.asyncio` markers resolve. | `test_tasks.py` is out of scope and already fails collection for unrelated reasons. Adding a package to service an out-of-scope failing module is exactly the enhancement the Minimal Change Clause forbids. | The new security tests must therefore use the synchronous `TestClient` only. Async test support remains unavailable. |
| **DL-06** — Decline the newest available release of five packages: `fastapi` (0.125.0, not 0.128.8), `pydantic` (1.10.26, not 2.13.4), `bcrypt` (4.3.0, not 5.0.0), `starlette` (0.49.3 transitively, not 1.3.1), `python-multipart` (removed, not 0.0.32). | Take the latest of each. | Every later `fastapi`, `starlette` and `python-multipart` release requires Python 3.10 or newer. The `pydantic` and `bcrypt` ceilings are functional, not runtime — see DL-02 and DL-03. | Fifteen advisories remain unpatchable on this runtime, the count the shipped gate carries; see section 2.2 and DL-388. Migrating to Python 3.10 or newer is the only real remedy and is the highest-priority follow-on. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-01.** Option (b) leaves that path broken: measured on a clean install, `Settings()` raises `ImportError: python-dotenv is not installed` from `env_settings.py` as soon as a `.env` exists at the resolved path, and `config.py` builds `Settings()` at import scope, so the documented developer flow cannot start the application at all.

## 2. Dependency audit gate and advisory suppressions

`.github/workflows/ci.yml`

The gate runs `pip-audit --strict --no-deps` against the frozen closure and suppresses a fixed list
of advisories. Suppressing an advisory without a scheduled reconsideration is how a temporary
exception becomes permanent, so **each entry below carries a date and a review trigger, and the
register is part of the deliverable rather than a footnote.**

### 2.1 Gate design decisions

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-07** — Extend the suppression list from ten entries to fourteen, adding the four advisories measured in the resolved closure but absent from the original inventory. | (a) Ship the ten-entry list unchanged. (b) Raise the pins of `msgpack`, `filelock` and `pytest` to their fix releases. (c) Drop `--strict`, or stop failing the build on findings. | Option (a) installs a gate that fails on every run for reasons unrelated to any security finding — a permanently red gate teaches maintainers to ignore it, which is worse than no gate. Option (b) is impossible: every one of the three fix releases was probed directly and every probe returned a `Requires-Python >= 3.10` rejection, while the pinned versions download cleanly from the same index in the same session. Option (c) removes the gate's ability to detect anything. Each addition is justified individually in 2.2 with a reachability assessment, so the list was widened deliberately and visibly rather than silently. | Fourteen accepted advisories is a real exposure surface, not a clean bill of health. Eight of the fourteen are unreachable in this codebase, one is low and partial, one is partial with a compensating control available, and the four added here are unreachable from the network-facing application. The list is only defensible while the review trigger is honoured. **Superseded by DL-93.** |
| **DL-08** — Add a cross-check asserting that every suppression identifier still matches a live advisory, and fail the build when one does not. | Rely on the `--ignore-vuln` flags alone. | `pip-audit` 2.9.0 accepts an identifier that matches nothing **without emitting any diagnostic**: a run with a fabricated identifier reported `Found 14 known vulnerabilities in 8 packages` and said nothing about the unmatched entry. A mistyped or stale suppression therefore disables part of the gate invisibly. The cross-check converts that silent failure into a build failure. It is also what makes a pin change safe: an entry whose package leaves the closure is reported instead of ignored. That is how the `python-dotenv` entry was caught and removed when the pin first dropped the package, and how DL-61 knew to restore it when DL-01 declared the package again. | The cross-check adds a second `pip-audit` invocation to the CI step. It detects a stale entry but cannot decide whether the right response is to delete the entry or to investigate a changed closure — that judgment stays with the maintainer. **Superseded by DL-96.** |
| **DL-09** — Allow GitHub advisory (`GHSA-`) identifiers in the suppression list alongside `PYSEC-` identifiers. | Restrict the list to `PYSEC-` identifiers only. | The `msgpack` advisory `GHSA-6v7p-g79w-8964` has **no `PYSEC-` form and no aliases at all**, so a `PYSEC-`-only policy cannot express it. Alias matching was verified to work: suppressing `GHSA-86qp-5c8j-p5mr` (an alias of `PYSEC-2026-161`) produced `ignored 1`. | A mixed-namespace list is marginally harder to read. DL-08 mitigates the associated hazard by proving every entry, in either namespace, still matches. **Moot after DL-93, which leaves no `GHSA-` entry in the list. See DL-94.** |
| **DL-10** — Keep `pip-audit==2.9.0` inside `backend/requirements.txt`. | Install the audit tool outside the application manifest, so it stops contributing advisories to the closure it audits. | `pip-audit` is a declared requirement of the manifest contract, and its presence at the pinned version is a verified acceptance criterion. Removing it would violate an explicit requirement in order to improve a metric. | The audit tool injects two of the five added advisory-bearing packages into its own audit scope: `msgpack` and `filelock` reach the closure **only** through `pip-audit`'s `CacheControl[filecache] >= 0.13.0` dependency. Every future advisory in the audit tool's own tree therefore becomes a gate failure. Splitting build-time tooling out of the runtime manifest — and out of the production image, which currently installs the whole file — remains the correct long-term shape and is recommended as a follow-on. |
| **DL-11** — Place the gate immediately after the dependency-install step in `ci.yml`, and add nothing else to that workflow. | (a) Append it at the end of the workflow. (b) Add the repository-wide secret scan and the frontend client-secret guard in the same change. | A gate that runs early fails fast and cannot be reached by a job that never installed its dependencies. The secret scan and the client-secret guard serve different findings and belong with those findings, not here; bundling unrelated CI work into this change would violate the Minimal Change Clause. | Inserting a step shifts the line numbers of later steps. The affected cross-references in `backend/requirements.txt` were corrected in the same change so no stale pointer is left behind. |
| **DL-12** — Correct the record: `pip-audit` 2.9.0 **does** match suppression identifiers by alias. | Leave the earlier claim that alias-based suppression "silently fails to match" standing. | Measured directly: `--ignore-vuln GHSA-86qp-5c8j-p5mr` and `--ignore-vuln CVE-2026-48710`, both real aliases of `PYSEC-2026-161`, each produced `Found 13 known vulnerabilities, ignored 1 in 8 packages` with that advisory absent from the output. The original warning would send a maintainer looking for a namespace bug that does not exist, and it would also rule out the only identifier available for the `msgpack` advisory. | None to the gate: the prescribed `PYSEC-` identifiers all match, and DL-09 covers the one advisory that has no `PYSEC-` form. The superseded claim should also be corrected in `SECURITY.md` when that document is written. |

### 2.2 Advisory suppression register and held-visible advisories

Every entry shares one cause: **the release that fixes the advisory cannot be installed on the
Python version this project documents.** Each was probed directly; each probe returned a
`Requires-Python` rejection.

**Review trigger, shared by all fifteen suppressed entries: a Python runtime upgrade.** On that upgrade
this list is emptied, the pins are raised, and each advisory is re-measured rather than re-accepted.
Advisory data is a point-in-time snapshot, so the inventory must be re-measured at every security
review rather than copied forward.

**All fifteen rows are suppressed in the shipped gate, and the register is exactly the reported
set.** Every row carries its own acceptance date below. All fifteen were accepted on 2026-07-31,
under the single criterion this section states. Rows 11 to 15 entered the register on
2026-08-02, when the resolved closure was measured to include the audit tooling and the
`python-dotenv` pin, and each names that measurement date beside its acceptance date. The
identifiers, aliases, fix versions, installability verdicts and entry-point attributions in
every row were measured against the resolved closure.

Measured against the shipped workflow on 2026-08-02: without the register the gate reports `Found
15 known vulnerabilities in 9 packages` and exits 1; with it the gate reports `No known
vulnerabilities found, 15 ignored` and exits 0. DL-291 holds the count at fifteen and DL-294
corrects the earlier statement here that rows 11 to 15 were unsuppressed.

- **Row 1, `PYSEC-2026-161` in `starlette` 0.49.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-48710`, `GHSA-86qp-5c8j-p5mr`, `X41-2026-002`. Fix release 1.0.1; not
    installable on Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `fastapi`.
  - Reachability: **PARTIAL** — host-header URL reconstruction without validation. No
    application code relies on the reconstructed URL or on the `Host` header.
  - Accepted. Host-allow-list middleware is available in the pinned version as an optional
    compensating control and is recommended, not mandated.

- **Row 2, `PYSEC-2026-249` in `starlette` 0.49.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-54283`, `GHSA-82w8-qh3p-5jfq`. Fix release 1.3.1; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `fastapi`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — form-parsing limits silently ignored for URL-encoded bodies.
    No form parsing exists anywhere in the backend and the multipart package is absent (DL-04).
  - Accepted: no code path reaches it.

- **Row 3, `PYSEC-2026-248` in `starlette` 0.49.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-54282`, `GHSA-jp82-jpqv-5vv3`. Fix release 1.3.0; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `fastapi`.
  - Reachability: **LOW, partial** — a request path not beginning with a slash can shift the
    authority boundary during URL reconstruction. Same non-reliance as row 1.
  - Accepted with the same compensating control as row 1.

- **Row 4, `PYSEC-2026-2281` in `starlette` 0.49.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-48818`, `GHSA-wqp7-x3pw-xc5r`. Fix release 1.1.0; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `fastapi`.
  - Reachability: **DOUBLY UNREACHABLE** — static-file path traversal specific to Windows. No
    static-file mount exists and deployment is Linux containers.
  - Accepted: neither precondition holds.

- **Row 5, `PYSEC-2026-2280` in `starlette` 0.49.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-48817`, `GHSA-x746-7m8f-x49c`. Fix release 1.1.0; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `fastapi`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — unrestricted handler selection in the class-based endpoint
    API. This application uses decorator routes exclusively.
  - Accepted: the affected API is not used.

- **Row 6, `PYSEC-2026-1325` in `ecdsa` 0.19.2.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2024-23342`, `GHSA-wj6h-64fc-37mp`. No fix release exists, and none will ship;
    n/a. Enters the closure through `python-jose`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — timing side channel in elliptic-curve signing. This
    application signs with HMAC-SHA256 and never invokes the affected path.
  - Accepted, and converted from a risk into an enforced invariant by restricting the algorithm
    setting to the HMAC family. A pre-validated alternative that removes the package entirely is
    recorded at DL-13.

- **Row 7, `PYSEC-2026-2132` in `click` 8.1.8.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-7246`, `GHSA-47fr-3ffg-hgmw`. Fix release 8.3.3; not installable on Python
    3.9. Enters the closure through `uvicorn`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — command injection in the editor helper, which is never
    called.
  - Accepted: transitive, unused API.

- **Row 8, `PYSEC-2026-2275` in `requests` 2.32.5.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-25645`, `GHSA-gc5v-m9x4-r6x2`. Fix release 2.33.0; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through direct pin, plus `paypalrestsdk` and `pip-audit`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — predictable temporary filename in an archive helper,
    requiring a local attacker. The application calls only the HTTP verb helpers.
  - Accepted: the affected helper is never called and the attack requires local access.

- **Row 9, `PYSEC-2026-142` in `urllib3` 2.6.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-44432`, `GHSA-mf9v-mfxr-j63j`. Fix release 2.7.0; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `requests`, `paypalrestsdk`, `pip-audit`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — over-decompression requiring an optional compression backend
    that is not installed.
  - Accepted: the required backend is absent.

- **Row 10, `PYSEC-2026-141` in `urllib3` 2.6.3.** Accepted 2026-07-31.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-44431`, `GHSA-qccp-gfcp-xxvc`. Fix release 2.7.0; not installable on
    Python 3.9. Enters the closure through `requests`, `paypalrestsdk`, `pip-audit`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — header leakage on cross-origin redirect through a
    proxy-manager configuration this application never uses.
  - Accepted: the affected configuration is not used.

- **Row 11, `GHSA-6v7p-g79w-8964` in `msgpack` 1.1.2.** Accepted 2026-07-31, closure measured
  2026-08-02.
  - No aliases. Fix release 1.2.1; not installable on Python 3.9 — 1.2.0 and 1.2.1 both require
    Python >= 3.10; 1.1.2 is the highest installable. Enters the closure through **`pip-audit`
    only**, via `CacheControl[filecache] >= 0.13.0` → `msgpack >=0.5.2,<2.0.0`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE from the application** — a reused `Unpacker` can crash with SEGV
    after an error while decoding untrusted input. The only importer in the closure is
    `cachecontrol/serialize.py`, which deserializes `pip-audit`'s own HTTP response cache during
    the audit step. The backend contains zero references to `msgpack`.
  - Accepted. Build-time audit tooling only, never on the served request path, and the input it
    decodes is the advisory service's own cached responses rather than attacker-supplied data.
    This advisory has no `PYSEC-` identifier at all, which is why DL-09 permits `GHSA-` entries.
    **Suppressed; the register holds at fifteen entries under DL-291.**

- **Row 12, `PYSEC-2026-1375` in `filelock` 3.19.1.** Accepted 2026-07-31, closure measured
  2026-08-02.
  - Aliases `CVE-2025-68146`, `GHSA-w853-jp5j-5j7f`. Fix release 3.20.1; not installable on
    Python 3.9 — every release from 3.20.0 through 3.32.2 requires Python >= 3.10; 3.19.1 is the
    highest installable. Enters the closure through **`pip-audit` only**, via
    `CacheControl[filecache]` → `filelock >= 3.8.0`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE from the network** — a time-of-check-to-time-of-use symlink race
    during lock-file creation lets a **local** attacker truncate an arbitrary file. Exploitation
    needs local filesystem access and the ability to create symlinks. The backend contains zero
    references to `filelock`.
  - Accepted. Reached only through `pip-audit`'s file cache on an ephemeral CI runner, and an
    attacker who already has local filesystem access on that runner has better options than this
    race. **Suppressed; the register holds at fifteen entries under DL-291.**

- **Row 13, `PYSEC-2026-1374` in `filelock` 3.19.1.** Accepted 2026-07-31, closure measured
  2026-08-02.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-22701`, `GHSA-qmgc-5h2g-mvrw`. Fix release 3.20.3; not installable on
    Python 3.9 — same ceiling as row 12. Enters the closure through **`pip-audit` only**, via
    `CacheControl[filecache]`.
  - Reachability: **DOUBLY UNREACHABLE** — the race is specific to `SoftFileLock`, and
    `cachecontrol/caches/file_cache.py` selects `FileLock` instead. Scored 5.6 Medium with a
    local attack vector.
  - Accepted: the affected class is not the one used on Linux where `fcntl` is available, and
    the attack additionally requires local access. **Suppressed; the register holds at fifteen
    entries under DL-291.**

- **Row 14, `PYSEC-2026-1845` in `pytest` 8.4.2.** Accepted 2026-07-31, closure measured
  2026-08-02.
  - Aliases `CVE-2025-71176`, `GHSA-6w46-j5rx-g56g`. Fix release 9.0.3; not installable on
    Python 3.9 — every release from 9.0.0 through 9.1.1 requires Python >= 3.10; 8.4.2 is the
    highest installable. Enters the closure through direct development pin, plus `pytest-cov`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE from the network** — a predictable `/tmp/pytest-of-{user}`
    directory lets a local user cause denial of service or possibly gain privileges.
    `backend/app` contains zero references to `pytest`, and the container command runs
    `uvicorn`, so the test runner is never executed in a deployed environment.
  - Accepted. Test-runner only, requiring a local attacker on a machine where the suite is being
    run. This advisory is the clearest argument for the DL-10 follow-on: the production image
    should not carry development tooling at all. **Suppressed; the register holds at fifteen
    entries under DL-291.**

- **Row 15, `PYSEC-2026-2270` in `python-dotenv` 1.2.1.** Accepted 2026-07-31, closure measured
  2026-08-02.
  - Aliases `CVE-2026-28684`, `GHSA-mf9w-mj56-hr94`. Fix release 1.2.2; not installable on
    Python 3.9 — 1.2.2 requires Python >= 3.10, probed directly and rejected; 1.2.1 is the
    highest installable, and every release below it is in range because the advisory is
    introduced at 0. Enters the closure through direct pin (DL-01), read by `pydantic` 1.10.26
    to load `env_file`.
  - Reachability: **UNREACHABLE** — `set_key()` and `unset_key()` follow symbolic links when
    **rewriting** a `.env` file, so a local attacker can overwrite an arbitrary file through a
    crafted symlink when the cross-device rename fallback runs. Pydantic uses the read helper
    `dotenv_values()` only, and `backend/` contains zero references to `dotenv`, `set_key` or
    `unset_key`. Scored 5.9 Medium with `AV:L/AC:L/PR:L/UI:R`, so exploitation also needs local
    access and operator interaction.
  - Accepted: the write API the advisory concerns is never called, and the read path the
    application uses is unaffected. Restored to the gate list by DL-61 and dropped by DL-135's
    ten-entry set. **Suppressed; the register holds at fifteen entries under DL-291.**

## 3. Alternatives held as costed contingencies

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-13** — Keep `python-jose[cryptography]==3.5.0` for token handling. | Replace it with `PyJWT[crypto]`, which drops both `ecdsa` and `rsa` from the closure and therefore removes register row 6 — the one advisory that will never receive a patch. | Keeping the pinned library is the smaller change, and the residual advisory is unreachable under the HMAC algorithm this application uses. The replacement was validated end to end: encoding returns a string, the subject claim round-trips, and all four failure modes reject correctly. Migration cost is two lines in `backend/app/core/security.py` — the import and the exception class. | An advisory with no fix release stays in the closure. Switch triggers: a policy forbidding suppression of unpatchable advisories, or any move to asymmetric token signing. Because the contingency is already validated, executing it later is a two-line change rather than a research project. |
| **DL-14** — Do not upgrade the Python runtime as part of this work. | Move to Python 3.10 or newer, which is the only real remedy for all fifteen suppressed advisories. | The version is hardcoded in four places spanning the container image, the CI workflow, a cloud function runtime identifier and a deployment script. None of those is among the security findings, and changing them is a platform migration rather than a security fix. | Fifteen advisories stay accepted — the count this row's alternative cell already gives, and the count the shipped gate carries — and it will grow as new ones are published against packages already at their Python 3.9 ceiling. This is the single highest-priority follow-on and must be named as such in `SECURITY.md`. |
| **DL-15** — Do not add a lock file. | Generate `frontend/package-lock.json` and a Python lock file so the transitive closure is pinned exactly. | Generating one commits a full transitive tree that has not been audited, and no audit of it is reproducible without first generating it. Direct dependencies are pinned exactly, which removes the variance that matters most. | Transitive resolution stays non-deterministic. Two independent clean resolutions in this environment were byte-identical, which is genuine evidence of determinism at one point in time against one index — not a guarantee. Lock files are a named follow-on. |

---

## 4. Informational observations reconciled from QA

Three observations were raised as informational rather than as defects: no behaviour is wrong, but a
stated value or an assumed exception hierarchy does not match what the pinned closure actually does.
Each is recorded here so the discrepancy is a settled fact rather than a surprise to whoever writes
the next assertion against it. Every value below was measured against the pinned closure on
CPython 3.9.25 on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-16** — Read the installed PayPal SDK version from distribution metadata, `importlib.metadata.version("paypalrestsdk")`, and never from the module's `__version__` attribute. | Assert against `paypalrestsdk.__version__`, which is the more obvious of the two surfaces and the one most test authors reach for first. Alternatively pin the SDK to whatever version its `__version__` reports, so that the two agree. | The two surfaces disagree: distribution metadata reports **1.13.3** while `paypalrestsdk.__version__` reports **1.13.1**. The distribution metadata is the value the manifest pin governs and the value the resolver, the audit tool and `pip check` all act on, so it is the only surface an assertion can correctly read. The disagreement is an upstream packaging inconsistency in the published artifact — the maintainers advanced the release version without updating the in-module constant — and is not something this repository can or should correct. Re-pinning to 1.13.1 to force agreement would move the dependency backwards to satisfy a cosmetic mismatch. | An assertion written against `__version__` will fail against a correctly pinned environment, and the failure will look like a wrong pin rather than a wrong assertion. Contained by recording the correct surface here; no code change is warranted because nothing in `backend/` reads either value. |
| **DL-17** — Record that `JWKError` and `JWSError` are **not** subclasses of `JWTError`, and defer the fix to the `ALGORITHM` allow-list at the `config.py` checkpoint rather than widening the exception clause here. | Widen `backend/app/core/security.py:64`, the `except jwt.JWTError` clause, to catch `JOSEError`, the common ancestor. Alternatively catch `JWKError` explicitly alongside `JWTError`. Alternatively do nothing and leave the observation unrecorded. | It is also narrowly gated: both escape routes share one precondition, `settings.ALGORITHM` holding a value outside the HMAC family, so constraining that setting closes both call sites while widening the clause closes only the decode path. Widening also needs a new import, because the `jose.jwt` namespace exports neither name — two lines at one call site against one validator covering two. AAP 0.4.6 already commits to the HMAC-family restriction and AAP 0.6.1 assigns the `config.py` validators to their own checkpoint, so editing `security.py` from a dependency-manifest checkpoint would be scope creep. | Until the `ALGORITHM` allow-list lands, a misconfigured setting turns every authenticated request into a 500 instead of a 401, and every login into a 500 instead of a token. That is an availability and error-uniformity defect, not an authentication bypass. **Review trigger: the `config.py` checkpoint implementing the HMAC-family allow-list.** |
| **DL-18** — Record the measured dependency-closure counts and treat them as superseding the count stated in AAP 0.7.3. | Leave the specification's figure of 40 unchallenged and describe the closure using it. Alternatively adjust the pins until the resolved closure matches 40. | The specification's figure is arithmetic drift rather than an unexplained gap: two packages account for the whole difference, and both are explained in the evidence below. Adjusting pins to hit 40 would mean removing a dependency the framework requires, which trades a working closure for a matching number. | A reader comparing the specification's figure against a real environment will find a mismatch and may suspect an unpinned or unwanted dependency. Contained by recording the arithmetic here. The counts will drift again whenever an upstream package adds or drops a dependency, since the manifest pins direct dependencies only — the granularity DL-15 accepts in the absence of a lock file. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-17.** The hierarchy is real and measured: `issubclass(JWKError, JWTError)` is **False**, and both classes descend from `JOSEError`. Nothing bypasses authentication in any case: the only accepting combination is a genuine HS256 token verified under an HS256 allow-list, so the defect surfaces as an unhandled 500 rather than as an accepted token.
- **DL-17, decode matrix.** Across seven decode combinations, `JWKError` is raised on exactly the two where `alg=none` appears in **both** the token header **and** the allowed-algorithms list. Those two are `["none"]` and `["HS256", "none"]`.
  - The second shows that including the correct algorithm alongside a bad one does not save the caller. Every other combination raises `JWTError` and is caught.
  - A misconfigured `ALGORITHM` on the minting path, the `jwt.encode` call at `security.py:39`, raises a third sibling, `JWSError`.
  - The `jose.jwt` namespace exports neither `JWKError` nor `JOSEError`, so widening the clause needs an added import as well.
- **DL-18, closure arithmetic.** The resolved runtime-only closure is **42** packages — 14 direct pins and 28 transitive — against the 40 stated in AAP 0.7.3. Two packages account for the whole difference: `annotated-doc`, which now enters through the FastAPI and Pydantic chain and was absent when the specification was written, and `python-dotenv`, the fourteenth direct pin DL-01 added.
  - Including the five development pins the closure is **75** distributions — 42 runtime-only plus 33 from development tooling, being 5 direct dev pins and 28 of their transitives.
  - Two readings differ in opposite directions and both are accounted for: `pip freeze` reports **74**, omitting only `pip`, which is inside the closure; `importlib.metadata` sees **77**, the closure plus `setuptools` and `wheel`, which sit outside it. DL-203 and DL-291 record why the gate audits the frozen 74.
  - Every package in the resolved runtime closure was confirmed present in the installed environment.

## 5. Origin allow-list and request-schema decisions (SEC-03, SEC-05)

The settings and request-schema milestone raised one defect and four informational observations, and a
later review of the same validator raised a second defect. Both defects are closed in code; each
observation is a deliberate decision to leave working behaviour alone, recorded so the omission reads
as a choice rather than an oversight. Every value below was measured against the pinned closure on
CPython 3.9.25 on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-19** — Extend the `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` entry test in `backend/app/core/config.py` with two terms that reject any entry carrying a non-ASCII, whitespace, or control character. | (a) Leave it, because QA measured the impact as fail-closed. (b) Strip the offending characters and accept the cleaned entry. (c) Test the character set with a regular expression. (d) Adopt QA's literal `ord(c) < 32 or ord(c) == 127` form. | `urlsplit` strips only TAB, CR and LF, so the existing reconstruction test caught exactly those three characters. A space, NUL, VT, FF and every non-ASCII character passed through and was stored verbatim: ten such entries were accepted before the change and all ten are rejected after it. Such an entry contradicts the validator's own error message and can never equal a browser-sent `Origin`, so the frontend is refused with no diagnostic and the startup failure SEC-03 exists to produce never happens. Option (b) would leave two spellings of one origin and invite the unanchored prefix match OWASP names. Option (c) needs a new import and reads as pattern matching, which SEC-03 keeps out of the origin path. Option (d) is equivalent for ASCII input but 88 columns wide, which adds a style finding. The chosen form appends two terms and touches no existing line. | The terms test the character set; they perform no IDNA conversion, so an operator who wants an internationalised host must supply the punycode form, which still passes. An uppercase percent-escape such as `%2D` also stays rejected, by the pre-existing lowercase-host term rather than by these. |
| **DL-20** — Record that an unsafe `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` value fails closed through two different exception classes, and leave both paths as they are. | Add a pre-parsing validator so every bad value reaches the field validator and raises `ValidationError`. Catch `SettingsError` and re-raise it as a `ValidationError`. Leave the difference undocumented. | A value that parses as JSON but is unsafe raises `pydantic.ValidationError` at `loc=('ALLOWED_ORIGINS',)`. A value that is not parseable JSON — comma-separated text, a bare `*`, an empty value, or a raw control character inside the JSON string — raises `pydantic.env_settings.SettingsError`, which names the field in lower case. Both subclass `ValueError`, both abort the module import, and neither echoes a value, so the security property is identical and only the class differs. A pre-parsing validator would accept the comma-separated form that `.env.example` tells developers not to use, and re-raising buys nothing but a second code path. | An assertion written as `pytest.raises(pydantic.ValidationError)` fails spuriously against the unquoted raw forms; assert `ValueError`, or the pair. **Review trigger: the milestone that creates `backend/tests/security/test_cors_policy.py`.** |
| **DL-21** — Leave the nested `Criteria` model in `backend/app/schema/filter.py` without `extra = "forbid"`. | Add the setting for defence in depth, matching the five create models. | Extra keys on a criterion are dropped, not absorbed: a `Criteria` built from a payload carrying `evil_flag` and `user_id` exposes only `field`, `operator` and `value`. `filters.py:28` names each criterion field explicitly onto a mapped `Criteria` row and never expands client data into a model constructor, so no mass-assignment path exists, and `Criteria` is not one of the five create models the requirements enumerate for the strict allow-list. | A client can post an unknown key inside a criterion and still receive a success response, so a typo is ignored rather than reported. |
| **DL-22** — Keep pydantic v1 type coercion on the create models rather than requiring strict types. | Annotate the numeric fields as strict types, or set a strict base configuration on the create models. | The coercions are ordinary v1 semantics that every pre-existing model in this repository already depends on: `"1500.50"` becomes `1500.5`, `True` becomes `1`, `2.9` becomes `2`, and `19` becomes `19.0`. No requirement asks for strict types, and tightening them would narrow request shapes on endpoints whose contracts are frozen. | A client sending a boolean where an integer belongs receives a coerced value instead of a 422, so a malformed client goes uncorrected. |
| **DL-23** — Add no value-range constraint to `ListingCreate.rent`. | Bound it at zero with a field constraint or a validator. | The requirements define `ListingCreate` as an allow-list of writable fields, the column carries no check constraint, and a negative rent is a business rule. The Minimal Change Clause keeps business rules out of a security pass. | A negative rent is schema-valid. Nothing is persisted today because that endpoint cannot create a record for unrelated reasons, so the gap becomes live only when the endpoint is repaired. |
| **DL-28** — Replace the raw-`netloc` entry test in the `ALLOWED_ORIGINS` validator with the module-level `_is_valid_origin` helper, which reads the parsed port inside a guarded block, rejects a scheme default port and a percent-escaped host, and compares every entry against its browser-canonical serialization. The authority rules it applies are recorded in DL-53. | (a) Leave the `netloc` test, since a malformed entry can never equal a browser-sent `Origin` and so fails closed at request time. (b) Add the port guard alone and keep reconstructing from raw `netloc`. (c) Canonicalise a non-canonical entry and accept the cleaned form. (d) Validate the host with a regular expression. | `urlsplit` leaves `:abc` and `:65536` sitting in `netloc` and raises only when `.port` is read, so both were stored verbatim. A trailing `:`, a zero-padded `:00443`, a percent-escaped host and an uppercase host passed the same test. `https://example.com:443` passed too, while a browser sends `https://example.com` for that site, so the entry was dead configuration that read as coverage. Option (a) leaves the startup failure SEC-03 exists to produce unreachable and hands the operator a silent no-match instead. Option (c) leaves two spellings of one origin and invites the unanchored prefix match OWASP names. Option (d) puts a pattern in the origin path that SEC-03 keeps out. | The helper performs no IDNA conversion, so an internationalised host must be supplied in punycode, and every percent character in a host is rejected, which also excludes a scoped IPv6 literal. An explicit default port now produces a startup failure rather than a silent no-match. A fully expanded IPv6 spelling is rejected in favour of the compressed form a browser sends. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-28.** Measured after the change: 46 rejection cases rejected and 12 legitimate origins still accepted, including a bracketed IPv6 literal and a punycode host. Option (b) closes two of the seven measured spellings.

## 6. Environment variable template decisions (SEC-01, SEC-12)

`.env.example`

The template enumerates the settings contract by name and shape while carrying no value. Every count
and every placeholder below was measured against `backend/app/core/config.py`, the pinned closure on
CPython 3.9.25, and the repository credential scan on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-24** — Document seventeen variables, not the fifteen the milestone brief names. | (a) Document exactly fifteen and omit the two login-throttle settings. (b) Document fifteen and describe the throttle in prose without declaring either variable. | `backend/app/core/config.py` declares seventeen fields, two of them `LOGIN_RATE_LIMIT_ATTEMPTS` and `LOGIN_RATE_LIMIT_WINDOW_MINUTES`. The brief makes that file the tie-breaker on any disagreement and instructs that whichever throttle settings it declares be documented with their defaults shown. Both carry defaults, so omitting them would leave two operator-tunable security thresholds undiscoverable in the one file whose purpose is to enumerate the contract. A parity assertion holds the template and the settings class to the same seventeen names in both directions. | The count in the milestone heading no longer matches the file, so a reader comparing the two needs this row. Any field later added to the settings class must be added here too, and the parity assertion fails until it is. |
| **DL-25** — Ship a `SECRET_KEY` placeholder of twenty-eight characters, short enough to fail the thirty-two character floor on purpose. | (a) A placeholder at or above thirty-two characters, so a verbatim copy constructs. (b) An empty value. (c) A generated key committed as the worked example. | The first draft measured thirty-five characters and passed the floor. A developer copying the template unchanged would then boot the application with a signing key that anyone reading the repository can see, which is the known-credential fallback SEC-01 exists to remove and is worse than a missing value because it works silently. At twenty-eight characters the same copy aborts with `ensure this value has at least 32 characters`, so the failure states its own remedy. Option (b) also fails closed but teaches nothing about the length requirement, and option (c) would commit real key material to version control. | A developer may read the startup failure as a defect in the template rather than as an instruction, so the comment above the line states the thirty-two character minimum. |
| **DL-26** — Write every placeholder in angle brackets instead of the conventional bare-word style. | (a) The bare-word convention, such as a `changeme` or `your_value_here` placeholder. (b) Leave every value empty after the equals sign. | The repository credential scan matches a signing-key assignment followed by any word character, plus two connection-string patterns, and its only exclusion is `.git`, so it reads this file. Measured against that pattern, `changeme`, `CHANGE_ME`, `__REPLACE_ME__` and `your_value_here` all match while every angle-bracketed form does not. The conventional style would make the file written to close SEC-01 the reason SEC-01's own gate fails. Option (b) satisfies the gate but discards the shape information the deliverable exists to carry, such as the connection-string layout and the JSON-array form of the origin list. | The `DATABASE_URL` placeholder stays clean only because the closing bracket of its password component separates that word from the following delimiter. An editor who strips the brackets reintroduces a scan hit; the gate catches it. |
| **DL-27** — Show protocol constants and threshold defaults as literal values while every credential stays a placeholder. | (a) Bracket every value uniformly, including `HS256` and the numeric defaults. (b) Omit the optional settings entirely, since the class already defaults them. | A developer needs the exact expected value for a constant that carries no secret, and `HS256` in particular records the HMAC-only signing domain that keeps the unpatchable `ecdsa` timing advisory unreachable. The eight literals shown are asserted to equal the class defaults, so the template cannot drift from `config.py`. Option (a) would hide the one value the validator will accept; option (b) would leave the throttle window and the cookie flag invisible to whoever tunes them. | Showing `PAYPAL_MODE=sandbox` means a verbatim copy runs against the sandbox payment environment, which is the safe direction. The live environment is reached only by an explicit edit. |
| **DL-29** — Document the working-directory resolution of `env_file` in this template and declare `python-dotenv` so the file the template tells a developer to create actually loads. **Amends the earlier decision recorded here, which documented the resolution but left file loading unavailable; see DL-01.** | (a) Anchor `env_file` to a path derived from `__file__` so a repository-root `.env` loads from any working directory. (b) Describe the working-directory resolution and the `ImportError` a `.env` then triggers, leaving the copy instruction unusable — the earlier decision. (c) Drop `env_file` from `Settings.Config` altogether. | Option (a) rewrites two lines the requirements freeze verbatim. Option (b) leaves the template's own primary instruction, copy this file to `.env`, guaranteed to fail on a clean install: a template that instructs an action and then states the action breaks the application is a defect in the deliverable, not documentation of a constraint. Option (c) removes a frozen line and leaves the template describing a mechanism that no longer exists. | Exported variables and platform-injected variables override file values, which the header now states; a developer debugging a value that appears in `.env` but not in the process still has to check the environment first. `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh` writes a four-variable `.env` into the invoking directory, so that file supplies three settings and the remaining required variables must still come from a copy of this template or from the environment. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-29.** Measured on the pinned closure: a `.env` at the repository root with the process
  started from `backend/` does not load, which is direct proof that resolution follows the
  working directory rather than the repository root, so both launch directories are named. The
  repository root for `uvicorn backend.app.main:app` and `backend/` for the CI style and test
  steps.

## 7. Login throttle wiring decisions (SEC-07)

`backend/app/core/security.py`, `backend/app/main.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`

A later review found the throttle registered on one limiter object and enforced on another. Every
value below was measured against the pinned closure on CPython 3.9.25 on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-30** — Define the one application `Limiter` in `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, beside the login route it serves, and import that single instance into `backend/app/main.py`, which binds it to `app.state.limiter`. | (a) Leave the two separate `Limiter` objects in place. (b) Keep the object in `main.py` and have the route reach it through `request.app.state.limiter`. (c) Keep the object in `auth.py` and import it into `main.py`. (d) Add a module whose only content is the limiter. | Option (a) leaves the control unwired. Option (b) cannot work: `@limiter.limit(...)` is applied at import time and needs the object, not a request. Option (c) was chosen: `main` already reaches `endpoints.auth` through `api.router`, so importing the limiter in that direction adds no cycle, and the throttle state, its keys and its window all live in the same module as the route they bound. Placing it in `core/security.py` would have made the password and token helpers carry a `slowapi` dependency they do not use, and option (d) adds a module for one statement. | The composition root depends on a leaf endpoint module for one cross-cutting object, so a second throttled route would import from the auth endpoint or move the limiter again. Throttle state stays in-process and per worker, which SEC-07 has always been a partial remediation for. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-30.** Measured before the change: `id(main.limiter)` and `id(auth.limiter)` differed and
  `app.state.limiter is auth.limiter` was `False`, so the instance bound to the app and driving
  `SlowAPIMiddleware` was not the instance the route decorator registered its limit on, and
  `main.LOGIN_RATE_LIMIT` was dead code. Measured after: the three names are one object, the
  route limit `5 per 15 minute` is registered on it, `SlowAPIMiddleware` appears once in the
  stack, and six consecutive failed logins return 401, 401, 401, 401, 401, 429. The same
  sequence as before, which is what shows a shared instance does not double-count.

## 8. Developer provisioning failure propagation (SEC-01, SEC-11)

`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`

A later review found two security-critical commands whose exit status the script discarded, so
a failure was reported as success. Every value below was measured by running the script and its
two functions against stubbed commands on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-31** — Wrap each security-critical provisioning command in an explicit `if ! …; then echo … >&2; return 1; fi` block, let `main` stop on the non-zero return, and add no global `set -e`. The secret-file write and the role batch that these guards cover take the shapes recorded in DL-55 and DL-56. | (a) Add `set -e`, or `set -euo pipefail`, at the top of the script instead of per-command checks. (b) Use the postfix `\|\| { …; exit 1; }` form the review names as equivalent. (c) Install a `trap … ERR` handler covering the whole script. | Option (a) would abort provisioning on commands the finding does not name — the evidence below lists them — and it does not fire for a command already tested in a condition, so it adds nothing to the two blocks that matter. Option (b) is identical in effect and was declined only on legibility. Option (c) has the same reach as option (a) and moves each remedy message away from the command it belongs to. Each message goes to standard error and each guard returns non-zero rather than calling `exit`, so `configure_env_vars \|\| exit 1` and `init_database \|\| exit 1` in `main` stop the run at the failing step and each function stays testable on its own. | `chmod 600` acts on a relative path, so it protects the `.env` in the invoking directory, which under DL-01 and DL-29 is the file a backend started from that same directory loads. |
| **DL-322** — Cover exactly the two security-critical commands the finding names at this checkpoint, and leave the provisioning commands around them unguarded. Narrowed out of DL-31 so the guard mechanism and the guard scope are each one decision. **Later widened, and this row corrects two claims DL-31 carried:** DL-62 guarded the dependency install and the activation, DL-179 guarded the frontend install, and the shipped `init_database` guards `createdb`, so `python3 manage.py migrate` is now the only command of the five that discards its status. | (a) Guard `createdb dbname` on the same terms as the two security-critical commands. (b) Guard every command in the script. | Option (a) sat outside the reported line ranges at the time and would abort a legitimate re-run against an already-present database, while the guarded role batch behind it still catches a genuinely missing one because `ON_ERROR_STOP=1` ends the batch non-zero on the first failing statement. What changed the answer later was DL-182's prerequisite helper: once a missing import aborts `init_database` before it creates anything, a guard at `createdb` reports the first real failure rather than pre-empting a re-run. Option (b) is option (a) of DL-31 by another route and changes the behaviour of commands no finding names. | `python3 manage.py migrate` still discards its status, so the script can report a completed setup with no migrations applied, and `check_software` keeps its own internal `exit 1` calls with no guard at its call site, as DL-179 records. Both are functional failures outside this finding. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-31.** Measured before the change: the script declares no `set -e`, `set -u` or `set -o
  pipefail`. A failing `chmod` was therefore followed by `Environment variables configured`, and
  the run continued through `init_database` and `run_migrations` to `Development environment
  setup completed successfully!`. Measured after: with `chmod` forced to exit 1 the run stops with
  status 1 and prints none of those three lines. With `psql` forced to exit 3 the run stops with
  status 1 after the environment step succeeds and prints none of `Local database initialized`,
  `Running initial data migrations` or the final message.
  - The success path is unchanged and still exits zero. The quoted heredoc still delivers all ten
    statements to `psql` on standard input with `ON_ERROR_STOP=1` in its argument vector, so the
    `if !` wrap did not disturb the redirection.
- **DL-31, commands a global `set -e` would have caught.** Five were unchecked by the script's own design when the decision was taken: `pip3 install`, `npm install`, `createdb`, `source venv/bin/activate` and the `manage.py migrate` call the file itself flags as needing human attention. Three were guarded later — the dependency install and the activation by DL-62, the frontend install by DL-179 — and the shipped `init_database` guards `createdb`, so `manage.py migrate` is the one that still discards its status. A global `set -e` would have changed the behaviour of six commands the finding does not name.

## 9. Filter create ORM contract and local provisioning decisions (SEC-05, SEC-11, SEC-01)

`backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py`, `backend/app/schema/filter.py`, `backend/app/main.py`,
`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, `backend/requirements.txt`

The database and provisioning milestone raised one critical defect, four major defects and one rule
violation. Two of the fixes reverse a decision recorded earlier in this log, so each reversal gets
its own row. Every value below was measured on 2026-07-31 against PostgreSQL 13.23 and the pinned
closure on CPython 3.9.25.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-32** — Fix the filter create defect in `backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py`, a file the requirements table marks REFERENCE with no change. | (a) Leave the endpoint untouched and convert inside `backend/app/schema/filter.py`, giving the request model a method that returns mapped children. (b) Treat the broken create path as a documented residual, as the listing and subscription create paths already are. (c) Give `filters.created_at` a column default in `backend/app/db/models.py`. | The defect is in the endpoint: it hands Pydantic `Criteria` objects to a SQLAlchemy relationship, which raises `AttributeError: 'Criteria' object has no attribute '_sa_instance_state'`, and it omits the non-null `created_at`. Nothing in the schema file closes either half — option (a) would import mapped models into the schema layer and would still leave the timestamp unset, and the finding's own resolution names the endpoint as a valid site. Residual status is granted only to the listing and subscription create paths, while the same requirements set keeps `POST /filters/` on the regression list and requires strict filter input to persist through the ORM contract, so option (b) contradicts them. Option (c) edits the file the requirements deliberately freeze, and with no migration tooling a new default never reaches an existing table. | The requirements file table now disagrees with the tree on this one path, so a reader diffing the two finds an UPDATE listed as REFERENCE. This row is the reconciliation. The exception is confined to the filter create path: the listing and subscription endpoints stay untouched and stay residual. **Withdrawn by DL-286 and reinstated by DL-296.** |
| **DL-33** — Build the mapped children with three named keyword arguments in a comprehension at the call site. | (a) `CriteriaModel(**item.dict())`. (b) A separate schema-to-model mapper module. (c) A `to_orm()` classmethod on the request model. | Option (a) restores dictionary expansion into a mapped constructor, which is the mass-assignment shape the strict allow-list exists to remove, and it would absorb any field later added to the nested model without review. Options (b) and (c) add a file or invert a layer for one call site carrying three fields, and (c) additionally imports mapped models into the schema package. Naming the three fields keeps the copy auditable and touches one function. | The field list is hand-maintained at that call site. A field added to the nested model later must be added here too, and the omission fails closed rather than silently: all three `criteria` columns are non-null, so a dropped value raises `IntegrityError` instead of persisting a partial row. |
| **DL-34** — Add `orm_mode = True` to `Filter`, `Criteria` and `ZipCode`, reversing the earlier decision to withhold it. | (a) Keep it withheld and build the response by hand at the call site. (b) Keep it withheld and drop `response_model=Filter` from the route. (c) Add it to `Filter` only. | Both filter routes already call `Filter.from_orm` on mapped rows, at `filters.py:43` and `filters.py:51`. Without the setting the request fails after the commit with `ConfigError: You must have the config attribute orm_mode=True to use from_orm`, so the mapper alone moves the 500 rather than removing it. The minimal set was measured: `Filter` alone raises two validation errors, `Filter` plus `Criteria` raises one for `zip_codes`, and all three together return the declared seven-key shape, so option (c) is not viable. Option (a) duplicates the response shape inside the endpoint; option (b) breaks a frozen contract. | The setting lets those three models read attributes off any mapped object passed to them, so a future route could hand them a richer row. Only declared fields serialise and none of the three declares a credential field, so nothing leaks today; the widening is in what `from_orm` accepts without complaint. |
| **DL-35** — Supply `created_at=datetime.utcnow()` in the endpoint. | (a) A `server_default` on the column. (b) A Python-side `default` on the column. (c) Accept the value in the request body. | Options (a) and (b) edit the frozen model file, and with no migration tooling a new default never reaches an existing table. Option (c) hands a server-owned audit field to the client, which the finding and the strict allow-list both forbid. The chosen form is one keyword argument and follows the precedent the requirements already accepted for the registration insert. | The timestamp is naive UTC from the application clock, consistent with every other insert in the codebase. Two writers on skewed clocks can order rows inconsistently, and no requirement asks for a database-side clock. |
| **DL-36** — Add `REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC` to the provisioning SQL, reversing the earlier decision to omit it. | (a) `REVOKE ALL ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC`. (b) Move the application objects into a dedicated schema owned by the owner role. (c) Rely on the application never issuing DDL. (d) Revoke through `ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES`. | After the revoke that entry reads `=U/postgres` and the same statement returns `permission denied for schema public`. Option (a) also removes PUBLIC USAGE, denying schema access to any role not granted it explicitly, which is wider than the finding. Option (b) changes the connection string and search path for every consumer. Option (c) fails on the facts: table creation runs at import scope on every start, so the privilege is exercised rather than merely held. Option (d) governs future objects, not schema-level CREATE. | The revoke is scoped to the one database this script creates and hardens no other database on the server. PostgreSQL 15 and newer already revoke this privilege by default, where the statement is a harmless no-op. A deployment provisioned through Terraform rather than this script gains nothing from it, so role separation there remains the partial remediation the requirements record. |
| **DL-37** — Create the schema once inside the provisioning run as the owner role, with the owner credential passed through the environment and discarded afterwards. | (a) Grant the application role CREATE permanently and let startup create the tables. (b) Grant CREATE, let startup create, then revoke in a second pass. (c) Write the DDL out as SQL in the psql heredoc. (d) Introduce migration tooling. | The owner credential exists only during provisioning, so the schema has to be created while it is in scope. Option (a) is the defect. Option (b) leaves a window in which the application role holds DDL and needs a second operator pass. Option (c) copies the model definitions into shell by hand and drifts on the first model change. Option (d) is excluded from this work. The credential travels as an inline environment assignment, so it never enters an argument list. | The owner password is never written to `.env`, so it cannot be recovered after the run: adding a table later needs a re-provision or an operator-supplied owner credential. That is the intended custody trade and it was hit during verification. The bootstrap needs SQLAlchemy, `psycopg2` and the backend package importable for the interpreter that runs it. That did not hold when this row was written; DL-62 fixed the install order and the manifest path, and DL-63 proves the three imports before the first database object exists. |
| **DL-38** — State `checkfirst=True` explicitly at `backend/app/main.py:create_tables`, changing no behaviour. | (a) Leave the call bare and rely on the default. (b) Remove the startup call and require provisioning. (c) Wrap the call in a handler that swallows a permission error. | The finding names this call as the cross-file participant because startup DDL is what made the application role own the tables. With PUBLIC CREATE revoked and the schema pre-provisioned the call issues no DDL, measured as a clean startup under the application role followed by a successful register and filter create. `create_all` already defaults to this argument, so naming it records the expectation at the site the finding cites and is the smallest possible participation. Option (b) removes table creation the local and test flows depend on — importing the application against an empty SQLite file was measured to create all six tables. Option (c) hides a real misconfiguration behind a silent success. | The keyword is documentation rather than a behaviour change, and a reader could mistake it for one; this row is the correction. Against an unprovisioned database the call fails closed at import with `ProgrammingError: (psycopg2.errors.InsufficientPrivilege) permission denied for schema public` and exit 1, which is loud rather than degraded. The remedy is provisioning, and the message names the schema. |
| **DL-39** — Feed the two role passwords to psql as `\set` meta-commands on stdin, guarded by a character-set check. | (a) Keep `-v name=value`. (b) Expand the passwords directly into the SQL heredoc. (c) Write the SQL to a temporary file with a restricted mode. | Option (a) is the defect: a probe run showed `psql -v probe_pw=...` verbatim in `/proc/<pid>/cmdline`, which is world readable at mode 0444. Option (b) requires unquoting a heredoc the file's authoring contract keeps quoted, and it embeds the value as a quoted SQL literal directly after the role-creation keyword. That is one of the three forms the repository credential scan matches, so the file written to close the credential finding would fail that finding's own gate. Option (c) adds a file on disk, its cleanup, and a window in which it exists. Stdin keeps psql's own `:'name'` literal quoting, which the SQL already used, and the captured argument list after the change is `-v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -d dbname` with zero password occurrences. The guard asserts the generator's own alphabet, since a newline or a quote in the value would break the meta-command line rather than being quoted by it. | Exposure narrows from any local reader to the invoking user's own pipe, not to nothing. The guard also couples the script to the current generator: a generator that emits punctuation makes provisioning refuse to run until this row is revisited, which is the fail-closed direction. |
| **DL-40** — Create the secret file under `umask 077` rather than relying on the `chmod` alone, and keep the `chmod` as well. **Superseded in part by DL-55**, which applies the umask to a `mktemp` file that is renamed over `.env`, so no umask save-and-restore around the heredoc remains. | (a) Rely on the `chmod` alone. (b) Set the umask once at the top of the script. (c) Create the file with a restricted-mode command, then append. | The process umask was measured at 0022, which creates the file at mode 0644 and leaves it readable until the `chmod` lands — the window the finding names. With the umask in place the file is created at 0600, proven by neutralising the `chmod` and re-running. Option (b) would also narrow every other artifact the script creates, which is wider than the finding. Option (c) changes the heredoc shape and still leaves a smaller window. The `chmod` stays because truncating an existing `.env` preserves its old mode, so the umask alone cannot repair a file that is already permissive. | The umask is restored from a saved value with only the heredoc between the two statements, and the failure branch exits the process, so no later step inherits 0077. The generated `.env` legitimately matches the repository credential scan, which is why that gate is measured against the tracked tree rather than a working directory after a run. |
| **DL-41** — Propagate failure with an explicit check after each provisioning step, reading `${PIPESTATUS[1]}` for the piped psql call. | (a) A global `set -e` or `set -euo pipefail`. (b) An `ERR` trap. (c) `set -e` inside a subshell around the function. (d) Test `$?` after each command. | The finding is that a failure is followed by a success message. Options (a) and (b) change the failure behaviour of every untouched function in the script, including the software check and the migration step, which the authoring contract and the Minimal Change Clause both rule out. Option (c) loses which step failed and produces no actionable message. Option (d) is equivalent for the plain calls, while `if ! cmd` cannot read a stale status. For the pipeline, `$?` happens to carry psql's status today because psql is the last stage, so naming the element keeps that true if a stage is ever appended. Three failure paths were exercised end to end — an existing database, pre-existing roles, and a wrong owner credential — and each printed its own remedy and exited 1. | Coverage is per-step and hand-maintained, so a step added later without a check reintroduces the finding; the script carries no global guard by design. A failure exits rather than rolling back, so a half-provisioned database can remain, which is why each message names the objects to drop before retrying. |
| **DL-42** — Delete the three inline version-selection comments from `backend/requirements.txt` and add nothing in their place. | (a) Keep them and add a cross-reference to this log. (b) Replace them with terse `SEC-NN` markers. (c) Move the text into this log. | Rule 1 puts rationale here and nowhere else, so option (a) still duplicates it. The manifest is not a security-control site, so option (b) would attach a threat marker to a version pin. Option (c) required no work: the same reasoning already sits at DL-01, DL-02, DL-03 and DL-06, and the file's own header already points here. The diff is three comment-only hunks, the requirement lines are byte-identical to the previous commit, resolution is unchanged, and the audit gate still reports no known vulnerabilities with fifteen ignored. | A reader of the manifest alone no longer sees why the framework is held at 0.125.0, pydantic on the 1.x line, or bcrypt below 5.0.0, and could raise a pin. Contained by the header pointer and the four rows above. The manifest carries nineteen requirement lines both before and after this comment-only change. A reader working from the eighteen-line authoring contract should read DL-01: it records why a nineteenth line was added, and why the extras spelling that would have avoided it was rejected. |
| **DL-43** — Leave transport encryption out of the provisioning bootstrap and out of the generated `.env`. | (a) Append `sslmode=require` to the bootstrap connection string and to the generated `DATABASE_URL`. (b) Add a `DB_SSLMODE` line to the four-variable heredoc. | A local server started by a developer has no TLS configured, so option (a) makes provisioning fail on a stock installation. Option (b) is excluded by the file's authoring contract, which fixes the heredoc at four variables and assigns the transport setting to the settings class. Encryption is enforced where the requirements place it: the engine passes `sslmode` from `DB_SSLMODE` for PostgreSQL URLs only, the template declares it, and the container topology carries `DB_SSLMODE=disable` as the documented local exception. On the verification instance `require` and `prefer` both negotiated TLSv1.3 while `disable` was plaintext, so the setting is live where it is declared. | The bootstrap's own loopback connection inherits libpq's `prefer`. More consequentially, the generated `.env` declares no `DB_SSLMODE`, so the application falls back to the class default of `require` and refuses to connect to a local server without TLS until the developer adds `DB_SSLMODE=disable`. That mismatch is pre-existing and outside this milestone; it is recorded here because provisioning succeeding while the application then fails to connect reads as a defect rather than as configuration. |
| **DL-44** — Record that `GET /listings/` answers 500 to a negative `skip` value, and leave the endpoint's input domain unchanged. | (a) Bound the parameter with `Query(0, ge=0)`. (b) Clamp a negative value to zero inside the handler. (c) Leave the observation unrecorded. | Runtime validation of this milestone probed boundary values and found that `skip=-1` reaches the database as a negative `OFFSET`, which PostgreSQL rejects with `InvalidRowCountInResultOffsetClause`, surfacing as a 500. The error boundary behaved correctly: the traceback, the SQL text, the table names and the interpreter paths all stayed in the server log, and the client received the same three-key sanitized envelope as every other failure. This is a robustness gap, not an information-disclosure defect. It is also pre-existing — the change under review touches the filter create path and never the listing read path — and no finding in the review names it. Options (a) and (b) both narrow or rewrite the accepted input domain of the public read path the requirements freeze, including its `skip` and `limit` pagination. A value-range rule is a business rule the Minimal Change Clause keeps out of a security pass, exactly as DL-23 records for the rent field. | A public unauthenticated endpoint returns 500 rather than 422 for a negative offset, which is an availability and error-correctness gap that remains open. The observed client body was `{"detail":"Internal server error","error_id":"<hex>","fields":[]}` with no leakage, so nothing is disclosed. **Review trigger: the next milestone that owns `backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py` beyond its import repair** — the minimal fix is a lower bound of zero on `skip`, and optionally a lower bound and cap on `limit`. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-36.** Measured before the change on PostgreSQL 13.23, the public schema ACL read `{postgres=UC/postgres,=UC/postgres,app_owner=UC/postgres,app_user=U/postgres}`; the bare entry is PUBLIC holding USAGE and CREATE, and the application role used it to create a table it then owned.
- **DL-37.** The bootstrap imports the same `Base` the application uses and runs `create_all` against the owner URL, so the schema comes from the single authority and every table is owned by the owner role — measured as six tables, all `app_owner`.
- **DL-37, credential custody on the bootstrap invocation.** The credential travels as an inline environment assignment, so it appears in `/proc/<pid>/environ` at mode 0400 and never in `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` at mode 0444; the captured python argument list is a single `-`.
- **DL-37, import preconditions.** Before DL-62, `main` installed dependencies outside the virtual environment from a `requirements.txt` path that does not exist, so the bootstrap could not import SQLAlchemy on any run, in order or alone. DL-62 fixed the order and the manifest path, and DL-63 proves the three imports before the first database object exists, so a missing dependency now aborts the run with nothing provisioned rather than leaving a renamed database and two roles behind.
