## 10. Security code review remediation decisions (F-01 through F-09)

`backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, the four schema
modules, `backend/app/core/config.py`, `backend/app/db/database.py`, `backend/app/main.py`,
`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, `.gitignore`

A security code review of the checkpoint returned nine findings, referenced below by their review
identifiers `F-01` through `F-09`. Two rows in section 5 are **superseded** here: DL-21, which left
the nested `Criteria` model permissive, and DL-22, which kept coercive request typing. Both were
defensible when written and both are now defects the review measured, so the rows below record the
reversal rather than quietly overwriting it. Every measurement cited was taken on CPython 3.9.25
against the live PostgreSQL 13 instance on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-45** (F-01) — Verify the client-supplied payment reference with PayPal inside `process_payment` and return `False` on every unverified path, deviating from the instruction that the wrapper be self-contained with no SDK call and no network I/O. | (a) Keep the local shape check the file's authoring contract prescribes. (b) Return `False` unconditionally until a payment flow exists. (c) Verify inside `subscriptions.py` instead, leaving the wrapper untouched. | The prescribed shape check authorizes a payment from an attacker-controlled string: `process_payment('attacker-controlled-token', 0.01)` returned `True`, and the subscription endpoint treats that Boolean as proof of payment. No AAP body section excludes provider verification — 0.5.13 fixes only the wrapper's signature and truthiness, and 0.5.9 is explicitly about payment-path security — so the finding governs the file prompt. Option (b) closes the hole but discards the adapter's purpose. Option (c) moves an authorization decision into a route whose contract is frozen and leaves the reusable wrapper unsafe for the next caller. | The SDK exposes no request timeout, so a slow provider occupies a worker thread until its own socket timeout. Local and CI stacks carry placeholder PayPal credentials, so verification always fails there and no subscription can be created — the safe direction, and consistent with the endpoint already being unable to persist a record for unrelated reasons. |
| **DL-46** (F-01) — Look the reference up as a `Payment` first and then as a `BillingAgreement`, accepting either. | (a) Payment only, matching the two existing functions in the module. (b) Billing agreement only. (c) Branch on the reference prefix, treating `I-` as an agreement. | The browser obtains `data.subscriptionID` from PayPal Buttons, so the value the backend receives is an agreement identifier, while the rest of the module speaks `Payment`. A payment-only lookup would leave the control permanently fail-closed for the real client flow, which is safe but useless. Option (c) hardcodes an identifier convention PayPal does not contract. | The miss path costs two provider round trips. The agreement amount is read from `plan.payment_definitions[].amount.value`, a shape exercised only against a constructed fixture because no sandbox credentials are available here; any deviation from that shape fails closed rather than approving. |
| **DL-47** (F-01) — Require exact equality, at two decimal places, between the charged amount and the summed totals the provider reports. | (a) Accept a provider total greater than or equal to the charge. (b) Compare floats directly. (c) Allow a small tolerance. (d) Compare only the first transaction. | A `>=` comparison lets a one-cent authorization satisfy any charge, which is the same fail-open shape the finding describes. Float equality is unreliable for money. A tolerance is an unbounded discount with no upper bound stated anywhere. Summing every transaction or payment definition prevents a split-total bypass that comparing only the first entry would allow. | Currency is not compared, so an authorization whose numeric total matches in a different currency would pass. The application charges a single currency today; a multi-currency price list needs a currency test added here. |
| **DL-48** (F-02) — Count the login attempt and decide admission inside one lock acquisition, before the password is verified, and clear the counter on success. | (a) Keep the check-then-record split but hold the lock across credential verification. (b) Take a per-account lock. (c) Retry a compare-and-swap loop on an unlocked map. | The split let twenty concurrent attempts at the threshold minus one all pass: the read released the lock, then every request verified in parallel and recorded afterwards. Holding one global lock across the roughly 250 ms bcrypt verification would serialize every login in the worker. Per-account locks need their own lifecycle and unbounded map, which is more state than the finding warrants. Reserving before verification makes the counter monotonic under concurrency: twenty concurrent attempts at the threshold minus one now yield one 401 and nineteen 429s. | An attempt that fails for an unrelated reason, such as a database outage, still consumes an allowance. Counter state stays per-worker and non-durable, which the review explicitly accepts. |
| **DL-49** (F-02) — Never evict an entry that has reached the limit; when the map is full and nothing else can be freed, deny the new account's attempt. | (a) Keep evicting the earliest-expiring entry regardless of its count. (b) Raise the cap. (c) Evict the newest entry. (d) Allow the attempt and skip tracking it. | Evicting by expiry alone let an attacker flood the map with fresh keys and drop a victim's active lockout, which is the second half of the finding. Raising the cap moves the same bypass to a larger number. Option (d) is the bypass restated: an untracked attempt is an unlimited attempt. | An attacker who drives 4,096 distinct accounts to the limit denies tracking slots to any further new account, whose first attempt then returns 429 until an entry expires. Reaching that state needs roughly 4,096 distinct source addresses, because the address-keyed limiter allows five attempts per address per window. Durable or shared lockout state is out of scope for this pass. |
| **DL-50** (F-03) — Require the declared JSON type on every writable field: `StrictStr` and `StrictInt` where the type is exact. Money fields take a `pre=True` guard that accepts a JSON number and rejects strings and booleans; date-time fields take a `pre=True` guard that requires an ISO-8601 string. **Supersedes DL-22.** | (a) DL-22's position: keep v1 coercion because no requirement asked for strict types. (b) Set a strict base configuration on the create models. (c) Annotate money fields `StrictFloat` alone. | The review rates the coercion a MAJOR CWE-20 defect, which overrides the earlier judgement. Option (b) would also make `EmailStr` and the read models strict, widening the change beyond the write path. Option (c) is wrong in a way that only testing reveals: `StrictFloat` rejects a legitimate JSON integer such as `2500`, so the guard-plus-`float` pairing is the accurate contract. The date-time guard is not optional either — pydantic reads `100` as a Unix timestamp and `True` as one second past the epoch, so a boolean would otherwise become a valid date. | A client that previously sent numeric strings now receives 422. That is the finding's intent, and no frontend code posts to these endpoints today. Read models keep permissive coercion, so ORM-sourced values are unaffected. |
| **DL-51** (F-03) — Give `subscription.py` its own copy of the numeric and date-time guards rather than importing them from `listing.py`. | (a) Import both helpers from `backend.app.schema.listing`. (b) Add a shared `backend/app/schema/strict.py`. (c) Write the guards inline as a method validator per field. | A listing-to-subscription import invents a dependency the domain does not have and makes an unrelated schema module a load-bearing import for payments. A shared module is not in the AAP file inventory. Each guard is a three-line type test with no policy in it, so the duplication carries almost no divergence risk, and option (c) would repeat the same body more times than the module-level form does. | Two copies must change together if the guard contract ever changes. The names are module-private, so nothing outside each file can depend on them. |
| **DL-52** (F-04) — Add `extra = "forbid"` to the shared nested `Criteria` model. **Supersedes DL-21.** | (a) DL-21's position: leave it permissive because no mass-assignment path exists. (b) Add a separate `CriteriaCreate` used only by `FilterCreate`, leaving the shared read model untouched. | The review rates the silent drop a contract defect: a criterion carrying `id` or `filter_id` returned success while discarding both. Option (b) is the more conservative structure, but it adds a model to work around a read path that cannot reach nested validation anyway. `Filter.from_orm` raises `ConfigError` because `Filter` declares no `orm_mode`, verified directly, and constructing `Filter` from clean nested dictionaries still validates after the change. In-place hardening is therefore the smaller change with no measured read-path regression. | If a later lane gives `Filter` and `Criteria` `orm_mode=True`, ORM attribute keys would then meet `extra = "forbid"` during nested validation. That lane must either introduce `CriteriaCreate` or scope the setting; recording it here means the interaction is read, not discovered. |
| **DL-53** (F-05) — Validate the authority component with `urlsplit` host and port semantics: reject a trailing or repeated colon, resolve `.port` inside `try/except ValueError`, and require 1 to 65535. Require either an IP literal or a DNS name whose labels are 1 to 63 characters of `[a-z0-9-]` not starting or ending with a hyphen. **Extends DL-19.** | (a) Keep the reconstruction and character-set tests alone. (b) Match the whole origin with a regular expression. (c) Accept any port token because the browser will never send a malformed one. | Measurement decided this. `urlsplit(...).port` raises `ValueError` for `:abc` and `:99999` but returns `None` for a trailing colon, and port `0` parses cleanly; `example..com`, `-bad-.com`, `example.com.` and `bad_host.com` all survive `urlsplit` untouched. Each of those was accepted before the change and each is rejected after it, so the startup failure SEC-03 exists to produce now happens for a malformed allow-list. A regular expression would reintroduce pattern matching into the one path SEC-03 keeps free of it; option (c) misses the point, since the risk is a misconfigured deployment, not a hostile browser. | No IDNA conversion is performed, so an internationalised host must be supplied in punycode. A syntactically valid but unresolvable name is still accepted, which is a deployment concern rather than a validation one. Exact-equality matching, no reflection and no prefix logic are unchanged. |
| **DL-54** (F-09) — Couple the `SECRET_KEY` floor to the configured algorithm — 32, 48 and 64 UTF-8 bytes for HS256, HS384 and HS512 — through a root validator, keeping `min_length=32` as the character-count baseline. | (a) Leave the flat 32-character floor. (b) Raise the floor to 64 for every algorithm. (c) Restrict `ALGORITHM` to HS256 so one floor suffices. | RFC 7518 section 3.2 requires a key at least as long as the hash output, so a 32-byte key under HS512 is half the required strength — the finding's point. A flat 64 would reject valid HS256 deployments and contradict the documented 32-character minimum. Option (c) removes an operator choice the settings contract already offers and would be a wider behavioural change than the finding asks for. | `min_length` counts characters while the floor counts encoded bytes, so a multibyte key can satisfy one and fail the other; `.env.example` documents both. Raising `ALGORITHM` without lengthening the key now aborts startup, which is the intended fail-closed direction. DL-25's twenty-eight character placeholder still fails on `min_length` first, so that row stands unchanged. |
| **DL-55** (F-06) — Write the generated secrets to a `mktemp` file created under `umask 077` in the target directory, then `mv -f` it over `.env`. | (a) `touch .env && chmod 600 .env` before the heredoc. (b) `install -m 600 /dev/null .env`, then append. (c) Enable `noclobber` so an existing path is refused. (d) Write under `/tmp` and copy into place. | Options (a) and (b) still resolve an existing symlink at `.env` and write through it; `rename(2)` replaces the directory entry instead, so the symlink is discarded. Option (c) aborts setup rather than provisioning it and leaves the attacker's symlink in place. Option (d) crosses a filesystem boundary, which turns the move into a copy — no longer atomic — and exposes the secret outside the project directory on the way. Measured contrast: the pre-fix script wrote 212 bytes of secrets through a planted symlink and then changed the attacker's file to mode 600, while the current script leaves that file empty at mode 644. | The transient `.env.tmp.*` name lives in the repository root for the duration of the write, which is why `.gitignore` gained that pattern. The pattern is `.env.tmp.*` and not `.env.*` because the latter would also ignore the tracked `.env.example`. |
| **DL-56** (F-07) — Send the two role passwords to psql on standard input as `\set` lines emitted by the `printf` builtin, and drop both `-v` arguments. | (a) Keep `-v owner_pw=…`. (b) Write the SQL and the values to a temporary file and run `psql -f`. (c) Use `echo` or an external `printf` binary. (d) Export the values and read them with a psql variable from the environment. | Arguments are world-readable through the process list: the pre-fix invocation exposed both values, confirmed by reading `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` inside a stub psql and by a full process snapshot taken at that instant. The current invocation reads `psql -v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -d dbname`, and neither value appears anywhere in that snapshot. A temporary SQL file reintroduces exactly the on-disk secret file F-06 exists to remove. A builtin forks no process, so it has no argument list of its own; an external binary would. psql offers no environment-variable equivalent of `\set`, so option (d) does not exist. Verified against the live server that `\set` over standard input preserves `:'var'` literal quoting for the generated alphabet. | The values remain readable to anything that can already read the psql process's memory or attach to it, which is the ordinary trust boundary for a local provisioning script. A shell trace would echo them, so tracing must stay off; the script sets none. |
| **DL-57** (F-08) — Withhold driver message text as well as bound parameters from database-error diagnostics: set `hide_parameters=True` on the engine, re-assert it on the raised exception, and log the exception type, the driver exception type, SQLSTATE, the route, the correlation identifier and the traceback frames. | (a) The review's literal minimum: `hide_parameters=True` plus `exc_info=exc`. (b) Scrub the formatted text against sensitive patterns. (c) Drop the traceback and log a single line. | Option (a) leaves a measurable leak. With parameter hiding enabled the `[parameters: …]` block is redacted and the password hash disappears. For a unique violation, psycopg2's own `DETAIL: Key (email)=(…)` line still carried the email into the log, because that text is a driver diagnostic rather than a bound parameter. Logging frames plus type, origin and SQLSTATE keeps everything the finding asks to retain — type, traceback, route, correlation identifier — while removing every provider-supplied string. Option (b) cannot enumerate what a driver may echo. Option (c) discards the diagnostics the finding explicitly preserves. | The driver's human-readable message and the violated constraint name are no longer logged, so triage relies on SQLSTATE, `23505` for a unique violation, and the driver exception class, `UniqueViolation`. `handle_unhandled_exception` still uses `exc_info`; the review passed that handler and F-08 does not cover it, so widening the change there would trace to no finding. |


---

## 11. Login throttle shape and error-boundary ownership (SEC-07, SEC-08)

`backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/app/main.py`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

Review of the throttle in operation showed that the control counted the wrong events and reported
them twice. Both are closed here. Every value below was measured against the pinned closure on
CPython 3.9.25 and a running uvicorn worker on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-58** — Drop the pre-handler `@limiter.limit` decorator from `POST /auth/login` and keep the address window in the same in-process map as the account window, under the `addr:` and `acct:` key prefixes. Retain the limiter, `SlowAPIMiddleware` and the `RateLimitExceeded` handler registration. | (a) Keep the decorator alongside the account counter. (b) Keep the decorator and drop the account counter. (c) Remove the limiter, the middleware and the 429 handler as well. | The decorator counts requests, not failures, so five successful logins from one address exhausted the window and the sixth returned 429 — measured as `[200, 200, 200, 200, 200, 429]` before the change and `[200 × 6]` after. Six consecutive failures still return `[401, 401, 401, 401, 401, 429]`, which is the criterion the requirements state, and a second identity from the same address is still refused, so no coverage was lost. Option (a) keeps the successful-login lockout. Option (b) drops the address dimension the requirements ask for. Option (c) would remove the uniform 429 envelope and the registration the file map calls for, and it would leave any future throttled route without a limiter to decorate. | With no decorated route the middleware enforces nothing today, so the limiter object exists for the handler contract and for future routes rather than for live enforcement. Both windows remain in-process and per worker, which SEC-07 has always been a partial remediation for. **Superseded by DL-292.** |
| **DL-59** — Raise a typed `AccountThrottled` carrying only a 16-hex account marker and let the global error boundary emit the single record, instead of logging the throttled attempt in the endpoint. | (a) Log in the endpoint and raise a bare `HTTPException`. (b) Carry the marker on `request.state` and read it in the boundary. (c) Log in both places. | The endpoint record named the client address and was written under no correlation identifier, so one throttled attempt produced two log lines and neither could be joined to the reference the client received. Routing the marker through the exception gives one record per response, keyed by the `error_id` in the body, and removes the address and the submitted email from the record. Measured: 12 error responses produced exactly 12 records, with zero IPv4 literals and zero email matches across them. Option (a) keeps the double record. Option (b) needs a second mechanism for a value the exception can carry itself. Option (c) is the defect. | Triage now depends on the client quoting its `error_id`; there is no per-address record of a throttled attempt in the application log, and the reverse proxy or the server access log remains the only place a client address appears. |
| **DL-60** — Keep the dependency audit gate in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and this log in the repository, and remove the one suppression entry the manifest made stale. **Superseded in part by DL-61: the manifest now declares `python-dotenv` again, so that entry is live and has been restored.** | (a) Restore the workflow to its pre-remediation content and delete this log, on the grounds that both are scheduled later in the file map. (b) Keep the gate and leave the stale entry in place. | Both files are named as in-scope deliverables carrying the audit gate, the repository scans and the Rule 1 rationale, and the gate is the only mechanism that keeps an unpatchable advisory from silently re-entering the closure. `PYSEC-2026-2270` covered `python-dotenv`, which left the closure when the manifest pinned `pydantic` without the `dotenv` extra, and the gate fails the build when a suppression matches no live advisory. The pre-change list reported `stale entries detected: ['PYSEC-2026-2270']` and exited 1, so option (b) turns the manifest pin into a build failure. Option (a) removes a control and a required artifact to satisfy a sequencing preference. | The suppression register and the workflow list must be re-measured together; a future pin change that alters the closure can make another entry stale, which the gate reports as a failure rather than a warning. |

## 12. Dotenv file-loading contract and provisioning bootstrap order (F-01, F-02)

`backend/requirements.txt`, `.env.example`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`

A compensating review found the two paths a developer is told to follow both fail on a clean
install: the template's copy-to-`.env` instruction, and the provisioning script's owner bootstrap.
Both are closed here. Every value below was measured on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure
and a clean virtual environment on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-61** — Restore `PYSEC-2026-2270` to the suppression list in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, taking the list from fourteen entries to fifteen, in the same change that declares `python-dotenv==1.2.1`. | (a) Declare the package and leave the list at fourteen. (b) Pin a lower `python-dotenv` release in the hope of avoiding the advisory. (c) Keep the package undeclared so the list needs no entry. | The gate fails the build in both directions, which is what makes the pairing mandatory rather than tidy: an unsuppressed finding fails `pip-audit --strict`, and DL-08's cross-check fails when a suppression matches nothing. Option (a) therefore turns a one-line manifest change into a red pipeline. Option (b) does not exist as an escape: the advisory range is introduced at 0 and every release from 1.0.1 through 1.2.1 was audited directly, each reporting the same identifier. Option (c) is the state the review rejected as a major defect. Register row 15 carries the reachability evidence — the affected write helpers are never called from `backend/`. | The suppression list grows by one, and the shared review trigger still applies: on a Python 3.10 or newer runtime, `python-dotenv` 1.2.2 installs and this entry must be removed rather than carried forward. A pin change that drops the package again makes the entry stale, which the cross-check reports as a build failure — the same coupling recorded at DL-60. **Superseded by DL-93.** |
| **DL-62** — Create and activate the virtual environment before installing dependencies, install the tracked `backend/requirements.txt` with `python3 -m pip`, and stop the run when either step fails. | (a) Leave the order and the install path alone and let the owner bootstrap fail, which is the reviewed state. (b) Have the bootstrap install its own dependencies. (c) Point the bootstrap at an absolute interpreter path, such as the repository virtual environment, instead of the active one. (d) Drop the owner bootstrap and grant the application role CREATE so startup can build the schema. | Order and path were both wrong, and together they made SEC-11's owner bootstrap unreachable on every run. `pip3 install -r requirements.txt` names a file that does not exist; the tracked manifest is `backend/requirements.txt`. It also ran before `setup_virtual_env`, so nothing was installed, and nothing would have landed in the virtual environment had it been. `python3 -m pip` binds the install to the active interpreter, which after activation is the virtual environment's — the same `python3` the bootstrap heredoc runs. Option (a) leaves SEC-11's schema-ownership control inoperative. Option (b) duplicates dependency installation inside a database function. Option (c) hard-codes a path the script does not own and breaks for a developer using a different environment. Option (d) is the defect SEC-11 exists to remove. | The install step now aborts on any resolver or network failure where before it continued silently, so a developer behind a broken mirror gets no `.env` and no database. `npm install` stayed unguarded here and was guarded later by DL-179, which also moved it into a subshell. `main` remains without `set -e`, so each guard is explicit. |
| **DL-323** — Deviate from the change contract that exempts `install_dependencies` and `setup_virtual_env` as pre-existing functional defects, and change both. Narrowed out of DL-62 so the install fix and the scope deviation it required are each one decision. | (a) Honour the exemption and leave both functions alone, which keeps the owner bootstrap unreachable. (b) Reproduce the working install inside `init_database` so neither exempt function is touched. | The exemption stopped being available once SEC-11's owner bootstrap started depending on both functions: an exempt defect that disables an in-scope control is no longer out of scope. Option (a) preserves the exemption and the inoperative control together. Option (b) keeps the letter of the exemption by duplicating dependency installation inside a database function, which is DL-62's option (b) and worse than the deviation. Every changed line traces to review finding F-02. | The deviation is deliberate and recorded rather than silent. Both functions keep the rest of their pre-existing behaviour, including the unguarded `npm install` DL-322 records. |
| **DL-63** — Prove that `sqlalchemy`, `psycopg2` and `backend.app.db.models` all import with the running interpreter before `init_database` creates its first database object. | (a) Verify after `createdb`, next to the bootstrap that needs the imports. (b) Verify only `sqlalchemy` and the models package. (c) Add a cleanup path that drops the database and both roles when a later step fails. (d) Add no check and rely on the operator reading the traceback. | An interpreter carrying SQLAlchemy 2.0.51 but no `psycopg2` passed a two-import check. The run then created `dbname`, created both roles, transferred database ownership and revoked PUBLIC CREATE before failing inside `create_engine`. That is exactly the partial state the finding describes, and the application role cannot repair it because schema CREATE is revoked. Adding the driver import moved the same failure to before `createdb`, with the cluster left empty of `dbname`, `app_owner` and `app_user`. Option (a) preserves the partial state. Option (c) would have to tell objects it created from objects that already existed, and would hold the owner credential open across a failure path. Option (d) leaves the operator to clean up by hand. | The check proves importability, not connectivity: a wrong host, port or password still fails at `create_all` after the roles exist, and the guard's message then points at the wrong remedy. It also starts one extra interpreter per run. |
| **DL-64** — Reach the Starlette HTTP exception class through `fastapi.exceptions.StarletteHTTPException` and keep `starlette` out of the manifest. | (a) Add the direct pin `starlette==0.49.3` so the import names a declared dependency. (b) Keep importing `starlette.exceptions` directly and leave the package undeclared. (c) Hand-pin the whole advisory-bearing transitive set — `starlette`, `ecdsa`, `urllib3`, `click` — so every import site names a declared pin. | A review raised the import of an undeclared transitive package as a defect and offered two remedies: declare it, or stop importing it. The manifest contract takes the second choice out of the abstract, because it forbids pinning any transitive by hand and requires the resolution to supply `starlette` 0.49.3. Option (a) contradicts the manifest contract to fix an import style. Option (b) leaves the application importing a package nothing declares. Option (c) multiplies that contradiction and would freeze packages whose versions the resolution already fixes. | The re-export is a FastAPI convenience name rather than a documented stability contract, so a future FastAPI that dropped it would break the import at startup — loudly, and only on a pin change, since `fastapi` is pinned exactly. The closure's `starlette` version is still fixed only indirectly, which is the lock-file gap DL-15 accepts. |
| **DL-65** — Keep the audit gate in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and this log at their current content, and keep maintaining both, rather than reverting them to the pre-remediation state because this checkpoint's file list does not name them. | (a) Revert both to baseline and re-add the same content at the checkpoint that schedules them. (b) Revert the workflow only, keeping the log. (c) Keep both but stop updating them until their scheduled checkpoint. | Both files are named deliverables of the change set: the workflow carries the dependency audit gate and the repository scans, and this log is the artifact Rule 1 requires. Reverting them removes a control and a mandatory artifact to satisfy an ordering preference, which DL-60 already settled on the same grounds. Two further facts make reversion actively harmful now. The compensating review read this log as an authority and required corrections to it, so an empty log would have failed that review outright. And the manifest change recorded at DL-01 cannot ship without the workflow's fifteenth suppression entry: reverting the workflow, or freezing it under option (c), turns a one-line manifest change into a red pipeline. | Both files were changed ahead of the checkpoint that schedules them, so a reviewer comparing this milestone's file list against the diff sees two files the list does not name; that ordering deviation is what this row exists to record. |
| **DL-66** — Leave the browser-storage scan over `frontend/src/` reading three hits at this checkpoint and change no frontend file. | (a) Delete the three `localStorage` calls in `frontend/src/services/auth.ts` now so the scan reads zero. (b) Narrow the scan's path so it reports zero while the calls stay. | That file is an AAP operation no checkpoint has processed. No finding assigns them: one review records them as a sibling handoff and not a current Rule finding, another lists them among the observations that are pre-existing and unchanged, and the compensating review places the unprocessed files out of scope. Option (a) edits outside this pass's boundary, which is the out-of-boundary work DL-65 records a review objecting to. Option (b) would report a clean gate over unclean code. | The scan stays non-zero until the frontend operation is processed, so the end-state value of zero is not met at this milestone. `getToken()` stays dead code reading a key nothing writes, and any caller of it receives null rather than a token. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-63.** Measured on a throwaway PostgreSQL 13.23 cluster, option (b) is not enough. Verified after the fix on the same throwaway cluster: six tables and six sequences owned by `app_owner`, `app_user` holding SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on all six plus sequence usage, `has_schema_privilege('app_user','public','CREATE')` false, and a `CREATE TABLE` as `app_user` refused with `permission denied for schema public`.
- **DL-64.** Measured on the pinned closure, `fastapi.exceptions.StarletteHTTPException is starlette.exceptions.HTTPException` is **True** at fastapi 0.125.0 with starlette 0.49.3, so the re-export is the same class object and the handler registration is unchanged.
- **DL-65.** The workflow's only change against the pre-remediation baseline remains the inserted audit step, and it must be re-measured together with the suppression register whenever a pin changes the closure.
- **DL-66.** Its last commit is the original sample import, `git status` shows it unmodified,
  and all three hits sit on the lines the AAP records for it, so the measurement is the
  pre-remediation baseline rather than a regression. The server half of SEC-06 is already in
  place and re-measured here — both auth routes set `access_token` with `httponly`, `secure`
  from `COOKIE_SECURE`, `samesite=strict` and `path=/`, `get_current_user` reads that cookie
  before the bearer header, and `POST /auth/logout` clears it. So no token needs to reach
  browser storage for the flow to work.

## 13. Listing read-path input bounds, nullable response fields, and the unhandled-exception CORS boundary (F-01, F-02, SEC-03, SEC-08)

`backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py`, `backend/app/schema/listing.py`, `backend/app/main.py`

Runtime validation of the listing read path closed the review trigger DL-44 set for itself and found
the gap wider than that row described. Browser validation of the error boundary then found one
further gap, which this section records and deliberately leaves open. Every value below was measured
on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure, on PostgreSQL 13.23 over TLSv1.3 and on SQLite, and in
headless Chrome from an allow-listed origin, on 2026-07-31.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-67** — Bound both pagination parameters at the request boundary with `Query(0, ge=0, le=2**63-1)` on `skip` and `Query(100, ge=0, le=2**63-1)` on `limit`, superseding DL-44. | (a) Leave DL-44 standing and the parameters unbounded. (b) Clamp an out-of-range value to the nearest legal one inside the handler. (c) Bound `skip` only, as DL-44's minimum. | DL-44 recorded the unbounded state, named its review trigger — the next milestone owning this file beyond its import repair — and nominated a lower bound of zero on `skip` plus an optional bound and cap on `limit`. A negative `skip` was the smallest of the shapes; the evidence below records the two shapes measured on the two engines. The ceiling is the signed 64-bit range the `OFFSET` and `LIMIT` bindings accept, so the bound is the driver's own domain rather than an invented business rule — which is what separates this from the rent-field value range DL-23 keeps out of a security pass. Option (a) leaves a public unauthenticated endpoint answering 500 to a client-controlled integer. Option (b) silently rewrites a caller's value, which hides the error rather than reporting it. Option (c) leaves the engine-dependent divergence on `limit` open, and that is the more dangerous of the two because it fails silently. | The accepted input domain of the frozen public read path narrows: an over-wide or negative value now draws 422 rather than a 500 or, on SQLite, every row. Both prior behaviours were defects, and the frozen contract is the pagination itself, which keeps its names, positions, defaults and meaning. DL-44's review trigger is discharged and that row superseded; read the two together. **Superseded by DL-187**, which reverts these bounds and restores the frozen signature. |
| **DL-68** — Set `orm_mode` on the `Listing` response model and declare its seven nullable columns `Optional` with a `None` default, deviating from that file's per-file change contract. | (a) Leave the model unchanged. (b) Add `orm_mode` alone. (c) Have the endpoint assemble dictionaries instead of calling `from_orm`. | `GET /listings/` returned 500 for every non-empty table, so the public read path was inoperative whenever data existed. Two independent causes had to be closed together. The endpoint calls `Listing.from_orm(...)`, which pydantic v1 refuses without `orm_mode = True`, raising `ConfigError`; and seven of the eleven mapped columns are nullable at `models.py:26-32` while the model declared them required, so any row carrying a NULL failed validation even once `from_orm` was permitted. Option (a) leaves the whole endpoint returning 500. Option (b) closes the `ConfigError` and leaves the NULL failure, which is the more common case. Option (c) rewrites the endpoint body and abandons the response contract the schema exists to define. | This file's per-file change contract did not schedule a response-model change, so the deviation is recorded here rather than left implicit, as Rule 1 requires. The added fields are `Optional` with a `None` default, so a fully populated row's key set is unchanged and a sparse row gains explicit nulls rather than omitted keys. |
| **DL-69** — Record that a 500 raised through the generic exception handler reaches the client without CORS response headers, and change no middleware wiring at this milestone. **Superseded by DL-176, which shipped option (c).** | (a) Re-apply the CORS response headers inside `handle_unhandled_exception`. (b) Register the generic handler under the integer key `500` instead of `Exception`. (c) Catch the generic exception in a middleware nested inside `CORSMiddleware`. (d) Leave the observation unrecorded. | The cause is upstream wiring, not application code: Starlette's `build_middleware_stack` routes the `Exception` and `500` keys into `ServerErrorMiddleware` and places that middleware outside `user_middleware`, where `CORSMiddleware` sits, so the response never passes through the layer that would add the headers. Option (a) duplicates the middleware's origin-matching logic in the error boundary, giving two places that must agree about the allow-list. The Minimal Change Clause keeps (a) through (c) out of this pass. Option (d) leaves a real gap invisible. | A cross-origin single-page application cannot read the correlation identifier for a truly unexpected server error, which weakens the support reference the identifier exists to serve. Nothing is disclosed: the browser withholds a sanitized body rather than revealing more, so the failure direction is closed. |
| **DL-321** — Record separately that the sanitized body is correct and unaffected by the header gap, so the two are reviewed apart. | Treat the header gap and the body as one finding. | They have different remedies and different severities: the body is the disclosure control and it holds, while the headers are a usability property of the correlation identifier. Folding them together would let a reader conclude the envelope itself was at fault. | None. The body is asserted independently by the error-handling suite. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-67.** That milestone arrived, and measurement showed the defect was not one case but two
  shapes on two engines: `2**63` and wider spellings raised `OverflowError` before any query
  ran. And on SQLite `limit=-1` returned every row silently rather than failing, so one
  client-controlled integer was a 500 on one engine and an unbounded full-table read on the
  other. Measured after the fix: all ten out-of-range spellings across both parameters return
  422 naming the offending field in the same three-key envelope, both names appear when both are
  bad, `?skip=1&limit=3` returns the correct three-row slice, `limit=0` returns `[]`. And the
  OpenAPI document publishes `minimum` 0 and `maximum` 9223372036854775807 for both.
- **DL-68.** Measured after the fix on a table seeded with a row whose every nullable column is
  NULL and a second fully populated row: 200 with the null columns serialized as JSON `null`,
  the populated row round-tripping unchanged, the key set matching the schema exactly. An empty
  table still returns `[]`. Verified live against one seeded database from two servers:
  the pre-change tree answers 500 and this tree answers 200 with both rows.
- **DL-69.** Measured across all five registered handlers, four are inside that boundary and
  unaffected — `HTTPException`, `RequestValidationError`, `RateLimitExceeded` and
  `SQLAlchemyError` each return the header, confirmed at 400, 401, 404, 405, 422, 429 and at a
  genuine `SQLAlchemyError` 500 — and only the generic handler is outside it.
  - Option (b) does not work, and that was measured rather than assumed: Starlette treats `500`
    and `Exception` as the same key, so the handler still lands outside the CORS layer and the
    header is still absent.
  - Option (c) is the approach that does work — an inner catch-all middleware returns the
    envelope with the header present. But it converts the error boundary from a handler
    registration into a middleware, restructuring the layer whose uniformity and leak-tightness
    were just re-measured, for a gap no requirement names.
  - The only routes reaching this handler today are the ones already recorded as non-functional
    for schema reasons — `POST /listings/` raising `TypeError` for the absent `owner_id` column,
    and the subscription routes raising `NameError` for the missing `datetime` import — so no
    working flow is affected.
  - **Review trigger: the next milestone that owns `backend/app/main.py`** — the minimal fix is
    option (c), a catch-all nested inside `CORSMiddleware`, and it must be re-measured against
    the CORS and envelope checks together because it moves the boundary both depend on.
