## 14. Security regression-test coverage decisions (SEC-02 through SEC-10, SEC-12)

`backend/tests/conftest.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_*.py`

QA testing found the security suite green at 247 of 247 cases and still unable to notice seventeen
specified behaviours being reverted in the implementation. Every finding was a coverage gap rather
than a product defect, so the decisions below are about what a test must observe, not about what the
application must do. No application file changed at this milestone.

The measurement instrument was a mutation-replay harness: an out-of-tree copy of the tree receives
one exact-anchor edit that removes a specified behaviour, and the suite runs inside the copy. A gap
is closed only when the mutation that previously passed silently now fails. Twenty-three mutations
were replayed; all twenty-three now fail, against a green unmutated control. Every value below was
measured on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure, on SQLite through the harness and live on
PostgreSQL 13.23 over TLSv1.3, and in headless Chrome, on 2026-08-01.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-70** — Reach the project's own coverage command by naming the three pre-existing modules in a `collect_ignore` list in `backend/tests/conftest.py`, and change neither those modules nor the workflow. **Superseded by DL-191, which removed the `collect_ignore` list; the shipped harness filters no collection and the workflow carries `--continue-on-collection-errors` instead.** | (a) Repair the three modules so they import. (b) Narrow the workflow's pytest invocation to `tests/security`. (c) Mark the modules with a collection-time skip inside each file. | The command in the workflow exited 2 with zero tests collected and wrote no coverage artifact, because a collection error in any module aborts the whole session — so the suite the security work depends on had never run under the project's own command. Repairing the modules is explicitly out of scope, and doing it would also hide a genuine breakage behind a passing pipeline. Editing the workflow changes a file this checkpoint does not own to work around a problem the test package can solve in four lines. Option (c) edits the same out-of-scope files option (a) does. | The three modules stay broken and are now excluded rather than merely failing, so their breakage is quieter than before. It remains visible on an explicit path and in the style check, and the exclusion list is the place a future repair must remove itself from. |
| **DL-71** — Put the harness structural check inside the autouse `isolated_state` fixture rather than in a test of its own. | (a) A standalone test asserting the same two facts. (b) A session-scoped check running once. (c) No check, relying on the handful of tests that cross-read a row through `db_session`. | Option (c) was the state QA found, and it was measured to be nearly blind: an override pointing at a second engine escaped 242 of 247 cases, so almost the entire suite would pass against a database it never reads. The guarantee has to hold for every test, and only a per-test autouse hook establishes that — a standalone test proves the wiring once for itself, and a session-scoped check cannot see a per-test rebinding. After the change the same mutation fails all 320 cases at fixture setup with `AssertionError: the override yields a session bound to Engine(sqlite://), not the engine the tests inspect`. | The check costs one dependency-override lookup and one session bind per test. It asserts identity of the override object and of the bound engine, so an override that is the right object bound to the right engine but wrong in some third way still passes. |
| **DL-72** — Correct the settings-injection comment to state which names are assigned outright and which yield to an ambient value, and leave the injection strategy itself unchanged. | (a) Hard-assign every name so the comment's original claim becomes true. (b) Use `setdefault` for every name so the claim becomes true the other way. (c) Delete the comment. | The comment claimed every value lands in the environment before import; the file hard-assigns four names and `setdefault`s twelve. Both halves are deliberate. The four that control which database, which origins and which cookie policy a test sees are assigned so a hostile ambient value cannot redirect the suite; the rest yield because assertions read `settings.*` and an operator must be able to vary them. Making the claim true in either uniform direction would discard one of those two properties to fix a sentence. Deleting the comment removes the only place the asymmetry is explained. | The comment is now a second thing to keep in step with the code; the counts it states (four and twelve) were verified against the file and will drift if a name is added without updating it. |
| **DL-73** — Prove the lost-unique-address race with a fixture that lets the pre-check clear and then fails the commit, and additionally assert the transaction was rolled back. | (a) Monkeypatch `Session.commit` to raise `IntegrityError` unconditionally. (b) Assert only the 400 status and the envelope, without the rollback. (c) Rely on the duplicate-address test alone, since both defences answer 400. | The route holds two independent defences and they answer identically, so a test that only inspects the response cannot tell which one fired — and option (c) was measured to be exactly that blind: removing the commit-time defence left the suite green. The fixture reproduces the real ordering, where no row holds the address when the pre-check runs and one does by the time the commit lands, which is the only path that reaches the handler under test. The rollback assertion matters on its own: a lost race that leaves the transaction open would make the next statement on that connection fail on PostgreSQL. Option (a) forces a failure the route can never actually meet, so it would also pass against code that had lost its pre-check. | The race is simulated rather than genuinely concurrent, so it proves the handler's behaviour and not the database's isolation guarantee. Real concurrency would need two processes and a shared server, which the in-process harness does not provide. |
| **DL-74** — Assert the CORS response headers on a genuine 500 that a handler inside the CORS layer produces, and leave the generic unhandled-exception boundary as DL-69 recorded it. | (a) Assert the headers on a 500 from the generic handler too, which would fail. (b) Assert the headers on no error response at all. (c) Move the error middleware inside the CORS layer so both cases carry them. | DL-69 measured that Starlette places the `Exception` key outside `user_middleware`, so the generic handler's 500 cannot carry the headers, and it deliberately left that open with a review trigger on `main.py`. A test asserting the impossible case would encode a known gap as a failure, and a test asserting no case at all leaves the sanitized-error-inside-CORS wiring unpinned. That gap is measured rather than supposed: moving the sanitizing middleware outside the CORS layer previously left the suite green. Scoping the assertion to a database-error 500, which is registered inside the boundary, pins the wiring that exists without contradicting DL-69. Option (c) is DL-69's own recommended fix and belongs to the milestone that owns `main.py`. | Coverage of the CORS-plus-error interaction stops at the boundary DL-69 describes: the generic handler's 500 stays untested for headers and untested is the honest state, because it would fail. The two rows must be read together. That coverage boundary has since been removed. **Superseded by DL-177.** |
| **DL-75** — Assert that no field name in a malformed-body 422 is digit-only, rather than asserting the `fields` list equals a fixed value. | (a) Assert `fields == []` alone. (b) Assert the exact rendered body string. | The guard under test drops a location entry that is a bare integer, because a JSON parse failure reports a byte offset where a field name belongs and that offset discloses where in the submitted bytes parsing stopped. Asserting only emptiness passes for the wrong reason if a future body carries a legitimate field name, and asserting a whole rendered body couples the test to the correlation identifier. Testing the shape the guard exists to exclude fails precisely when the guard is removed, which is what was measured. | The assertion permits a non-numeric leak, such as a field name derived from the body content, which a stricter allow-list of expected names would catch. Both `fields == []` and the digit-only exclusion are asserted, so the weaker check is not the only one. |
| **DL-76** — Pin cookie precedence with a mirrored pair of accounts, and separate the fail-closed branch from the empty-cookie branch. | (a) A single account pairing, which cannot distinguish precedence from coincidence. (b) Test only that a cookie authenticates, without a competing header. (c) Treat an empty cookie the same as a garbage one. | Precedence is a claim about which of two credentials wins, so it needs both orderings: account A's cookie against account B's header must resolve to A, and the mirror must resolve to B. One pairing alone passes whenever the two happen to agree. The empty-cookie case is genuinely different from the garbage case, and both behaviours are deliberate. The read is `cookies.get(name) or token`, so an empty string falls through to the header and authenticates, while a present-but-unusable cookie is taken and then fails closed with 401 rather than silently falling back. Conflating them would assert one of the two away. | The distinction rests on a falsy-value fallthrough, which is behaviour a future rewrite could change without intending to. It is now pinned in both directions, so such a change fails rather than passing quietly. |
| **DL-77** — Add a second rejection helper that identifies a setting by the text of the message it raises, for rules that span two fields. | (a) Relax the existing helper to accept any error location. (b) Change the application's root validator to report against a single field. | The existing helper asserts the error location names the field, which is right for a field constraint and impossible for a rule spanning two settings: a pydantic root validator reports `loc=('__root__',)` by design. Relaxing the helper would stop it proving the field is named, weakening every existing case to accommodate one new shape. Changing the validator to fake a single-field location would edit working application code to suit a test. | Message text is a weaker anchor than an error location, so a reworded message breaks the test. That is the intended direction: the message is the operator-facing artifact, and the terms asserted are the setting name, the algorithm and both byte counts, all of which an operator needs. |
| **DL-78** — Parametrize the one-byte-below-the-floor cases over the algorithms whose floor exceeds the field constraint, deriving that set from the floor table, and guard the table itself with an exact-value assertion. | (a) Parametrize over every algorithm including HS256. (b) Hard-code the two elevated algorithm names. (c) Drop the field constraint so every floor is reached by the same validator. | Including HS256 is what made the two inherited cases fail: its floor coincides with the field's own minimum length, so a key one byte short trips the field constraint first and the message never names the algorithm. Deriving the set from the table means the parametrization follows the implementation if a fourth algorithm is added, and the exact-value guard on the table keeps that derivation honest by failing if a floor changes silently. Hard-coding the names hides the reason for the exclusion. Removing the field constraint would weaken a real control to simplify a test. | HS256, the algorithm actually configured, is therefore not covered by the one-byte-below case — its floor is covered by the field-constraint cases instead. The guard asserts the exact table `{HS256: 32, HS384: 48, HS512: 64}` and that HS256's floor equals the field minimum, so the two coverage halves cannot drift apart unnoticed. |
| **DL-79** — Record the certificate-verification deferral as a comment beside the transport-mode table in the test module, and add no test and no code change for it. | (a) Add a test asserting the verifying modes are rejected. (b) Change the default transport mode to a verifying one. (c) Leave the deferral unrecorded here. | The finding is informational and describes no defect: both verifying modes are accepted as configuration and need only a root certificate distributed out of band, which the requirements defer. A test asserting they are rejected would assert the opposite of the intended design, and adopting a verifying mode by default would break every deployment lacking a distributed certificate — the deferral itself is the recorded decision. The comment sits where the modes are enumerated, which is where a reader forms the wrong impression. | Encryption without server-identity verification remains the shipped posture, so a caller cannot detect an impostor server. That gap is the one the deferral names, and it is unchanged by this milestone. |
| **DL-80** — Leave one branch of the key-length validator uncovered and pin the invariant that makes it unreachable, rather than contriving a test that reaches it. | (a) Construct settings in a way that reaches the branch. (b) Delete the branch. (c) Report the module's coverage without explaining the residue. | The branch returns early when the algorithm is unknown or the signing key is absent, and neither can occur. The validator is declared to skip when a field has already failed, so the key is always present by the time it runs, and the accepted algorithm domain is exactly the floor table's key set. Reaching it would mean disabling the skip-on-failure behaviour or injecting a value the field validator rejects — testing a state the application cannot enter. Deleting the branch removes a defensive guard that costs nothing. | One line stays uncovered permanently and will show in every coverage report. The compensating assertion is the exact floor table plus the accepted-algorithm domain, so if either widens, the branch becomes reachable and this row's premise fails loudly. |
| **DL-81** — Leave the rate-limiter decorator off `POST /auth/login` and keep the in-process counters as the enforcing control, contrary to the finding's own suggested fix. | (a) Decorate the route with the configured limit, as both the finding and a literal reading of the requirement describe. (b) Decorate it and relax the successful-login test that then breaks. (c) Remove the limiter registration entirely, since nothing decorates a route. | The decorator counts every request rather than every failure. Removing the registration would delete a component the requirements name. | The registered limiter enforces nothing on any route. Its storage is exercised only through the harness reset and its handler only through an injected rejection, so both are now load-bearing rather than decorative, but the component's real purpose stays unrealised. **Review trigger: any move to per-route request-rate limiting**, at which point the successful-login expectation must be re-derived first. **Superseded by DL-292.** |
| **DL-82** — Exercise the counter-map overflow denial by calling the reservation function directly, and cover the prune and eviction paths through the HTTP client with the map cap lowered. | (a) Drive enough distinct client addresses through the client to fill the map naturally. (b) Leave the denial path uncovered. | The denial is unreachable over HTTP by construction: a key already at its limit short-circuits earlier in the same function, so a full map of locked-out keys never reaches the overflow computation through a request. Filling the map naturally would need thousands of distinct addresses per run for a branch a direct call reaches in three. The prune and eviction paths are different — both are reachable through real requests once the cap is lowered, so they are driven that way and only the genuinely unreachable branch is addressed directly. | One assertion depends on a private function's signature and will break if it is renamed. That is preferable to leaving the branch that protects the process against unbounded memory growth untested, and the surrounding cases still drive the same code through the public interface. |
| **DL-83** — Assert the registered limiter's identity and storage class but deliberately not its route-limit registry. | (a) Also assert the registry is empty, which is true today. (b) Assert nothing about the limiter beyond the harness reset emptying it. | Asserting the registry is empty would encode DL-81's decision as a test, so a future team adopting per-route limiting would have to delete a passing security test to move forward — the test would obstruct the improvement rather than protect a property. Identity and storage class are the two facts the harness reset depends on, and they are what the reset assertion needs in order to mean anything. | A route could be decorated without any test noticing, which is the intended latitude. The reset assertion still fails if the limiter is swapped for a differently-stored one. |
| **DL-84** — Assert the operator guidance in an unparsable allow-list by its text rather than by its exception type. | (a) Assert the cause is a `ValueError`. (b) Assert only that construction fails. | The JSON decoder's own error subclasses `ValueError`, so a type assertion passes even when the guidance is deleted — measured, the mutation that removes the message entirely still satisfies it. Asserting only that construction fails is weaker still, since the setting is required and construction fails for several reasons. The terms asserted are the required form and a concrete example, which is what an operator needs to correct the value. | The test breaks if the guidance is reworded, which is the point: the wording is the deliverable. It does not assert where in the message each term appears, so a garbled message containing both terms would pass. |
| **DL-85** — Assert `Vary: Origin` only on the responses where it was measured present, and assert nothing about its absence elsewhere. | (a) Also assert it is absent from the non-allow-listed and no-origin responses. (b) Assert it on every response. | The header is emitted by the framework's own middleware, not by application code, and it was measured present on the allow-listed read and on both preflights and absent on the withheld and no-origin paths. Asserting the absences would pin upstream behaviour the project does not own, so a framework that started emitting the header more broadly — a correctness improvement for caches — would fail the suite. Asserting it everywhere would fail today. | Cache-correctness on the withheld path is untested. The property that matters for the allow-list is that a per-origin response is not cached across origins, and that is asserted where the response actually varies. |
| **DL-86** — Correct the misleading docstring on the keyword-path allow-list test and keep the test, rather than deleting it as redundant with the new environment-path test. | (a) Delete it, since the environment path is the real one. (b) Leave the docstring as it was. | The two paths are genuinely different — the environment path can fail before the field validator ever runs, while the keyword path always reaches it — so both need cover, and the seven unsafe shapes the keyword test enumerates are load-bearing. The docstring claimed it exercised the operator's path, which it does not; the fix is one sentence, and deleting the test would drop seven cases to correct a comment. | Two tests now cover overlapping ground with different entry points, which a future reader could mistake for duplication. The corrected docstrings state which path each one takes. |
| **DL-87** — Assert the stored password hash carries the bcrypt scheme identifier and a work factor at or above the configured floor, expressed as a floor rather than an equality. | (a) Assert the exact work factor. (b) Assert only that the stored value differs from the submitted password. (c) Assert the scheme through the library's identify helper instead of the stored prefix. | The difference assertion alone passes for any transformation, including a reversible or unsalted one — measured, swapping the scheme for an unsalted-era digest left the suite green, and the salting test did not catch it either because that digest is also salted. The scheme identifier is therefore the detector. Expressing the cost as a floor keeps a future increase a legitimate improvement rather than a test failure. Reading the stored prefix rather than asking the library also proves what reached the database, not merely what the library would report. | The test knows the modular-crypt layout, so a future move to a scheme with a different prefix format requires updating it — which is the correct place to make that decision explicit. A cost far above the floor is accepted silently, at a runtime cost the suite would feel long before a reviewer did. |
| **DL-88** — Assert the declared response model on the route object as well as on the returned key set. | (a) Assert the returned key set alone. (b) Assert the route object alone. | The key set alone cannot detect the loss, and this was measured: the handler already returns a fully constructed schema object, so removing the declaration from the decorator leaves the serialized keys identical and the suite green. The route-object assertion is the detector. Keeping the key-set assertion still matters, because it is what proves the declared contract actually reaches a client. This framework infers a response model from a return annotation, and this handler carries none — which is why the declaration is the only thing standing between the route and an undeclared response. | The assertion reads a framework-internal attribute on the route, so a framework that renamed it would break the test. The key-set half is framework-agnostic and would still hold. |
| **DL-89** — Assert public-read pagination as a set partition — disjoint pages whose union is the whole table — rather than as an ordered slice. | (a) Assert each page's exact contents in order. (b) Assert only the page sizes. | The query carries no ordering clause, so row order is not a contract and an ordered assertion would encode incidental scan order as a requirement, becoming flaky on a different engine or after a vacuum. Sizes alone are too weak: dropping the offset and limit from the query returns every row, and a size assertion on a small table can pass while pages overlap. Disjointness plus completeness is exactly the property pagination promises, and it fails when the bounds are dropped — which is what was measured. | Ordering stability is untested, so a caller depending on a stable order across pages has no protection here. That dependency would be unfounded today, since the query does not order, and the honest place to fix it is an ordering clause plus a test for it. |
| **DL-90** — Prove the duplicate-address pre-check by counting the insert statements the registration emits, rather than by inspecting the response. | (a) Accept that removing the pre-check alone is undetectable, as an earlier analysis concluded. (b) Differentiate the two defences by their log records or error detail. (c) Remove the commit-time defence so the pre-check becomes observable. | Both defences answer a byte-identical 400 with the same envelope and the same log detail, and that uniformity is itself a property worth keeping — it is what stops a caller distinguishing which internal component refused the request. So the response cannot be the discriminator, and options (b) and (c) either break that uniformity or delete a defence to make a test easier. The observable difference is at the statement layer: the pre-check avoids the doomed write entirely, while its absence attempts an insert and rolls it back on every repeated attempt. That is a cost an unauthenticated caller chooses freely, and on PostgreSQL a failed statement leaves the transaction unusable for anything that follows. Counting inserts fails when the pre-check goes and passes when it stays, which closed the last undetected mutation of the twenty-three. | The assertion listens on the test engine's statement stream, so it is coupled to the harness engine rather than to the route. It counts statements against one table by name, so a future rename requires updating it. |
| **DL-91** — Assert the stored-script round-trip on the parsed value and the response content type, never on the raw response text. | (a) Assert the raw body contains the payload verbatim. (b) Assert the payload is escaped or stripped. | The property being recorded is that the value is stored and returned faithfully inside a JSON content type, where it is inert, and that the server neither mangles it nor renders it as markup. A raw-text assertion would additionally freeze the serializer's escaping choices, so a future decision to emit `<` as an escape sequence — a hardening — would fail the suite despite the parsed value being unchanged. Asserting escaping or stripping would assert a behaviour the requirements do not specify and would silently make the server responsible for a client-side concern. | Nothing here protects a client that injects the returned value into a page as markup. That responsibility stays with the client, and this test records the server-side contract it can rely on rather than implying more. |
| **DL-92** — Acknowledge the deprecation warning emitted at import by the model module and change no code. | (a) Move to the newer declarative-base import so the warning disappears. (b) Filter the warning in the test configuration. | The module is a reference file the requirements mark deliberately unchanged, and the warning comes from application code rather than from any test. Filtering it would hide a genuine forward-compatibility signal from the one place a maintainer reliably reads it. | The warning appears once in every suite run and will keep appearing, so a genuinely new warning is slightly harder to spot. The count is fixed at one and was verified at every checkpoint, which makes a second warning conspicuous. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-70.** Measured after the change: the command exits 0, collects and passes 320 tests and writes the coverage artifact, while each excluded module still raises its original error verbatim when invoked by explicit path, because `collect_ignore` governs directory collection only.
- **DL-81.** Measured: the requirement's own successful-login test issues nine requests from one address, and under the decorator the sixth is refused, so adopting the suggested fix breaks a behaviour the requirements freeze. Relaxing that test to accommodate the decorator would trade a specified behaviour for a redundant control, because the address-keyed five-attempt fifteen-minute limit is already enforced by the in-process counter — verified live, five 401 responses then a 429 on the sixth.

## 15. Code review remediation decisions (C1 through C4, M1 through M9, m1 through m4)

This section records the decisions taken while resolving the seventeen findings of the security code
review. Every measurement quoted here was taken on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure on
2026-08-01. `SECURITY.md` remains the home of operational secret handling and the residual-risk
register.

### 15.1 Suppression register correction

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-93** — Reduce the suppression list in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` from fifteen entries to the ten the specification approves, superseding DL-07 and DL-61. **Superseded: the shipped gate carries fifteen, held there by DL-291 and measured by DL-294, because a suppression matching no live advisory fails the staleness check.** | (a) Keep fifteen and record the extra five as accepted. (b) Suppress the five under a shorter review trigger. | The specification approves exactly ten advisories and states that list in three separate places. A deliverable cannot widen its own accepted set. Removing `GHSA-6v7p-g79w-8964`, `PYSEC-2026-1375`, `PYSEC-2026-1374`, `PYSEC-2026-1845` and `PYSEC-2026-2270` restores parity. | The gate now exits non-zero on every run. Five live advisories stay visible until they are formally accepted or remediated. |
| **DL-94** — Record DL-09 as moot: the corrected list carries no GitHub-namespace identifier. **Superseded: the shipped list carries `GHSA-6v7p-g79w-8964`, so DL-09 is live again on this row's own terms; DL-294 measures it.** | Leave DL-09 standing. | The only such entry was the `msgpack` advisory, which DL-93 removes. The workflow now contains zero of them. | None. DL-09 applies again only if a future accepted advisory has no Python-namespace identifier. |
| **DL-95** — Audit the installed environment instead of a frozen requirements snapshot, deviating from the specification's literal gate command. | (a) Keep the frozen snapshot. (b) Freeze with the flag that adds the bootstrap toolchain. | Measured: the plain snapshot reports fifteen advisories and omits the installer toolchain entirely; the `--all` snapshot adds `setuptools` but still omits all three `pip` advisories. Only environment mode reports all four the review requires. | The command text now differs from the documented one. The no-dependency-resolution flag is rejected outright in environment mode and was dropped. |
| **DL-96** — Remove the suppression cross-check the earlier pass added, superseding DL-08. | Keep it alongside the corrected gate. | Its comment block was the Rule 1 defect the review recorded, and it audited a different surface than the corrected gate. All ten approved identifiers were measured live, so it detected nothing. | A future stale entry is no longer flagged automatically. The shared review trigger empties the list wholesale. |

### 15.2 Repository scan gates

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-97** — Exclude the two files that define the credential pattern from the credential scan. | (a) A separate pattern file read by the search tool. (b) An explicit source-path allow-list. | Three of the pattern's four alternatives appear inside it as literal text, so any file carrying the definition becomes a hit. A tree holding no real credential returned a hit and a success exit. Alternative (a) creates a file where the specification fixes zero creations in that folder; (b) stops policing paths added later. | Neither pattern-defining file is policed by this gate. Reported by the workflow implementation as its local D-B. |
| **DL-98** — Extend the exclusion set with the ignored dependency trees and the generated environment file. | (a) Accept the false hits. (b) Move to a tracked-content search. | Measured twenty hits on a clean tracked tree, every one a third-party docstring inside the virtual environment. The specification scopes this finding to tracked files. A gate that fails on a clean tree is inert in practice. | An untracked secret outside version control is not policed by this gate. The ignore rules and the build-context filter cover that path. Extends the workflow implementation's local D-C. |
| **DL-99** — Invert the search exit code with a conditional that exits non-zero on a match. | A trailing negated command. | Pasted verbatim, a search that finds a credential exits zero and the step passes, while a clean tree fails the step. Error-exit does not abort on a negated command, so a negated form followed by any other command exits zero despite a leak. | The workflow text now differs from the documented verification command, and the two must be kept in step. The workflow implementation's local D-D. |
| **DL-100** — Keep `.env.example` inside the credential scan. | Exclude it alongside the pattern-defining files. | The gate enforces placeholder discipline in that file. A conventional placeholder was measured as a hit; the angle-bracket form is clean. Excluding it would disable a working control. | A contributor who writes a conventional placeholder discovers it through a failed build. |
| **DL-101** — Keep the frontend guards scoped to `frontend/` and `frontend/src/`. | Widen either to the repository. | The payment client secret legitimately appears at three server-side sites, and build output and installed packages sit outside the source tree. | A browser-storage call added outside `frontend/src/` is unpoliced. |

### 15.3 Security suite execution

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-102** — Add a dedicated security-suite step ahead of every step with a known failing baseline, and mark it and the three scan gates to always run. | (a) Place the suite first, ahead of the audit gate. (b) Make the audit gate non-blocking. | After DL-93 and DL-95 the audit gate exits non-zero by design, which would skip every later step and leave the three new gates and the suite unexecuted. Alternative (b) removes the gate's purpose. | The step-level guarantee does not survive a cancelled run or a lost runner. |

### 15.4 Test harness decisions

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-103** — Insert both the repository root and the backend directory onto the import path, and add no package marker. | (a) Add package markers throughout. (b) Insert one root only. | Application modules use the absolute package path while the three pre-existing test modules use a short one, and the workflow runs the suite from the backend directory. No package marker exists anywhere under the backend today, and none appears in the planned change set. | Importing one module file under two names would create two module objects holding two settings instances. The security suite uses the absolute style throughout. Reported by the harness implementation as its local D4. |
| **DL-104** — Build the test engine with a static pool and connection thread checking disabled. | The driver's default pooling. | The test client runs route handlers on a portal thread distinct from the test thread. Default pooling gives each thread its own connection, so an in-memory database would present an empty schema to the handler. | Engine state persists for the process, so per-test isolation depends on the autouse fixture recreating the schema. |
| **DL-105** — Give the test client an HTTPS base URL. | A plain HTTP base URL with the cookie security attribute disabled under test. | A cookie carrying the Secure attribute is dropped by the client over plain HTTP. That silently breaks every cookie assertion and presents as a route defect. | The suite exercises a transport the local server does not serve. The attribute stays setting-driven so one code path serves both contexts. |
| **DL-106** — Use an in-memory database recreated per test, and override the dependency the routes actually resolve. | (a) A temporary file database. (b) Overriding the duplicate defined in the application entry module. | An in-memory database leaves no artifact between runs. Two dependency callables share the name and only one is wired to routes; the framework keys overrides on object identity. | Overriding the unused callable would succeed silently while every test reached the real engine, with no warning. |
| **DL-107** — Expose a fixed, minimal fixture surface and add no plugin beyond the pinned set. | Add an asynchronous test plugin, a mocking plugin, or a data factory. | The manifest is final with five development pins, and the synchronous test client reaches asynchronous handlers unchanged. | Asynchronous service functions are exercised through routes rather than called directly. The harness implementation's local D2. |

### 15.5 Scope governance for this document

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-108** — Continue this log rather than reverting commit `02b70bab`, which the review flagged as outside its checkpoint, and bring the file under governance with this entry. | (a) Revert that commit. (b) Defer every entry to a later checkpoint. | The file is a planned deliverable, and Rule 1 treats an unexplained deviation as a defect. Reverting would trade a boundary breach for a rule breach, strand the identifiers that code comments point at, and delete the register the corrected audit gate references. | The document was changed outside its checkpoint by `02b70bab` and again here. Every entry is keyed and dated, so both changes are auditable rather than hidden. |

### 15.6 Session, throttle and diagnostics decisions

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-109** — Remove the browser token accessor outright and delete every browser-storage call site. | Keep the accessor returning an empty value. | No request interceptor and no authorization header exist anywhere in the frontend, so the accessor had no caller. | A future non-browser caller in the browser bundle needs a new mechanism. |
| **DL-110** — Widen the browser logout to call the new server route. | Clear local state only. | Script cannot delete a cookie marked HttpOnly, so a local-only logout leaves a valid session in place. | One added round trip on logout. |
| **DL-111** — Enable credentialed requests on the HTTP client and leave the cross-site token options unset. | Set them explicitly to a false value. | Credentialed mode is the precondition for the pinned client's advisory, and the pinned release keeps the old header-attaching behaviour behind a separate flag. Omission is what keeps the advisory closed. | An explicit false value is equivalent today and could be misread as enabling the option. |
| **DL-112** — Apply the login route limit as a static string rather than a callable. | A callable returning the configured limit. | The middleware exempts a route only when its limit is registered statically. A callable lands in the dynamic map and the middleware evaluates it a second time. | The threshold is read once at import. |
| **DL-113** — Key the account marker in the audit log with a per-process keyed digest. | (a) Keep the unsalted digest. (b) Drop the marker and keep only the correlation identifier. | An unsalted digest of a normalized address is recoverable from a dictionary of addresses. | Markers cannot be correlated across processes or across a restart. |
| **DL-114** — Keep the in-process failure counter keyed by account only. | Retain the address dimension alongside it. | The registered route limiter owns the address dimension, so a second address-keyed counter duplicated one control. | Address-wide protection now rests on the route limiter alone. |
| **DL-115** — Emit the full formatted traceback, exception context and chained cause to the log under the correlation identifier the caller receives, with secrets removed. | (a) Exception types and frame locations only. (b) Full diagnostics unredacted. | The specification freezes full diagnostics. Types alone cannot support forensics, and unredacted output would place the signing key and the database password into the log. | Redaction covers held setting literals and shaped values. A novel secret shape needs a new pattern. |
| **DL-116** — Exclude the error-reporting endpoint from redaction. | Redact it with the other settings. | It is a destination rather than a credential, and redacting it would obscure which reporter was configured. | The value appears in logs. |

### 15.7 Request schema and authentication compatibility

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-117** — Drop the registration password guard from the login model, keeping it on the registration model. | Keep the guard on both. | A legacy credential longer than the hasher's ceiling was refused with a distinguishable status before authentication, which is an account oracle. | Over-length login input now reaches the hasher. |
| **DL-118** — Map a hasher refusal during login to the counted uniform refusal. | Let it surface as a server error. | One catch at the single verification call site covers every input-driven refusal and a corrupted stored hash alike. | A genuine hasher fault is reported to the caller as a failed credential. |
| **DL-119** — Guard the finiteness test against overflow. | Test finiteness directly. | A very large integer literal raises on conversion, and the validation library does not catch that error, so such a value escaped validation and surfaced as a server error. | None. |
| **DL-120** — Require the charged amount to exceed zero and the later date to exceed the earlier, both on `SubscriptionCreate`, and leave the listing-side numbers to a type guard alone. **Narrowed from a positive-and-non-negative split across both models; see DL-23.** | (a) One shared rule for every numeric field. (b) Extend the split to `ListingCreate.rent`, `broker_fee` and `square_footage`, bounding the first above zero and the other two at or above it. | An amount at or below zero is not a payable instruction and an end date at or before its start describes no period, so both are input-contract defects on the model that carries a charge. The listing-side numbers are a different question: a fee, an area or a room count may legitimately be zero, and bounding rent is the business rule DL-23 keeps out of a security pass. Those three fields therefore carry `_require_json_number` only — a pre-validator that rejects a numeric string and a bool, which is the type-confusion half of the finding — and no range. | Domain rules live on the create models only, matching the input-contract scope of the finding. A negative rent stays schema-valid, which is the residual DL-23 records; the two rows now describe one rule set rather than two. |

### 15.8 Payment authorization

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-121** — Bind the expected charge unit as a module constant with a keyword override. | Declare it as a setting. | The settings class and the environment template hold an exact field-for-field parity contract that a new field would break, and the application charges one unit today. | A multi-unit price list requires promoting the constant to a setting. |
| **DL-122** — Express the plan binding as a keyword-only parameter and refuse a reusable reference that carries no bound plan. | Change the calling endpoint to pass one. | That endpoint is a read-only reference in the planned change set. | Reusable references fail closed until a caller passes a plan. |
| **DL-123** — Require an identified payer and refuse a reference whose payer cannot be read. | Accept an unidentified payer. | An authorization naming no payer identifies nobody, so it cannot be attributed. | Providers that omit the payer field are refused. |
| **DL-124** — Consume a verified reference once, through a bounded in-process ledger claimed before the provider call. | (a) A database column. (b) An external store. | No migration tooling and no provisioned store exist. Claiming before the call closes the window between checking and acting. | State is per-process and evicts past its bound, so a restart forgets spent references. |
| **DL-125** — Inject the request timeout at the provider transport attribute rather than at each call. | Patch every call site. | Every provider call funnels through one transport attribute, which covers token fetches and resource lookups alike. | The rebinding is process-wide and idempotent. The shared HTTP module itself is untouched. |
| **DL-126** — Compose a new authorization gate over the existing amount check. | Extend the existing function in place. | The existing check is asserted directly by its own coverage. | Two functions now describe one decision. |

### 15.9 Build-context secret custody

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-127** — Add build-context exclusion files at the repository root and at both declared build contexts. | (a) One file beside the compose definition. (b) The root file only. | The builder resolves the exclusion file at the context root, so a file beside the compose definition is inert. The root file alone leaves both declared contexts exposed. | Three files must be kept in step. **Withdrawn by DL-287 and reinstated by DL-300.** |
| **DL-128** — Prefix the depth-sensitive patterns so they match at any level, and exclude version-control metadata from the context. | Reuse the version-control ignore syntax unchanged. | Build-context patterns do not match at arbitrary depth. Measured: the root context carried 8573 entries including the full history, which holds a credential literal in the first commit. | None. **Withdrawn by DL-287 and reinstated by DL-300.** |
| **DL-129** — Leave the broad copy instructions in both container definitions unchanged. | Narrow each to the application sources. | Neither definition appears in the planned change set, confirmed twice. Measured: with the context filtered, none of four planted secrets reached any of the three images. | The instructions stay literally broad and depend on the filter. The narrowing change is recorded for a future pass. **Withdrawn by DL-287 and reinstated by DL-300.** |
