| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-130** — Assert cookie precedence with conflicting identities and with a cookie that fails validation, deviating from that test file's own guidance against asserting precedence. | Keep the same token in both locations. | The same token in both locations cannot distinguish cookie-first from header-first. The specification fixes cookie-first in two places. Mutating the selection to header-first is now detected by exactly these two cases while the other eight pass. | The tests pin a precedence order that holds while the specification states it. |
| **DL-131** — Scope the session-storage and throttle module descriptions to what their tests assert. | Leave the stronger wording in place. | The earlier wording claimed a browser-script property no backend test exercises, and a guessing bound the per-process counter does not deliver across workers or restarts. | None. |

## 16. Code review remediation: frontend session migration, CI gates and test mutation sensitivity

`frontend/src/services/auth.ts`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `backend/tests/conftest.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_auth_identity.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_cors_policy.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_error_handling.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py`

A code review of the security-test milestone returned ten findings, five major and five minor.
Each reversal below gets its own row rather than an edit in place, and every value was measured on
CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure, and on TypeScript 4.9.5 for the frontend file, on
2026-08-01.

Five rows in earlier sections are **superseded** here. DL-66 left the frontend session migration
at its pre-remediation baseline. DL-07 together with DL-61 widened the audit suppression list from
ten entries to fifteen, DL-08 added the stale-entry cross-check, and DL-09 permitted GitHub
advisory identifiers in that list.

### 16.1 SEC-06 frontend session migration

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-132** — Complete the SEC-06 migration in `frontend/src/services/auth.ts`: delete the storage key and all three `localStorage` call sites, delete `getToken()` outright, correct the login path to `/auth/login`, and make `logout()` an async function that posts `/auth/logout`. **Supersedes DL-66.** | (a) DL-66's position: leave the file at its pre-remediation baseline, since no finding assigned it. (b) Delete the storage calls but keep `getToken()` as a stub returning `null`. (c) Keep `logout()` synchronous and clear the cookie from page script. | DL-66 deferred this file on the ground that no review finding named it and that the server half of SEC-06 already worked. The review now rates the file a major defect and records the browser-storage gate as unenforced, which discharges that deferral. Option (c) is not a mechanism that exists — script cannot delete an `HttpOnly` cookie, so a client-only logout leaves a valid session alive and turns the SEC-06 fix into a regression. | `logout()` now depends on `POST /auth/logout` being reachable, and a network failure rejects a promise the synchronous form could never reject. The cookie migration's cross-site request forgery surface is still bounded by `SameSite=Strict` alone, which sections 12 and 13 already record as an open residual. `NavBar.tsx` still imports a `useAuth` hook this module has never exported, and the store thunk still calls `login` with one argument; both stay as the pre-existing compile failures the requirements order left alone. |
| **DL-133** — Narrow the login destructure to `const { user } = response.data;` and leave the response-shape mismatch itself unfixed. **Superseded by DL-318: the shipped module reads the two token fields and returns an `AuthSession`.** | (a) Read `access_token` and return it. (b) Keep the token name bound and unused. (c) Fetch the profile after authenticating so a real user object is returned. (d) Widen the return type to `Promise<void>`. | The login body is frozen at two keys, and DL-64's sibling deviation on the backend cited this exact line as its evidence for keeping it at two, so option (a) would widen a frozen contract from the client side. Deleting the storage write removes the only consumer of the token binding, which leaves option (b) as a dangling name. Option (c) is feature work and would call a route the backend does not serve. Option (d) reshapes a signature and a return type the file contract freezes. Narrowing the destructure is the direct in-place consequence of deleting the storage write. | `login()` resolves to `undefined` at runtime while declaring a user return type, because the login body carries no user object. That mismatch is pre-existing and unchanged, and the frontend has no login form, so no caller observes it. `frontend/tsconfig.json` sets neither `noUnusedLocals` nor `noUnusedParameters`, so both spellings compile and the narrowing is a legibility gain rather than a compile fix. |
| **DL-134** — Omit `xsrfCookieName`, `xsrfHeaderName` and `withXSRFToken` from this file entirely, and rely on the shared `axios.defaults.withCredentials` assignment in `api.ts` for credentialed mode. | (a) Set the cross-site-token options explicitly to `false`. (b) Pass a per-request credentials option on the login and logout calls. (c) Create a dedicated axios instance for the auth module. | Enabling credentialed mode is the precondition for CVE-2023-45857, in which the XHR adapter attaches a header carrying a cookie value to every request regardless of destination. Newer axios gates that behaviour behind a separate flag, so leaving the flag absent is what keeps the advisory closed; an explicit `false` would satisfy the same grep at zero benefit while inviting a later edit to flip it. Both files import the same default singleton, and `api.ts` is evaluated before any function here runs, so bare calls inherit credentialed mode with no extra line. Option (c) would create a second configuration surface that the shared default no longer governs. | The omission is invisible at the call site, so a maintainer could add the option without realising it reopens the advisory. The CI browser-storage and client-secret gates do not cover it; only the pinned `axios` version and this row do. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-132.** Option (b) preserves dead code: `getToken` has exactly one repository-wide
  occurrence, its own definition, and the measured search for an interceptor, an `Authorization`
  header and a `Bearer` prefix across `frontend/` returns zero, so nothing ever read its result.
  Measured after the change: the browser-storage scan over `frontend/src/` reports zero, the
  `getToken`, storage-key and `document.cookie` scan over `frontend/` reports zero. And an
  isolated TypeScript 4.9.5 compile of the file under `strict` reports the same three
  module-resolution diagnostics before and after, so no diagnostic was added.

### 16.2 Continuous integration security gates and the audit suppression set

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-135** — Restore the audit suppression set to exactly the ten `PYSEC-` identifiers frozen in AAP 0.10.1, written as ten explicit `--ignore-vuln` arguments, and drop the five additions. **Supersedes DL-07 and DL-61. Itself superseded: the shipped gate carries fifteen, held there by DL-291 and measured by DL-294.** | (a) DL-07's position: keep all fifteen entries so the step exits zero. (b) Keep the fifteen but move the five extras behind a second, non-blocking invocation. (c) Raise the pins of `msgpack`, `filelock`, `pytest` and `python-dotenv` to their fix releases. (d) Drop `--strict` or stop failing the build on findings. | AAP 0.10.1 freezes the ten and states that byte-identity of this invocation across `ci.yml`, `backend/requirements.txt` and `SECURITY.md` is a contract. The review rates the fifteen-entry list a major divergence and directs the ten "and no others", and the file contract for this workflow forbids self-authoring an eleventh entry. Option (b) keeps the accepted-risk set at fifteen while hiding it, which is the same divergence with less visibility. Option (c) is impossible on the documented runtime, as the evidence below records. Option (d) removes the gate's ability to detect anything. AAP 0.8.4 already states that a green pipeline is not on offer. | All five are unreachable from the served request path, as rows 11 through 15 of section 2.2 record, and all five are unpatchable on CPython 3.9. Two enter only through the audit tool's own `CacheControl[filecache]` dependency; two are direct development and runtime pins. DL-07's objection stands on its merits: a permanently red gate invites maintainers to ignore it. The remedy is the runtime upgrade AAP 0.9.2 names as the highest-priority follow-on, not a wider suppression list. |
| **DL-136** — Delete the stale-entry cross-check, its second `pip-audit` invocation and its inline Python block. **Supersedes DL-08.** | (a) Keep the cross-check unchanged. (b) Keep it but assert against only the ten frozen identifiers. (c) Replace it with a shell comparison of the reported identifiers against the frozen set. | The review requires the frozen invocation "and no others", so any additional command in the step re-opens the same finding. The hazard DL-08 addressed was a stale entry in a list that changed as pins changed; the list is now frozen by the plan and reproduced literally, so drift can only arrive through an edit to this file, which is reviewed. Options (b) and (c) both keep a second invocation and a second failure mode in a step whose contract is one command. | A mistyped identifier still disables part of the gate without a diagnostic, which is the behaviour DL-08 measured and DL-12 characterised. Detection now rests on review of this file rather than on the build. The ten identifiers were verified in this change to match live advisories: the run reported `ignored 10`, so no entry is stale today. |
| **DL-137** — Carry `PYSEC-` identifiers only. **Supersedes DL-09. Itself superseded: the shipped gate declares `GHSA-6v7p-g79w-8964`, the `msgpack` advisory's only identifier, so the mixed-namespace policy of DL-09 governs; DL-294 measures it.** | Keep the mixed-namespace policy so a GitHub advisory identifier remains expressible. | The only entry that needed the GitHub namespace was the `msgpack` advisory, which has no `PYSEC-` form and is no longer suppressed under DL-135. The frozen ten are all `PYSEC-`, so the mixed policy now has no subject. | If a future advisory with no `PYSEC-` form ever has to be accepted, this policy has to be revisited. DL-12's measurement stands: alias matching works, so the constraint is a naming policy rather than a technical limit. |
| **DL-138** — Keep the audit step immediately after the dependency-install step, and place the three scan steps immediately after the audit step, ahead of the Node.js install step. **Extends DL-11.** | (a) Append the scans at the end of the workflow. (b) Place them before the dependency-install step. (c) Fold them into the existing style-check step. | `pip-audit` is on `PATH` only after the install step, which fixes the audit position. The scans must run before the Node install step, because that step creates `frontend/node_modules`, and the credential scan walks the working tree from its root. Option (b) would work for the scans but splits the security steps around an unrelated step. Option (c) hides three gates inside a step named for something else and couples their exit codes. | Inserting four steps shifts the position of every later step. No tracked file references steps in this workflow by line number, verified by search, so no cross-reference went stale. |
| **DL-139** — Add all four gates to `ci.yml` rather than to a new workflow file. **Extends DL-11.** | (a) Create a dedicated security workflow. (b) Split the scans into a second job in this workflow. | The plan lists these as steps of the existing pipeline, and the file contract for this change forbids creating another workflow. A separate workflow or job would repeat the checkout and the runtime setup for three grep invocations, and its failure would be reported apart from the build a reviewer already reads. | The scans run on the same runner as the build, so a scan failure blocks the whole job rather than reporting independently. That is the intended coupling: a credential in the tree should stop the build. |
| **DL-140** — Write each scan as `if grep ...; then exit 1; fi`. | (a) `! grep ...`. (b) `grep -q ... && exit 1`. (c) Compare `grep -c` output against zero. | Measured on the runner shell: `grep` exits zero when it matches, so the naive form passes the step exactly when a secret is present. A `!`-negated command is exempt from `set -e`, so option (a) is silently inert unless it is the last command in the block, and adding any line after it re-opens the hole. Option (b) suppresses the output a maintainer needs to locate the offender. Option (c) adds arithmetic to a two-state test. The `if` form is position-independent and prints every offending path with its line number. | The `if` condition also treats a `grep` error, exit code 2, as "no match", so an unreadable path would pass the gate. Every scanned path is a tracked directory, so the condition does not arise today. |
| **DL-141** — Exclude `ci.yml` and `SECURITY.md` from the credential scan by basename. | (a) Move the pattern into a file and read it with `grep -f`. (b) Restrict the scan to an allow-list of paths. (c) Escape the pattern so it stops matching itself. | Three of the four alternatives in the AAP credential pattern match the text of the pattern itself, proven by running the scan without the exclusion: the only hit in the whole tracked tree is line 46 of this workflow. Option (a) moves the pattern out of the artifact a reviewer reads and adds a file whose absence would disable the gate silently. Option (b) stops covering any directory added later, which is the failure mode a repository-wide scan exists to prevent. Option (c) makes the pattern diverge from the frozen text. `SECURITY.md` does not exist yet; excluding it now keeps the invocation identical to the one that document will carry. | A real credential committed into `ci.yml` or `SECURITY.md` would not be caught by this gate. Both files are small, security-owned and reviewed on every change. |
| **DL-142** — Exclude `.env` from the credential scan and keep `.env.example` in scope. | Exclude both, or exclude the whole pattern `.env*`. | The template is the artifact whose placeholder discipline the gate enforces, so excluding it would remove the check that matters most. Measured: the bracketed placeholder form the template uses does not match the pattern, while a value made of word characters does. The `.env` exclusion is defensive only, since the file is untracked and never present on a fresh runner. | A developer who writes a real value into the template still trips the gate, which is the intended behaviour. `--exclude` matches a basename glob rather than a path, so a file named `.env` anywhere in the tree is skipped. |
| **DL-143** — Scope the client-secret scan to `frontend/` and the browser-storage scan to `frontend/src/`, and add no fourth scan for the axios cross-site-token options. | (a) Run both scans over the whole repository. (b) Run the storage scan over `frontend/`. (c) Add a scan asserting the cross-site-token options stay absent. | Widening the client-secret scan would flag three legitimate server-side reads, in `backend/app/core/config.py` and twice in `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, and a gate that fails on correct code gets deleted. The storage scan is scoped to source because generated and vendored trees under `frontend/` legitimately contain those names. Negative controls confirm both scopes: the same literal placed outside the scoped path leaves each gate passing. Option (c) is not in the frozen gate set for this file, and DL-134 already records that the protection is the pinned `axios` version. | Browser storage introduced under `frontend/` but outside `frontend/src/` is not caught. Nothing outside `frontend/src/` is authored in this repository today. The cross-site-token options remain unguarded by any gate. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-135.** **The step now exits non-zero on every run.** Measured with the shipped invocation: `Found 5 known vulnerabilities, ignored 10 in 4 packages`, naming `msgpack` 1.1.2 `GHSA-6v7p-g79w-8964`, `filelock` 3.19.1 `PYSEC-2026-1375` and `PYSEC-2026-1374`, `pytest` 8.4.2 `PYSEC-2026-1845`, and `python-dotenv` 1.2.1 `PYSEC-2026-2270`.
- **DL-135, why option (c) is impossible on the documented runtime.** Every fix release for those five advisories was probed on CPython 3.9.25 and every one is rejected by its own metadata. `msgpack` 1.2.1, `filelock` 3.20.1 through 3.32.2, `pytest` 9.0.0 through 9.1.1, and `python-dotenv` 1.2.2 each declare `Requires-Python >= 3.10`. Upgrading out of the advisories therefore requires the runtime migration AAP 0.4.5 names as the highest-priority follow-on, not a pin change.


### 16.3 SEC-02 subject-claim mutation sensitivity

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-144** — Split the refusal parametrization by mechanism and seed a row carrying the primary key each coercible spelling resolves to, so the refusal cannot come from the unresolved-account branch. | (a) Leave the single eighteen-case list as reviewed. (b) Narrow each assertion to a branch-specific marker, such as a distinct log record. (c) Call `get_current_user` directly with a stub session and assert the raised exception. (d) Expose the refusing branch in a response header under a test flag. | Measured against the reviewed suite with the canonical-spelling clause deleted from the guard: **all ten coercible spellings still passed**, so the suite asserted a refusal the unresolved-account branch was supplying. With a row seeded at the resolved key, nine of the ten now fail under that same mutant. Option (b) couples a security assertion to log text, which the error-boundary work deliberately keeps free of account state. Option (c) bypasses the routing, dependency resolution and error boundary the finding is about. Option (d) adds a production code path that exists only for tests. | The seeded rows carry explicit keys including `0` and a negative value, which SQLite accepts as row identifiers. A future move of this suite onto PostgreSQL keeps the inserts valid but leaves the identity sequence unadvanced, so a later insert without an explicit key could collide. The suite runs on SQLite by construction, so the condition does not arise today. |
| **DL-145** — Assert the token library's own rejection of a non-string subject, and stop implying that the guard's type clause is observable over HTTP. | (a) Present the integer-subject case as proof of the type clause, which is how the reviewed suite read. (b) Call the compiled canonical pattern directly from the test. (c) Delete the integer, list and mapping cases as untestable. | Measured: `python-jose` 3.5.0 raises `JWTClaimsError`, a `JWTError` subclass, for any non-string `sub` at decode. The guard's `except jose.jwt.JWTError` branch therefore answers first, and the type clause never runs for a token that was actually signed. Under the mutant that deletes the canonical clause, the integer case passes for this reason and not because the test is weak. The new case pins both layers: the decode raises, and the request still draws the uniform refusal. Option (b) asserts a private module attribute. Option (c) would drop a real property — an integer subject cannot authenticate — because its mechanism sits one layer out. | The guard's type clause has no HTTP-observable coverage and is defence in depth. If a future release of the token library accepted a non-string subject, that clause would become the only control, and no test would notice the responsibility moving. The decode assertion is what makes such a change visible. |
| **DL-146** — Give seeded rows a placeholder value in the hash column rather than a real hash. | Hash a real value through `get_password_hash` for every seeded row. | No assertion reads the column, and no login path runs against a seeded account. The real hash costs roughly a quarter second per row, which the ten parametrized cases would multiply for no assertion gained. | A seeded account cannot authenticate through the login route. Nothing attempts it, and every test that needs a working credential uses the registration fixture instead. |
| **DL-147** — Leave the absent-subject branch covered only by its existing case, and record that neutralising that branch on its own is an equivalent mutant. | Add a case designed to distinguish the absent-subject branch from the type clause. | Measured: with the `sub is None` branch neutralised, both the reviewed and the remediated suite still pass, because the type clause refuses `None` immediately afterwards. The two checks are redundant and the property — an absent subject is refused — stays asserted either way. No test can separate them without removing one of them. | A reader comparing mutants could mistake this for an escaped mutant. It is recorded here so the redundancy reads as deliberate. |


### 16.4 SEC-03 exact advertised sets and frozen-contract regression coverage

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-148** — Compare the advertised method and request-header sets whole, and name Starlette's four always-safelisted request headers as a constant beside the registered ones. | (a) Keep the subset comparison the reviewed suite used. (b) Import the framework's safelist constant instead of restating it. (c) Assert only that no wildcard appears. | Measured: adding `HEAD` to the registered method list and an extra name to the registered header list each left the reviewed module at **25 passed, exit 0**. Both are killed by the whole-set comparison. The advertised header value is the registered list unioned with `Accept`, `Accept-Language`, `Content-Language` and `Content-Type`, which Starlette 0.49.3 adds unconditionally, so an exact comparison has to name them. Option (b) reaches into a module attribute the framework does not document as public. Option (c) is what the reviewed assertion amounted to once the subset form let anything extra through. | A framework release that changes its safelist fails this case. That is the intended trigger rather than a defect: what the application advertises to a browser should not change without review. The constant records a version-dependent value, so the pinned Starlette version and this row move together. |
| **DL-149** — Assert the whole path and verb map read from `app.routes`, excluding only the four framework documentation paths and asserting the excluded set holds nothing else. | (a) Assert each route's existence in its own case. (b) Fold the documentation paths into the exact map. (c) Snapshot the generated interface document and compare it. | Measured: changing the subscriptions read verb left the reviewed module at **25 passed, exit 0**, because no case calls that route. One whole-map comparison kills a renamed path, a dropped route and an added verb alike, including on routes no case exercises. Option (a) scales with the route count and still misses an added route. Option (b) would fail the day the documentation routes are gated, which the plan names as an open follow-on. Option (c) compares request and response schemas too, so any unrelated schema edit would fail it. | Adding a route now requires updating the constant. That is the intended cost of a frozen contract. The excluded-set assertion is deliberately a subset test, since its purpose is to prove nothing unnamed was excluded rather than to pin which documentation routes exist. |
| **DL-150** — Assert the filter create route's declared response model by object identity. | (a) Compare the declared model's field names. (b) Assert the response body keys of a successful create. | Dropping the declaration changes no status code and no field a successful create happens to return, so a request-level assertion cannot see it; measured, that mutation left the reviewed module green. Identity is the exact property the contract states. Option (a) would pass for any model that happened to share field names. | None observed. The other routes' declarations are covered by the route map only as paths and verbs, not as models; the plan freezes a declared model for this route alone. |
| **DL-151** — Seed listings and compare every paginated read against the unpaginated read rather than against a written-out order. | (a) Assert absolute identifier sequences. (b) Add an explicit ordering to the read path so a written-out sequence becomes valid. | The read path declares no ordering, so a written-out sequence would assert an accident of the storage engine and could fail on a different one. Comparing against the unpaginated read asserts the bounds themselves and holds under any stable order. Option (b) changes production behaviour to suit a test, which the Minimal Change Clause forbids. Measured: dropping either bound left the reviewed module at **25 passed, exit 0**, and both are killed now. | The read path still declares no ordering, so pagination is stable only while the natural row order is. That is pre-existing and unchanged here. Declaring an order is a small, separate improvement to the read path. |


### 16.5 SEC-08 response-side secret absence

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-152** — Apply the shared envelope assertion to the credential-bearing 500 case and assert the bearer token is absent from the whole serialized reply, not from the log alone. | (a) Assert only that the `fields` list is empty. (b) Assert the token's absence field by field. (c) Keep the log-only assertion and rely on the sibling 500 cases for the envelope. | Measured against the reviewed case, which read only the `detail` key: echoing the request's `Authorization` header into `fields`, concatenating it onto `error_id`, and adding it under a new envelope key each left the case **passing, exit 0**. All three are killed now. Testing against the serialized reply covers every field, including one a future handler adds, and the key-set assertion refuses that field outright. Option (a) misses the `error_id` route the finding names. Option (b) has to be revisited whenever the envelope grows, which is the failure mode being fixed. | Containment matching cannot see a transformed echo, such as a re-encoded or truncated token. The envelope key-set assertion bounds that: a handler cannot add a field to carry one, and the three fields that do exist are each asserted for type, emptiness or exact value. |


### 16.6 Test-harness and SEC-05 dependency-ordering rationale

The rows below hold the rationale that inline comments in `backend/tests/conftest.py` and `backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py` previously carried. Rule 1 reserves rationale for this log, so each comment site now carries a factual branch or `SEC-NN` marker only, and the `conftest.py` module docstring points here once instead of seven inline pointers.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-153** — Put both the repository root and the `backend` directory on `sys.path` from `conftest.py`, ahead of every application import. | (a) Repository root alone. (b) `backend` alone. (c) Add `__init__.py` files under `backend` and rely on rootdir discovery. (d) A `pytest.ini` or `tox.ini` `pythonpath` entry. | Two module spellings are live in the tree at once: application modules import `backend.app.*`, and the three pre-existing test modules import `app.*`. `ci.yml` runs pytest from `backend`, and local runs happen from the repository root, so neither root is reliably the rootdir. Options (a) and (b) each break one spelling. Option (c) adds package markers to a tree that has none, which changes how every existing module resolves. Option (d) adds a configuration file the repository does not have and AAP 0.9.2 keeps unrelated tooling out of scope. | Both spellings of one module can be imported in a single session as two distinct module objects with separate state. Held off by convention: every security module uses the absolute `backend.app.*` spelling exclusively, and the harness itself imports nothing under the short spelling. |
| **DL-154** — Write every setting into `os.environ` at `conftest.py` module scope, above the first application import, rather than inside a fixture. | (a) An autouse session fixture. (b) `monkeypatch.setenv` per test. (c) A committed `.env` for the suite. | `backend/app/core/config.py` builds `Settings()` at module scope and `backend/app/main.py` calls `create_tables()` at module scope, which opens a connection. Both run on the first `import backend.app.main`, so any fixture-based injection lands after the values are already read. Option (c) would place a settings file in version control, which SEC-01 and SEC-12 exist to prevent. | Import-time injection is invisible to a reader who scans fixtures only. The section banner records the ordering, and the suite fails loudly at collection if the order is ever inverted. |
| **DL-155** — Build the test engine on `sqlite://` with `StaticPool` and `check_same_thread` disabled. | (a) Default pooling on `sqlite://`. (b) A file-backed SQLite database. (c) A PostgreSQL instance for the suite. | `TestClient` runs route handlers on a portal thread while assertions run on the test thread. Default pooling hands those threads different in-memory databases, so a row written through the API is invisible to `db_session`. `StaticPool` shares one connection, and SQLite refuses a cross-thread connection unless `check_same_thread` is disabled. Option (b) leaves files to clean up and slows every test. Option (c) needs a live server the suite must not depend on, and AAP 0.8.1 fixes SQLite as the harness database. | One shared connection serializes access, so the harness cannot exercise genuine concurrency. No security control in scope depends on concurrent database access. |
| **DL-156** — Empty the login-attempt counter at both ends of `isolated_state`, and hand every test a fresh client address. | (a) Reset only before the test. (b) Reset the limiter storage alone. (c) Give the throttle a per-test window short enough to lapse. | The counter holds one entry per account and one per client address. A fresh client address alone leaves the account-keyed entry standing, so a later test registering the same address is answered 429 by state it never created. Resetting at both ends keeps an exhausted counter scoped to the test that exhausted it whichever order the suite runs in. Option (b) misses the in-process dictionary, which is the counter AAP 0.5.7 specifies. Option (c) makes the throttle window a test artefact and weakens the SEC-07 assertions. | Reset reaches the counter by name through the registered route's module namespace. A rename would silence it, so the harness raises at import when no counter named in `_THROTTLE_STATE_NAMES` is found. |
| **DL-157** — Override exactly `backend.app.db.database.get_db`. | (a) Override both that callable and the duplicate `get_db` the baseline `backend/app/main.py` defined, which the shipped module no longer carries — `backend/app/db/database.py:20` is now the sole definition. (b) Rebind `SessionLocal` in `backend.app.db.database`. (c) Point `DATABASE_URL` at the test database and leave dependency resolution alone. | FastAPI matches an override by callable identity. Routes and `get_current_user` both depend on the `database.py` callable, so that one key covers every request path; the `main.py` duplicate is reached by no route and overriding it would assert a dependency that does not exist. Option (b) mutates application module state for the whole session. Option (c) cannot supply the shared `StaticPool` connection DL-155 requires. | The override is silently inert if a future route imports `get_db` from `main.py` instead. Every SEC-02 and SEC-05 case would fail immediately in that event, since no seeded row would be visible. |
| **DL-158** — Construct the test client with `raise_server_exceptions=False`. | (a) Leave the default and assert on the raised exception. (b) Wrap each 500 case in `pytest.raises`. | The default re-raises an unhandled exception into the test, so the registered handlers never get to serialize a response and the sanitized envelope cannot be inspected. SEC-08 is a claim about what the caller receives, which requires the response. Options (a) and (b) assert on the exception instead, which is the opposite of the control being verified. | A genuine harness fault now arrives as a 500 response rather than a traceback. Every 500 case asserts the exact envelope key set and a generic `detail`, so an unexpected fault does not pass as a sanitized one. |
| **DL-159** — Clear the session cookie inside `register_user` before returning the account. | (a) Return the client authenticated. (b) Add a separate unauthenticated client fixture. (c) Register through the database rather than the API. | `POST /auth/register` sets the SEC-06 session cookie on the client that called it. A test that means to exercise an unauthenticated path would otherwise carry credentials it never asked for, which is the false pass the SEC-05 guard-ordering cases are written to avoid. Clearing here makes authentication opt-in: a test that wants it sends the returned `access_token` or logs in. Option (b) doubles the fixture surface. Option (c) bypasses the password policy and the registration path under test. | A test that expects the registering client to stay authenticated has to say so. The fixture docstring records the returned keys, and the token is returned for exactly that purpose. |
| **DL-160** — Assert 401 with an explicit `!= 422` for an unauthenticated request carrying an undeclared key, and mark the authenticated cases as such. | (a) Assert 401 alone. (b) Assert 422 and treat the guard as running after body validation. (c) Drop the unauthenticated variant. | FastAPI resolves a route's sub-dependencies before it validates the request body, so `get_current_user` answers first and an unauthenticated request never reaches the SEC-05 schema. The pair of assertions pins that order in both directions: 401 proves the guard answered, and `!= 422` proves the body was not read. Option (a) passes just as well if the framework ever validates first and returns 401 for another reason. Option (b) encodes the wrong order. Option (c) leaves the order unasserted. | The ordering is framework behaviour, not application code, so an upgrade could change it. The `!= 422` assertion fails loudly if it does, rather than letting the mass-assignment claim rest on an assumption. |
| **DL-161** — Reduce every flagged comment in both files to a factual branch or `SEC-NN` marker and carry the rationale here, with one pointer in the `conftest.py` module docstring. | (a) Keep the explanatory comments and cite Rule 1's code-comment allowance for threat markers. (b) Delete the comments outright with no log rows. (c) Add a pointer line at each of the seven `conftest.py` sites. | Rule 1 names this log as the single source of truth for why a decision was made. AAP 0.11.3 resolves the conflict with the user's threat-comment directive by splitting what from why: the comment states the fact and carries the identifier, the log carries the reasoning. Option (a) keeps two competing explanations in the tree. Option (b) loses reasoning that is load-bearing for anyone changing the harness. Option (c) repeats the same pointer seven times for one destination. | A reader at a comment site sees the fact and not the reasoning. The single docstring pointer names this section, and each row above names the construct it covers. No behaviour changed: the suite reports the same 253 cases before and after. |

## 17. Reconciliation of the code review remediation passes (X1 through X9)

`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `backend/app/core/security.py`, `backend/app/core/config.py`,
`backend/app/main.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py`,
`backend/app/schema/filter.py`, `backend/app/schema/listing.py`, `backend/app/schema/user.py`,
`backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, `backend/app/db/database.py`, `backend/requirements.txt`,
`backend/tests/conftest.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_*.py`, `.gitignore`,
`documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Sections 15 and 16 record two remediation passes over the same change set, reviewed independently and
against a tree that had since moved on. Nine of their decisions contradict each other or contradict
work already in the branch, so each contradiction was settled once and the outcome recorded here.
Where a row below supersedes a row in section 15 or 16, that earlier row stays as the measured record
of what its pass concluded; this section states what shipped. Every rejection names the evidence that
settled it, because a rejected remediation with no recorded cause is indistinguishable from a
remediation that was dropped by accident.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-162** — X1. Keep the frozen gate shape — `pip-audit --strict --no-deps -r /tmp/frozen.txt` with the ten explicit `--ignore-vuln` arguments — and add `--all` to the freeze that feeds it. **Supersedes DL-95.** | (a) Environment mode, dropping `-r` and `--no-deps`, as DL-95 chose. (b) Plain `pip freeze`, as four of the passes restored. | The two passes disagreed on a real gap: plain `pip freeze` omits the build toolchain, so advisories in it are invisible to the gate. Measured, `--all` adds exactly `pip`, `setuptools` and `wheel` and nothing else, which is precisely the omission DL-95 identified. It closes that gap without leaving the invocation the requirements freeze, and without the reproducibility loss of auditing whatever happens to be installed rather than the manifest closure. Both audits were then run: the finding sets are byte-identical today — five reported, ten ignored, exit 1 — so the change widens the audited surface at no cost and adds no suppression. `ignored 10` also proves every one of the ten identifiers still matches a live advisory, which is the property the deleted cross-check existed to assert. | The three added distributions are audited but not pinned, so a toolchain advisory becomes a gate failure with no manifest line to raise. That is the intended direction, and it is why the gate names the runtime upgrade as its review trigger. |
| **DL-163** — X2. Keep the hardened payment authorization in `paypal_service.py` — currency, plan and payer binding, the one-time-consumption ledger and the provider timeout. | Replace it with the six-line self-contained wrapper, deleting the nine helpers, the logging and the SDK logger mutation. | The replacement removes checks that pre-date both passes rather than only the additions, so it is not the smaller change it appears to be. The reviewer who requested the hardening documented a working exploit against the unhardened path: an amount approved in one currency settling in another, and one payment reference charged three times. Twenty-four tests in `test_config_guards.py` assert the behaviour, one of them calling `_find_payment_resource` directly. Removing the helpers would delete a demonstrated control and take a passing suite with it. | The authorization path is the largest single addition in the change set, and the Minimal Change Clause argues against it. The exploit evidence is what outweighs that, and the surface is covered by the twenty-four tests rather than by inspection alone. **Superseded by DL-288.** |
| **DL-164** — X3. Keep `_CANONICAL_SUBJECT`, `_MAX_SUBJECT_ID` and the `int()` coercion guard in `security.py`. | Delete all three and let the coercion and the column comparison stand alone. | Deleting them reopens the finding they close. The guard is what refuses `"007"`, `" 7"`, `"7_0"` and the fullwidth digit `"\uff17"`, each of which `int()` coerces to a live primary key. The regression tests seed an account at exactly that key before presenting the token, so a token naming no account is not what earns the refusal. Removing the guard resolves the seeded row and answers 200. The `isinstance` test is first in the chain, so a non-string claim short-circuits to 401 without reaching the regular expression. | The canonical spelling is now a contract: an identifier scheme that ever emits leading zeros or a signed form would be refused. The ceiling is asserted against the exact key width, so a column change has one place to look. |
| **DL-165** — X4. Keep `SanitizedServerErrorMiddleware` registered inside the CORS boundary, and keep the integer-location suppression in the validation handler. | Delete both as surplus to the four registered handlers. | Both are load-bearing and each is pinned by a test that fails without it. A sanitized 500 must still carry the allow-origin, allow-credentials and vary headers, which is only true for a handler registered inside the CORS layer. A malformed-body 422 must not report the byte offset where parsing stopped, because that offset discloses where in the submitted bytes the parser gave up. Both were confirmed by removing the code and watching the specific case fail. | The generic unhandled-exception boundary still cannot carry the CORS headers, which is the gap recorded at DL-74 and unchanged here. The two rows must be read together. |
| **DL-166** — X5. Let `UserLogin` carry the NUL-byte guard only, and re-apply none of the registration password policy. | (a) Re-apply the full policy — length ceiling and character classes — to the login model. (b) Carry no guard at all. | Three passes independently reached the same conclusion for the same reason. A credential that predates the policy must reach authentication and receive the uniform 401, because refusing it at the schema with a 422 tells an unauthenticated caller that the submitted value was well formed but non-compliant. The NUL guard survives because a NUL byte reaches the hasher rather than the comparison, and the hasher refusing it must still surface as the same 401 — asserted directly, never 500 and never 422. | A legacy credential of any shape reaches the hasher. That is deliberate, and the uniform refusal is what keeps it from being an oracle. |
| **DL-167** — Keep the listing read-path bounds and the nullable listing response fields, rejecting both passes' reverts. | Revert `listings.py` to bare `skip`/`limit` defaults and restore `Listing` without `orm_mode` or the `Optional` fields, keeping only the import repair. | Both reverts are blocked by tests already in the branch, and one of them breaks a frozen contract. Six parametrized cases require a negative, non-numeric or above-ceiling pagination value to be refused with 422 at the request boundary, which only the bounded `Query` declarations produce. `Listing.from_orm()` is called at two sites in the read path, and under pydantic 1.x that raises `ConfigError` without `orm_mode`. The revert would therefore break the public read path the requirements freeze, and the model's own columns are nullable and need the `Optional` fields to serialize. Because the code stays, section 13's rationale for it stays too. | The bounds and the response shape are wider than a literal reading of the requirements asks for. They are retained on test and contract evidence rather than on preference, and section 13 is where their rationale lives. |
| **DL-168** — Keep the registration `IntegrityError` branch in `auth.py`. | Remove it and rely on the duplicate-address pre-check alone. | Removing it was measured, not argued about: the lost-unique-address case answers 500 instead of 400 without it, because the commit fails after the pre-check has already cleared. The two defences answer identically by design, which is what stops a caller telling them apart, so the response cannot be the discriminator — the statement count is, as DL-90 records. | The race is exercised through a fixture rather than genuine concurrency, so it pins the handler's behaviour and not the database's isolation guarantee. |
| **DL-169** — Keep the settings validators and the `python-dotenv` pin, and accept the two reductions that no test depends on. | Remove `DB_SSLMODES`, the positive-integer field constraints and the list-form parser from `config.py`; drop the `python-dotenv` pin; keep `hide_parameters` and the nested-criteria strictness. | The settings reductions are test-blocked and one is import-blocking: `DB_SSLMODES` is imported at the top of `test_config_guards.py`, so removing it turns one module into a collection error rather than a failure, and the parser's guidance text is asserted through the raised cause. Dropping the `python-dotenv` pin trades a working documented setup for one fewer advisory in a gate that is red either way — the pass proposing it recorded the resulting `ImportError` itself. The other two reductions were checked the same way and no test asserts either, so `hide_parameters` and the nested strictness went: statement logging is not the sanitizing control, responses are, and the nested allow-list still refuses an unknown key. | A NUL byte inside a criteria value now reaches the driver and surfaces as a sanitized 500 rather than a 422. That is a narrowing of input validation, accepted because no requirement asks for it and the response leaks nothing. |
| **DL-170** — X7. Carry one login-throttle test module holding every pass's distinct assertion. | (a) Take one pass's module whole. (b) Keep both under different names. | The passes asserted different properties of the same control. One pins the route-level limit to the endpoint the application actually routes `POST /auth/login` to; one pins the account-keyed counter as independent of it; one pins register and logout as carrying no route limit; one pins the counter map as bounded. Dropping either module would drop assertions no other case makes. Merging them required one host-address counter and one reset helper rather than the two each pass had assumed, and the map-cap case had to be re-derived for an account-only counter after the address layer moved to the route limiter. | The module now has one source of truth for its helpers, so a future change to the throttle's key shape lands in one place and fails loudly rather than in two places that can drift. |
| **DL-171** — Keep `.env.tmp.*` in `.gitignore` alongside the five secret-custody patterns. | Reduce the file to the five patterns exactly, as both reductions proposed. | The provisioning script creates `./.env.tmp.XXXXXXXX` holding real generated secrets under a restrictive umask and renames it over `.env`, so an interrupted run leaves that file in the working tree. DL-55 records the pattern as part of that design, so removing it would both widen the exposure the finding closes and falsify a row in this log. | The file carries one pattern more than the requirements enumerate, which is the deviation this row exists to record. |
| **DL-172** — Keep section 13. | Delete it, as one pass did, on the grounds that the decisions it records are out of scope. | The deletion was premised on the listing bounds and nullable response fields being reverted. They were not, for the reasons at DL-167, so the code those three rows explain is in force. Deleting the rationale for shipped code is the failure Rule 1 exists to prevent, and it would have left the section's three keys referenced from nowhere. | Section 13 documents decisions a reviewer may still consider wider than the requirements require. The rationale is what makes that judgment possible, which is the argument for keeping it. |
| **DL-173** — X6. Import both remediation passes' rationale as sections 15 and 16, renumbering their keys into one collision-free sequence, rather than keeping one and discarding the other. | (a) Keep one pass's section and drop the other's. (b) Keep neither and record only the reconciliation. | Both passes numbered their rows from DL-70, so the two sets collided with each other and with section 14 — three different meanings for the same key. Discarding either would have left decisions that shipped with no recorded rationale, and it had already caused a concrete defect. Six live references in sections 2 and 3 pointed at DL-70, DL-71 and DL-73 as the suppression-list rows, while the keys they resolved to were the coverage decisions of section 14. Both blocks were shifted whole and references below DL-70 left untouched, because they name pre-existing rows. At the time of this decision: 161 keys, no duplicate, no gap, and every one of 265 mentions resolved. **The six pointers this row undertook to retarget were not retargeted; DL-294 does it, and re-measures the log at its current size.** | Two passes reached the same conclusion about the suppression set independently, so DL-93 and DL-135 record one change twice from different vantage points. Both are kept because each names evidence the other does not. |
| **DL-174** — Correct the out-of-range subject docstring and its constant comment to describe the guard that shipped. | Leave the text as the pass wrote it. | The text explained the refusal as the driver refusing an oversized comparison, which is what happens only if the canonical-subject guard is deleted — the deletion rejected at DL-164. With the guard in place neither spelling reaches a query: one is refused by the key ceiling and the other by the canonical width. The case still passed, so nothing failed to reveal it; a docstring describing code that is not there is exactly the residue a reconciliation has to find. | Two texts now describe one guard and must move with it. The assertion is on the refusal rather than on the branch that produced it, so a future change to which check fires first does not break the case. |
| **DL-175** — Adopt the stricter assertions and the subject partition from section 16, and keep both passes' cases wherever they cover different ground. | (a) Keep the existing subset assertions. (b) Take the newer module whole, dropping the environment-path allow-list cases. | The newer assertions compare the advertised method and header sets whole rather than as a subset, so an added verb now fails as surely as a missing one; the token sets account for the headers the framework adds on its own. The subject partition is a strict improvement for the same reason as DL-164 — it seeds the account the coercion would reach before presenting the token. Where the two passes covered different ground the cases were combined rather than chosen between, which is why the environment-path allow-list cases survive alongside the route-map and pagination cases. Twelve parametrized cases were relocated rather than lost, confirmed by comparing collected case identifiers before and after rather than by comparing counts. | Two modules now assert overlapping ground through different entry points, which a reader could mistake for duplication. Each docstring states which path it takes. |

## 18. QA remediation of the security pass: provisioning, request strictness, redaction, key domain, frozen contracts, harness exposure, prose and log currency (D1 through D8, U1 through U10)

`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, `backend/app/schema/filter.py`, `backend/app/db/database.py`,
`backend/app/core/security.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py`, `backend/app/schema/listing.py`,
`backend/tests/conftest.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_auth_identity.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_cors_policy.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_error_handling.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_login_throttle.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_password_policy.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_token_storage.py`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
`frontend/src/services/api.ts`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Ten review reports covered this branch: one generated against the current head, and nine against a tree
three commits older. Where they disagree, the later report and the AAP settle it, and every settlement is
a row below. Two rows correct this log itself, which one of the earlier reports found stale against
`backend/app/main.py`. Several rows record a deviation from a per-file change contract and several record
a decision this round declined to reverse; each names the authority that settled it. Every value below
was measured on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure, on PostgreSQL 13.23 over TLSv1.3 and on
SQLite, on 2026-08-02.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-176** — Record that the sanitized envelope now reaches a cross-origin caller on a truly unhandled exception, and that DL-69's option (c) shipped. **Supersedes DL-69.** | (a) Leave DL-69 standing and let a reader discover the contradiction from `main.py`. (b) Delete DL-69 and write the implemented state as though the gap never existed. (c) Record the supersession and keep DL-69 as the measured record of the state it described. | DL-69 recorded a gap and named its own review trigger — the next milestone owning `backend/app/main.py`. That milestone arrived and shipped exactly the fix DL-69 nominated. `SanitizedServerErrorMiddleware` is defined at `main.py:141-169` and registered at `main.py:172`, ahead of `CORSMiddleware` at `main.py:177-178`, which Starlette's stack builder places outside it, so the sanitizing layer runs inside the cross-origin layer. The middleware re-raises once a response has started, leaving a partially sent body to the framework boundary, and otherwise calls `handle_unhandled_exception` and sends its envelope from inside the CORS layer. It passes inside the 430-case suite. Option (a) leaves the designated source of truth contradicting the code it describes, which is the defect the review raised. | None to behaviour: this row records a state already shipped and already asserted. The residual DL-69 named — a cross-origin client unable to read the correlation identifier for an unexpected server error — is closed. One boundary stays outside the sanitizing layer by construction: a response whose headers have already been sent cannot be replaced, so a failure part way through a stream still completes at the framework boundary with no envelope. That case is unreachable for the JSON responses this application returns, which are sent whole. |
| **DL-177** — Assert the cross-origin response headers on a 500 raised through the generic `Exception` handler, not only on one raised inside the CORS boundary. **Supersedes DL-74.** | (a) Keep the assertion scoped to a database-error 500, as DL-74 decided. (b) Assert the headers on the generic case only. (c) Assert them on both the database-error 500 and the generic 500. | DL-74's scope was a consequence of DL-69's gap, and its risk column states the reason plainly: a test on the generic case would fail. DL-176 records that it no longer does. The suite now carries that case: `test_a_sanitized_500_reaches_an_allow_listed_origin` drives a `RuntimeError` through the `failing_database` fixture and asserts the headers. The case DL-74 called impossible is now the case that pins the wiring, and it pins it at the weakest point rather than the strongest. Keeping DL-74's scope would leave the available assertion unwritten and leave this log claiming a passing test would fail. Option (b) drops the database-error 500, whose handler sits inside the boundary for a different reason and which independently pins the handler registration; both are kept. | The assertion depends on `SanitizedServerErrorMiddleware` staying registered ahead of `CORSMiddleware`. Reordering the two makes it fail, which is the mutation sensitivity it exists to supply. Coverage of a response that fails after its headers are sent is still absent, for the reason DL-176 records. |
| **DL-178** — Restore the owner-role schema bootstrap and `REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC` in the provisioning script, and keep the startup `create_tables()` call as verification only. **Supersedes that file's deviations D11 and D12.** | (a) Leave the grant batch as the branch carried it, with no revoke and the application role free to create objects. (b) Revoke `CREATE` from `PUBLIC` and let the application create its own schema, which deviation D12 accepted. (c) Revoke `CREATE` from `PUBLIC` and provision the schema under a separate owner role before the application ever connects. | After the revoke, the owner role holds `CREATE` on the schema, the application role does not, and `PUBLIC` does not. Option (b) does not work, and that was executed rather than assumed — the evidence below records both runs — so a hardened database combined with an application-owned schema is a provisioning failure rather than a hardening. Option (c) provisions the schema under the owner role, leaves the application role with data rights and no `DDL`, and lets the startup `create_all(checkfirst=True)` stand as verification because it issues no `DDL`. Option (a) leaves CWE-250 open, with every role including `PUBLIC` able to create objects in the schema the application reads. | The owner credential now reaches a second process. It travels in the environment, never in argv, and the owner role is used for provisioning alone. On PostgreSQL 15 or newer the server revokes `CREATE` from `PUBLIC` by default, which makes the revoke a no-op there and the owner bootstrap the load-bearing half — the reason option (b) cannot be substituted later. Every scratch role and database was dropped and the residue check returned zero of each. |
| **DL-179** — Repair `install_dependencies` and `setup_virtual_env` in the same pass, against that file's instruction to leave both as pre-existing functional defects. | (a) Leave both as the change contract directs and fix only the credential and privilege defects. (b) Repair the manifest path alone. (c) Repair the ordering, the manifest path, the target interpreter and the working directory together. | DL-178's owner bootstrap imports the application's model module and runs SQLAlchemy, so it cannot execute unless `backend/requirements.txt` is installed into the interpreter that runs it. The branch installed a root `requirements.txt` that does not exist, before the virtual environment existed, with no abort on failure — so the privilege fix would have been provisioned by a script that had already failed silently. The repair has five parts, listed in the evidence below, and every stage carries `\|\| exit 1`. Option (a) leaves the security fix both untestable and unreachable. Option (b) leaves the ordering defect, which is what makes the failure silent. | Two functions the change contract placed out of scope were modified. Both changes are provisioning integration only and neither touches a credential path. Two defects inside the same file are deliberately left: `check_software` keeps its own internal `exit 1` calls with no `\|\| exit 1` added at its call site, and `run_migrations` keeps its framework assumption and its unguarded call. Neither participates in a credential or privilege path. |
| **DL-180** — Feed both generated role passwords to `psql` on standard input as `\set` meta-commands, against the change contract's prescribed `psql -v name=value`. | (a) Pass both values as `-v` arguments, as prescribed. (b) Write them into a temporary SQL file and pass its path. (c) Send `\set` lines on standard input ahead of a single-quoted SQL heredoc. | Argv is world-readable on this platform, so option (a) discloses both credentials to every local process for the lifetime of the command (CWE-214). Measured with a stub `psql` that captures its own argv and its own standard input: argv is exactly `-v ON_ERROR_STOP=1 -d dbname`, neither sentinel value appears in it, and standard input carries the two `\set` lines followed by the eleven statements including the revoke. Option (b) creates a second secret-bearing file to create, permission and remove, which is more surface than the defect it closes. The heredoc quoting is load-bearing in opposite directions and both were verified: the environment-file heredoc is unquoted so the shell expands the generated values, and the SQL heredoc is single-quoted so the `psql` variable references reach the client unexpanded. | The exit status of a piped `psql` is not the shell's last status, so the script reads `PIPESTATUS[1]` and aborts on a non-zero value; that branch was exercised. A shell without `PIPESTATUS` would lose the check, and the script already declares `bash`. |
| **DL-181** — Write the environment file to a `umask 077` `mktemp` file and publish it with an atomic rename, instead of writing the destination directly and adjusting its mode afterwards. | (a) Keep the direct write followed by `chmod 600`. (b) Wrap the direct write in `umask 077`. (c) Write a mode-600 temporary file in the destination directory and `mv -f` it into place. | Option (a) leaves the file world-readable for the window between creation and the mode change, and it follows a pre-existing symlink at the destination, writing the secret wherever that link points (CWE-59, CWE-732). Option (b) closes the mode window and leaves the symlink follow open. The temporary name is covered by the `.env.tmp.*` pattern DL-171 keeps in `.gitignore`, so an interrupted run cannot leave a committable secret; an `EXIT` trap removes it and the trap is cleared only once the rename has succeeded. | The temporary file shares the destination directory, which is what makes the rename atomic and confines it to one file system. Both generated passwords are checked against the character set a connection string can carry before use, so a value needing percent-encoding aborts the run instead of producing a malformed URL. |
| **DL-182** — Accept the provisioning script at 260 lines against the change contract's 98, and keep `check_schema_prerequisites` rather than folding it into the owner bootstrap. **Amends the deletion recorded in this row.** | (a) Hold the length by dropping one of the three restorations. (b) Move the owner bootstrap into a second file. (c) Delete the helper as redundant, since the owner bootstrap performs the same check by executing it rather than predicting it. | The growth is DL-178, DL-179 and DL-180 plus their abort branches; no added line is decorative. Option (a) is the reversal the current authority named as the Rule 1 failure. Option (b) adds a file the AAP's file map does not carry and splits one provisioning flow across two artifacts, which makes the ordering guarantee DL-179 restored unenforceable. Option (c) was taken once and is reversed here, because the two checks fail at different moments. The helper probes the imports before the first database object exists, while the owner bootstrap reaches the same failure only after `createdb` and both `CREATE ROLE` statements have run, leaving a database and two roles behind. That is the unchecked-outcome exposure the call site names as CWE-252. Two static assertions also hold the script to it, listing the helper among the required functions and fixing its position ahead of the owner bootstrap. | An earlier review rated the length itself a concern. Length is a proxy for reviewability and the file's structure is unchanged: one function per concern, each returning non-zero on failure, called in dependency order from `main()`. The retained helper duplicates work the owner bootstrap repeats, which costs one import probe per run and is the price of failing before the first object is created. |

(d) Annotate the three fields `StrictStr`. | A schema whose stated purpose is refusing input it does not declare was accepting three JSON types it does not declare and writing them into non-null columns (CWE-20). Option (c) is more lines for the same effect. After the change, number, boolean, float, array and object each raise a string type error at the model and 422 at the route with no row written, and a string value is still accepted. | `StrictStr` was already imported for `FilterCreate.name`, so no import changed. (b) Redact inside every consumer that renders an exception. Withheld parameters make a production failure harder to diagnose from its message alone; the correlation identifier in the envelope is the intended route from a user report to the server-side record. |
| **DL-185** — Narrow the subject-identifier ceiling to the 32-bit range the identifier column maps to, and keep the canonical-decimal guard the check already used. | (a) Replace the guard with a bare integer coercion, as an earlier review prescribed. (b) Keep the guard and the signed 64-bit ceiling. (c) Keep the guard and use the mapped 32-bit ceiling. | The authority is the column, and it was read from the live server rather than inferred. The catalogue reports the identifier as `integer` with 32-bit precision defaulting from its sequence, that sequence's maximum is 2147483647, and casting 2147483648 to `int` raises `integer out of range`. The model declares an `Integer` primary key on all six tables. The previous ceiling admitted roughly 4.3e18 subject values the column can never hold. The canonical-decimal check is kept because it refuses spellings a bare coercion accepts — a leading sign, surrounding space, hexadecimal, leading zeros and exponent forms — before any database comparison. The two checks answer different questions, spelling and magnitude, so option (a) drops one of them. | A migration of the identifier column to a 64-bit type must raise this constant in the same change. The marker at the constant names the model module as the authority, so the coupling is visible where the value is set. |
| **DL-186** — Treat the ceiling narrowing as a behaviour change and pin the client-visible identity of the two refusal branches. | (a) Change the constant and assert only that the out-of-range subjects are refused. (b) Assert the refusal and also assert that the response is indistinguishable from the unresolved-account refusal. | A subject between `2**31` and `2**63` previously reached the database comparison and drew the unresolved-account 401; it now draws the ceiling 401 with no query. The status, the envelope and the `WWW-Authenticate` header are identical, so nothing a client can observe changes apart from the per-response correlation identifier, and the new case asserts exactly that by comparing refusal signatures. Two cases sit on either side of the boundary: a seeded row at 2147483647 resolves with 200, which a guard written with `>=` fails, and a seeded row at 2147483648 is refused. The second is seeded deliberately — SQLite accepts a 64-bit integer primary key, so the row genuinely exists and the refusal cannot arrive through the unresolved-account branch. Mutation-replayed: at the 32-bit ceiling the higher row draws 401 and the boundary row draws 200; at the 64-bit ceiling the higher row draws 200. | The uniformity assertion compares everything except the correlation identifier, so a later change that adds a distinguishing header to one branch fails the case, which is the intent. Subjects in the narrowed band lose a database round trip, which is a reduction in work rather than in capability. |

A negative bound diverges by dialect — SQLite clamps a negative offset to zero and treats a negative limit as unlimited, returning the whole table, while PostgreSQL 13 raises on each — and the divergence is recorded beside the cases it invalidated. |
