**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-176.** Measured:
  `test_error_handling.py::test_a_sanitized_500_reaches_an_allow_listed_origin` injects a
  `RuntimeError` — the generic `Exception` key, not the `SQLAlchemyError` key DL-74 scoped
  itself to. That case asserts 500, the three-key envelope, `detail` exactly `Internal server
  error`,
  `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` equal to the allow-listed origin,
  `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials` equal to `true`, `origin` present in `Vary`, a JSON content
  type, no forbidden substring, and no exception sentinel in the body. Option (b) destroys the
  measured record of the boundary as it stood, which the supersession convention exists to
  preserve.
- **DL-178.** Measured live against PostgreSQL 13.23 on a scratch database with scratch roles.
  Option (b): `Base.metadata.create_all` as the application role is refused with `permission
  denied for schema public`. Option (c): all six tables owned by the owner role, after which the
  application role performed `INSERT`, `SELECT`, `UPDATE` and `DELETE` — the insert proving the
  sequence grants a `SERIAL` column needs. And its `DDL` was refused;
  `create_all(checkfirst=True)` as the application role against the provisioned schema then
  succeeded and issued no `DDL`.
- **DL-181.** Measured in a scratch directory after the change. A symlink planted at the
  destination is replaced and its target is never created, the published file's mode is 600, the
  generated signing key is 64 characters against the 32-character floor the settings enforce,
  and no temporary file survives.
- **DL-179.** The repair's five parts: derive the repository root once; install
  `"$repo_root/backend/requirements.txt"` through `python3 -m pip` so the active interpreter is
  the target. Run the frontend install in a subshell from `$repo_root/frontend` so the caller's
  directory is untouched; guard both the virtual-environment creation and its activation;
  reorder `main()` to create the environment before installing into it.
- **DL-183.** Measured before the change, at both layers: the model coerced a JSON number, a boolean and a float into strings, and `POST /filters/` answered 200 and persisted a criterion row for each. None measured in the response direction: all three mapped columns are `String`, and `from_orm` on a stored row was re-verified.
- **DL-184.** Measured on a real duplicate-key failure rather than a constructed one. Option (a) protects only what reaches the HTTP boundary, and `tasks/listing_updater.py:29` prints a rendered exception from a background job no handler wraps, which is the measured exposure. None measured.
- **DL-187.** Measured: `skip` or `limit` at `2**63` returns a sanitized 500 carrying the three-key envelope and no driver text, and a new case asserts that, so the failure is bounded to a disclosure-free 500. Recommended follow-on: authorise bounded pagination in the AAP, at which point DL-67's measured values apply unchanged.

## 19. PayPal subscription plan binding at the endpoint caller (F-01)

`backend/app/api/endpoints/subscriptions.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py`,
`documentation/security/decision-log.md`

One review generated against the current head reports a single finding: the hardened payment verifier
refuses every active billing agreement when no plan is bound, while its only endpoint caller still
invokes it with two positional arguments. A real PayPal subscription reference therefore reaches
`plan_unbound` and `POST /subscriptions/` answers 400 before persistence, which breaks the PayPal
sandbox checkout continuity AAP 0.9.1 preserves. Every value below was measured on CPython 3.9.25
against the pinned closure on 2026-08-02.

**This section was superseded once and then reinstated, and it describes the tree again.** A
review of the same boundary found the verifier out of scope and withdrew it, which DL-288 in
section 38 records. The security assessment that followed found that the withdrawal had left
every charge refused. So the verifier and this caller binding are both back: DL-332 in section
41 carries the restoration with its measurements, and the endpoint names the requested plan on
the call once more.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-201** — Pass `plan_id=subscription.plan_id` from the subscription endpoint into the verifier's existing keyword-only contract. **Supersedes DL-122 and extends DL-163.** | (a) Leave the caller alone and treat an absent plan as no expectation inside the verifier, so an agreement authorizes with nothing bound. (b) Revert the verifier to the minimal two-argument wrapper the enabling-repair contract prescribes, which drops the plan check along with everything else. (c) Bind the plan at the caller. | DL-122 chose the keyword-only parameter and named this exact residual: reusable references fail closed until a caller passes a plan. The caller now passes one. The expected plan exists in one place only, the validated request body, so binding it at all requires the caller to supply it. Option (a) reopens the defect DL-163 records as a demonstrated exploit, a reusable agreement issued for one plan authorizing a charge for another, and the review classifies the present state as fail-closed, so relaxing the gate trades an availability regression for an authorization one. Option (b) was already settled at DL-163 on the ground that it deletes controls predating the finding. The endpoint rejects a blank plan with 400 at its first statement, so the keyword cannot carry an empty value to the gate. | The endpoint is a reference artifact in AAP 0.6.1 and the verifier's own change contract directs that the caller stay unchanged; this row is that deviation. The change is one keyword on one existing call, adding no import, no branch and no new failure mode. The endpoint still cannot persist a subscription, for the three schema reasons AAP 0.8.3 records, so the route answers a sanitized 500 once the charge authorizes. **Superseded by DL-288; its option (b) is the shipped choice.** |
| **DL-202** — Cover the binding with route-level cases that inspect the call the endpoint makes, rather than adding another verifier-level case. | (a) Add a verifier-level case asserting `plan_unbound` for an unbound agreement, which the module already carries. (b) Assert the response status alone. (c) Capture the endpoint's call and assert the keyword, and drive the real verifier through the route in both directions. | The finding survived a 430-case green suite precisely because every payment case called the verifier directly, so option (a) reproduces the blind spot. Option (b) cannot distinguish the two 400 branches: the error boundary replaces a raised detail with the status phrase, so `Invalid subscription data` and `Payment processing failed` are identical to a client. Four cases were added instead. One records the positional pair, the keyword set and the plan value. One drives an agreement for the requested plan and asserts the route clears the payment gate and spends the reference once. One drives an agreement for another plan and asserts the refusal with the reference left unspent. One asserts an unauthenticated request drives no provider lookup. Mutation-replayed: removing the keyword fails the first two, and the log for the second names `plan_unbound`. | The refusal case passes under both call shapes, since an unbound plan and a mismatched plan both refuse. Its value is the opposite direction, that a correct binding still refuses the wrong plan. The route cases assert against the payment gate rather than a 200, which the persistence gap makes unreachable. **Superseded by DL-288.** |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-201.** Measured with the provider library replaced: under the two-argument call an active
  agreement is refused `plan_unbound`, `POST /subscriptions/` answers 400 and the reference is
  left unspent. Under the keyword call the same agreement authorizes, the reference is spent
  once, and the request passes the payment gate.

---

## 20. Dependency advisory gate — audited surface, failure isolation, register staleness

`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Runtime testing of the continuous-integration boundary reports the advisory gate as the
checkpoint's single critical finding: the step exits non-zero on a pristine tree. And because it
carried no failure isolation its exit status suppressed the seven steps that follow it, so every
frontend job and both coverage uploads were skipped. Two further findings concern the same step
— the audited surface was widened past the manifest closure, and a fabricated suppression
identifier was accepted in silence. Every value below was measured on CPython 3.9.25 against the
pinned closure on 2026-08-02.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-203** — Restore the specification's `pip freeze > /tmp/frozen.txt` as the audited surface, dropping `--all`. **Supersedes DL-162 and DL-194.** | (a) Keep `--all`, as DL-162 chose and DL-194 defended. (b) Keep `--all` and pin `pip`, `setuptools` and `wheel` in the manifest so the three added distributions become manifest-determined. (c) Audit `backend/requirements.txt` directly. | Substituting the versions a `setup-python` 3.9 runner typically provides — `pip 22.0.2`, `setuptools 65.5.0`, `wheel 0.37.1` — turns five reported advisories into sixteen: six against `pip`, four against `setuptools`, one against `wheel`. The gate verdict was therefore a function of the runner image, not of the manifest, and the same commit could pass on one runner and fail on another. Option (b) would place three build-toolchain versions under manifest control, which reaches past this finding into the dependency contract AAP 0.7.1 fixes. Option (c) loses the transitive closure the ten approved suppressions are written against, and nine of them go stale immediately. The specification states the plain-freeze invocation in section 0.10.1 and that file's change contract requires it byte-exactly, so restoring it also removes a documented deviation. | Advisories in the build toolchain are no longer visible to this gate, which is the coverage gap DL-95 identified and DL-162 closed. The gap is bounded: `pip`, `setuptools` and `wheel` participate in installation, not in the served application, and neither is imported anywhere under `backend/`. Toolchain currency belongs to the runner image and to the runtime upgrade this register already names as its review trigger. |
| **DL-204** — Isolate the gate's failure rather than widen its suppression register: the audit step carries `id: dependency_audit` and `continue-on-error: true`, and a terminal `Enforce dependency audit result` step with `if: always()` fails the job unless that step's outcome is success. **Extends DL-93.** | (a) Add `if: always()` to the seven downstream steps. (b) Drop `--strict`, or end the step with a zero exit, so the gate reports without failing. | DL-93 accepted that the gate exits non-zero on every run and left the consequence unexamined; that consequence is this finding — one reported advisory cost the job its frontend and coverage steps. Option (a) edits seven steps the change contract declares untouchable and the AAP lists as preserved. Option (b) turns a blocking gate into a report, the one outcome no reading of the requirements permits. Isolation is the report's own third suggestion and the only one meeting every constraint: the audit step's exit status stops scheduling nothing, and the job still fails. | The gate exits non-zero on a pristine tree, so a clean run is still not on offer — the position AAP 0.8.4 states in terms. What changes is the blast radius: the five live advisories are reported once, at one step, costing the pipeline neither its frontend jobs nor its coverage artifacts. A red audit step beside a red job sends a reader to this section, where the five identifiers are recorded as known and unpatchable here rather than new. |
| **DL-325** — Report the five live advisories rather than absorbing them: neither suppress them nor narrow the audited surface. Narrowed out of DL-204 so the register's integrity and the gate's failure isolation are each one decision. | (a) Suppress the five advisories the gate reports, the review's first suggestion. (b) Narrow the audited surface to the manifest so the transitive findings disappear, its second. | Option (a) is forbidden twice over: that file's change contract reports an advisory outside the approved list rather than suppressing it on the deliverable's own initiative, and AAP 0.4.5 fixes the approved set at ten. Option (b) still reports two advisories and misses exit zero as well, for the reason the evidence records, and it trades nine suppressions into staleness. Reporting keeps the register a record of approved exceptions rather than a place to hide new ones. | The five stay visible and unpatched on this runtime, so every run carries a red audit step until the runtime upgrade DL-14 names. DL-291 and DL-294 hold the declared count at the reported one. |
| **DL-205** — Reinstate a suppression staleness check, comparing every `PYSEC` identifier declared in the workflow against the identifiers a JSON audit of the same frozen closure reports, and failing the step on any declared identifier the report does not contain. **Supersedes DL-96.** | (a) Leave the check out, as DL-96 chose. (b) Compare the declared count against the `ignored N` figure the gate prints. (c) Maintain the expected identifier list as a second literal inside the step. | DL-96 removed the earlier check on two grounds, a Rule 1 comment defect and a surface mismatch, and both are now addressed. The step carries a threat marker and a pointer to this section with no rationale, and the check reads the same `/tmp/frozen.txt` the gate audits, so the two can no longer disagree about what is installed. Option (b) was tested and is strictly weaker: the gate prints `ignored N` even when it finds nothing, so a count comparison catches a fabricated eleventh entry but not a real identifier that has stopped applying. The check as built catches both, which the evidence below measures on two mutations. Option (c) duplicates the register, so the two copies drift. Deriving the list from the workflow file means the register has exactly one home, and the extraction pattern cannot match itself, since the four characters after `PYSEC-` in it are not digits. | The check asserts only that a declared identifier still applies; it cannot assert that suppressing it remains justified, which stays a human judgement recorded in section 2.2. It reads the workflow at a fixed repository-relative path, so it is coupled to the checkout layout. An identifier written into any other part of the file, a comment included, counts as declared. |
| **DL-206** — Record that the report's remediation-by-upgrade route for the five live advisories does not exist on this runtime, against its claim that `python-dotenv==1.2.2` is installable on Python 3.9. | (a) Accept the claim and raise the pin. (b) Raise the pin and let installation fail in the pipeline. | Register row 15 of section 2.2 states the ceiling and the report contradicts it, so one of the two is wrong and the difference decides whether option (a) of DL-204 was available. Probed directly against the package index on the documented interpreter, every candidate is refused: `python-dotenv 1.2.2`, `msgpack 1.2.0` and `msgpack 1.2.1` all declare `Requires-Python >=3.10`; the highest `filelock` obtainable is 3.19.1 and the highest `pytest` is 8.4.2, below their respective fix releases. A control download of the pinned versions from the same index in the same session succeeded, so the refusals are version gates rather than a network artifact. The register is correct and the report's suggestion is not actionable. Option (b) would break installation for every job, and the manifest's `python-dotenv` pin is load-bearing: without it `Settings()` raises `ImportError` whenever a dotenv file is present. | The five advisories stay live until the runtime moves, which is the single follow-on AAP 0.4.5 ranks highest and which this register already carries as its review trigger. Nothing here re-verifies the reachability assessments in section 2.2; those rest on their own measurements. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-205, mutation sensitivity.** A fabricated `PYSEC-9999-99999` is named and the step exits 1 with the declared count at eleven; substituting a real but inapplicable `PYSEC-2022-43012` is named with the declared count still at ten, which a count comparison would have missed.
- **DL-203.** DL-162 rested its case on a measurement that has since stopped holding: the two invocations produced identical finding sets only while the installed toolchain happened to carry no advisory. Measured now, `--all` adds exactly `pip`, `setuptools` and `wheel`, none of them a manifest pin, so their versions come from whatever the runner's Python distribution ships.
- **DL-204, why narrowing the surface fails too.** `pytest` and `python-dotenv` are themselves manifest pins, so narrowing the audit to the manifest still reports two advisories while trading nine suppressions into staleness.
- **DL-204.** It is also unavailable on the merits: no fix release for any of the five installs
  on the documented runtime, measured directly as `python-dotenv 1.2.2` and `msgpack 1.2.0` and
  `1.2.1` rejected for `Requires-Python >=3.10`, the highest installable `filelock` being 3.19.1
  against fix releases 3.20.1 and 3.20.3. The highest installable `pytest` is 8.4.2 against fix
  release 9.0.3. Measured against a scheduling emulation of the workflow — with the
  audit step failing, no step is skipped and the job concludes failure; with it passing, the job
  concludes success; with it skipped because installation failed, the terminal step still fails
  the job.

## 21. Repository scan gates — coverage, breadth and scan roots

`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Runtime testing reports four findings against the three repository scan gates. Two concern the
tracked-content credential scan: it exempted two whole files from inspection, and its pattern matched
only the four literals the remediation had already removed. One concerns the client-secret guard, whose
single underscore spelling missed the idiomatic camel-case form. One concerns the browser-storage
guard, whose scan root stopped at the application source directory and so left the served HTML
document uninspected. Every count below was measured on the pristine tree and in a disposable clone on
2026-08-02.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-207** — Drop both whole-file exemptions from the credential scan and suppress the gate's own invocation line instead, by filtering the one output line that contains the gate's command prefix. | (a) Keep the two exemptions. (b) Keep the exemptions and narrow them to the offending line numbers. (c) Hold the pattern in a shell variable and filter output lines containing that variable's value. (d) Escape each self-matching alternation so the pattern cannot describe itself. | The exemptions existed for a real reason: three of the four original alternations occur inside the gate's own pattern text, so the scan reported itself. They bought that at the price of two files no scan inspected, one of them the workflow that holds every gate and the other the security document the requirements plan for the repository root. Option (b) is brittle, since a line number moves with every edit to the file. Option (d) rewrites four working alternations to defeat one line of self-reference, which is more change for less coverage. The filter removes exactly one line shape from consideration. | One line in the repository is exempt: any line containing the gate's command prefix. A credential written on that line escapes the scan. This is the whole residual, against two entire files before, and this log deliberately avoids reproducing that prefix so the exempt shape occurs exactly once in the repository. |
| **DL-208** — Add six alternation families to the credential pattern and remove none of the original four; stop the breadth at the point where measured false positives begin, leaving four of the reported spellings uncovered. | (a) Leave the pattern as the requirements state it and amend the prose to "regression scan for known literals", which is the report's own second suggestion. (b) Take the report's first suggestion in full: case-insensitive, quote-agnostic, every common key-name family. (c) Replace the pattern with a general high-entropy value detector. | Option (a) is honest but leaves a scan that cannot catch a credential introduced tomorrow, and the requirements describe this gate as repository-wide rather than as a regression check. The discriminator that makes the key-name family safe is the absence of whitespace around the assignment, which is required in an environment file and forbidden by the formatter in Python source. Constants and settings reads are therefore excluded by construction, and bracketed and interpolated values exclude the documented placeholders. Option (c) belongs to a scanning tool, not to a shell pipeline. | Four spellings remain uncovered by choice, each recorded above with the false-positive count that justifies it. The gate is a pattern matcher, so an attacker who knows the pattern can evade it; its purpose is to stop an accident, not a determined author. A true secret scanner with entropy analysis stays the correct long-term control and is named as a follow-on. |
| **DL-209** — Make the client-secret guard case-insensitive and tolerant of the separator, and extend it to the payment-provider spelling. | (a) Add the camel-case spelling as a second literal. (b) Enumerate every spelling the report names. (c) Match the word *secret* alone anywhere under the frontend tree. | The guard's purpose is that a payment client secret never reaches browser-delivered code, and the spelling a developer would actually write in TypeScript is the camel-case one the report shows evading. Case-insensitive matching with an optional separator covers the underscore, hyphen, camel-case and concatenated forms in one expression, which is fewer characters than option (a) plus option (b) and reaches spellings neither enumerates. Measured: 9 of the 11 spellings tried are detected, including both originals, and the guard reports at all three placements tried, among them the served HTML document and the frontend tree root. A raw base64 blob and a character-by-character obfuscation are not, and both are recorded as out of reach for a keyword matcher. The whole frontend tree stays at zero matches, so the change is coverage without cost. Option (c) matches the word in ordinary prose and comments. | Two forms stay uncovered, one of which needs entropy analysis and the other of which is deliberate obfuscation. The guard reads the working tree, so it asserts what is present rather than what a bundler would emit. |
| **DL-210** — Move the browser-storage guard's scan root from the application source directory up to the whole frontend tree, add the four storage channels the report measured as evading, and exclude the installed-dependency, build and coverage directories in both frontend gates. | (a) Widen the root and accept whatever the widened root reports. (b) Add the served HTML document as a second explicit path. (c) Widen the root and switch both gates to tracked content, as the credential gate already does. | The served HTML document sits outside the old root and reaches the browser, which is the gap the report names, and a second explicit path would leave the next file outside the root as well. The three exclusions change no verdict in the pipeline, where both gates run before dependency installation and none of the three directories exists, and they make the gate reproducible locally. They are applied to the client-secret gate as well, which had no exclusions and no need for them today, so that both frontend gates answer the same question about the same content — the inconsistency the report raises. Option (c) is cleaner still, but the requirements state both invocations as recursive filesystem searches, and matching that form keeps the pipeline step and the documented manual command identical. | Runtime string construction still evades, which no fixed pattern reaches. The exclusions mean a secret committed inside a vendored dependency directory would not be reported; nothing under those three paths is tracked, and the credential gate covers tracked content independently. The guard asserts source content, not the contents of a produced bundle. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-207.** Measured in a clone: a credential planted in the workflow and a credential planted in a root security document are both now reported, and both were previously invisible. Option (c) was built and abandoned on measurement: the pattern must be escaped for the shell, so the text stored in the file and the value the filter compares against are not the same string and the filter never matches.
- **DL-208.** Option (b) was built and measured, and it does not survive contact with this
  repository: dropping case sensitivity alone produces 13 matches on legitimate content, twelve
  of them keyword arguments in the test suite and the thirteenth the model construction in the
  registration endpoint.
  - Taking the suggestion in full, case-insensitive and tolerant of whitespace around the
    assignment, produces 28 matches across 14 files. Among them: the password-hashing call in
    the registration endpoint, the stored-hash column declaration, three service-module settings
    reads, six test-suite password constants, a variable reference in the Terraform
    configuration and three lines of documentation.
  - Three further forms were measured and excluded on the same ground. An unquoted value after
    the keyword collides with four test assertions checking that a password reaches neither a
    log nor a response body. One signing-key name collides with a stale placeholder in a readme
    outside this scope. A scheme-less credential pair collides with eight lines, three of them
    connection-string fixtures including the one the error-handling suite plants deliberately,
    and five of them account-keyed throttle identifiers.
  - Measured after the change: 10 of the report's 14 spellings are detected, all four original
    literals still are, three additional key-name families are, eight legitimate-content
    controls stay clean, and the tracked tree stays at zero matches.
- **DL-210.** Widening the root alone is not viable: measured, the storage pattern matches 134
  files under the installed dependency tree, so the gate becomes unusable on a developer machine
  and its verdict starts depending on what happens to be installed rather than on repository
  content. Adding the four channels closes the alternative storage routes the report measured,
  and bracket-notation access was already caught by substring. Measured: 8 of the 9 storage
  channels tried are detected, the ninth being runtime string construction; the guard reports at
  all three placements tried, among them the served HTML document. The four dependency-directory
  controls stay clean; and both gates stay at zero matches on the pristine tree.

## 22. Workflow step preservation

`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Runtime testing reports that the integration-test step present since the workflow was first added is
absent from the current head, removed without a record in commit `84d71577`. The step list went from
twelve entries to eleven while the requirements list the workflow's existing steps as preserved, and
Rule 1 requires a row for any deviation, so the deletion is a defect twice over. Every value below was
measured against commit `ebe15938`, which added the workflow, on 2026-08-02.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-211** — Restore the step byte-for-byte from `ebe15938` at its original relative position, between the backend unit tests and the frontend coverage upload, and record the deletion and its reversal here. | (a) Leave it out and record the deletion, on the ground that its body is three comment lines and executes nothing. (b) Restore it and replace the placeholder with real integration commands. (c) Restore it at the end of the step list. | The step's body is a placeholder, so its absence changes no behaviour — which is exactly why removing it was easy to do silently and why leaving it out would be hard to defend. The requirements name the workflow's steps as preserved and no finding asked for its removal, so option (a) trades a documented restoration for a permanent unexplained deviation in a deliverable whose whole purpose is auditability. Option (b) writes new functionality into a security remediation, which the minimal-change clause forbids and which nothing in scope asks for. Option (c) preserves the name but not the position, and position is part of what the reviewer diffed. The six added steps are the five gates and the terminal enforcement step. Executed in a job emulation, the restored body exits 0 and occupies position 15 of 18. | The step still runs nothing. It is a marker for work outside this scope, and it will pass silently until someone fills it in, which is the same state it was in before the deletion. Its presence adds one no-op step to every run. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-211.** Verified after restoring: the step list holds all twelve original names, their relative order is unchanged, and all twelve original step definitions compare equal to `ebe15938` — no original step was edited by any of the four gates or by this restoration.

## 23. Version-control exclusions for infrastructure state and build artifacts

`.gitignore`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Runtime testing reports that the exclusion file meets its prescription literally while leaving a
credential-bearing class of file committable. The least-privilege work declares a sensitive
database password variable with no default, and neither Terraform state nor the variable files
that conventionally hold that value were excluded. The state half of that exposure was later
removed at its source — see DL-294 — while the exclusions stayed. A second finding records that
build and environment artifacts were likewise unexcluded, so a careless staging command would
sweep the virtual environment and the installed dependency tree into a security commit. Every
verdict below was measured with `git check-ignore` on 2026-08-02.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-212** — Exclude Terraform state and variable files — the working directory, plain and backup state, workspace state directories, and variable files with an example-template negation — and deliberately leave the provider lock file committable, against the report's suggestion to exclude it. | (a) Exclude only the two exact filenames the report names. (b) Exclude everything the report names, the provider lock file included. (c) Exclude nothing and rely on operators never committing state. | The concrete exposure at the time was that an applied plan wrote the application database password into state in cleartext, which the report confirmed by reading a plan back as JSON, and that the conventional home for that value is a variable file. **That exposure no longer exists: the shipped declaration uses `password_wo` with an `ephemeral` variable, so the value reaches neither state nor plan.** The provider lock file is the one item in the report's list that carries no credential and serves the opposite purpose. It pins provider versions by hash, the same supply-chain control the requirements apply to the Python manifest and name as a follow-on for the frontend. Excluding it would work against that direction, so it stays visible. The example-template negation keeps a documented variable template committable while its real counterpart is not. | State can still leak through a remote backend, an operator's shell history, or a plan file written outside the module directory. Excluding state from version control is not the same as protecting it; a remote backend with encryption is the correct control and remains outside this scope. The provider lock file stays visible by choice, so it appears as an untracked entry after any initialization until someone commits it. |
| **DL-213** — Exclude build, cache and coverage artifacts, adding the frontend build and coverage directories alongside the five paths the report names. | (a) Exclude only the five paths the report names. (b) Exclude nothing, on the ground that the finding is informational and the container ignore file already covers these paths. (c) Add the scan-gate exclusion list verbatim. | The finding is informational precisely because nothing breaks today, but the exposure is a staging accident during a security commit. The two directories the frontend gates were just taught to skip belong on the same list, so both mechanisms answer the same question about the same content. The container ignore file governs image builds, not staging, so it cannot substitute. The compiled-bytecode and cache spellings are added because the cache directory is covered today only by a file the test runner generates inside it, which disappears the moment that directory is cleaned. No tracked file becomes excluded. | Excluding coverage output from version control does not affect the pipeline's coverage upload, which reads the file from disk; that was checked. A future artifact directory not on this list stays visible, which is the safe direction. The dependency directory exclusion means a secret committed inside a vendored package would be neither staged nor scanned, and the tracked-content credential gate covers tracked files independently. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-212.** The exclusions stay as defence in depth and because a variable file still holds the operator-supplied value; DL-294 records this. Option (a) misses the workspace state directory and the automatically loaded variable-file spellings, both of which were measured and are now covered. Measured: the four credential-bearing shapes the report lists are now excluded with the matching rule cited, and four further Terraform artifact shapes with them. A planted state file and a planted variable file holding a plaintext password both disappear from the staging surface, the example template stays visible, and no tracked file becomes excluded.
- **DL-213.** Measured: the untracked entry count falls from 16 to 2, the fourteen that
  disappear are exactly the environment, dependency, bytecode and coverage artifacts, and the
  two that remain are visible by choice. The frontend lock file, which the requirements name as
  a follow-on, and this run's scratch directory.

---

## 24. Developer provisioning script — environment file install path, operator output, maintenance-database reachability

`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Runtime testing of the provisioning script confirms every credential property the
credential-exposure and least-privilege work claims for it. Owner-only file mode, a signing key
above the algorithm's byte floor, a fresh key on every run, replacement rather than following of
a pre-existing symbolic link, no credential literal anywhere in the file, no password on any
command line. Five concurrent invocations return a clean result. Three findings remain
against the surrounding behaviour: the install step reports success when the target path exists
as a directory, the closing message understates what an operator still has to do, and the
application role can open a session against the cluster's maintenance database. Every verdict
below was measured on 2026-08-02, each case in its own temporary directory.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-214** — Refuse the install and return non-zero when the target path exists and is not a regular file, checked immediately before the rename. | (a) Leave the rename unguarded and record the behaviour as a known operator hazard. (b) Remove the offending path automatically and proceed. (c) Rely on the coreutils no-target-directory option so the rename itself fails. (d) Test for a directory only, which is the shape the finding reports. | Option (a) leaves a silent failure in the one path whose whole job is to place credentials correctly. Option (b) deletes operator data unprompted, which a bootstrap script has no standing to do. Option (c) does fail, but it fails with a rename diagnostic that names no remedy and it depends on a coreutils-specific option. Testing for existence together with the absence of a regular file covers every non-regular shape at once. | The guard tests and then acts, so a path replaced between the two is outside its reach. The window is a same-user local race inside a developer script, and the temporary-file-then-rename construction still prevents a partial or group-readable result. A target that is an unrelated regular file is still overwritten, which is the documented behaviour for re-runs. |
| **DL-215** — Tell the operator that the generated file is a subset and that the template is the complete list, and tell them the owner-role password was not retained together with the statement that resets it. Do not print the password itself. | (a) Print the generated owner-role password so the operator can keep it. (b) Persist it to a second file. (c) Say nothing, on the ground that the generated file's contents are unchanged and correct. (d) Generate the full variable set into the file. | The finding measures a real gap in two directions. The generated file carries four keys while startup requires eleven that have no default, so a closing line inviting the operator to update the values understates what is left to do. The owner-role password is generated, used for one schema-creation connection, and discarded, so an operator who needs it later has neither the value nor any signal that it is gone. Options (a) and (b) put a live credential into terminal scrollback, into whatever captures that output, and into any log a wrapper keeps — the exposure class this pass exists to remove — for a role whose password one statement resets. The message therefore states the ephemerality and names that statement in place of the value. Option (c) leaves both gaps. | The message is advisory. An operator who ignores it still meets a startup failure that names the missing variable, which is the fail-closed behaviour the settings guards provide, but nothing enforces that the remaining variables get copied. The reset statement has to be run by hand against the right cluster; the script does not verify that it was. |
| **DL-216** — Accept that the application role can open a session against the cluster's maintenance database and read catalog metadata. Record it; do not revoke in this script. | (a) Revoke connect on the maintenance database from the public pseudo-role inside the script. (b) Revoke it from the application role specifically. (c) Leave the observation unrecorded. | The reachability is real and it is bounded. Closing it from a developer convenience script means a cluster-wide privilege change. The public pseudo-role governs every role on a shared instance, including roles this script never created and has no mandate over, and a per-role revocation on a database the script does not own is not something a bootstrap path should do unasked. The scope boundary places per-service database roles and the wider data-layer privilege redesign outside this pass and labels the least-privilege work a partial remediation; catalog visibility across a shared cluster sits inside that deferred work, not beside it. Recorded as accepted rather than fixed or ignored. | Any role on the instance can enumerate role and database names through the catalogs. On a shared or production cluster the revocation is the right control and stays a follow-on. This is the developer path only: the deployed instance provisions its application role through the infrastructure configuration, where the same least-privilege intent is expressed as a declared resource. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-214.** Measured before the change: with the target existing as a directory the temporary
  file is absorbed into it at owner-only mode and the function returns success. So a freshly
  generated signing key and database password come to rest at a path nothing reads while the
  operator is told configuration succeeded, and the process that later loads the file finds a
  directory.
  - Option (d) leaves two further shapes open, both of which were measured: a symbolic link
    pointing at a directory, and a named pipe.
  - Measured after the change: directory, link-to-directory and named pipe each return non-zero
    with a diagnostic naming the remedy, absorbed temporary files fall from one to none, and the
    target is left untouched.
  - A dangling link returns success, is replaced by an owner-only regular file, and its target
    is not created; a link to a regular file returns success, is replaced, and the victim file
    is byte-identical afterwards, so the previously verified link-replacement result stands.
  - A read-only target is still rotated; an unusable interpreter still returns non-zero with no
    file written; five concurrent invocations still leave one file of four lines at owner-only
    mode with no temporary files behind.
- **DL-215.** Option (d) would change the generated file's contents, whose variable names the requirements freeze and whose construction was verified end to end; the template is already the authoritative list and is what the message points at. Measured: the three lines appear on a successful run, and a scan of the run's whole output for any token-shaped value returns none. Also measured after the change: the generated file still constructs the settings object when merged with the template's documented values, with a signing key of 64 bytes against the 32-byte floor for the configured algorithm and a connection URL naming the least-privilege application role.
- **DL-216.** The role can open a session against the maintenance database and enumerate catalog metadata such as role and database names; it holds nothing on any object inside the application database beyond the grants the script issues, which was measured directly.

---

## 25. Cloud SQL IP configuration — the attribute the encryption block makes explicit

`infrastructure/terraform/main.tf`, `infrastructure/terraform/variables.tf`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Runtime testing confirms the transport-encryption and least-privilege changes to the managed
database resources on every check it applied. The encryption mode reads exactly as specified and
the deprecated argument it replaces appears nowhere. The application account's name, its write-only
password and its rotation version all come from variables: the password reaches the instance through
the write-only `password_wo` argument paired with a `password_wo_version` rotation counter. No
Terraform role assignment remains — DL-293 removed it and DL-324 moved the narrowing out of band —
so the declaration grants nothing. The variable behind the password is marked sensitive and ephemeral
with no default. Re-measured, a plan without that value stops at `No value for required variable`
rather than falling back, `password =` appears nowhere in the configuration, and a differential plan
shows no widening of network reachability.

One informational finding remains: the block that carries the encryption mode also materialises a
provider default that was previously implicit. The measurements below were taken on 2026-08-02
from a scratch module holding the provider block, the three managed database resources copied
byte-for-byte, and their four variables.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-217** — Leave the infrastructure files unchanged and record the consequence: the block that carries the encryption mode pins the provider's IPv4 default as an explicit value in the plan and in state. | (a) Remove the block and express the encryption requirement some other way. (b) Declare the IPv4 flag explicitly in the source so the pinned value is visible where it is read rather than only in the plan. (c) Add the private-network arguments now so a later adoption needs no edit. (d) Record nothing, on the ground that the effective value is unchanged. | Effective reachability is identical either way and the encryption mode is the only behavioural addition, which the evidence below measures against a baseline-shaped instance. Option (a) is not available: the encryption argument exists only inside that block, and the older top-level argument that once served the same purpose is deprecated and deliberately unused. Option (b) adds a source line that changes nothing today and creates a second place that has to stay correct. Option (c) is network architecture, which the scope boundary places outside this pass. Option (d) leaves a real future trap unrecorded, since adopting a private address means setting that flag to false and an explicit true already resting in state reads as deliberate to whoever finds it. | The flag is now pinned in state at true. Whoever adopts private networking has to set it to false or drop the pin as part of that change, and nothing at the source signals this — this row is the signal. Two further measurements sit in the evidence below, on enum-only validation of the encryption argument and on why the version-control exclusions stand regardless. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-217, reachability.** A differential plan against a baseline-shaped instance carrying no block at all renders the entire block as null, while the current configuration renders empty authorized networks, a null private network and an empty service-connect list.
- **DL-217, validation floor.** The provider validates the encryption argument against enum membership and not against a security floor, so weaker modes validate successfully and correctness rests on the source text, which was checked and is exact.
- **DL-217, exclusions.** The version-control exclusions recorded in the preceding section are kept regardless, because state still carries other resource attributes and a plan file is not a publishable artifact.
- **DL-217.** Measured: declaring the block materialises ten attributes in the plan, of which
  exactly two carry a value that is neither null nor empty — the encryption mode this pass sets,
  and the IPv4 flag at the provider default of true. And the earlier measurement that found the
  application role's password in the machine-readable plan in cleartext exactly once was taken
  against the plain `password` argument this configuration no longer uses. The role now takes
  `password_wo` fed by an ephemeral variable, so the value is supplied during apply and
  persisted neither to state nor to a plan file, and a rotation is driven by incrementing
  `password_wo_version` rather than by editing a stored value.

## 26. The shape and scope of this log

`documentation/security/decision-log.md`

Sections 1 through 25 were written by the implementing work as it went, one section per checkpoint.
Sections 26 onward close the gaps that accretion left: two findings had no entry at all, several
design-level choices were never recorded, and the file had no way in. The rows below record the
decisions about the log itself, because the brief for this file prescribed a shape the finished
document does not match, and Rule 1 makes an unexplained departure a defect.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-218** — Keep all 217 rows of sections 1 through 25 unchanged and add the missing reasoning as new sections, rather than rewriting the log to the twenty-five to thirty-five entries its brief prescribes. **Amended by DL-329:** four of those rows were split and their prose tightened, with no alternative, rationale or residual dropped. | (a) Rewrite to the prescribed entry count, folding the accreted rows into summaries. (b) Rewrite and drop whatever does not fit. (c) Split the accreted rows into a second file and keep this one short. | Each of those rows is first-hand: the agent that made the decision wrote the alternatives it weighed and the residual it accepted. None of that is recoverable from a diff, so a summary would be a reconstruction presented as a record. Rule 1 also treats an unexplained deviation as a defect, and 217 silent deletions would be 217 of them. The brief itself directs this file to absorb reported deviations as a floor rather than a ceiling, which is what the accreted sections are. Option (c) splits the single source of truth in two and leaves every inbound pointer ambiguous. | The document is long, and length is its own failure mode: a log nobody reads has recorded nothing. The mitigation is structural, not editorial — the finding index and the section list give a reviewer a way in, and every new cell is held to one or two sentences. A reviewer who needs one finding should never read the whole file. |
| **DL-219** — Leave the section numbering of 1 through 25 exactly as it stands. | (a) Renumber into a reviewer-facing order, grouping by finding. (b) Reorder by finding and update every inbound pointer. | Three comments in `.github/workflows/ci.yml` cite section numbers directly — `:38` points at section 2.2 and `:41` and `:128` at section 20. Renumbering silently redirects those pointers at whatever now occupies the number, which is worse than an ugly order because it fails without any signal. Option (b) is defensible but edits a workflow file to improve documentation ergonomics, which the Minimal Change Clause excludes. | Reading order stays chronological rather than thematic. The finding index compensates by naming the sections that carry each finding. |
| **DL-220** — Present the advisory suppression register in section 2.2 as a nine-column table, leaving it as the one table here that is not the four-column decision shape. | (a) Collapse it to four columns. (b) Move the six extra columns into prose beneath the table. (c) Split it into four-column decision rows plus a separate evidence file. | The register is evidence, not a decision: its rows carry the identifier, its aliases, the fix release, the installability verdict, the entry point and the reachability assessment, and each of those is a thing a reviewer checks independently. Collapsing them into one cell destroys the auditability that makes accepting an advisory defensible. The decisions *about* the register are four-column rows, in sections 2.1, 20 and 29. | A reader scanning for the four-column shape finds one table that breaks it. The heading names it a register rather than a decision table, which is the only signal available. |
| **DL-221** — Carry GitHub-namespace identifiers in the register's Aliases column while the gate declares one canonical identifier per advisory — the `PYSEC-` identifier wherever the advisory database publishes one, and the `GHSA-` identifier for the one entry it does not. | (a) Omit the aliases entirely, so nothing in the file can be copied into the gate by mistake. (b) Declare every namespace of every advisory in the gate. | A reviewer arriving from a vendor bulletin or a scanner report holds the `CVE-` or `GHSA-` identifier, not the `PYSEC-` one, and with no alias column has no way to find the row. One canonical identifier per advisory keeps the declared set comparable to the reported set. DL-373 records the check that makes a mixed-namespace register safe: it reads both `id` and `aliases` from the audit report, so an advisory declared under either namespace is still matched. Option (b) would declare each advisory twice and report the spare declaration as stale. | Someone could still copy a second alias for an already-declared advisory into the gate, which the staleness check reports as declared-but-not-reported. The Aliases column is labelled as aliases and the gate's own comment points at section 2.2, so the authoritative list is one hop away. |
| **DL-222** — Describe the credential-scan pattern by what it detects and name `.github/workflows/ci.yml` as its only runnable definition, rather than reproducing the expression here. | (a) Quote the expression in a fenced block. (b) Quote it with the self-matching alternatives elided. | The expression carries several of the strings it searches for as literal alternatives, so any file reproducing it becomes a hit. Measured on a tree holding no credential at all, a Markdown file that quoted the pattern inside a code fence produced a match and a zero exit — the gate failing on a clean repository. Option (b) still leaves matching fragments and invites a reader to reassemble an inaccurate pattern. | The prose description and the workflow can drift apart. `SECURITY.md` takes the same approach for the same reason, so both documents point at one definition rather than holding two copies. |
| **DL-223** — State the number of files the scan finds, and the file each hit sits in, without quoting any matched line. | (a) Quote the four matched lines as evidence. (b) Give no counts, only prose. | Counts and locations are what make the acceptance metric checkable, and a reviewer can re-run the gate to see the lines. Quoting them reintroduces the credential into a tracked file, which is the defect. Option (b) leaves the reader unable to tell a measurement from an assertion. | A reader has to run the gate to see the matched text. The file and line are given, so that is one command. |
| **DL-285** — Hold the prose standard to the sections written here and leave the inherited cells of sections 1 through 25 unedited, rather than rewriting them for style. **Superseded by DL-329, which takes option (b).** | (a) Rewrite every cell to the sentence-length target. (b) Rewrite only the worst offenders. (c) Claim the whole file meets the standard uniformly. | Measured: the sections added here carry no sentence over 40 words and no paragraph over five sentences. Sections 1 through 25 carry 91 sentences over 40 words, the longest at 108. Editing them means rewording first-hand findings whose measurements cannot be re-verified from a diff, which risks changing a fact in order to shorten a sentence. Option (c) would be a false claim in a document whose whole purpose is review evidence. | Prose density is uneven across the file, and a reviewer moving from section 27 back into section 15 will feel it. The finding index and section list are the way in, so nobody has to read a dense section to reach the one that answers their question. |

## 27. SEC-04 — the server-side password policy

`backend/app/schema/user.py`, `frontend/src/utils/validators.ts` as the authoritative character set

Before this work the policy existed in exactly one place and ran in none. `isValidPassword` at
`frontend/src/utils/validators.ts:18` declares a twelve-character minimum plus four character
classes, and a repository-wide search finds that name once — its own declaration. There are no call
sites, and no file under `frontend/src/` mentions registration at all, so the frontend has no form
that could call it. `SRS:809` asks for "Login and registration forms"; neither exists.

Meanwhile `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py:18` hashed whatever arrived. The policy was enforced in zero
places, not one.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-224** — Enforce the policy at the request-schema boundary, as a validator on `UserCreate`, and change no frontend file. | (a) Enforce it in the endpoint before hashing. (b) Wire the existing client validator into a new registration form and enforce it there. (c) Enforce it in both places. | The schema boundary is the one place the server controls that every write to the password field must cross, so a second endpoint added later inherits the rule instead of forgetting it. An endpoint check protects one route. Option (b) moves a server-side control to the client, where an attacker chooses whether to run it, and it needs a form that does not exist — feature work, not a fix. | The client validator stays dead code, which will read as an inconsistency to the next developer. It is left untouched deliberately: it is the authoritative character set the server mirrors, and rewriting it would put the two definitions out of step. |
| **DL-225** — Mirror the client rule exactly — twelve characters, one uppercase, one lowercase, one digit, one special character from the set at `validators.ts:23` — rather than adopting a stronger policy. | (a) Raise the minimum length. (b) Require a longer passphrase and drop the class rules, which is the current guidance. (c) Add a breached-password check. | Two definitions of one rule diverge the moment either is edited, so mirroring is what keeps them honest, and `validators.ts` is named as the reference so the direction of authority is unambiguous. The finding asks for the existing rule to be enforced somewhere, not for a better rule; strengthening it here is the enhancement the Minimal Change Clause excludes. | The class-composition style is weaker than length-first guidance and pushes users toward predictable substitutions. Raising the policy is a one-line change in both files once someone decides to. |
| **DL-226** — Bound the maximum at 72 **bytes** of the UTF-8 encoding, not 72 characters. | (a) A 72-character maximum. (b) No maximum, relying on the library to refuse over-length input. (c) Reject non-ASCII outright. | The hashing backend truncates at 72 bytes, and a character count does not bound a byte count: a password of 40 accented or CJK characters exceeds the limit while passing a 72-character check, and everything past the boundary is silently discarded. Option (b) was tested against the pinned backend and a 100-character password was accepted without error, so the library supplies no protection at the version this project must pin — see DL-03. Option (c) penalises non-Latin scripts for an implementation detail. | Users whose passwords are long and non-ASCII see a byte limit expressed in a unit they cannot count. The message names the unit and the number, which is the most that can be done without truncating for them. |
| **DL-227** — Apply the policy to registration only, and give `UserLogin` the NUL-byte guard alone. | (a) Apply the full policy on login too. (b) Apply it on login and force a reset when it fails. | A credential minted before the policy existed must still be able to log in, or the fix locks out every existing account — availability damage from a hardening change. The login route's job is to decide whether the presented secret matches, and a composition rule there answers a question nobody asked. Option (b) is a credential-rotation feature. | Accounts created before this change keep weak passwords indefinitely, and nothing here migrates them. A forced-reset campaign is the remedy and is operational rather than code. |

## 28. SEC-09 — the payment environment and the client-secret guard

`backend/app/core/config.py`, `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`

The literal was at `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py:11`, inside the provider configuration
call, carrying a comment instructing a human to edit it before production. The second half of the
finding is different in kind: it asks for verification that the client secret stays out of the
browser bundle, and measurement shows that property already holds. A repository-wide search finds the
secret only server-side, at `config.py:149` and `paypal_service.py:21`, `:77` and `:308`, and zero times
anywhere under `frontend/`, which correctly uses only the public client identifier.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-228** — Read the payment environment from a validated `PAYPAL_MODE` setting restricted to `sandbox` or `live`, and refuse any other value at startup. | (a) Read an unconstrained string from the environment. (b) Derive the mode from another signal, such as a debug flag or the hostname. (c) Keep the literal and rely on the comment. | An unconstrained string turns a typo into a silent fallback, and the provider SDK's behaviour on an unrecognised mode is not a thing to discover in production; the domain check makes the failure a startup error naming the field. Deriving the mode couples the payment environment to something that can be changed for an unrelated reason, which is exactly how a live charge reaches a test account. Option (c) leaves a code edit as the deployment mechanism. | The setting has to be present and correct in every environment. It defaults to `sandbox`, so a missing value fails safe toward the test environment rather than toward live money. |
| **DL-229** — Add a CI step asserting that no client-secret pattern appears anywhere under `frontend/`, even though the current count is zero. | (a) Record the measurement in documentation and add no gate. (b) Scan the produced bundle instead of the source. | The property holds today by accident: nothing prevented a developer from reading the secret into a build-time variable, which is how this class of leak normally happens. A measurement documents a moment; a gate makes it an invariant, and the finding asks for verification rather than for a fix. Option (b) is stronger in principle but the frontend does not build today for reasons unrelated to security, so a bundle scan would be a step that never runs. | The gate asserts source content, not the contents of a produced bundle, so a secret injected by the build system would pass. DL-209 and DL-210 record the pattern breadth the gate settled on. |
| **DL-230** — Bring `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py` into scope. | Treat it as out of scope, since it is absent from the file list supplied with the findings. | The finding text names the file and the line directly, and unlike the other service modules it appears on no exclusion list. Reading a setting requires editing the file that holds the literal; there is no other place to make the change. | The file also carries the two annotation defects and the missing wrapper that block import, repaired under DL-243. Its unrelated defects are recorded and left alone. |

## 29. Version choices where the newest release was declined

`backend/requirements.txt`, `frontend/package.json`

Sections 1 and 2 record the manifest decisions as they were taken. The rows here add the reasoning
those sections left implicit: the empirical comparison that settled the framework version, the three
runtime dependencies no earlier inventory contained, and the frontend pin whose whole purpose is to
close an advisory that the session-cookie fix switches on.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-231** — Pin `fastapi==0.125.0` rather than the more conservative `0.115.14` candidate, and rather than the current 0.128.8. | (a) `fastapi==0.115.14`, which resolves `starlette==0.46.2`. (b) The latest release. (c) An unpinned floor. | 0.128.8 and everything above 0.125.0 require Python 3.10 or newer, so the choice was only ever between the two candidates. Measured against the resolved closure, the conservative set carries eight advisories in two packages and the chosen set carries ten in five. Two of the conservative eight are fixable on Python 3.9, and the transitive `starlette==0.49.3` this pin brings closes both. One is a quadratic-time denial of service in file-response range handling, reachable by an unauthenticated crafted request header and fixed in 0.49.1. The other is a main-thread block when a multipart form exceeds the default spool size, fixed in 0.47.2. Advisory count is not the metric. Reachability is, and the chosen set closes two reachable advisories at no compatibility cost. | Pinned well below the current release line, and 0.125.0 will stop receiving fixes long before the runtime moves. Section 2.2 carries the ten that remain, each with its reachability assessment. |
| **DL-232** — Declare `requests`, `paypalrestsdk` and `sendgrid` as runtime dependencies, none of which appeared in any earlier inventory. | Ship the manifest without them, matching the inventory as received. | Static analysis of the imports found all three in use: `requests` at `backend/app/services/zillow_service.py:1`, `paypalrestsdk` at `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py:1`, and `sendgrid` at `backend/app/services/email_service.py:1-2`. A manifest missing them installs cleanly and then fails on first use of the ingestion, payment or mail path, which is a worse outcome than a manifest that refuses to resolve. | `requests` and its transitive `urllib3` bring three of the fifteen accepted advisories, all unreachable — register rows 8, 9 and 10. Declaring `requests` explicitly is correct rather than redundant, because `zillow_service.py` imports it directly as well as receiving it transitively. |
| **DL-233** — Pin `axios` to an exact `1.19.0`, replacing the `^1.4.0` range at `frontend/package.json:8`. | (a) Keep the caret range, which resolves to 1.19.0 today. (b) Raise the floor to `^1.6.0`, above the advisory range, and keep the range form. | Enabling credentialed requests for the session-cookie fix is the precondition for CVE-2023-45857, rated High at 7.1 and classified CWE-359, which affects axios 1.0.0 through 1.5.1. The defect being fixed is not that a vulnerable version is installed — it is not; the current registry `latest` is 1.19.0 and resolution reaches it. The defect is that the declared floor sat inside the advisory range with no lock file, so resolution was not deterministic: a stale cache, an offline mirror or a parent-pinned environment can legitimately produce a lower 1.x release. Option (b) narrows the window without closing it. | Exact pinning removes the variance for this one direct dependency. Transitive resolution stays non-deterministic without a lock file, which DL-15 declines. Registry verification also found version 1.14.1 absent from the registry, consistent with the compromised release having been unpublished. |
| **DL-234** — Leave the cross-site token options unset in `frontend/src/services/api.ts` while turning credentialed requests on. | (a) Set them explicitly to their conventional values. (b) Set them to the backend's own names. | In the affected axios versions the browser adapter attached a cross-site header carrying a cookie value to every request, to any destination, whenever credentialed mode was on. Newer axios separates the two concerns and keeps the old behaviour behind a separate flag, so leaving that flag unset is precisely what keeps the advisory closed after the credentials flag is turned on. Setting the options would re-enable the header the advisory is about. | No cross-site token is sent, so the browser-side half of a token scheme is absent. `SameSite=Strict` is the control — DL-238 records that trade and the surface it leaves open. |

---

## 30. The token library kept despite an unpatchable advisory

`backend/app/core/security.py`, `backend/app/core/config.py`

DL-13 records the decision. This section adds what makes it defensible: the replacement was validated
end to end rather than assessed on paper, its migration cost was measured, and the residual risk was
converted into an enforced invariant rather than merely accepted.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-235** — Keep `python-jose[cryptography]==3.5.0`, and hold PyJWT as a costed, pre-validated contingency rather than adopting it. | (a) Replace `python-jose` with PyJWT now. (b) Keep `python-jose` and accept the advisory with no compensating control. | Replacing the library removes the one advisory in the closure that will never receive a patch, because `ecdsa` has no fix release and none is coming — register row 6. PyJWT was validated rather than assumed. `PyJWT[crypto]` resolves with four transitive packages, drops both `ecdsa` and `rsa` from the closure entirely, and reproduces the current behaviour across all four failure modes. Those modes are an invalid signature, a disallowed algorithm, an expired token and a malformed token. Migration is two lines in `backend/app/core/security.py` — the import, and the exception class at `:42`, which reads `except jwt.JWTError:` and has no same-named counterpart in PyJWT. Two lines is small, but it is two lines inside the authentication path to remove an advisory that no code path reaches, and keeping the current library is the smaller change. | One unpatchable advisory stays in the closure. Two triggers flip this decision: a policy forbidding suppression of unpatchable advisories, or any move to asymmetric token signing. Because the alternative is already validated, executing it later is a two-line change rather than a research task. |
| **DL-236** — Constrain the `ALGORITHM` setting to `HS256`, `HS384` and `HS512`, turning the unreachability argument into an enforced invariant. | (a) Leave `ALGORITHM` unconstrained and rest on the reachability assessment. (b) Hard-code `HS256` and drop the setting. | "Unreachable because this application signs with HMAC" is only true while nothing can change the algorithm, and the algorithm is a configuration value. The allow-list makes the premise enforced rather than assumed: an operator who sets an elliptic-curve algorithm gets a startup failure instead of quietly entering the affected code path. Option (b) is stronger still but removes a name the requirements freeze. DL-17 records the related finding that `JWKError` and `JWSError` are not subclasses of `JWTError`, which this allow-list is what closes. | The three permitted algorithms all take a shared secret, so this repository cannot move to asymmetric signing without revisiting both this row and DL-235. The signing-key floor is coupled to the chosen algorithm by a root validator, so raising the algorithm raises the required key length with it. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-235.** Pinning 3.5.0 separately addresses CVE-2024-33663 and CVE-2024-33664; 3.5.0 was verified to reject a token declaring `alg=none` by raising `JWTError`.
