## 31. Design choices where a stronger option was out of proportion

`backend/app/core/security.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/app/core/config.py`,
`frontend/src/services/auth.ts`, `frontend/src/services/api.ts`,
`infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml`

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-237** — Mint the subject claim as `str(user.id)` and coerce it back with `int()` inside a guard that answers 401, rather than loosening the comparison to accept what the claim already carried. | (a) Cast the identifier column to text so the existing email subject matches. (b) Look the user up by email instead of by primary key. (c) Accept either shape during a transition window. | This is the one finding where a careless repair is more dangerous than the defect. Before this change the claim was read as a string and compared directly against `User.id`, declared `Column(Integer, primary_key=True)` at `backend/app/db/models.py:10`, so every authenticated request failed — the defect fails closed. The shipped guard reads the claim at `backend/app/core/security.py:57`, screens it at `:75`, and compares `int(user_id)` at `:82`. Options (a) and (b) make it fail open: they turn a broken lookup into a working one keyed on a mutable field, which is an authentication bypass rather than a fix. The direction matters and only one direction is safe, so it is stated explicitly: string in, integer out, with a 401 on anything that will not coerce. Option (c) keeps the broken path alive for the length of the window. | Every token already issued becomes invalid, and no gentler option exists — a dual-acceptance window preserves the defect it is meant to retire. Active sessions re-authenticate once. Rollback repeats the invalidation, which is the one consequence that does not revert cleanly. |
| **DL-238** — Set `SameSite=Strict` on the session cookie rather than adding an anti-forgery token scheme. | (a) A full token scheme with a per-session token echoed on every mutating request. (b) A double-submit cookie. (c) Ship the cookie with no cross-site control. | A token scheme touches every mutating endpoint and every client call site, which is the opposite of a minimal change, and the strict attribute stops the browser sending the cookie on any cross-site navigation, which is the vector. Option (c) is not available: the fix would then trade one weakness for another. | **The cookie migration creates a cross-site request forgery surface that does not exist today.** A bearer header must be attached deliberately by script; a cookie rides along by itself. The strict attribute closes the common case and leaves same-site subdomain scenarios and any future relaxation of the attribute open. A token scheme is the follow-on. |
| **DL-239** — Set the cookie alongside the existing response body, leaving both authentication response shapes byte-identical. | (a) Return the token only in the cookie and remove it from the body. (b) Add a separate identity endpoint the client calls after login. | Both response shapes are frozen contracts, and removing a key from one breaks any consumer reading it. Adding the cookie is additive: the browser stops needing the body value, and a non-browser client keeps working unchanged. Option (b) adds a round trip and a route to avoid an additive field. | The token still appears in the response body, so a client that chooses to store it can. The browser client no longer does, because DL-109 removed all three storage call sites, and the browser-storage gate keeps it that way. |
| **DL-240** — Add no `jti` claim and no revocation list, while adding `iat`. | (a) Add `jti` plus a server-side revocation store. (b) Add `jti` alone. | Revocation needs durable storage that survives a restart and is shared across replicas, and this repository has neither a cache service nor migration tooling — it is a feature build, not a fix. `iat` is one line, costs nothing and serves the audit-trail requirement, so it is included; the token payload carried `exp` alone before this change, at `backend/app/core/security.py:26` in the pre-fix tree. A `jti` with nothing consuming it is a claim that implies revocation exists. | An issued token cannot be revoked before it expires. The expiry window is the only bound, and shortening it is a configuration change. |
| **DL-241** — Normalise the bare 404 at `backend/app/core/security.py:49-50` in the pre-fix tree into a 401 carrying `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`. | (a) Keep the 404 and treat the type mismatch as the whole of the finding. (b) Answer 403. | A 404 for a token whose signature verified tells the caller two things at once: the token was well formed, and no account matches its subject. That difference is an account-state oracle, and the uniformity requirement in the error-handling guidance makes a differential response a finding in its own right rather than a cosmetic detail. The 401 with the challenge header is also the correct answer: the request is unauthenticated, not forbidden. | None identified. The response now matches every other authentication failure, which is the point. |
| **DL-242** — Correct the secret variable name from `JWT_SECRET` to `SECRET_KEY`, at `infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml:31` in the pre-fix tree and `:41` as shipped. | (a) Leave the name and treat it as cosmetic. (b) Add an alias in the settings class accepting both names. | The pre-fix line read `- JWT_SECRET=${JWT_SECRET}` while the backend reads `SECRET_KEY`, declared at `backend/app/core/config.py:6` in the pre-fix tree and at `:143` as shipped, so under the old name the signing secret never reaches the application at all — the container starts with the variable absent. This is the one variable rename in the change set and it fixes a name that never matched anything. Option (b) preserves a name no code reads. | Any deployment environment exporting the old name has to be updated, and the application now refuses to start without the correct one. `.env.example` and `SECURITY.md` are the authoritative list. |
| **DL-243** — Repair the five import-time defects that belong to no finding, and repair nothing else in those files. | (a) Leave them and verify the fixes some other way. (b) Repair them and the surrounding functional defects at the same time. | Nothing could be tested otherwise: five layers of import failure stand between the repository and a running process, each hiding the next, and ten of the twelve findings are unverifiable until all five clear. Each repair is one or two lines. Two are imports: extend the model import at `backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py:7` to include `User`, resolving the annotation at `:18`, and add a typing import to `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, fixing both annotation sites at `:9` and `:41`. The other three add the async wrapper matching the call at `backend/app/api/endpoints/subscriptions.py:24`, correct the `Base` import, at `backend/app/main.py:6` in the pre-fix tree, to name `db.models` where `backend/app/db/models.py:5` defines it. The third supplies `created_at`, at `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py:19` in the pre-fix tree, without which registration raises an integrity error against the non-null column at `models.py:13`. Option (b) crosses from security work into feature work. | Three functional defects are deliberately left: the wrapper's callers, the missing datetime import, used at `subscriptions.py:60`, and the columns those endpoints would need. DL-244 records what that means for the routes concerned. |
| **DL-244** — Restate the record-creation criterion for `POST /listings/` and `POST /subscriptions/` rather than deliver it. | (a) Add the missing columns and make both routes work. (b) Report the criterion as met. | Neither route can create a record today and neither will after this work, for reasons that are not security defects. Verified against `backend/app/db/models.py`. `Listing` at `:19-32` has no owner column while `listings.py:21` supplies one, and its `created_at` at `:23` and `updated_at` at `:24` are non-null and never populated. `Subscription` at `:67-76` has no plan column and a `status` at `:74` that is non-null and never supplied. Separately, `subscriptions.py:46` assigns an attribute `User` does not define, and `subscriptions.py:60` calls a function it never imports. Option (a) needs columns, which needs migration tooling this repository does not have — see DL-247. Option (b) is a false claim in a document whose purpose is review evidence. | The honest criterion: both routes stay importable with their paths, verbs and request contracts unchanged, and the strict schemas close the mass-assignment vector on them. Record creation is a functional gap, unchanged by this work in either direction. |
| **DL-245** — Set `DB_SSLMODE=disable` for the Compose stack. | (a) Set `require` everywhere, including Compose. (b) Omit the variable and inherit the default. | The service named `db` is not a database. `infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml:66` pins `gcr.io/cloudsql-docker/gce-proxy:1.19.1`, so it is the Cloud SQL Auth Proxy, which terminates the encrypted tunnel itself and listens on plain local TCP. Requiring encryption on the application-to-proxy hop would break the stack outright, and hardcoding `require` in code would make that a latent outage rather than a configuration choice. Option (b) inherits a negotiated mode the database vendor's own documentation advises against. | A documented local exception exists and could be copied into a deployed environment by mistake. It lives in the Compose file rather than in code, so the copy would be visible in a diff, and the setting defaults to `require` everywhere else. |
| **DL-246** — Change two lines in `frontend/src/services/api.ts`, a file just outside the supplied frontend list. | Leave the file alone and confine the change to `frontend/src/services/auth.ts`. | Without both changes the cookie fix is inert. the pre-fix `:6` declared `const API_BASE_URL = process.env.REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL;` with no `export` — shipped as an export at `:10` — while `frontend/src/services/auth.ts:2` imports that name — the module does not resolve as written. And the browser transmits no cookie at all unless credentialed mode is on. A fix that cannot take effect is not a fix. | None identified for the export. Credentialed mode is what activates the axios advisory, closed by DL-233 and DL-234. |


---

## 32. The four partial remediations

Four findings are closed in part, not in whole. Each is labelled partial here with the remaining gap
named, because a partial remediation reported as complete is the more serious failure.
[`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) section 3.2 carries the same four as operator-facing residual risk;
the rows below carry the decision that produced them.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-247** — Add no column to `backend/app/db/models.py`. This one decision is why three separate capabilities are deferred. | (a) Add the columns each capability needs and introduce a migration framework. (b) Add the columns and create the schema from the model definitions alone. | The repository has no migration tooling, so adding a column means one of two things. Either introduce a framework, which is a platform change well outside a security fix, or rely on create-if-absent semantics. Those semantics never alter an existing table, so they would silently leave deployed databases without the new column. A control that exists in the model and not in the database is worse than an absent control, because it reads as present. | Three deferrals trace here and nowhere else: durable account lockout has no columns to hold its state, `Listing` keeps no owner column, and per-tenant row-level security has no tenant column to key a policy on. All three become available the moment migration tooling exists. |
| **DL-248** — **SEC-07, partial.** Throttling lands; durable, replica-safe lockout does not. | (a) New columns on the user record holding the failure count and lockout expiry. (b) An external counter service shared across replicas. (c) Address-keyed limiting only. | Option (a) needs a column, which DL-247 forecloses. Option (b) provisions infrastructure that exists in neither the Compose stack nor the Terraform configuration, and the brief named an in-process limiter as the preferred least-invasive approach. Option (c) alone is defeated by a distributed attempt and punishes users behind a shared address, so an account-keyed counter is layered beside it — DL-58 and DL-114 record the shape that shipped. Measured on six consecutive failures: five refusals then a throttle. | **In-process state is per-worker and lost on restart.** With more than one worker an attacker gets the limit multiplied by the worker count, and a restart clears every counter. The threshold and window are configurable, and the durable version needs either of the two foreclosed options. |
| **DL-249** — **SEC-10, partial.** Encryption is required at both ends; certificate verification is not performed. | (a) `verify-full` client-side with a distributed root certificate. (b) `verify-ca`. (c) Configure only the instance, or only the client. | The database vendor's own documentation is decisive on two points. The client default is a negotiated mode it describes as making no sense from a security point of view and not recommended in secure deployments. And a connection is only known to be secured when it is configured at both ends. `backend/app/db/database.py` passed no connection arguments and so inherited that default; the shipped engine at `:9-17` applies `sslmode` conditionally, which is why the engine is fixed as well as the instance. The stronger modes need a root certificate distributed to and rotated on every client, which is infrastructure work rather than a configuration value. | **`require` guarantees encryption and verifies nothing about the server's identity**, so it does not stop an attacker who can already answer as the database. Two halves are easy to conflate and are stated separately: `ENCRYPTED_ONLY` on the instance does not require a *client* certificate, and `require` on the client does not verify the *server* certificate. `verify-full` is the named follow-on. |
| **DL-250** — **SEC-11, partial, and more partial than first assessed.** Role separation lands; least-privilege grants land in one place only. | (a) Declare the grants in Terraform beside the role. (b) Add a provisioning step that connects and issues the grants after apply. (c) Per-service roles. | Two facts found during implementation narrow what infrastructure code can deliver. The managed database service grants its superuser role to every built-in user it creates, and the Terraform provider exposes no resource for a grant or a revoke. So `google_sql_user` at `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf:93` delivers **role separation only**, and `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh` is the sole place the least-privilege grants actually exist. Option (b) needs network reachability to the instance from wherever the apply runs, which the configuration does not establish. | Four gaps remain, each named rather than implied. The cloud application role keeps role-creation, database-creation and login rights until the automatic grant is revoked out of band. On PostgreSQL 14 and earlier the default public create grant on the `public` schema persists unless revoked — DL-36 records adding that revoke to the provisioning path. Creating tables from the model definitions as the application role fails on PostgreSQL 15 and later, because that role holds no create right; DL-37 gives schema creation to the owner role instead. And an insert grant is inert without sequence usage, since every table keys on an integer primary key that the database backs with a sequence. **A later role assignment contradicted this row and has been removed; DL-293 completes it and carries the out-of-band statements.** |
| **DL-251** — Deliver no row-level security, and say so. | (a) Add a policy per table plus a session-variable convention. (b) Describe row-level security as in scope and defer the implementation. | A per-tenant policy needs a tenant column on every table, which DL-247 forecloses, plus a convention that every connection sets and no connection can clear — a data-layer redesign rather than a fix. The design documents in `documentation/` describe application-layer role-based access control, at `Technical Specifications.md:616-618`, `SRS:393`, `SRS:509` and `Software Project Proposal.md:465`, and none of them mentions row-level security at all; the earlier claim that they list it as design intent is unsupported and is not repeated. Option (b) would let a reader infer partial delivery. | **Nothing in this work delivers row-level security.** No policy exists anywhere in the repository, and none of the grants here substitutes for one. A compromised application credential reads every tenant's rows. |
| **DL-252** — Rename the local database role to `app_user` as part of the privilege split. | Keep the original name and treat the rename as cosmetic. | The original name is a reserved word in PostgreSQL, so the pre-fix statements at `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh:69-70` were not valid SQL as written — the provisioning path fails before any grant is evaluated. Renaming is therefore what makes the least-privilege grants executable at all, which places it inside SEC-11 rather than beside it. | Any developer with a database provisioned by the old script has a role under the old name. The script creates the roles it needs and does not migrate an existing installation. |
| **DL-253** — **SEC-12, partial.** Custody discipline lands; a managed secret store does not, and the deployment pipeline keeps its long-lived credential. | (a) Provision a managed secret store and read secrets from it at startup. (b) Migrate the pipeline to federated identity. (c) Report SEC-12 as complete on the strength of the ignore rules and the template. | The finding scopes itself to credential injection through environment variables, which is what shipped: exclusion rules, a values-free template, and startup validators that refuse a weak or missing security value. A managed store is stated design intent rather than an idea nobody had — `SRS:908-913` carries a Google Secret Manager example, importing the client at `:908` and accessing a secret version at `:911-913`. Option (b) needs provider-side identity configuration that cannot be created or verified from this repository. | Secrets still reach the process as environment variables, with no central rotation, no access audit and no automatic expiry. `.github/workflows/cd.yml:26` still authenticates with a long-lived service-account key and `:27` still exports it as default credentials. Both are named follow-ons in [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) section 4. |

---

## 33. Boundary decisions

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-254** — Bring `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh` into scope, although no finding names it. | Confine the credential work to the file the finding names. | Measurement settles it. The credential gate finds four files' worth of hits today and **three of the four are in this script** — at `:54`, `:55` and `:69` in the pre-fix tree — with the fourth at `infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml:30`, also pre-fix. The acceptance metric is repository-wide and requires zero, so excluding the script leaves that metric failing at three quarters. A tracked script that creates a database role with a literal password is the defect, whichever document lists it. | Scope reaches past the supplied file list, which this row exists to make visible rather than silent. Sections 8, 9, 12, 18 and 24 record what changed inside it. |
| **DL-255** — Add `POST /auth/logout`, against the no-new-features boundary. | (a) Clear the cookie from the client. (b) Clear it on the next authenticated request. (c) Ship no logout path and let the cookie expire. | JavaScript cannot delete a cookie it cannot read, which is the entire point of the HttpOnly attribute, so option (a) is not available — it is not a preference. Without a server route the previous logout call becomes a no-op and a user who logs out keeps a valid session, which would make the session fix a regression rather than an improvement. The regression list names frontend logout explicitly. Option (c) leaves the session alive for the whole expiry window. | One additive route. No existing path or verb changes, and the route needs no request body. DL-110 records the client change that calls it. |
| **DL-256** — Leave `infrastructure/terraform/outputs.tf` unchanged, although it blocks plan validation. | (a) Declare the missing resources. (b) Delete the outputs that reference them. (c) Declare the missing variable only. | Set-differencing the two files shows `outputs.tf` referencing **exactly seven** resources `main.tf` never declares: `google_storage_bucket.raw_data`, `.processed_data` and `.artifacts`, `google_pubsub_topic.raw_data` and `.processed_data`, and `google_cloudfunctions_function.data_processor` and `.data_analyzer`. **A second, independent blocker exists**: `main.tf:45` reads `var.gke_num_nodes`, which `variables.tf` never declares. Declaring seven cloud resources to make a validation command pass would provision infrastructure nobody asked for; deleting the outputs would turn validation green while discarding two working outputs and silently changing the module's public interface. Neither is a security change. | Anyone following the documented Terraform verification step meets a failure unrelated to this work. Two mitigations: validate the changed resource in isolation or through a targeted plan, and **neither `ci.yml` nor `cd.yml` invokes Terraform at all**, so the blocker breaks no automated gate. |
| **DL-257** — Add `ip_configuration` carrying `ssl_mode` alone, with no `authorized_networks` and no network variable. | (a) Add an authorized-network list restricting inbound addresses. (b) Add the block with an empty authorized-network list, stated explicitly. | A block containing only `ssl_mode` references no network value, and the vendor documentation confirms that an instance with no authorized network configured stays unreachable from outside — so omission is the **more restrictive** posture, not a gap. Adding a list means choosing addresses this repository has no basis to choose, and any list is broader than none. Option (b) writes down a default without changing it. | The instance's public-address exposure is unchanged, which was already the more restrictive state. DL-217 and section 25 record the provider default the block does make explicit. |
| **DL-258** — Write `ssl_mode` as a literal, and leave `require_ssl` unset. | (a) Parameterise `ssl_mode` through a variable. (b) Set `require_ssl = true` alongside it, for older provider versions. | A literal cannot be weakened by a variable default or a command-line override, which is the whole value of hardcoding the secure end of a two-value choice. `require_ssl` is not merely deprecated — it conflicts: only certain pairs of the two arguments are valid, the provider defaults it to `false`, and setting it beside a raised `ssl_mode` can fail at apply time. | Changing the transport posture now needs a code edit rather than a variable change, which is the intended asymmetry. |
| **DL-259** — Declare the database password variable with `sensitive = true` and no default. | (a) Give it a default. (b) Declare it without the sensitive marker. | Without the marker the value appears in plan output and in state diffs, so the credential leaks into wherever those are captured — which is the class of exposure this work exists to remove. A default would put a literal credential back into a tracked file, reintroducing SEC-01 in the file that is meant to fix it. This is the first use of the marker anywhere in that file. | A missing value fails at plan time, which is the intended fail-closed behaviour rather than a defect. The marker hides the value from output; it does not encrypt state. At the time of this decision Terraform state still held the value in clear, which DL-212 addressed by excluding state from version control. **Superseded in part: the shipped declaration passes the value through `password_wo` from an `ephemeral` variable, so it reaches neither state nor a plan file. DL-294 records the correction; the exclusions remain as defence in depth.** |
| **DL-260** — Leave `@paypal/react-paypal-js` and `dayjs` undeclared in `frontend/package.json`. | (a) Declare both at a chosen version. (b) Declare both and generate a lock file. | Both are imported and neither is declared: `@paypal/react-paypal-js` at `frontend/src/services/paypal.ts:1`, and `dayjs` at `frontend/src/utils/formatters.ts:1` and used at `:15`. Declaring them means choosing versions and auditing a transitive tree, which is the lock-file problem DL-15 declines, and neither package participates in any of the twelve findings. | The frontend build cannot succeed, which is already true for several reasons unrelated to security. Both are pre-existing declaration gaps and are named as follow-ons in [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md). |
| **DL-261** — Add the four gates to the existing `.github/workflows/ci.yml` rather than to a new workflow. | A dedicated security workflow, which would run clean of the pre-existing install and lint failures. | The requirements specify no file creation under `.github`, and the minimality test prefers one file touched to two files existing. Placement inside the job is forced in one respect: the dependency-audit gate has to follow the dependency-install step, because the audit tool does not exist before it. DL-138 records the ordering that shipped and DL-102 records marking the scan steps to run regardless of an earlier failure. | The gates share a job with steps that fail for pre-existing reasons, so a red workflow does not by itself mean a gate failed. DL-204 isolates the audit gate's own result so it cannot be masked. |
| **DL-262** — Leave the three sibling documents in `documentation/` byte-identical. | (a) Reconcile them with the delivered state. (b) Annotate the passages describing controls this work does not deliver. | They are design documents describing intent, and editing intent to match an implementation destroys the record of what was intended. Every gap between them and the delivered state is either on the exclusion list or is a labelled partial remediation, and section 35 records each one as a decision — which is the correct response, rather than an edit. Reconciling specifications is documentation work the Minimal Change Clause excludes. | A reader of those documents sees controls that do not exist. Section 35 is the reconciliation, and this log is linked from [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md). |

---

## 34. Weaknesses found and deliberately not fixed

Nine further weaknesses surfaced during the work. None is among the twelve findings, and the brief
directs that additional concerns be noted rather than fixed. Each gets a row so the omission reads as
a decision. [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) section 3.4 carries the same list as residual risk.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-263** — Do not change how the ingestion client transmits its API key. | Move the key to a request header. | `backend/app/services/zillow_service.py:17-21` passes the key as a URL query parameter, where it lands in access logs, proxy records and browser-style referrer chains. Moving it changes an external integration contract that cannot be verified without provider access, and no finding covers it. | The ingestion key is exposed to anything that logs a URL. Rotating it is cheap; the transport stays as it is. |
| **DL-264** — Do not remove the password-hash field from the outbound user model as a separate fix. | Leave it and say nothing. | The field was removed, because `backend/app/schema/user.py` was already being rewritten to add the create models and the change stays inside that one file. It is recorded here rather than claimed as a finding closed, since nothing currently returns that model and no live exposure existed. Leaving a model that would serialise a password hash the moment someone attached it to a response is an avoidable trap. | None remaining for this field. The general pattern — an outbound model mirroring a table — persists elsewhere and is not audited here. |
| **DL-265** — Do not restrict invocation of the deployed functions. | Require authenticated invocation in both the script and the Terraform configuration. | `scripts/deploy.sh:24` deploys with unauthenticated invocation permitted, and the Terraform function resource sets no invoker restriction. Changing it is infrastructure authorization work outside the twelve findings, and doing it correctly needs the caller identities, which this repository does not name. | Anyone who learns a function URL can invoke it. |
| **DL-266** — Do not add private networking or an authorized-network restriction to the container cluster. | Configure a private cluster with authorized control-plane access. | Network architecture, outside the twelve findings, and the change is not safely reversible once nodes exist. `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf:142-146` already carries a note listing network settings and IAM roles as outstanding configuration. | The cluster's control plane and nodes stay on their current exposure. |
| **DL-267** — Leave the pipeline actions pinned to mutable major tags. | Pin every action to an immutable commit digest. | The workflows reference five actions by major tag and one by minor tag, so an upstream force-push changes what executes. Digest pinning is supply-chain hardening, outside the twelve findings, and it requires resolving and then maintaining six digests. | A compromised upstream action runs with the workflow's permissions, including the deployment credential. |
| **DL-268** — Leave the database proxy image at its pinned version. | Raise it to a current release. | `infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml:66` pins a superseded proxy image. Base-image currency is outside the twelve findings, and the service is local-development topology rather than a deployed component. | Local developers run a superseded proxy. No deployed path uses this image. |
| **DL-269** — Leave both container base images at their pinned versions. **Superseded in part by DL-359 and DL-375: only the backend image is past end of life, and the frontend image now pins Node 22, in maintenance support until April 2027.** | Raise both to supported releases. | The runtime freeze applies: `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend:2` pins the Python version declared in four places, and raising it is the platform migration DL-14 declines. The frontend image was raised to Node 22 under DL-375, which is why the original claim that both images were equally past support no longer holds. | The backend image stops receiving security updates, and Python 3.9 is the only runtime here currently past end of life. The runtime upgrade is the highest-priority follow-on. The frontend row needs revisiting before April 2027, when Node 22 maintenance support ends. |
| **DL-270** — Leave the containers running as the root user. | Add an unprivileged user and switch to it before the entrypoint. | Neither container definition declares a user. Container hardening is outside the twelve findings, and the change needs file ownership adjusted for every path the process writes, which is a build change rather than a configuration one. | A container escape starts from root inside the container. |
| **DL-271** — Leave the interactive API documentation reachable. | Disable it, or gate it behind an environment check. | It is the framework default and may well be intentional: the documentation routes describe an API whose read path is public by design. Gating it in a deployed environment is a one-line change whenever the team decides it should be gated. DL-149 records that the route assertions exclude these four paths explicitly rather than by accident. | The schema, every route and every request shape are readable by anyone. That is disclosure of design, not of data. |


---

## 35. Design intent in the sibling specifications that this work does not deliver

The three documents in `documentation/` describe security controls the implementation does not have.
Every gap below is either on the exclusion list supplied with the findings or is a labelled partial
remediation. DL-262 records why the documents are not edited; these rows record each gap.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-272** — Deliver no multi-factor authentication, although `Technical Specifications.md:601-603` specifies time-based one-time passwords and `SRS:506` names it as an optional feature. | Implement enrolment, verification and recovery codes. | Named on the exclusion list supplied with the findings. It is also a feature build: enrolment, a secret store per user, recovery codes and a reset path, all of which need columns that DL-247 forecloses. | Single-factor authentication throughout. A stolen password is a full account compromise, bounded only by the throttle at DL-248. |
| **DL-273** — Deliver no federated authentication, although `SRS:505` specifies OAuth 2.0. | Migrate authentication to an external identity provider. | Named on the exclusion list. The local credential store and bearer scheme are retained, which is what the twelve findings are written against. | Credential storage and password policy stay this application's responsibility, which is what SEC-04, SEC-06 and SEC-07 address. |
| **DL-274** — Deliver no application-managed encryption at rest, although `Technical Specifications.md:651-653` specifies AES-256 with keys held in a managed key service. | Encrypt selected columns and manage keys through the key service. | A managed key service is a provisioning change with no resource in the current Terraform configuration, and column-level encryption needs schema changes that DL-247 forecloses. The managed database service encrypts at rest by default, so the gap is key custody rather than the absence of encryption. | Keys stay under the cloud provider's control rather than the project's. SEC-10 covers data in transit; nothing here changes data at rest. |
| **DL-275** — Deliver no role-based access control, although `Technical Specifications.md:616-618` names four roles and `SRS:393`, `SRS:509` and `Software Project Proposal.md:465` all require it. | Add a role column and authorize each route against it. | Needs a column on the user record, which DL-247 forecloses, plus an authorization decision at every route — a feature build rather than a fix. No finding covers it. SEC-11 addresses privilege at the database layer, which is a different layer and no substitute. | Every authenticated user has identical rights. The only distinction the code makes is authenticated versus not. |
| **DL-276** — Make no compliance claim, while naming the frameworks the specifications name. | (a) Claim alignment with the frameworks. (b) Omit the frameworks entirely. | `SRS:517-518` requires compliance with the GDPR and CCPA and gives users export and deletion rights. Naming a framework in a technical document is context for why credential and transport hygiene matter; it is not an assessment, and presenting it as one would be false. Omitting it loses the reason several of these findings matter for personal data — email addresses and credential hashes are personal data, so SEC-01, SEC-10 and SEC-11 all touch obligations to protect it, and SEC-09 touches the payment path. | This work performed no gap assessment and produced no evidence for an assessor. Data-subject rights, retention, deletion and breach notification are untouched, and several depend on controls deferred here. |

---

## 36. Deviations from a literal reading of the requirements

Rule 1 requires an explicit entry for any departure from a literal or obvious reading. Five are
recorded here, and each one exists because following the instruction literally would have broken
something the instruction was meant to protect.

This section is not the whole inventory. Four further departures are labelled as such where the work
that produced them is recorded: DL-42 on the manifest comments, DL-45 on provider verification inside
the payment wrapper, DL-303 on the browser type declarations, and DL-375 on the frontend Node line.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-277** — Suppress the credential gate's own pattern line from its results, rather than letting the gate report the file that defines it. | (a) Exempt the defining files by name, the earlier approach. (b) Keep the pattern in a separate file consumed by the search tool, exempting that file too. (c) Maintain an allow-list of source paths to scan. | The pattern carries several of the strings it searches for as literal alternatives. So the file defining it is always a hit and the required count of zero is unreachable, confirmed by measurement on a tree holding no credential at all. Option (a) works but stops policing two whole files, one of them the workflow itself, and option (b) needs a new file under `.github`, where the requirements specify no file creation. Option (c) is the worst of the three, because it silently stops policing any path added later and so degrades quietly. Filtering the single output line that is the invocation keeps every file in scope. DL-207 records the switch and DL-97, DL-98, DL-141 and DL-142 record the exemptions it replaced. **One mechanical detail worth knowing:** the search tool's exclude option matches a basename, so a path-shaped exclusion suppresses nothing. | A credential that happened to appear on the same output line as the invocation would be filtered. That line is the workflow's own command, which is reviewed as code. |
| **DL-278** — Write every placeholder in `.env.example` inside angle brackets rather than as a conventional replace-me word. | (a) Conventional bare-word placeholders. (b) A double-underscore marker style. (c) Empty values throughout. | Measured against the real gate pattern, the conventional forms all match, because letters and the underscore fall inside the character class the pattern accepts after an assignment. A conventional template would make the file created to close SEC-01 the reason SEC-01's own metric fails. Angle-bracket and empty values were both verified clean; angle brackets were chosen because they read unmistakably as a placeholder where an empty value reads as an oversight. | The style is unconventional and a developer may not recognise it, mitigated by a header comment in the file. **`.env.example` is deliberately not exempted from the gate**, so the discipline is enforced rather than trusted; exempting it would disable a working control. |
| **DL-279** — Restrict `.env.example` to the backend settings contract, omitting the two frontend build variables and the two variables no code reads. **Its frontend half is superseded by DL-309; the two unread variables stay omitted.** | (a) Document every variable that appears anywhere in the repository. (b) Document the backend contract plus the frontend variables. | The two frontend variables are build-time values for the client bundle, read at `frontend/src/services/api.ts:10`, `frontend/src/services/paypal.ts:3` and `frontend/src/index.tsx:8`, and they are absent from the settings class entirely. The other two are written into a generated environment file by the provisioning script at `:53` and `:56` and are read by no application code at all — the settings class declares neither. Documenting a variable the application ignores tells a developer to set something that does nothing, which is worse than silence. | A developer using the Compose path or the client build finds their variables undocumented. Both already carry them: the Compose file declares its own, and the client build reads its own. DL-24 and DL-29 record the variable set and the loading contract that shipped. |
| **DL-280** — Leave `README.md` unchanged, although its setup instructions describe a different application. | (a) Correct the environment-variable block. (b) Rewrite the document to match this application. | Lines 37 to 44 tell a developer to create an environment file holding a port, a MongoDB connection string and a signing key named `JWT_SECRET`. This application reads none of the three, and the last is the exact wrong name DL-242 corrects in the Compose file. The whole document is framed around a Node, Express and MongoDB stack. The file appears in no requirements row and produces zero hits on all three measured gates, so it is neither a finding nor a metric failure. Patching only the environment block yields a document that is half accurate and reads as authoritative; rewriting it is documentation work the Minimal Change Clause excludes. | **A developer following the README creates an environment file this application cannot use.** `.env.example` and [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) are the authoritative sources, and the application fails at startup naming the missing variable rather than starting misconfigured. |
| **DL-281** — Invert the search exit code so the three repository scans behave as gates, departing from the verification-command form the requirements give. | (a) Paste the commands verbatim into run steps. (b) Append a negated search after each command. | Pasted verbatim under the default shell with error-exit enabled, the commands behave backwards: a search that finds a credential exits zero and the step **passes**, while a clean tree exits one and the step **fails**. Worse, error-exit does not abort on a negated command, so a negated search followed by any other command exits zero despite a leak — the gate becomes inert while appearing to run. The conditional form is position-independent, fails on a match, and prints the offending lines. DL-99 and DL-140 record the form that shipped. | The command text in the workflow now differs from the documented verification command, so the two have to be kept in step. [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) section 2.6 carries the manual form and the workflow carries the gate form, with each labelled as such. |

---

## 37. The two rule conflicts and how they were resolved

Two instructions given for this work contradict each other, and one rule contradicts itself in scope.
Both are resolved here rather than silently in favour of one side.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-282** — Separate *what* from *why*: each security-critical change carries a short comment stating the threat it closes, prefixed with a stable `SEC-NN` identifier, and every scrap of rationale lives only in this log keyed by that identifier. | (a) Follow the comment instruction literally and put the reasoning at each change site. (b) Follow Rule 1 literally and write no security comments at all. (c) Duplicate the rationale in both places. | The two instructions collide on *why*, not on *what*. The brief asks that a security-critical change carry a comment explaining the threat mitigated; Rule 1 forbids rationale in code comments and names this log the single source of truth for why. A factual threat marker plus a resolvable identifier satisfies both purposes completely: a maintainer at the change site learns what is being defended, and the reasoning is in one place rather than scattered. Option (c) guarantees the two copies diverge, at which point neither is trustworthy. The user's own example of the form and tone: `// Fixes sub/User.id identity mismatch — prevents auth guard bypass` | A marker pointing at nothing defeats the whole arrangement, so completeness here is load-bearing rather than tidy. The finding index at the top of this file is what makes every marker in the repository resolve, and it is the thing to re-check whenever a marker is added. DL-161 and DL-199 record reducing over-dense markers to a factual branch or an identifier. |
| **DL-283** — Scope the prose validator to the five prose artifacts and exclude code, configuration, dependency manifests and shell commands. | (a) Apply it to everything generated, per the rule's opening sentence. (b) Apply it to nothing, on the ground that this is a technical repository. | Rule 2 opens broadly and then excludes code blocks and inline code in its special-handling clause, so it constrains its own scope; the more specific instruction controls. The five artifacts it governs are this log, the traceability matrix, `SECURITY.md`, the explanatory comments in `.env.example` and the docstrings in the eight security test modules. Applying prose principles to a pinned dependency list or a search pattern would corrupt artifacts whose correctness depends on exactness rather than readability — a rewritten pattern is a broken gate. | Identifiers, version pins, commands and quoted rule text inside this log are reproduced byte-exactly and are not smoothed for readability. That is deliberate, and it is why some cells read as lists of coordinates rather than as sentences. |
| **DL-284** — Treat the blog-content rules and their synonym dictionary as out of scope for this log. | (a) Apply them, since they appear in the same rules document. (b) Apply the punctuation rule alone, as a style improvement. | They sit beneath a heading that scopes them to blog content and state that they layer on top of the general principles for that content. Applying a blog punctuation rule to a technical document degrades exactly the clarity the binding principles demand — and the rules document itself uses the punctuation its blog rule prohibits, including inside the clause that exempts code. Option (b) is over-compliance dressed as caution. | This log uses em dashes, begins some sentences with a pronoun, and repeats domain terms rather than rotating synonyms. All three are correct for a technical document and would be violations only in blog content. |

## 38. Withdrawals that restored the file map

`backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/subscriptions.py`,
`backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, `backend/app/schema/listing.py`,
`backend/app/schema/subscription.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py`,
`backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/requirements.txt`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
`.dockerignore`, `backend/.dockerignore`, `frontend/.dockerignore`,
`backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py`,
`documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

A final review of every external boundary rated five controls this log had defended as scope breaches
rather than hardening, and at that pass the tree matched AAP 0.6.1 exactly: 16 created, 18 updated,
5 references byte-identical, no deletions, 39 paths, nothing left over. Sections 40 and 41 record what
later passes restored and added, and [`traceability-matrix.md`](traceability-matrix.md) section 3.4
carries the delivered totals. Each withdrawal is recorded below with what it gave up, because a control
removed without a reason is indistinguishable from a control lost. Every
value was measured on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure, with 514 security cases passing, the
three repository scans at zero hits and the audit gate verified in both directions.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-286** — Return `backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py` to its baseline content, so a REFERENCE file is byte-identical again. **Superseded by DL-296, which restored the repair and reinstated DL-32 through DL-35.** | (a) Keep the endpoint repair, as DL-32 chose. (b) Move the conversion into the request model, DL-32's own option (a). (c) Keep the repair and reclassify the file in the matrix. | The repaired defect is real but is not a security defect: the route hands nested request objects to a mapped relationship and supplies no non-null timestamp, which is the same schema gap the listing and subscription create paths already carry as documented residuals. Editing a frozen reference file to fix it is the scope breach the final review rates critical, and it paid for a functional repair with the one property the file map exists to guarantee. Option (b) moves the same repair one file left and still ships it. Option (c) documents a deviation instead of removing it. Nothing security-relevant is given up: the strict schema still refuses unknown keys, and the route answers the sanitized envelope the error boundary produces. | This row cost the one write path that persists, which is why DL-296 reverses it. |
| **DL-287** — Remove `.dockerignore`, `backend/.dockerignore` and `frontend/.dockerignore`. **Superseded by DL-300, which restored all three and reinstated DL-127 through DL-129.** | (a) Keep all three, as DL-127 chose. (b) Keep the root file only. (c) Keep them and add matrix rows classifying them as delivered but unplanned. | The measurement behind DL-128 stands — the unfiltered root context carried 8573 entries including the history that holds a credential literal — but it describes a weakness none of the twelve findings names, and the file map is the scope statement. The final review rates the three files a critical unplanned addition. Option (c) records a deviation rather than closing it, and the minimal-change clause does not permit new files for a weakness outside the twelve. Nothing else depends on the filter: the broad copy instructions in both container definitions were deliberately left unchanged at DL-129, so removing the files restores the baseline rather than breaking a chain. | This row reopened the one route a local secret can take that version control cannot close, which is why DL-300 reverses it. |
| **DL-288** — Restore `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py` to the three callables the enabling-repair contract prescribes. **Superseded by DL-332, which restored the verifier after the withdrawal left every charge refused.** — `create_payment`, `execute_payment` and the async `process_payment` wrapper — withdrawing the network verifier, the plan and payer binding, the one-time-consumption ledger and the timeout transport, and leave `backend/app/api/endpoints/subscriptions.py` byte-identical. **Supersedes DL-163, DL-201 and DL-202; DL-201's option (b) is the shipped choice.** | (a) Keep the verifier, as DL-163 chose and DL-201 extended. (b) Keep it and treat an absent plan as no expectation, DL-201's option (a). (c) Keep the verifier and revert only its caller. | The final review rates the verifier five critical and major findings that compound rather than overlap; the evidence below lists them. Option (a) keeps a control its own review found refuses real traffic. Option (b) authorizes an agreement with nothing bound, the weaker failure direction. Option (c) leaves the caller unable to satisfy a contract the service still demands. AAP 0.5.13 defines this file's change as three lines and AAP 0.6.1 repeats it; the withdrawal restores exactly that, and the module surface is now asserted to be those three callables and nothing else. | `process_payment` returns `False` without contacting the provider, so no subscription authorizes and the route's own schema gaps remain why it cannot persist. The direction is closed rather than open. Payer binding, plan binding and replay protection are real controls with no home in this pass; they belong to the payment-integration follow-on `SECURITY.md` names. |
| **DL-289** — Keep the provider logger capped at `WARNING` at import time, while the transport replacement that shipped beside it is withdrawn. | (a) Remove both, as the integration finding directs. (b) Keep both. (c) Cap the level inside each callable rather than at import. | Two reviews reach opposite conclusions about the same three lines, and the difference is what each covers. One rates an import-time global mutation a defect because the transport swap altered create, find, execute and token behaviour. The other rates the provider's own records a major exposure, because the client logs the request URL at `INFO` and that URL carries the client-supplied reference, with the authorization header and both bodies at `DEBUG`. Setting a level on one named third-party logger changes no call behaviour, so only the transport swap answers the first objection, and it is gone. The cap also cannot be left to the library default now that the application installs its own handler and level at import: an operator lowering the application level would otherwise re-enable provider records. Option (c) repeats the call once per invocation for no gain and leaves the window open until the first one. | A global side effect at import remains, narrowed to one logger and one level. An operator who wants provider diagnostics must raise it deliberately, which is the safe direction. Three cases assert the level and that neither the reference, the authorization header nor the response body reaches a record, with a negative control at the recording level. |
| **DL-290** — Withdraw the pagination bounds and the listing-side value-range policy; keep the wire-type guards and the charge-side amount and date-order rules. **Narrows DL-120 and answers the expansion finding.** | (a) Withdraw every addition, leaving `extra = "forbid"` alone, as the finding directs. (b) Keep all of it. (c) Withdraw the pagination bounds only. | The finding is right about the two additions that change something the requirements freeze or decide a business question. Query bounds alter the public read path's frozen `skip` and `limit` contract, and a positive-or-non-negative split across rent, fee and area encodes the business rule DL-23 already keeps out of a security pass. Both are gone, and the read path is back at its baseline defaults. The charge-side rules stay on the one model a payment path reads: an amount at or below zero is not a payable instruction, and an end date at or before its start describes no period. | The tree carries more input policy than the strictest reading of the finding allows and less than the version before it. The line is drawn at frozen contracts and business rules on one side, type confusion and self-contradictory charge instructions on the other. A negative rent stays schema-valid, which DL-23 records. |
| **DL-291** — Drop the sixteenth suppression, `PYSEC-2026-3447`, and hold the register at fifteen entries. **Extends DL-203.** | (a) Keep it, so the register covers the build-toolchain advisory. (b) Keep it and keep `--all`, restoring the wider surface it was written against. (c) Pin `pip`, `setuptools` and `wheel` in the manifest so the entry has a manifest line behind it. | The entry only ever matched under `--all`: it is an advisory against `setuptools`, which a plain freeze does not report, and DL-203 restored the plain freeze the specification states byte-exactly. It is stale twice over, because the installed toolchain has also moved past the affected range. Option (b) reopens the runner-dependence DL-203 rejected. Option (c) reaches past this finding into the dependency contract AAP 0.7.1 fixes. | An advisory in the build toolchain stays invisible to this gate, the bounded gap DL-203 records. The register is now exactly the reported set, so a newly published advisory fails the build rather than being absorbed by a suppression written for something else. |
| **DL-292** — Record the login throttle as it ships: the `@limiter.limit` decorator on `POST /auth/login` bounding one client address, and the in-process counter keyed by normalized lowercase email bounding one account. **Supersedes DL-58 and DL-81.** | (a) Leave the decorator off and enforce both windows inside the counter, as DL-58 and DL-81 chose. (b) Decorate only, dropping the account counter. (c) Keep the address window in the counter map under a second key prefix, DL-58's shape. | AAP 0.5.7 prescribes both layers in these terms, and the code carries them: the decorator refuses a burst from one address, and the counter refuses one account reached from many addresses. DL-81's objection was that the decorator counts requests rather than failures, so a sequence of successful logins from one address is bounded too. That consequence is real, deliberate and asserted rather than discovered, in the case the evidence below records. Option (a) leaves the registered limiter enforcing nothing on any route, which DL-81 accepted in its own risk column. Option (b) drops the account layer the guidance asks for. Option (c) puts two keying schemes in one map, which is what made the earlier eviction logic hard to reason about. | Re-authenticating more than the threshold from one address inside the window is refused, and behind a shared address that budget is shared. The account layer exists for the converse case, throttled attempts are logged rather than dropped, and the threshold is the specified one. Neither layer is durable or shared across replicas, the partial remediation section 32 already records that. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-288, the five compounding findings.** A self-contained wrapper became a network verifier
  on legacy endpoints; the frozen caller supplies no plan, so every active agreement is refused
  as unbound. A reference is consumed before persistence against a model carrying no plan
  column; a one-off payment is bound to no payer; and the one-use ledger is per-process,
  restart-lost, worker-local and evicts after 4,096 entries.
- **DL-290.** The wire-type guards are a different category, and dropping them was measured to
  cost real coverage. AAP 0.8.2 requires a wrongly typed field to answer 422 and never 500,
  while pydantic v1 coerces `"12"` to a number, `True` to 1.0 and an integer date to a
  timestamp. Without them four required cases pass vacuously, which the same review found when
  it removed the strict field types and had to retype its own payloads.
- **DL-292, the address window bounds successes too.** One case drives the threshold from a
  single address. It confirms the success clears the account counter and spends no less of the
  address budget, that the next request answers 429 even with valid credentials, and that the
  same credentials succeed from another address.
- **DL-291.** Left in place it matches nothing, and the staleness check fails the build when a suppression matches no live advisory — measured, the gate reported it as declared-but-not-reported and exited 1. Measured after the change: fifteen advisories reported across nine packages and fifteen suppressions, matching exactly — with the register the gate reports no known vulnerabilities and exits 0, without it the gate exits 1, and the staleness check passes.

A seam review of the backend and database boundary read the tree section 38 left behind and raised
nine findings; re-testing the earlier documentation review against that same tree found six more it
had never re-checked. Nothing in this section adds a control. Every row either removes something the
repository cannot execute or corrects a statement to the value the documented command now measures.
Values were measured on CPython 3.9.25 against the pinned closure, with the security suite passing
and the three repository scans at zero hits.

Re-measured for this section, superseding the figures DL-173 recorded when it was written: 295 rows
keyed DL-01 to DL-295, no duplicate and no gap, and every one of 598 mentions resolves to a row.
Any row count stated in a row goes stale as soon as another row is added, so a count belongs to the
measurement that produced it and carries its date.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-293** — Remove `database_roles = [var.db_app_role]` from `google_sql_user.app` and delete the `db_app_role` variable, leaving the declaration at the arguments its file contract prescribes. **Completes DL-250, which the assignment had contradicted. Amended by DL-368: `password_wo_version` makes the current block four arguments.** | (a) Keep the assignment and add a Terraform mechanism that creates the role and its grants ahead of the user. (b) Keep it and require an operator to provision the role before every apply. (c) Keep it and add a second provider, or a `local-exec` bootstrap, that connects and issues the grants. (d) Remove it. | The assignment named a role — `app_runtime` by default — that nothing here creates and nothing grants anything to. A default apply therefore cannot produce a usable application account: the role is either absent or, if an operator made one by hand, unprivileged. Option (a) does not exist in this provider, which exposes no resource for a `GRANT`, a `REVOKE` or a `CREATE ROLE` — the limitation DL-250 recorded. Option (b) makes a manual step a precondition of every apply, and the assignment carried no record at all — the evidence below measures how completely. Option (c) needs network reachability from the apply host plus infrastructure that file may not add. Removal returns the declaration to three arguments and makes DL-250's residual true in code as well as prose. The variable went with it: a variable the configuration never reads is a knob that changes nothing. | The cloud half of SEC-11 is account separation with no restriction. A default apply creates a usable account holding the `cloudsqlsuperuser` grant Cloud SQL gives every built-in user, and it keeps role-creation, database-creation and DDL rights until an operator narrows it. DL-324 records the narrowing statements behind that residual. |
| **DL-324** — Publish the operator statements that narrow the cloud application account in `SECURITY.md` section 3.2, and assert in `backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py` that the document still carries every one, that the removed assignment and variable stay absent, and that each remaining variable is read by the declaration. Narrowed out of DL-293 so the removal and its compensating record are each one decision. | (a) Record the residual in this log alone, which is where DL-250 left it. (b) Publish the statements and leave them unasserted. (c) Publish nothing and treat the operator step as folklore. | A residual that names no remedy leaves an operator with a privileged account and no instructions, which is how DL-293's option (b) failed. Option (a) reaches only a reader of this log, not the operator running the apply. Option (b) lets the statements drift out of the document unnoticed, which is the failure DL-205 guards against for the advisory register. Option (c) is the state the review found. The statement set mirrors the local one the provisioning script issues and verifies. | Unlike the local set, these statements were not executed against a Cloud SQL instance, because none is provisioned here; they are reviewed text, not measured behaviour. |
| **DL-294** — Correct the advisory register in this log and in `SECURITY.md` to the fifteen entries the shipped gate carries. | (a) Reduce the gate to ten entries so the documents become true, restoring DL-93 and DL-135. (b) Leave the register text and correct only `SECURITY.md`. | The reported set equals the declared set exactly, so the shipped position is fifteen, which DL-291 holds. The five omitted entries were accepted risks with no disclosure in the operational document, which is the failure mode the register exists to prevent. Option (a) inverts the dependency: DL-291 shows the ten-entry set makes the staleness check fail the build, because a suppression matching no live advisory is reported as declared-but-not-reported, so ten is not a state this gate can hold. Option (b) leaves this log contradicting itself and the gate. | The register is now the reported set exactly, so a newly published advisory fails the build rather than being absorbed by an entry written for something else — and the count will grow until the runtime upgrade DL-14 names. Five of the fifteen sit outside the served application: four reach the closure through audit and test tooling, and `PYSEC-2026-2270` reaches it through the direct `python-dotenv` pin DL-01 declares, which `pydantic` reads to load `env_file`. A reader comparing this register against the application's own dependency surface therefore finds more entries than that surface explains, and section 3.1 separates the two groups for exactly that reason. |
| **DL-326** — Retarget the six suppression pointers DL-173 undertook to retarget and left resolving to `DL-70`. Narrowed out of DL-294 so the count correction and the pointer correction are each one decision. | (a) Correct the counts without retargeting, treating a resolvable-but-wrong key as cosmetic. (b) Delete the six pointers instead of retargeting them. | `DL-70` resolves to the `collect_ignore` decision, so each pointer is live and points at the wrong meaning — the precise defect DL-173 diagnosed, undertook to fix in six places, and did not. A key that resolves to the wrong row is worse than a missing one, because nothing flags it to the reader who follows it. Option (b) leaves six suppressions with no justification behind them, which is what section 2.2 exists to carry. | Pointer correctness is not machine-checked; the staleness check DL-205 reinstated asserts the identifier set, not the keys beside it. |
| **DL-327** — Refresh every stale source anchor in this log and in the matrix. Narrowed out of DL-294 so the anchor sweep is one decision rather than a clause. | (a) Leave the anchors as written. (b) Drop line numbers and cite files only, so nothing can go stale. | An anchor citing a line that has since moved sends a reader to the wrong text and reads as a measurement of it, which is the more misleading of the two failures. Each refreshed anchor carries the token it names, so a later drift is recoverable by search rather than by guesswork. Option (b) removes the precision a reviewer uses to check a claim against the file. | Anchors drift again whenever a cited file changes above the cited line; the token beside each anchor is the remedy, not a guarantee. |
| **DL-295** — State the `POST /filters/` write defect alongside the other two non-persisting writes, and drop the claim that Terraform state carries the database password. **Its filter half is superseded by DL-296 and its migration-tooling claim is retracted by DL-299; the Terraform half stands.** | (a) Keep the residual list at two endpoints, on the grounds that the filter route validates correctly and only fails at the flush. (b) Repair the filter write so the count is genuinely two. (c) Keep the state-carries-the-password wording, since the exclusion it justifies is still correct. | Three write routes cannot persist a row, not two. Option (a) reports a residual register that a reader can falsify with one request, which costs more credibility than the omission saves. Option (b) needs a column and a relationship change, so it needs migration tooling this repository does not have, and it is feature work outside the twelve findings. On the second half: the exclusions DL-212 added are still right, but the reason given for them stopped being true when the declaration moved to `password_wo` with an `ephemeral` variable, so the value now reaches neither state nor a plan file. Justifying a correct control with a false fact invites its removal the moment someone checks the fact. | All three routes stay broken, and this work does not repair them. The Terraform exclusions now rest on the variable file being the conventional home for an operator-supplied value and on state recording other resource attributes, which is a weaker justification than a cleartext credential and a correct one. |

**Measured evidence.** Each entry belongs to the decision it names.

- **DL-293.** Outside the two Terraform files, no file in this repository mentioned `database_roles`, `db_app_role` or `app_runtime`, so a control was asserted in code with nothing in this log, in `SECURITY.md` or in the matrix behind it.
- **DL-294.** Measured against the shipped workflow on 2026-08-02: bare, the gate reports `Found
  15 known vulnerabilities in 9 packages` and exits 1; with the register it reports `No known
  vulnerabilities found, 15 ignored` and exits 0. Against that measurement this log stated the
  opposite in seven places.
  - The shared review trigger said "all ten suppressed entries", the register header said rows
    11 to 15 were unsuppressed and instructed the reader to treat any row past ten as not an
    exception, and five justification cells said "Not suppressed after DL-70". In `SECURITY.md`,
    section 2.4 reproduced a ten-entry command, section 3.1 listed ten of the fifteen, and
    section 4 named ten.
  - Anchors were re-verified at that pass by reading each cited line rather than by arithmetic.
    That pass retargeted `security.py:11` to `:13`, `:27` to `:42` and `:42` to `:69`, restated
    the pre-fix `:35` and `:48` as pre-fix, and moved `listings.py:23` to `:26`.
  - It also moved the two `Filter.from_orm` call sites to `filters.py:28` and `:36`,
    `docker-compose.yml:40` to `:44` twice, `main.tf:28` to `:34`, `main.tf:111` to `:132-138`,
    `database.py:5` to the conditional engine at `:9-17`, and `main.py:29`, which no longer
    exists, restated as the single definition at `database.py:20`.
  - **Those targets are a record of that pass and several have since moved; DL-389 carries the
    current sweep, and the anchors in this log are the ones it left.**
- **DL-295.** In the tree this row was written against, `filters.py:20` handed the mapped
  `criteria` relationship a list of `FilterCreate` models where `Criteria` rows are expected,
  and supplies no value for the non-null `created_at` column, so the flush fails after

## 40. Corrections after the frontend and client-call contract review

`backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py`,
`SECURITY.md`, `documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`,
