A client-call contract review read the tree section 39 left behind and raised ten findings: one
critical, six major and three minor. This section records every decision taken in answer to them.
Rows are keyed from `DL-296` and each carries one decision; measurements sit in the evidence
subsections rather than inside a cell.

### 40.1 The filter create path

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-296** — Restore the mapped `Criteria` children and the server-owned `created_at` in `backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py`. **Supersedes DL-286 and reinstates DL-32 through DL-35.** | (a) Hold the withdrawal, as DL-286 chose. (b) Move the conversion into the request model. (c) Give `filters.created_at` a column default. | DL-286 withdrew a working repair to keep a file map classification exact, and it cost the one write path in this repository that persists. The route is on the frozen regression list, so a scope label is the weaker claim. Option (b) imports mapped models into the schema layer and still leaves the timestamp unset. Option (c) edits the model file this work freezes, and a new default never reaches an existing table without migration tooling. | The file map lists this path REFERENCE while the tree carries an UPDATE. DL-297 reconciles that rather than leaving a reader to find it in a diff. |
| **DL-297** — Record `filters.py` in the matrix as a delivered UPDATE carrying a SEC-05 control, not as a byte-identical REFERENCE. | (a) Leave the matrix describing a withdrawal. (b) Add a footnote to the REFERENCE row. | The matrix asserts coverage in both directions, so a row describing a withdrawal that no longer holds makes the coverage claim unverifiable. A footnote leaves the mode column wrong, which is the column a reader compares against the tree. | The delivered set is now one path wider than the planned set. Section 3.4 of the matrix is where that difference is stated. |
| **DL-298** — Assert the successful write: the stored row, its criteria children, its ownership, the cross-account isolation of the read path, and the refusal of a body naming an owner. **Replaces the case that asserted a sanitized 500.** | (a) Keep the sanitized-fault case and add success cases beside it. (b) Assert only the reply body. | A suite that expects a 500 certifies the regression, which is how the defect survived a passing run. Option (a) keeps two mutually exclusive expectations of one request. Option (b) cannot tell a persisted write from a body the handler assembled and dropped, so the stored row is read directly. | The cases now depend on the mapped `Criteria` relationship, so a model change that renames a child column fails them. That is the intent. |
| **DL-299** — Retract DL-295's statement that repairing this route needs migration tooling. | Leave the claim and note the repair separately. | The claim is false as written and it was the stated reason for not repairing the route. `Criteria` and the `Filter.criteria` relationship already exist, so the repair adds and retypes no column. A false justification invites the same withdrawal again. | The listing and subscription write paths do still need columns, so the two-endpoint residual in `SECURITY.md` stands on its own evidence. |

**Measured evidence for 40.1.** Against the restored endpoint on the SQLite harness and against
the local PostgreSQL 13 instance. A valid `POST /filters/` returns 200 with the declared
seven-key body, writes one `filters` row and one `criteria` row, sets `user_id` to the
authenticated account, and an immediate `GET /filters/` returns that row to its author and an
empty list to a second account. A body carrying `user_id` is refused with 422. The security
suite reports 516 passing cases, two more than the 514 recorded in section 39, which is the
three new cases less the one withdrawn.

### 40.2 Build-context secret custody

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-300** — Restore `.dockerignore`, `backend/.dockerignore` and `frontend/.dockerignore`. **Supersedes DL-287 and reinstates DL-127 through DL-129.** | (a) Hold the removal, as DL-287 chose. (b) Restore the root file only. (c) Narrow both `COPY . .` instructions to an allow-list instead. | DL-287 traded a working control for an exact path count. The gap it left is the one custody gap `.gitignore` cannot cover: an untracked `.env` is invisible to version control and fully visible to a build context. Option (b) leaves both declared contexts exposed, because the builder resolves the ignore file at the context root. Option (c) is the stronger form but edits two files no finding names and cannot be verified without building both images. | The broad copy instructions stay, so the control is an exclusion list rather than an allow-list. Named as a follow-on in `SECURITY.md` section 4. |
| **DL-301** — Assert the exclusions per context against a path for each class of excluded material, and against the paths each build needs. | (a) Assert only that the three files exist. (b) Run `docker build` inside the test. | A file that exists proves nothing; a pattern can be present and still match nothing. Option (b) makes the suite depend on a container runtime the pipeline may not offer, and on a network pull. Evaluating the declared patterns under Docker's documented matching gives the same verdict with no runtime. | The matcher implements only the constructs these three files use. A pattern form outside that set would need it extended, which the assertion on the pattern list makes visible. |
| **DL-302** — Add `frontend/coverage/` to the root ignore file, which the earlier set omitted. | Leave coverage output to the two context-local files. | The root context is the one a `docker build -f infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.frontend .` invocation uses, and it excluded `frontend/build/` while admitting `frontend/coverage/`. Coverage output is not a secret, but it is bulk that has no business in a layer, and the asymmetry reads as an oversight. | None. |

**Measured evidence for 40.2.** Verified against Docker Engine 28 rather than by reading the
patterns. A synthetic backend context holding fifteen planted paths — the environment file and
its temporary form, a credential JSON in `secrets/`, a certificate, a key, a root-level
credential JSON, two virtual environments, bytecode, a cache directory, three coverage artifacts
and version-control metadata. Built to an image carrying only `requirements.txt`, `app/main.py`,
`tests/conftest.py` and the two build files.

The same exercise on a synthetic root context holding twenty planted paths left only
`.env.example`, `backend/requirements.txt`, `frontend/package.json` and the two build files. The
in-suite matcher returns the same verdict for every one of those paths.

### 40.3 The client/server wire contract

`frontend/src/schema/listing.ts`, `frontend/src/schema/filter.ts`,
`frontend/src/services/api.ts`

The two browser declarations this section governs are `frontend/src/schema/listing.ts` and
`frontend/src/schema/filter.ts`. Both are named here so each row below resolves to a file without
inference. DL-387 records the naming decision.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-303** — Align the two browser declarations with the bodies the server sends: integer identifiers, snake_case names and ISO-8601 date-time strings. | (a) Revert the response models to string identifiers so the old declarations become true. (b) Keep the camelCase declarations and add a parallel wire type with mappers. (c) Leave the drift and record it as a residual. | The mapped columns are integers, so the response models are right and the declarations were wrong. Option (a) would retype a body to match a stale claim. Option (b) is the reviewer's other offer, and it was measured to cost nothing in consumer diagnostics either: every consumer resolves these types through an import path the project has never resolved, so all of them already see `any`. It ships two shapes for one body, though, and leaves a reader to work out which is authoritative. Option (c) leaves an unchecked cast asserting a shape the server does not send. | Consumers still read camelCase and phantom fields. Those reads compile today only because their import paths do not resolve, so aligning the declarations does not add a diagnostic and does not repair them either. AAP 0.9.2 places the import mapping and those components outside this work. |
| **DL-304** — Export every interface in both files. | Add a bare `export {}` to make each a module. | Five files already import these names as named exports, so the export is the shape they assume. A bare marker would satisfy the module rule and leave those imports unresolved. | None. Two diagnostics go, three more go with the client's import paths. |
| **DL-305** — Declare the response type on each axios call instead of casting the response body. | (a) Keep the casts. (b) Validate the body at runtime with a schema library. | A cast makes a claim the compiler cannot check, which is exactly how the identifier drift survived. A declared generic makes the same claim checkable at every use site. Option (b) adds a dependency and a validation layer no finding asks for. | A declared generic is still a claim about a body no client-side test observes, which is why the contract cases in the input-validation suite assert the two sides against each other. |
| **DL-306** — Give `createFilter` a `FilterCreate` parameter and send that allow-list only. | (a) Keep sending the read-model object. (b) Strip the server-owned keys inside the caller. | The route declares `FilterCreate`, and `extra = "forbid"` refuses anything else, so sending a read-model object earns a 422. Option (b) puts the contract in the component rather than in the client that owns the request. | The one caller passes a value typed through an unresolved import, so this correction is not enforced at that call site until the import mapping is repaired. |
| **DL-307** — Address both endpoints at their canonical trailing-slash paths. | Keep the redirect-triggering spellings. | `/listings` and `/filters` both answer 307 to the trailing-slash form, so every call cost a redirect and a credentialed cross-origin POST depended on the redirect carrying the allow-origin header. The frozen path is the trailing-slash form. | None. Both spellings still reach the same route. |
| **DL-308** — Assert the two sides against each other in the backend suite: field-name parity, identifier types, and the absence of a response cast. | (a) Assert only inside the backend. (b) Add a frontend contract test. | Neither side alone can catch a rename on the other. Option (b) needs a frontend runner and a build the project does not have, which AAP 0.9.2 excludes. Reading the declaration files from the backend suite is the only gate available that runs today. | The cases read declaration text, so they cannot see a consumer that ignores the declaration. Verified mutation-sensitive in both directions: renaming one field or retyping one identifier fails them. |

**Measured evidence for 40.3.** A targeted TypeScript 4.9.5 compile under `--strict` over the ten
changed and consuming files reported 18 diagnostics before and 13 after: five removed — two global-script
errors and the client's three unresolved schema imports — and **none added**. The project-wide compile
is unchanged at the 18 pre-existing parse errors in `frontend/src/services/paypal.ts` that AAP 0.9.2
places out of scope.

### 40.4 The frontend build-variable contract

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-309** — Document `REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL` and `REACT_APP_PAYPAL_CLIENT_ID` in `.env.example`, in a final section separate from the backend settings. **Supersedes the frontend half of DL-279.** | (a) Hold the omission, as DL-279 chose. (b) Document them in a second template file under `frontend/`. (c) Document them in `SECURITY.md` prose only. | DL-279 reasoned that these are build-time client values rather than backend settings, which is true and is not a reason to leave them undocumented. The same file states that it is authoritative for every variable an operator must supply, and both are required for the bundle to reach the API or render the payment button. Option (b) creates a second file to protect and rotate for values that carry no secret. Option (c) leaves the authority claim false. | The template now mixes two contracts, which is why the heading names the difference and a case asserts that neither section absorbs the other. |
| **DL-310** — Supply both names as build arguments in `Dockerfile.frontend`, forwarded by the Compose front-end service, and retire the runtime `REACT_APP_API_URL` entry. | (a) Keep the runtime environment entry. (b) Add a runtime entry under the correct names. (c) Serve the values from a runtime-fetched configuration document. | create-react-app substitutes these while the bundle compiles, so a runtime value is inert. The retired entry also used a third name no source reads, so it was inert twice over and read as though it worked. Option (b) repeats that mistake under a better name. Option (c) is a client architecture change no finding asks for. | An argument value is visible in image history, so neither name may ever carry a secret. The section header in the template and the comment at the build stage both say so, and the PayPal client secret stays a backend setting. |
| **DL-311** — Scope the template's backend parity check to the backend section and add a second check for the build section. | (a) Exempt the two names from the existing check. (b) Compare the whole file against the union of both contracts. | The existing check compares the template against `Settings` in both directions, and a build variable is not a setting, so an unscoped file would fail it. Option (a) makes the two names undetectable in either direction. Option (b) conflates the contracts and would let a backend setting be documented in the build section. | The split depends on the heading text, which the assertion reads, so renaming the heading fails the case rather than silently widening it. |

**Measured evidence for 40.4.** The two names were taken from the sources rather than from a list:
a search for `process.env.REACT_APP_*` across `frontend/src` returns exactly
`REACT_APP_API_BASE_URL` and `REACT_APP_PAYPAL_CLIENT_ID`, and the assertions compare the template
against that search in both directions. `docker compose config` resolves both build arguments onto
the front-end service from the process environment, and the front-end service now declares no
runtime environment block at all.

### 40.5 Rule 1 — rationale removed from production, configuration, infrastructure and workflow code

Rule 1 makes this log the single source of truth for *why*, and AAP 0.11.3 fixes the code-comment
shape as a terse factual threat marker carrying a `SEC-NN` identifier. Twelve files still carried
reasoning in comments. Every explanation removed from them is recorded below or already sat in an
earlier section, so the removal is non-lossy.

**This section closed the residual as it stood at that pass, and section 41 reopened it.** The pass
that answered the security assessment rewrote comments in ten of these files and reintroduced
reasoning into them. Section 42 records the second removal and carries the remeasurement; the
closure claim below belongs to the tree this section left, not to the shipped one.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-312** — Reduce every comment in the twelve files to a factual statement of what it defends against, keeping the `SEC-NN` marker and the CWE reference. | (a) Delete the comments outright. (b) Keep them and argue AAP 0.12.2 requires the explanation. (c) Replace each with a pointer to a log section. | AAP 0.11.3 already resolved this conflict: the comment says *what*, the log says *why*, and the identifier joins them. Option (a) loses the marker AAP 0.12.2 requires. Option (b) is the violation. Option (c) fills the code with addresses that go stale when a section is renumbered, which is the churn the flat key sequence exists to avoid. | A maintainer at a change site now sees the threat and not the trade-off, and has to come here for the reasoning. That is the arrangement both instructions ask for. |
| **DL-313** — Keep the CWE reference inside the marker rather than moving it here with the reasoning. | Strip the CWE and leave the finding identifier alone. | A CWE identifier is a classification of the threat, not a justification of a choice, so it belongs to the *what*. It also survives renumbering, which a section pointer does not. | A reader who does not recognise a CWE has to look it up. |
| **DL-314** — State the removed reasoning in this section by subject rather than transcribing each comment. | Transcribe every removed comment verbatim into a row. | A verbatim transcription would restate reasoning that sections 5, 7, 11, 13, 14, 18, 21, 24, 25, 27 and 28 already carry, which is duplication in the other direction. The subjects below are the ones with no earlier row. | Recovering the exact prior comment text needs the version history rather than this log. |

**Reasoning removed from code, recorded here.**

- **The subject-claim ceiling** (`core/security.py`). The bound on an accepted `sub` claim is the range the `INTEGER` primary key holds, which PostgreSQL emits as `SERIAL`. A wider ceiling would let a value no key can hold reach the comparison, where the driver refuses it as a server fault instead of the guard refusing it as a 401. Narrower would refuse a legitimate key. Governed by DL-397.
- **Why `UserLogin` carries no password policy** (`schema/user.py`). Applying the policy there would answer a non-compliant stored credential with a distinguishable 422 instead of the counted uniform 401, which is an account-state oracle. Every credential reaches authentication and every refusal is the same 401. Governed by DL-227.
- **Why the listing write path is left as it is** (`api/endpoints/listings.py`). The route clears the request boundary and then fails on a model gap: `Listing` declares no owner column and two non-null timestamps the body never carries. Repairing it needs columns, so it stays a disclosed residual in `SECURITY.md` section 3.3. Governed by DL-247.
- **What the provider log cap withholds** (`services/paypal_service.py`). The provider client records the request URL at INFO, and that URL carries the client-supplied reference; at DEBUG it records the authorization header and both bodies. The cap is what keeps all of that out of a record. Governed by DL-333.
- **Why one record occupies one line** (`main.py`). A traceback split across lines loses the correlation identifier a line-oriented collector files it under, so a multi-line record is unattributable. Governed by DL-364.
- **Why this package configures its own logger** (`main.py`). The server configures its own loggers and leaves this package on `logging.lastResort`, which emits the message alone: no level, no timestamp, no logger name. Governed by DL-364.
- **Why the request path and method are substituted** (`main.py`). Both are client-supplied text, and a link sent to a victim can put a token or an address in the request line. A record names the matched route template and a served verb instead. Governed by DL-396.
- **Why an undeclared key is named by position** (`main.py`). The validation library reports an undeclared key by name, and that name is text the caller chose, so quoting it writes caller-controlled text into the log. Governed by DL-396.
- **Why secret literals are replaced longest first** (`main.py`). A shorter secret nested inside a longer one would otherwise leave a remainder of the longer one in the record. Governed by DL-396.
- **Why every chain member has its bound parameters suppressed** (`main.py`). A driver failure caught and re-raised as an ordinary error is still rendered through its context, so suppressing only the raised exception leaves the submitted row values in the report. Governed by DL-396.
- **Why the eviction rule never drops a key at the limit** (`api/endpoints/auth.py`). Evicting a key at the limit to make room would drop a live lockout, so a full map denies the attempt instead. Ordering by expiry evicts the entry closest to expiring. Governed by DL-365.
- **Why the account counter is keyed** (`api/endpoints/auth.py`). An unkeyed digest of an email address is recoverable from a candidate dictionary, so the audit marker is keyed per process. Governed by DL-365.
- **Why the account counter runs before the credentials are read** (`api/endpoints/auth.py`). Counting first is what bounds guessing against one account spread across many client addresses, which the address-keyed limiter cannot see. Governed by DL-398.
- **Why the run-time file guards exist** (`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`). A move over a `.env` path that is not a regular file would absorb the temporary file while the run still reported success, and a symlink there would be written through. `link(2)` refuses every existing destination instead, leaves whatever stands there unchanged and aborts the run, and the temporary file is owner-only before publication so no byte is ever world-readable. Governed by DL-352 and DL-370, which replaced the rename this bullet previously described.
- **Why the credential alphabet is constrained** (`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`). Both role passwords reach `psql` as quoted SQL literals, so the generator's character set is the guard that keeps a quote out of one. Governed by DL-39.
- **Why `create_tables` is safe for the application role** (`main.py`). Against a database provisioned by the setup script every table already exists and is owned by the owner role, so the runtime role is never asked to issue DDL; an unprovisioned database fails closed with `InsufficientPrivilege`. Governed by DL-396.
- **Why the credential scan pattern is bracket-split** (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`). Several alternatives would otherwise match the line that declares them, which previously forced two files to be excluded from the scan and made the gate blind to anything committed into either. Governed by DL-207.
- **Why the plain freeze is used** (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`). The audited set is the manifest closure, so the verdict cannot drift with the runner image's own tooling. Governed by DL-203.
- **Why the Node line is pinned where it is** (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.frontend`). A platform runtime floor interdicts the 20.x releases below 20.20.2 and the 22.x releases below 22.12.0, so the documented `14` is not installable; 22 is the lowest permitted line that still builds the pinned toolchain. Governed by DL-373 and DL-375.
- **Why the Terraform version floor is where it is** (`infrastructure/terraform/main.tf`). The write-only password argument and the ephemeral variable that feeds it both require Terraform 1.11 or newer. Governed by DL-368.
- **Why the Cloud application account is restricted out of band** (`infrastructure/terraform/main.tf`). Cloud SQL grants an elevated role to every built-in user it creates, and the provider carries no grant or revoke resource, so the narrowing statements live in `SECURITY.md` section 3.2. Governed by DL-368.

**Measured evidence for 40.5.** Every change was proven comment-only rather than reviewed by eye. For
the eight Python files the parsed syntax tree is identical to the pre-change revision. For the
workflow, the Terraform configuration, the provisioning script and the frontend authentication module,
the files compare identical once comment and blank lines are removed. At that pass the security suite reported the
same 543 passing cases, and `flake8` the same 110 findings with the same per-file distribution.

Section 41.11 carries the figures for that pass and section 42.6 the figures for the shipped tree,
whose residual is the one that stands.

### 40.6 Rule 1 — rationale removed from the test harness and the security suite

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-315** — Apply the same comment discipline to the harness and the eight security modules: markers state what is asserted or defended, and reasoning comes here. | (a) Exempt tests on the ground that a test is documentation. (b) Delete the docstrings. | Rule 1 scopes the prohibition to code, and a test module is code. Option (a) leaves the largest concentration of duplicated reasoning in the repository, which is where drift is hardest to notice. Option (b) removes the statement of what each case asserts, which is the part a reviewer needs at the case. | A reader of a case now sees what it asserts and not why the approach was chosen, and comes here for the second. |
| **DL-316** — Keep every docstring that states what a case asserts and why the property matters to security, and remove only the sentences that justify a design choice against an option not taken. | (a) Reduce every docstring to one line. (b) Keep them all unchanged. | A one-liner cannot say what a multi-part case pins, and a previous reviewer's one-line prescription was already declined at DL-196 for deleting honest disclosure. The violation is a *decision* recorded in code, not a description of an assertion. | The boundary is a judgment call, and a sentence stating a threat can read like a justification. The sweep below is what makes the result checkable. |
| **DL-317** — Prove the edits changed no assertion mechanically rather than by review. | Review the diff by eye. | Nine files and roughly 550 collected cases is more than a diff review reliably covers, and a silently weakened assertion is the worst outcome available here. | The mechanical check covers assertions and structure, not intent; a docstring can still misdescribe the case beneath it. |

**Reasoning removed from the test suite, recorded here.**

- **Why the harness installs canonical module aliases.** Both import roots sit on the path, so `app.core.config` and `backend.app.core.config` name one source file. Left alone, importing both executes it twice and yields two settings objects, two login-failure counters and two `get_db` functions, so a case would assert against state no request reaches. Importing the entry point first and binding each loaded module under its short name makes the second import a lookup. Governed by DL-376.
- **Why harness settings are assigned unconditionally.** `os.environ.setdefault` would let an ambient value from a developer machine or a pipeline secret decide what the suite tests, so the same suite would assert against different configuration in the two places. Governed by DL-72.
- **Why resolved source paths are cached.** Resolving every loaded module's `__file__` once per test costs tens of thousands of stat calls across a session, and `sys.modules` keeps the same string, so the string is the cache key. Governed by DL-377.
- **Why the SQLite pragma is set per connection.** SQLite ignores the four declared foreign keys
  unless the pragma is set on each connection. So without it a child row referencing no parent
  inserts cleanly and the harness is weaker than the PostgreSQL engine it stands in for. Governed by DL-399.
- **Why the Cloud SQL declaration is read as text rather than validated.** The Terraform directory carries pre-existing references to resources it never declares, so the whole-directory command fails for reasons unrelated to transport. Reading the declaration is what leaves the server end of SEC-10 covered. Governed by DL-400.
- **Why the frozen setting names are transcribed rather than read from the model.** Reading them from `Settings` would make the assertion agree with a rename, which is the change it exists to catch. Governed by DL-401.
- **Why every suppression identifier is shape-checked.** The audit tool accepts an identifier it does not recognise and silently suppresses nothing, so a typo becomes a hole rather than an error. Governed by DL-402.
- **Why the credential controls are assembled from fragments.** The repository credential scan reads every tracked file, this module included, so a literal control value would make the module its own hit. Governed by DL-382.
- **Why the provisioning cases stub two helpers.** Both reach a live cluster and an importable repository root, which a unit run does not provide; the `psql` batch between them then executes exactly as shipped, and the helpers' own bodies are covered by the statement cases. Governed by DL-383.
- **Why the payment call is replaced in the request-validation module.** The real callable reaches the provider from a worker thread, so a body that slipped past the request boundary would make the suite perform provider I/O and depend on a network answer. Governed by DL-383.
- **Why the sanitized layer's nesting is asserted.** Registered outside the cross-origin middleware it would leave a browser reading an opaque network failure instead of the envelope and the correlation identifier inside it. Governed by DL-176.
- **Why the error record is read at its handler.** `caplog` attaches to the root logger and proves only that a message was built, not that it was written where the application writes it. Governed by DL-379.
- **Why the throttle bound cases call the reservation function directly.** The bound is reached below the HTTP layer, so no sequence of requests can exercise it. Governed by DL-82.
- **Why the password maximum is fixed at its number.** The hashing scheme truncates beyond that many bytes, which is what fixes the maximum rather than leaving it a choice. Governed by DL-226.

**Measured evidence for 40.6.** The collected node identifiers are identical before and after, at 549
cases across the nine files, so nothing was added, removed or renamed. For the seven files this pass
touched alone, the parsed syntax tree with every docstring removed is identical to the pre-change
revision, so no assertion, fixture, parameter set or constant value moved. The two modules that also
gained cases earlier in this pass were diffed line by line, and every changed line outside those
additions is a comment or a docstring.

At that pass the suite reported the same 543 passing cases, and `flake8` zero findings across
`conftest.py` and all eight security modules. **The docstring residual this subsection reported
closed was reopened by the pass recorded in section 41 and closed again by section 42, which carries
the current measurement.**

### 40.7 Documents reconciled with the shipped code

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-318** — Record that `login()` reads `access_token` and `token_type` and returns an exported `AuthSession`. **Supersedes DL-133.** | (a) Leave DL-133 standing. (b) Change the module back to the shape DL-133 describes. | DL-133 described a narrowing to `{ user }` and a `Promise<User>` return, and the shipped module does neither: the login body carries no user object, so returning one meant resolving to `undefined` against a declared type. Reading the two keys the frozen body does carry is the correct shape. Option (a) leaves the log contradicting the code it governs, which is the one failure a decision log cannot survive. Option (b) restores a function that resolves to `undefined`. | The store thunk still expects a `User` from this call and passes one argument where two are declared. Both are pre-existing caller defects AAP 0.9.2 places outside this work. |
| **DL-319** — Remove the advisory identifier and its reasoning from the frontend HTTP client's marker, leaving the control and the constraint. **Restores the position DL-197 recorded.** | (a) Leave the identifier in place. (b) Delete the marker entirely. | DL-197 recorded that the identifier and all reasoning had been removed, and a later edit put both back, so the log and the code disagreed. The marker now states that the client sends the session cookie and that no cross-site-token option is set — the second being a constraint a maintainer must not violate. Option (a) is the Rule 1 violation. Option (b) drops the constraint, which is the part of the comment that prevents a regression. | A maintainer who wants the advisory identifier has to come here for it. The SEC-06 rows in section 31 and the axios pin carry it. |
| **DL-320** — Map the authentication response contract in the traceability matrix, on both the frontend and the backend side. | (a) Leave it to the SEC-06 rows. (b) Note it in `SECURITY.md` only. | The matrix asserts bidirectional coverage, and a response type that changed shape on the client is exactly the kind of non-trivial change a coverage claim has to name. Option (a) leaves a reader to infer it from a control description. Option (b) puts a traceability fact in an operator document. | The matrix now names a type the frontend build cannot type-check, so the mapping is a documented contract rather than a compiled one. |

### 40.8 Rule 2 — the prose density split and the scorecard on record

`documentation/security/decision-log.md`

The review counted thirty-six rows over 1,800 characters, a figure this pass reproduced exactly, cells that combined independent decisions
with the measurements behind them, and no scorecard anywhere on record. The rows below record how
that was closed. The scorecard beneath them is the re-run the finding asks for, and it reports a
residual rather than a clean sheet.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-328** — Split every row over 1,800 characters into one decision per row, and move the measurements and inventories those rows carried into the per-section evidence blocks. | (a) Leave the rows and argue that a four-column register cell is a record rather than prose. (b) Shorten the rows by deleting content. (c) Move the dense rows out of the table into prose beneath it. | A cell holding a decision, its four rejected options, three measurements and a residual makes a reader hold all of it at once to find the one part that answers their question. Option (a) is available only if the register is exempt from the prose rule, and the rule exempts code, not tables. Option (b) trades a density defect for a completeness defect, which Rule 1 treats as the more serious of the two. Option (c) leaves the table incomplete and splits one decision across two places. | Split rows carry a pointer to each other rather than repeating a shared premise, so a reader who lands on the narrowed row has one hop to make. Every split keeps the original key on the primary row, so no inbound pointer from `SECURITY.md`, the matrix or a code marker breaks. |
| **DL-329** — Take DL-285's option (b) and rewrite the worst offenders inside the inherited cells of sections 1 through 25. **Supersedes DL-285 and amends DL-218.** | (a) Hold DL-285 and split only the rows written after it. (b) Copy each dense inherited row into a new section and leave the original standing. | The review names those inherited rows specifically, so the exemption DL-285 granted is what the finding is against. Option (a) leaves most of the density in place and closes nothing. Option (b) doubles the file and leaves two versions of one decision, which is the ambiguity DL-218 rejected for a different reason. Each rewrite re-punctuates and relocates; none removes an alternative, a measurement or a residual. | Editing first-hand cells risks changing a fact while shortening a sentence, which is the danger DL-285 named. Every edit was applied by exact string replacement asserted to match once, and the row and sentence measurements before and after are in the evidence below. |
| **DL-330** — Define a worst offender as a row over 1,800 characters or a sentence over 45 words, and leave the 30-to-45-word band unedited. | (a) Rewrite every sentence over 30 words, 392 of them at the time. (b) Set the bar at 40 words, 151 sentences. (c) Rewrite nothing beyond the row splits. | The two tiers chosen are the ones the review can measure and the ones where a reader visibly suffers: the row threshold is the finding's own number, and a 45-word sentence in a table cell is past the point where a second reading is needed. Option (a) is a style rewrite of 392 first-hand sentences, which multiplies DL-329's risk for a diminishing return. Option (c) leaves 88 sentences standing, the longest at 108 words. | The 30-to-45-word band remains, at 290 sentences and 9.4 per cent of the register, down from 392 and 13.0 per cent, and the scorecard counts it as a soft violation rather than reporting it closed. Closing it is a follow-on that needs the same one-match discipline this pass used. |
| **DL-331** — Record the scorecard in this log, beneath the rows it judges, as a five-column table. | (a) Publish it as a separate document under `documentation/security/`. (b) Report the verdict in the change summary alone. (c) Keep it to the four-column decision shape. | The verdict is a judgement about this file, so it belongs in the file, next to the decisions that produced it and reachable by the same section pointer. Option (a) adds a document that can drift from the file it grades. Option (b) leaves nothing on record, which is the state the review found. Option (c) cannot carry the quote, the rewrite and the justification the rule's output format requires; DL-220 already accepts one table here that is not the decision shape. | A scorecard in the file it grades goes stale when the file changes, so it is dated and names the measurement that produced each verdict. |

**Rule 2 scorecard — this document, measured against the shipped file on 2026-08-03.** Classified as
technical documentation, so the Asimov agent applies. Code blocks and inline code are excluded under
the rule's special-handling clause.

Each principle carries its verdict, the worst offender quoted as it stood, the rewrite applied and why
the rewrite is better, which is the output the rule's format names. DL-386 records why those four
fields are a block per principle rather than a five-column row, and DL-394 records why the run was
repeated after section 41 and section 42 changed the file.

**Verdict: CLEAN.** Zero hard violations and two soft, which is the rule's allowance. The stricter
reading is the one recorded: both soft violations are counted even though the second is the
deliberate choice the special-handling clause permits.

Measured on the shipped file: 406 four-column decision rows keyed `DL-01` through `DL-406`, with no
gap and no duplicate. Zero rows over 1,800 characters, the longest at 1,796. Zero sentences over 45
words, at a median of 14 across roughly 4,900 of them, and zero body paragraphs over five sentences.
The file is about 522,000 characters. `DL-406` carries the decision to refresh these figures with each
later pass.

The counts that move by a handful with any later edit are given to that precision on purpose, since
this section is measuring the file it sits in.

- **A1 Plate Glass Clarity — Pass.** The offender the previous run recorded was a sentence fragment
  left by a mechanical split: `"…so a reader working from the eighteen-line authoring contract should
  read DL-01. Which records why a nineteenth line was added and why the extras spelling that would
  have avoided it was rejected."` (DL-42). Rewritten to `"A reader working from the eighteen-line
  authoring contract should read DL-01. It records why a nineteenth line was added, and why the extras
  spelling that would have avoided it was rejected."` The rewrite puts the instruction first and gives
  the relative clause a subject, so the reader stops hunting backwards for one. This run swept the
  whole file for the same shape and rewrote nine further fragments that later splits had introduced,
  each beginning with a coordinating conjunction or a bare relative pronoun; none remains.
- **A2 Short Words, Simple Structures — Soft violation.** Worst offender: `"…but psycopg2's own
  DETAIL: Key (email)=(…) line still carried the email into the log for a unique violation, because
  that text is a driver diagnostic rather than a bound parameter."` (DL-57). Rewritten to `"For a
  unique violation, psycopg2's own DETAIL: Key (email)=(…) line still carried the email into the log,
  because that text is a driver diagnostic rather than a bound parameter."` Moving the condition to
  the front and splitting the contrast leaves two clauses a reader takes in once each. The violation
  stands rather than closes because roughly 470 sentences, 10 per cent of the file, remain in the
  30-to-45 word band. DL-330 set that band as the deliberate residual and this run holds it.
- **A3 Logical Sequence — Pass.** No offender. The finding index and section list open the file,
  sections run in checkpoint order, and every measurement sits in the evidence block below the table
  it supports rather than inside a decision cell. No rewrite required.
- **A4 Ideas Carry the Weight — Pass.** No offender. Every cell states a decision, an alternative, a
  reason or a residual, and none exists for effect. No rewrite required.
- **A5 Conversational Informality — Soft violation, and a deliberate choice.** Measured: zero
  first-person constructions across 402 rows. The register is written impersonally on purpose, so each
  cell reads as a record an auditor can quote rather than as narration. No rewrite applied: adding a
  personal voice to a compliance register would make the cells harder to quote and would not help the
  reader who arrived through the finding index.
- **A6 No Ornamental Language — Pass.** No offender. No metaphor, no extended figure and no
  decorative modifier survives in the register. No rewrite required.
- **A7 Functional Dialogue — Not applicable.** The document contains no dialogue.
- **A8 Anticipate Reader Questions — Pass.** No offender. Every row carries its alternatives and its
  residual, every evidence block carries the measurement behind the claim that names it, and every
  subject bullet in sections 40.5 and 40.6 now names the row that governs it. No rewrite required.
- **A9 Efficiency Over Polish — Pass.** No offender. The measurements that were once restated inside
  decision cells appear once, in the evidence block for their section, and the reasoning removed from
  code appears once rather than in both a bullet and a row. No rewrite required.
- **A10 Respect the Reader's Intelligence — Pass.** The previous run measured three uses of "note
  that", one "simply" and one "clearly", each addressing a reader who does not need the nudge. Two
  were removed in this run and one reworded: `"and note that neither ci.yml nor cd.yml invokes
  Terraform"` became `"and neither ci.yml nor cd.yml invokes Terraform"`, which says the same thing
  without instructing the reader to pay attention. Measured after: the only remaining occurrences are
  one noun phrase, "the one-line note that the email validator rejects", one description of another
  document's prose, and this scorecard's own quotation of the words it counts. Zero hedges of the
  "somewhat" or "arguably" kind outside the same quotation.

**Measured evidence for 40.8.** Every figure below was measured on the file itself, with inline code
masked so no identifier counts as a long word.

- **DL-328.** Before: **36** rows over 1,800 characters, the longest at 3,693, and 61 over 1,500 — the review's count exactly. After: **zero** rows over 1,800, the longest at 1,796, 36 over 1,500, median 999, and the longest single cell at 1,044 characters. Row count rose from 320 to 331 as the splits landed, keys run DL-01 through DL-331 with no gap and no duplicate, and every row still parses as exactly four cells.
- **DL-329.** Four inherited rows were split — DL-31 into DL-31 and DL-322, DL-62 into DL-62 and DL-323, DL-69 into DL-69 and DL-321, DL-204 into DL-204 and DL-325 — and DL-293 and DL-294 were split in the later sections into DL-324, DL-326 and DL-327. Every replacement was asserted to match exactly once before it was applied.
- **DL-330.** Before: **88** sentences over 45 words, the longest at 108. After: **zero** over 45, the longest at 45. Sentences over 40 words fell from 131 to 39 inside sections 1 through 25, and from 151 to 49 across the file, none of them over 45; DL-285 measured 91 in those sections when it was written, before they grew. Sentences over 30 words stand at 290 of 3,076, down from 392 of 3,023, which is the residual the scorecard reports; median sentence length is 15 words.
- **DL-331.** Body prose carries no paragraph over five sentences, measured across 150 paragraphs, so the paragraph heuristic the rule names is clean independently of the register.

## 41. Corrections after the security assessment of the delivered work

`backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/subscriptions.py`,
`backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/app/main.py`, `backend/app/schema/listing.py`,
`frontend/src/services/api.ts`, `frontend/src/schema/filter.ts`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
`infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend`, `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.frontend`,
`infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml`, `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf`,
`infrastructure/terraform/variables.tf`, `infrastructure/terraform/.terraform.lock.hcl`,
`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, `SECURITY.md`,
`documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`,
and five modules of the security suite

A security assessment read the tree section 40 left behind and raised fifteen findings: seven major,
six medium and two low. All fifteen are closed. This section records every decision taken in answer to
them. Rows are keyed from `DL-332`, each carries one decision, and measurements sit in the evidence
subsection rather than inside a cell. One row supersedes two earlier decisions and says so.

### 41.1 Payment authorization restored (SEC-09)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-332** — Restore the provider-backed verifier in `paypal_service.py`, so a charge authorizes only against a reference the provider confirms for the requested amount, currency, payer and plan, and is refused on replay while retained in the bounded worker-local ledger. **Supersedes DL-288, which withdrew the verifier and returned the service to three self-contained callables, and reinstates the bindings DL-163, DL-201 and DL-202 recorded.** | (a) Keep the unconditional refusal. (b) Authorize on a locally generated reference with no provider call. (c) Follow the file's authoring note, which describes three self-contained callables. | A gate that refuses every attempt is an authorization outage, not a control: it denies the legitimate charge and teaches an operator to bypass it. Option (b) authorizes on data the caller supplies, which is the vector the gate exists to close. Option (c) would leave the outage in place, and the checkout path is on the frozen regression list, so the authoring note is the weaker claim where the two disagree. | The ledger is worker-local and bounded at 4,096 digests, evicting the oldest past that and losing everything on restart, so replay protection lasts only while a digest is retained. No case reaches a live provider; the library is replaced in every test, so provider-side behaviour is asserted against its documented resource shapes rather than observed. The route still cannot persist a row for the schema reasons in `SECURITY.md` section 3.3. |
| **DL-333** — Keep the provider logger capped at warning level, rather than the informational cap the earlier implementation carried. | (a) Restore the informational cap along with the rest of the verifier. | The assessment passed the warning cap explicitly, and a provider client at informational level narrates request and response metadata into the application log. Restoring the looser cap because it arrived in the same commit would trade a reviewed control for symmetry. | A refused charge logs its refusal reason at warning level and nothing about the successful path, so a support question about a charge that worked has no log line to read. |
| **DL-334** — Assert at the route that the gate is cleared, by requiring the response is not the refusal status, rather than requiring a success status. | (a) Assert a 2xx response. (b) Assert the stored subscription row. | The subscription model has no plan column and a non-null status column nothing supplies, so a cleared gate is followed by an insertion failure. An assertion on 2xx would fail for a reason the case is not about, and adding the column is migration work the change scope excludes. | The case cannot distinguish a cleared gate from a gate that was never reached, so it is paired with a case asserting the provider seam was called. |

### 41.2 The numeric domain of a listing (SEC-05)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-335** — Restore the finiteness check on all three listing float fields, and reinstate no value floor. | (a) Restore the finiteness check together with the positive-value floors an earlier pass carried. (b) Bound the magnitude instead of checking finiteness. | A non-finite value is outside the type's domain: it survives validation, reaches the column and compares false against everything, which is a data-integrity defect rather than a business rule. The floors are business rules, and they were withdrawn deliberately; reinstating them under a finiteness finding would smuggle a scope change through a bug fix. Option (b) needs a bound nobody has chosen, and an overflowing literal still has to be refused. | The schema accepts zero and negative rents, which the API contract does not forbid and no test asserts as desirable — the cases pin only that the check is a type-domain check. |
| **DL-336** — Drive the non-finite cases through a raw request body rather than through a serialized payload. | (a) Post a Python `float("nan")` through the test client. (b) Call the validator directly. | A JSON encoder either refuses those values or rewrites them, so a serialized payload never carries the literals `NaN`, `Infinity` or `1e309` that a real client sends. Only a raw body reaches the decoder path where they appear. Option (b) would assert the validator and not the boundary. | The raw-body helper writes the literal into the document text, so a future change to the request media type would need the helper updated with it. |

### 41.3 Throttle identity and equal hasher work (SEC-07)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-337** — Key the login-failure counter on the exact stored identity, removing the case folding and padding strip from the key function. **Supersedes DL-292 for the counter's key; the two layers it records are unchanged.** | (a) Adopt one canonical lower-case storage, login and uniqueness contract. (b) Keep the folded key and document the collision. | The lookup filters on the stored column exactly, so a folded key described a different account than the one being authenticated: a wrong-case attempt exhausted a victim's allowance, and a success on an attacker-held variant cleared it. Option (a) is the better long-term contract and needs a data migration plus a uniqueness change, which the change scope excludes. Option (b) leaves the bypass open. | Two accounts differing only in local-part case remain two accounts, which is a registration-policy weakness this work does not close; it is now visible rather than hidden behind a shared counter. |
| **DL-338** — Verify the submitted password against a per-process random stand-in hash when no account matches, so both branches perform one verification, and assert equality by counting hasher invocations. | (a) Return early when no row matches, as before. (b) Compare wall-clock durations in the test. (c) Use a fixed literal stand-in hash. | An early return answers an unknown address faster than a wrong password, which is an account-existence oracle. Counting invocations is deterministic; a timing threshold is flaky on shared runners and would be the first case disabled. A literal stand-in hash in a tracked file is a credential-shaped constant that the repository scan would have to exempt. | The two branches perform equal work at the hasher, not identical total work: the absent branch skips one query. The stand-in hash costs one bcrypt operation at import, measured at 0.81 seconds for the module. |

### 41.4 Log record integrity (SEC-08)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-339** — Fold application records to a single line in a log-record factory scoped by logger name, rather than in a filter on the package logger. | (a) Attach a filter to the package logger. (b) Set the package logger not to propagate. (c) Leave the formatter on the owned handler as the only control. | A filter on an ancestor logger never runs: the standard library applies the originating logger's filters, then each handler's own, and consults no ancestor's. Records originate on child loggers, so option (a) is inert — measured before it shipped. Option (b) silences every sink a deployment adds and breaks log capture in the suite. Option (c) is the reviewed defect: a root handler with a stock formatter re-emits the multiline text. | The factory is global to the interpreter, so it chains any factory installed earlier and marks itself to stay idempotent; a library installing its own factory afterwards and discarding the chain would bypass the fold. |
| **DL-340** — Fold every C0 control character and the delete character, not the newline alone. | (a) Replace newlines only. | A bare carriage return overwrites a rendered line, and an escape introduces terminal control sequences: both forge log content without a newline. Handling one character of the class and not the rest would close the demonstration and leave the weakness. | The folded text carries a marker in place of each control character, so a legitimate tab inside a logged value is rendered rather than preserved. |

### 41.5 Client-side wire contracts (SEC-05, SEC-06)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-341** — Map the filter form's value to the wire contract inside the service module, and declare the form's shape as an interface beside the wire type. | (a) Change the form component to hold the wire shape. (b) Convert inside the request model on the server. (c) Accept both shapes at the endpoint. | The component, the store and the routing are frozen, and the service module is the one layer already in scope. Option (b) moves a client concern into the server contract and would loosen a schema that exists to be strict. Option (c) widens the accepted input of a write endpoint to fix a client defect. | The form collects no name, so the mapper supplies a documented default; two saved filters therefore carry the same name until the form gains a field. |
| **DL-342** — Delete the client call to the unmounted profile route rather than adding the route. | (a) Add a profile endpoint. (b) Point the call at an existing route. | The route set is frozen and new features are out of scope; the call had zero consumers, so deleting it removes a declaration nothing depended on. Option (b) would invent a semantic for a call whose only caller does not exist. | A separate page still imports a differently named profile function that no module exports, which is a pre-existing compile failure this work does not close. The module also exports no update function while a component imports one; closing that needs a route the frozen set forbids. |
| **DL-343** — Assert the mapping by executing the shipped client module under the runtime's type-stripping mode, with the transport replaced by a recording stub. | (a) Re-implement the mapping in the test and compare. (b) Add a frontend test runner. | A test that restates the mapping passes when both copies are wrong together. Executing the shipped module means the assertion is about the code that ships, and the driven value is read out of the form component itself rather than typed into the test. Option (b) adds a toolchain the change scope excludes. | The execution copies the module with three substitutions, each asserted to apply exactly once, so a refactor that renames the transport import fails the case until the substitution is updated. |

### 41.6 The two pipeline gates (SEC-01, SEC-12, CWE-1104)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-344** — Widen the credential pattern to match an assignment with spaces and quotes, and add a second reviewed allow-list stage, rather than narrowing what the gate scans. | (a) Keep the narrow pattern. (b) Adopt a third-party secret scanner. (c) Exclude the test tree from the gate. | The formatter-compliant spelling is the one this repository's own style produces, so a gate that misses it misses the likely case. Widening alone produces 21 matches, none of them credentials, and a gate that reports 21 false hits is a gate operators learn to ignore; the allow-list admits four named classes instead. Option (b) adds a dependency and a service the change scope excludes. Option (c) exempts exactly the files most likely to hold a fixture credential. | The marker is a bypass by construction: any line carrying it is admitted. The count is pinned at four, every marked line must carry a reason and be matched by the pattern, and no application source line may carry it — but a reviewer who adds a fifth marked line and updates the count has widened the hole. |
| **DL-345** — Read the audit's JSON report for both `id` and `aliases` when checking the suppression list for staleness, and assemble the declaration pattern from two string fragments. | (a) Keep matching one namespace of identifiers. (b) Suppress the GitHub-namespace advisory under a Python-namespace identifier. | One accepted advisory exists only under the GitHub namespace, so a single-namespace check could never see it and that suppression could rot silently. Option (b) needs an identifier the database does not publish. The fragment split keeps the checking step out of its own declaration set, so the check does not read itself as a suppression. | The check depends on the report's alias field being populated for the advisories in question; an advisory published with no alias and suppressed under a namespace the report does not use would still read as stale. |

### 41.7 Build and deployment delivery (SEC-12)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-346** — Resolve the frontend install from the manifest alone, in both the image and the pipeline, rather than committing a lock file so the strict install can run. | (a) Generate and commit the lock file. (b) Leave the strict install and the missing lock in place. | The requirements exclude lock-file generation, because committing one commits a transitive tree this work has not audited. Option (b) leaves a build that cannot run from a clean checkout, which is the finding. Making the two invocations identical is what keeps the pipeline's answer and the image's answer the same. | Transitive resolution stays non-deterministic on the frontend, which the residual register already records; lock-file generation is a named follow-on. |
| **DL-347** — Copy the backend context into a subdirectory and start the process from its parent, rather than copying it to the working directory root. | (a) Rename the copy target so the package resolves. (b) Change the application's own import style. | The application imports itself through its repository-rooted package path, so the process has to start above that path. Keeping the wholesale copy intact preserves the context-local exclusion file as the control that keeps a local secret out of the layer, which a narrowed copy list would replace with a maintained allow-list. Option (b) edits import statements across the backend to satisfy a container layout. | The image carries the whole backend context, tests included; narrowing both copy instructions is a named container-hardening follow-on. |
| **DL-348** — Probe the schema document with the interpreter for the backend healthcheck and the busybox client for the frontend, rather than adding a health route or a tool to either image. | (a) Add a health endpoint. (b) Install a request tool into both images. | Neither base image ships the tool the previous healthchecks called, so both checks would have failed on a container that was in fact serving. The route set is frozen, so option (a) is unavailable, and the schema document is served without reaching the database, which makes it a liveness probe rather than a readiness probe. Option (b) enlarges both images to run a check. | A backend container answers the check while unable to reach its database, so the check reports liveness and not readiness. |

### 41.8 Provider supply chain and the secret input channel (SEC-10, SEC-11, SEC-12)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-349** — Bound the provider to the current minor line and below the next major, and bound the command line below its next major, rather than pinning the provider exactly. | (a) Pin the provider to one version. (b) Declare a floor with no ceiling. | The lock already fixes which build installs, so the constraint's job is to bar a release that could withdraw or redefine the arguments these controls rely on — the encryption mode and the write-only password pair. An exact pin blocks patch releases and forces a configuration edit for each one. Option (b) admits the next major, which is the drift the finding names. | A minor release inside the range can still change behaviour without changing an argument name; the lock means such a release installs only when someone re-locks deliberately. |
| **DL-350** — Lock the provider for four platforms, and assert the constraint the lock records rather than re-implementing the version solver in a test. | (a) Lock only the platform that generated the file. (b) Parse constraints and compare version ranges in the test. | A checkout on an unlocked platform fails installation, so the set has to cover the container and runner architectures and the two developer-machine architectures. The lock file records the constraint it was generated against, so comparing that string to the configuration detects a widened constraint with a stale lock — which is the failure a hand-written solver would exist to catch, without the solver. | The lock names no platform beside each hash, so a test can count hashes but cannot prove which platform each covers; the four names live in `SECURITY.md`, and the case asserts they are documented. |
| **DL-351** — Require the database password through the environment or a gitignored variable file, and keep the file channel while prohibiting the command-line flag. | (a) Remove both channels and name only the environment variable. (b) Leave the flag as one option among several. | A value passed on the command line is readable in the process arguments for the life of the command and lands in shell history; the environment and a protected file have neither property. Option (a) removes a channel operators need for multi-value runs, and the ignore rules already protect it — verified against the ignore file rather than assumed. Option (b) is the finding. | The description is guidance, not enforcement: nothing prevents an operator from passing the flag anyway, and the value is still readable to anything that can read the provisioning process's memory. |

### 41.9 Provisioning publish and default privileges (SEC-01, SEC-11)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-352** — Publish the generated environment file with a link call that fails when the destination exists in any form, then drop the temporary name and confirm what was published. **Supersedes DL-55 and DL-181, whose rename-based publish this replaces, and corrects DL-55's claim that the rename discards a planted symlink.** | (a) Keep the rename, as DL-55 and DL-181 chose. (b) Use the no-clobber flag of the move command. (c) Use the link command's no-target-directory option. (d) Keep the check that precedes the move. | A rename replaces whatever the destination names, and when that name is a directory the move succeeds by placing the file inside it — measured: the source was absorbed and the run would have reported success with no file published. Option (b) is not atomic and absorbs into a directory the same way. Option (c) depends on an extension the tool lacks on one common developer platform. Option (d) is the reviewed race: any check is separated from the act that follows it. | The publish now refuses instead of replacing, so a planted symlink at the destination is left standing and the operator is told to remove it — a behaviour change from DL-55, which replaced it. The link requires one file system, satisfied because the temporary file is created in the destination directory. A refused publish leaves the temporary file for the exit trap. |
| **DL-353** — Revoke the database connect and temporary privileges from the public pseudo-role, and leave schema usage with it. | (a) Revoke schema usage as well. (b) Grant the temporary privilege back to the application role. | The database privileges are granted to everyone by default, so the explicit connect grant described a privilege the application role already held and every other role held with it — measured: a role with no grant at all answered true to connect, temporary and schema create. With connect revoked, no unprivileged role reaches the database to use the schema, so option (a) adds risk of breaking a legitimate role for no reachable gain. Option (b) restores a capability the stated privilege set does not include. | Schema usage remains with the public pseudo-role, which `SECURITY.md` states rather than implies. Any role granted connect later inherits that usage. |
| **DL-354** — Close the provisioning batch with a block that reads the effective privileges and raises, substituting the default access list when the stored one is null. | (a) Trust the statements the batch issues. (b) Print the effective privileges for an operator to read. (c) Read the access-list column directly. | A statement that applied to the wrong role reads exactly like one that worked, which is why the finding asks for effective privileges rather than statement text. A printed report is ignored; a raise stops provisioning, because the batch runs with the stop-on-error setting. Option (c) is the trap: an untouched access-list column is null while the defaults still apply, so the naive query reports a clean database that grants everything it started with. | The block runs in the same batch, and the statements before it are already committed when it raises, so a failure leaves a partially provisioned database — which the operator message names, telling them to drop the database and its roles before rerunning. |
| **DL-355** — Return the batch's privilege statements and its verification block separately to the test suite. | (a) Return the whole batch as one list of lines. | The privilege set is compared whole, so probe lines that mention the application role would have to be enumerated in the expected set, and a real privilege added later could hide among them. The forbidden-privilege case still reads the whole batch, verification block included. | The split keys on the block's opening token, so a verification block written differently would be read as a privilege statement. |

### 41.10 Documents reconciled with the measured tree (SEC-12)

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-356** — State both dependency closure figures in `SECURITY.md` and name which one the gate reads. | (a) Keep the single approximate figure. (b) Replace it with the audited figure alone. | The runtime pins resolve to 42 packages and the installed environment freezes at 74; the gate reads the frozen environment, so a document naming only the smaller figure understates the audited surface. Naming only the larger one loses the runtime closure a deployment actually ships. | Both figures move with any pin change, so both are dated and attributed to a command a reader can rerun. |
| **DL-357** — Add the collection-error flag to the documented full-suite invocation, and record what the command does without it. | (a) Document the invocation without the flag and explain the errors afterwards. | Without the flag the command exits during collection and runs **zero tests** while appearing to be the full-suite command — measured. A documented command that silently runs nothing is worse than one that fails. | The flag also masks a genuinely new collection error, so the count of expected errors is stated with it and asserted at three. |
| **DL-358** — Replace the claim that gate one excludes no file with a description of the two-stage gate, and describe the reviewed-line marker without reproducing its text. | (a) Keep the original claim. (b) Reproduce the marker so a reader can search for it. | The claim was true when written and the allow-list made it false; leaving it would be the same class of defect the assessment raised. Option (b) fails on its own terms: a line quoting the marker is admitted by it, and the count case then reports five marked lines where four were reviewed — observed while writing this section. | A reader now needs the workflow open to see the marker's exact text, which the section says explicitly. |
| **DL-359** — Name only the backend base image as past end of life. | (a) Keep the claim that both images are. | The frontend image tracks a runtime in maintenance support until April 2027; the backend image tracks one whose upstream support ended in October 2025. A document that overstates a residual is as unreliable as one that understates it. | Maintenance support ends, so the row needs revisiting before April 2027; the date is stated so it can be checked. |
| **DL-360** — Remove the payment checkout from the preservation list and replace it with the two measured blockers. | (a) Keep it on the list, since no file in that path changed. (b) Move it to the residual register with no measurement. | Nothing in that path changed, and the path still does not work: the bundle does not build because a package it imports is declared nowhere, and the subscription route cannot persist. "Preserved" would be read as "works". Option (b) states the conclusion without the evidence for it. | Both blockers are pre-existing and remain open; the second is a named follow-on and the first is item 2 in the follow-on list. |
| **DL-361** — Record that logout clears the cookie without revoking the token. | (a) Leave the logout case's description as it stood. | A reader takes "logout invalidates the session" as revocation. Verification checks the signature and the expiry and consults no revocation record, so a captured token keeps working until it expires. Closing that needs an identifier claim and a persisted deny list, which is storage this repository does not have. | The residual is real and unmitigated beyond the token lifetime. `HttpOnly` prevents script reading the cookie, and no more: the frozen response body still returns `access_token` to page code. |
| **DL-362** — Record the assessment's fifteen findings in `SECURITY.md`, each with what closed it and what it leaves open. | (a) Record them in this log only. (b) Record the count without the per-finding detail. | The operational document is where a reviewer looks for current posture, and three of the fifteen close with a residual that belongs in the residual register beside the others. This log carries the reasoning; the table carries the state, which is the division the two documents already follow. | The table restates outcomes recorded in this section, so a later correction has to land in both; each row names the section that carries its detail to keep the duplication shallow. |

### 41.11 Measured evidence for section 41

Every figure below was measured against the tree this section describes.

- **DL-332 through DL-334.** The security suite grew from 543 to 688 passing cases across the fifteen findings. Reinstating the unconditional refusal fails 11 cases; removing the route's plan binding fails 2.
- **DL-335 and DL-336.** Removing the finiteness check fails 18 cases: 15 raw-body cases across three fields and five literals, and 3 overflow cases.
- **DL-337 and DL-338.** Restoring the case folding in the key function fails 2 cases; restoring the early return on the absent-account branch fails 1. The stand-in hash carries the same scheme and cost prefix as a stored hash, and the equal-work case observes exactly two hasher invocations across the two branches.
- **DL-339 and DL-340.** Removing the record factory and leaving the handler formatter — the reviewed condition — fails 8 cases: six control characters, one exception-information case, and the ancestor-handler case.
- **DL-341 through DL-343.** The mapper turns the form's own value into a body carrying exactly the wire contract's fields; type checking reports the same 18 pre-existing errors before and after, all in one unrelated module. Restoring the array mapping fails 3 cases; re-adding the unmounted route call fails 1.
- **DL-344 and DL-345.** The widened pattern matches 21 lines and the allow-list admits all 21, so the gate reports 0. Executed verbatim: a planted upper-case assignment and a planted lower-case assignment each exit 1 reporting path and line only. Reverting the pattern to its narrow form fails 13 cases. The audit step reports 15 declared suppressions against 44 reported identifiers and aliases, exits 0, and exits 1 naming a planted stale identifier in the GitHub namespace.
- **DL-346 through DL-348.** The Compose definition renders with the full contract and fails with a named message when one build variable is unset. The backend image builds, runs, answers its own healthcheck inside the container, and serves the six declared routes. The frontend image installs and then fails in the bundle step on the undeclared package.
- **DL-349 and DL-350.** The lock records version 7.42.0 under its constraint with four platform hashes and twelve registry checksums; installation selects that build under the constraint. Widening the constraint, deleting the provider block, staling the recorded constraint, and removing the lock from version control each fail the intended case.
- **DL-351.** Restoring the command-line recommendation fails the description case. The ignore rules were verified live: a variable file is excluded and its example form is not.
- **DL-352.** Executed against a planted destination of each kind: a directory, a symlink and a regular file each refuse with the source intact and the planted name unchanged, and a free destination receives an owner-only file with the temporary name dropped. Replacing the link with the previous move reproduces the absorption.
- **DL-353 and DL-354.** Measured on PostgreSQL 13.23. Before: the public pseudo-role held connect and temporary on the database and create and usage on the schema, and a role with no grant answered true to connect, temporary and schema create. After: nothing on the database, usage only on the schema, and the application role answered true to connect and schema usage and false to temporary and schema create, with the owner still connecting. The verification block passed clean and raised when the temporary privilege was granted back.
- **DL-356 through DL-362.** Every command in `SECURITY.md` section 2 was rerun.
  - The style check reports 109 findings with one undefined name, against a 129-finding baseline measured on the pre-work tree that carried four.
  - The full suite reports 688 passed with three collection errors and exit 1 with the flag, and zero tests with exit 2 without it.
  - The dependency gate reports no vulnerabilities with 15 ignored, and 15 vulnerabilities in 9 packages without the suppressions. All three repository scans report zero.
