## 42. Corrections after the final cumulative Rules review

`backend/app/main.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `frontend/src/services/api.ts`,
`infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml`, `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend`,
`infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.frontend`, `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf`,
`infrastructure/terraform/variables.tf`, `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, `.gitignore`,
`.env.example`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `backend/tests/conftest.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_auth_identity.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_cors_policy.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_password_policy.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_token_storage.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_login_throttle.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_error_handling.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py`, `SECURITY.md`,
`documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`, `documentation/security/decision-log.md`

A final cumulative review of both live Rules read the tree section 41 left behind and raised eighteen
findings: fifteen major and three minor. All eighteen are closed. Rows are keyed from `DL-363`, each
carries one decision, and the measurements sit in 42.6 rather than inside a cell.

The review's root finding is one sentence long: the pass recorded in section 41 rewrote comments and
docstrings across twenty-two files and put reasoning back into them, while sections 40.5, 40.6 and
40.8 still reported that residual closed. Rows `DL-363` through `DL-384`, together with `DL-396`, record the
second removal and the reasoning removed with it. Rows `DL-385` through `DL-395` record the
artifact and accuracy corrections in the three documents, and rows `DL-397` through `DL-402` key the
choices the review found stated as subject bullets with no key of their own.

### 42.1 The comment shape, and the reasoning removed from code a second time

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-363** — Let a comment carry a stable `DL-NNN` key beside its factual marker, and keep section pointers out of code. **Amends DL-312, which rejected pointers of both kinds.** | (a) Hold DL-312 and let each comment carry the marker alone. (b) Allow section pointers as well. (c) Transcribe the reasoning back into the comment. | DL-312 rejected pointers because a section number goes stale when sections are renumbered. A key does not: the sequence is flat, append-only and never renumbered, which DL-220 established for exactly this reason. The review asked in eight places for comments to carry a decision reference, and a key satisfies that without becoming a second explanation. Option (a) leaves a maintainer with a threat and no route to the reasoning. Option (c) is the violation. | A key resolves only through this log, so a reader outside the repository sees an opaque token. Roughly forty comments now carry one, and a key deleted from this log would leave every one of them dangling — which the identifier check in 42.6 measures. |
| **DL-364** — State the log-record folding controls in `backend/app/main.py` as facts and move their reasoning here. **Narrowed by DL-396, which carries the remaining comments in that file.** | (a) Leave the comments as section 41 wrote them. (b) Delete the comments outright. | Ten comments explained why an alternative fails, which is the Rule 1 violation the review names. Option (b) loses the threat marker AAP 0.12.2 requires. The reasoning for the folding group: the whole C0 range and DEL are translated because a bare carriage return rewrites a line a terminal already printed and the range carries terminal escapes. Folding happens in the record factory, at creation, because that point precedes `Logger.handle`, every handler and propagation to an ancestor sink. A formatter on the owned handler alone leaves a root or deployment handler emitting the raw multi-line record; `exc_info`, `exc_text` and `stack_info` are cleared so no formatter appends the diagnostics again on a later line. The owned formatter re-applies the same normalisation, so a record from another factory, and any text the format string contributes, is still one line. This package configures its own logger because a server that configures only its own leaves it on `logging.lastResort`, which emits the message alone. | A maintainer at each of those sites now reads the threat and not the trade-off, and comes here for the second. |
| **DL-396** — State the remaining `backend/app/main.py` comments — schema creation, the error-envelope substitutions and the secret redaction — as facts and move their reasoning here. **Narrowed out of DL-364 so no row carries two comment groups.** | (a) Leave them inside DL-364's row, which took that row past 1,800 characters. (b) Delete the comments outright. | DL-330 defines a row over 1,800 characters as a worst offender and DL-328 answers it by putting one decision in one row, so a row covering two comment groups is the shape those two rows rule out. The reasoning for this group: `create_all(checkfirst=True)` is safe for the runtime role because on a provisioned database every table already exists and is owned by the owner role. The request path and method are substituted because both are client-supplied text, and a link sent to a victim can put a token or an address in the request line. An undeclared key is named by position because the validation library reports it under a caller-chosen name. Secret literals are replaced longest first so a shorter secret nested in a longer one leaves no remainder, and bound parameters are suppressed on every chain member because a driver failure re-raised as an ordinary error is still rendered through its context. | One file is now governed by two rows, so a later edit to it has two rows to check rather than one; both carry the same `SEC-08` and `SEC-12` markers the code sites do. |
| **DL-365** — State the throttle, cookie and credential-branch constants in `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py` as facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Move the constants into the settings class so the comments become configuration documentation. | Option (b) widens the settings contract to avoid a comment edit, which the Minimal Change Clause forbids. The reasoning: the audit marker is keyed per process because an unkeyed digest of an email address is recoverable from a candidate dictionary; the cookie attributes are shared by the set and the clear so a cleared cookie matches the one set. The absent-account stand-in hash carries the scheme and cost of a stored hash over a per-process random secret, so no submitted password can match it and both credential branches do equal hasher work. The account counter keys on the exact value the credential query filters on, so no case variant shares a counter with another account. A key at the limit is never evicted because evicting one would drop a live lockout, and ordering by expiry evicts the entry closest to expiring; and counting and admission share one critical section so a concurrent burst cannot share an allowance. | The equal-work property is asserted by invocation count rather than by elapsed time, so it remains a property of the code path and not a timing guarantee under load. |
| **DL-366** — State the filter-mapping comments in `frontend/src/services/api.ts` as the resulting contract and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Move the mapping into the form component so the client carries no comment at all. | Option (b) puts a wire contract in a component, which DL-306 already declined. The reasoning: `DEFAULT_FILTER_NAME` exists because `POST /filters/` refuses an empty name with 400 while the form collects no name field. And the UI model keys criteria by input name while the wire body carries field, operator and value triples, so the two are mapped explicitly rather than assumed to share a shape. | The mapping is still exercised only through the driven-client cases, since the one component caller resolves its types through an unresolved import path. |

### 42.2 The reasoning removed from configuration, infrastructure and the pipeline

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-367** — State the Compose interpolation and healthcheck comments as facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Drop the healthcheck comments entirely. | The reasoning: every build argument and every no-default setting uses the `${VAR:?message}` form, so an unset or empty value stops the container start with a named message instead of interpolating to empty. Interpolating to empty would bake a wrong origin into the bundle or start the application part-way through import. Each healthcheck names a program its own base image carries, since the nginx image ships busybox wget and no curl and the slim Python image ships no curl, with the backend probe targeting the schema document, which is served without reaching the database. Option (b) leaves the next maintainer to rediscover the missing curl by watching a container fail every interval. | The comment now names the program without naming the absence, so a maintainer who swaps a base image has to consult this row or the guard case. |
| **DL-368** — State the Terraform version, lock and account comments as facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Move the whole delivery contract into `SECURITY.md`. | Option (b) puts a provider constraint in an operator document. The reasoning: the command-line floor is 1.11.0 because `password_wo` and the `ephemeral` variable that feeds it both require it. Both upper bounds plus the tracked lock stop an ambient install resolving to a build that withdraws `ssl_mode`, `password_wo` or `google_sql_user`, where the configuration would apply without enforcing what it reads as enforcing. And the cloud application account is narrowed out of band because Cloud SQL grants `cloudsqlsuperuser` to every built-in user it creates and this provider exposes no grant or revoke resource. | The bounds are now stated without their consequence, so the reason a major upgrade is a security event lives here alone. |
| **DL-369** — Keep the operational instruction in the `db_app_password` description and move its reasoning here. | (a) Leave the description as section 41 wrote it. (b) Delete the prohibition with the reasoning. | The description is the only text an operator reads before choosing a channel, so the instruction stays: supply the value through the environment variable, through a gitignored variable file, or from a secret manager via an ephemeral input, and never with `-var`. The reasoning moves: `-var` places the value in the process argument list, which any local user can read for the life of the command, and in shell history afterwards. Option (b) would drop the prohibition itself, which is the control. | The description is now shorter than the argument for it, so an operator who wants the argument comes here. The guard case still asserts that the flag appears only under a prohibition. |
| **DL-370** — State the provisioning comments as facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Move them into `SECURITY.md` beside the operator procedure. | Option (b) mixes a script's internals into an operator runbook. The reasoning: `link(2)` fails when the destination exists in any form and no check precedes it, so no window exists in which the destination can change, which a check-then-move flow had; the temporary file is owner-only before publication, so no byte is ever world-readable. The credential alphabet is constrained because both role passwords reach `psql` as quoted SQL literals; and both passwords travel on standard input because a value passed with `psql`'s own variable flag would enter an argument list any local process can read. | Eleven comments in that script now state a property without its threat model, and the guard cases are the only other place the shapes they replace are named. |
| **DL-371** — Reduce the `.gitignore` Terraform comment to a category label and move its reasoning here. | (a) Leave the four-line explanation. (b) Remove the comment and leave the patterns bare. | The reasoning: operator variable files are the conventional home for an operator-supplied value and state records other resource attributes, while the password itself reaches neither, since `main.tf` takes it through the write-only `password_wo` argument from an `ephemeral` variable. So these exclusions are defence in depth rather than the primary control, which is the correction DL-295 already made. Option (b) leaves a reader unable to tell a security exclusion from a convenience one. | The file no longer says which of its exclusions are load-bearing. The section-23 rows and this one are where that ordering lives. |
| **DL-372** — Keep the `.env.example` usage instructions and move the layout and build-channel reasoning here. | (a) Leave the template as section 41 wrote it. (b) Cut the layout guidance with the reasoning. | A template is read by an operator mid-task, so instructions stay and arguments go. The reasoning: `env_file` is a bare filename that pydantic resolves against the process working directory, so scattering copies to match each command's directory creates a second secret to protect, rotate and eventually forget. And create-react-app embeds the two frontend variables at compile time, so a value supplied at run time never reaches the browser and the build stage is the only place they can arrive. | The template states behaviour without the argument for the arrangement it recommends, which is why the recommendation itself is kept rather than reduced to a fact. **Nine comment lines in this file still contain a marker word — eight in the template and one in the provisioning script — and all nine state observable behaviour rather than justify a choice; 42.6 measures the distinction rather than claiming a zero.** |
| **DL-373** — State the pipeline gate comments as facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Point each gate comment at a document section. | Option (b) is the section pointer DL-363 declines. The reasoning: the staleness check reads the report's own identifier and its aliases so an advisory published only under the GitHub namespace is compared too; its expression is assembled from two fragments so the check does not read itself as a declaration. The credential pattern matches an assignment with or without spaces and quotes so the formatter-compliant spelling cannot pass, and the one-character bracket expressions keep it off its own line, which is what removed the two file exemptions the gate once needed. And the audit gate sits immediately after the install step because the audit tool does not exist before it. | A maintainer editing the pattern now has to come here to learn why it is bracket-split, and a rewrite that loses that property fails the guard case rather than the review. |
| **DL-374** — State the backend image comments as path facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Restructure the image so no comment is needed. | Option (b) is a build change to avoid a comment edit. The reasoning: the application imports itself as `backend.app.*`, so the context lands in `/app/backend` and the process starts one directory above it; and the definition copies its whole context, which is the premise the three ignore files rest on. | The two `WORKDIR` lines now read as bare facts, so their relationship to the import root lives here. |
| **DL-375** — State the frontend image comments as facts and move their reasoning here. | (a) Leave them. (b) Move the runtime-floor note into `SECURITY.md`. | The reasoning: the platform runtime floor interdicts Node 20.x below 20.20.2 and 22.x below 22.12.0, so the documented `14` is not installable, and 22 is the lowest permitted line that still builds react-scripts 5.0.1 without a legacy-OpenSSL workaround. No lock file is tracked, so the install resolves from the manifest alone; and create-react-app embeds both build arguments into the bundle. Option (b) separates the constraint from the line it constrains. | The image no longer records why it departs from the documented Node line. This row carries it, section 40.5's subject list names both files against it, and DL-269 records the consequence for base-image currency. Section 36 does not hold it: that inventory is the five deviations recorded there, and this one is recorded here. |

### 42.3 The reasoning removed from the harness and the security suite, and the techniques now keyed

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-376** — Bind every short `app.*` name to the canonical module object by importing the entry point first and aliasing what it loaded. | (a) Import each module twice under both names. (b) Rewrite the three pre-existing test modules to the absolute style. (c) Insert only the repository root on the path and let the short style fail. | One source file loaded under two names produces two module objects, two settings instances, two limiters and two counter maps, so a case that empties one throttle observes the other. Aliasing after the first import makes the second import a lookup rather than a second execution. Option (a) is the defect. Option (b) edits reference files AAP 0.9.2 places outside this work. Option (c) breaks collection for those three modules in a new way. | The aliases are installed by import side effect, so a module imported before `conftest.py` runs would escape them; `_assert_one_module_object_per_source_file` is what turns that into a failure rather than a silent divergence. |
| **DL-377** — Cache the resolved source path of each loaded module, keyed by the `__file__` string `sys.modules` holds. | (a) Resolve every path on every check. (b) Run the integrity check once, at import. | The check runs at import and again before every one of 689 cases, and each run resolves one path per loaded module — several hundred filesystem calls per case without the cache. Option (b) drops the per-test guarantee, which is what catches a case that imports a module under a second name. | The cache is keyed by the string rather than by the file, so a module whose `__file__` is rewritten in place during a run would read stale. Nothing in this suite rewrites one. |
| **DL-378** — Replace the single harness override key for the span of a context, and restore it afterwards. | (a) Add a second override key for the failing dependency. (b) Monkeypatch the session factory. (c) Clear all overrides and rebuild them. | The route resolves its session through one dependency key, so the failure has to arrive through that key or it is not the production path. Saving and restoring the harness value is what keeps the SQLite override intact for the rest of the case. Option (a) cannot work: a second key for the same dependency is the same key. Option (b) bypasses the dependency resolution the error boundary sits behind. Option (c) rebuilds state the autouse fixture owns. | The context manager restores on exit, so a case that raises inside it still leaves the harness override in place. A case that replaces the override itself, without the fixture, would defeat that. |
| **DL-379** — Attach a root handler carrying the stock formatter and read the record where that handler writes it. | (a) Read the record through `caplog` alone. (b) Assert the formatter on the owned handler. (c) Capture standard error. | `caplog` attaches to the root logger and proves only that a message was built, and a formatter assertion proves only what one handler does. A deployment sink above the package is the shape the fold has to survive, and reading the record at that sink is the only assertion that distinguishes folding at creation from folding in a formatter. `only` admits one logger subtree so a library record travelling the same path does not join the text under assertion. Option (c) couples the assertion to stream buffering. | The sink is attached to the root logger for the span of one case, so a case that leaves it attached would collect records from every later case. The fixture removes it in a finally block. |
| **DL-380** — Read the connect arguments through a `do_connect` listener that records the keywords and raises. | (a) Open a real connection and inspect the driver. (b) Read the engine's stored `connect_args` attribute. (c) Patch the driver's `connect`. | The assertion is what SQLAlchemy hands the driver, which is the last point the value can be dropped, and raising from the listener means no connection is ever opened — the suite has no PostgreSQL server and must not need one. Option (b) asserts what the engine was told rather than what it passes on. Option (c) patches a library the application does not own. | The listener is removed in a finally block, and the case asserts it fired, so a pooled engine that never reaches the hook fails rather than passing vacuously. |
| **DL-381** — Load the shipped database module as a fresh module under a probe name with `spec_from_file_location`. | (a) Reload the real module with `importlib.reload`. (b) Import it and monkeypatch the engine afterwards. (c) Reproduce the URL branch in the test. | The module builds its engine at import scope, so the settings pair has to be in place before the module body runs. Option (a) rebinds the application's own module object for every later case in the process. Option (b) asserts nothing about the branch under test. Option (c) tests a transcription rather than the shipped file. | Each probe builds an engine that owns a pool, so every probe engine is registered and disposed by an autouse fixture. An engine built outside that helper would leak. |
| **DL-382** — Assemble each fragment-based control from the shipped text with substitutions asserted to apply exactly once. | (a) Transcribe the control into the test. (b) Invoke the shipped control as a subprocess. | A transcription passes while the shipped control rots, which is the failure these cases exist to catch. Asserting each substitution applies exactly once is what makes the copy provably the shipped logic with one seam moved. Option (b) needs the surrounding job, its network access and its installed tooling. | A substitution that stops matching fails the case rather than silently testing an unmodified copy, but a control refactored past the substitution point needs the case updated with it. |
| **DL-383** — Stub the provider library and the provisioning primitives at the module boundary the application calls. | (a) Record HTTP traffic against a fake server. (b) Run the real provider sandbox. (c) Call the internal helpers directly. | The seam the application owns is the module attribute it calls, so replacing that attribute exercises every line of the application's own path and none of the network. Option (b) makes the suite depend on a third-party service. Option (c) skips the route, the dependency resolution and the error boundary. | A stub is a claim about the provider's contract. The charge cases pin the claim to the response shapes the provider documents, and a provider change would need them updated. |
| **DL-384** — Reduce every remaining docstring to what the case asserts and the security property it pins, and keep the counterfactuals here. | (a) Reduce every docstring to one line, as an earlier reviewer prescribed and DL-196 declined. (b) Keep them. (c) Delete them. | A docstring stating what a case pins is what a reviewer needs at the case; a docstring arguing why the approach was chosen is a decision recorded in code. The line between them is the sentence that names an option not taken, and 108 changed docstrings carried one. Option (a) cannot say what a multi-part case pins. The counterfactuals removed are recoverable here and in the rows above: a token whose subject is an email answers 401 rather than 500; a guard that stopped screening would answer 500 through the sanitized boundary. A seeded row is what distinguishes the screen from the unresolved-account branch; a row written straight to the session would assert the read path alone; and a bound accepted but ignored would return the whole table to every caller. | The boundary is a judgment call, and a sentence stating a threat can read like a justification. The mechanical sweep in 42.6 is what makes the result checkable. |

### 42.4 Artifact shape, accepted-risk dating and document accuracy

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-385** — Recast the advisory register as one dated block per entry, so no table in this log carries a shape other than the four decision columns. **Supersedes DL-220, which accepted one nine-column table here.** | (a) Keep the nine-column table and argue a register is a record rather than a decision table. (b) Move the register to a companion document. (c) Recast each entry as a four-column decision row. | The Rule delivers the decision log *as* a four-column table, and the review reads every table in the file against that shape, so a nine-column register is a shape defect however useful its columns are. A block per entry keeps all nine measured fields, gains the per-entry date DL-387 needs, and reads in one pass rather than by horizontal scrolling. Option (b) creates a document that drifts from the gate it justifies, which DL-331 declined for the scorecard. Option (c) folds nine fields into four cells and loses the field labels. | The register is no longer machine-readable as a table, so the guard case that compares it against the gate now parses the block heading instead. That parser is a second contract on the register's wording, and 42.6 measures both directions. |
| **DL-386** — Recast the Rule 2 scorecard as one block per principle. **Supersedes DL-331, which recorded it as a five-column table.** | (a) Keep the five-column table. (b) Publish the scorecard as a separate document. (c) Drop the quote and the rewrite so it fits four columns. | The same shape rule applies, and DL-331's own reason for the five columns — that the Rule's output format needs a quote, a rewrite and a justification — is satisfied by a block just as well as by a row. Option (b) is what DL-331 declined. Option (c) drops what the Rule requires. | A block is longer than a row, so the scorecard is now the longest single passage in the file. It carries its own measurement date, since a scorecard goes stale the moment the file changes. |
| **DL-387** — Give every one of the fifteen suppression entries its own acceptance date, and name the later measurement date on the five that were measured later. | (a) Date the register once, in its header. (b) Date rows 1 to 10 and leave 11 to 15 undated, as before. (c) Date rows 11 to 15 with the measurement date alone. | An accepted risk is an individual decision, and a reader who quotes one row needs the date in that row. All fifteen were accepted under the single criterion this register states, on 2026-07-31; rows 11 to 15 entered the register on 2026-08-02, when the closure was measured to include the audit tooling and the dotenv pin. Carrying both dates on those five is the only spelling that is true of each. Option (c) would date an acceptance by when the package was noticed. | Two dates on one entry can be misread as an acceptance that moved. The header states which is which, and the guard case asserts the acceptance date on every entry. |
| **DL-388** — Hold the accepted-advisory count at fifteen in every statement in this log, and correct the two rows that still said ten. **Amends DL-06 and DL-232.** | (a) Correct the count only where a reader is likely to look. (b) Add a note at the top of the file and leave the rows. | DL-294 moved the register to fifteen and this log still carried ten in two risk cells, which is the contradiction a decision log cannot survive. A count in a cell goes stale the moment another is added, so each corrected statement now names the gate as the authority and points at section 2.2. Option (b) leaves two rows that contradict the note. | Any future change to the suppression set has to land in the register, the workflow, `SECURITY.md` and these two rows. The staleness check catches a register-to-gate divergence; nothing catches a stale prose count except a reading pass. |
| **DL-389** — Refresh every source anchor against the shipped tree, and mark a pre-fix anchor as pre-fix in the sentence that cites it. | (a) Refresh the line numbers and leave the pre-fix ones unlabelled. (b) Drop line numbers and cite files alone, as DL-327's option (b) proposed. (c) Leave them. | An anchor is read as a measurement of the line it names. Two failures follow from not distinguishing them: a moved anchor sends a reader to unrelated text, and a pre-fix anchor read as current makes the log look wrong about its own subject. Twenty-five anchors were corrected and each cites a searchable symbol beside its number. Option (b) removes the precision a reviewer uses to check a claim. | Anchors drift again whenever a cited file changes above the cited line, and this pass's own comment edits moved several. The symbol beside each number is the remedy; the anchor tool in 42.6 is how the sweep was measured. |
| **DL-390** — Reconcile the three asymmetric edges the matrix carried, by naming each claimant in Direction A rather than by widening the exemption list. | (a) Widen the exemption note to cover them. (b) Remove the three claims from Direction B. | A matrix that asserts bidirectional coverage cannot carry an edge in one direction alone, and all three are real. The input-validation suite asserts the SEC-06 client contract, the config-guard suite asserts the SEC-01 credential gate, and the subscription endpoint is the authority whose imports fixed the SEC-05 module surface. Naming them makes the two directions agree on the facts. Option (a) exempts a gap rather than closing it. Option (b) deletes a true claim. | The exemption list is now the six rows it always described, and an automated two-way comparison is the only thing standing between a future edit and a new asymmetry. |
| **DL-391** — State the delivered path arithmetic as twenty created, twenty-four updated, three references and forty-seven paths, and list all eight extra paths. | (a) Keep the forty-five-path count and the six-path extras list. (b) Drop the arithmetic and state the row count alone. | The counts were computed when the matrix was smaller and never recomputed, so the section reporting delivery understated it by two paths — both frontend schema files, which the matrix already carried a row for. Option (b) removes the check that makes a coverage claim falsifiable. | Every future path added to Direction B has to land in the arithmetic as well. The comparison script in 42.6 recomputes both from the file rather than trusting either. |
| **DL-392** — Keep the matrix to source, target and control facts, and point at this log for every reason. | (a) Leave the rationale in the control cells. (b) Move the whole mechanism description here and leave the matrix as a bare index. | The matrix's own preamble names this log the single source of truth for why, and its control cells argued for the choices anyway. A control cell states what closes what and where; the reason belongs to the keyed row. Option (b) removes the mechanism a reviewer needs to judge coverage at all. | A control description and a justification are close relatives, and the boundary is a reading judgment. The matrix now carries a DL reference in each direction, so a reader who wants the argument has one hop. |
| **DL-393** — Keep `SECURITY.md` to controls, instructions, measured state and residuals, and replace its decision rationale with DL references. | (a) Leave it. (b) Cut the passages entirely. | The operational document declares this log the sole source of rationale and then reproduced the arguments for a dozen choices, which is the duplication Rule 1 exists to prevent. An operator needs the control and the command; a reviewer needs the argument and knows where it lives. Option (b) would remove measured state along with the argument. | An operator reading only `SECURITY.md` now sees what to do and not why the alternative was rejected. Each affected passage names the row that carries it. |
| **DL-394** — Rewrite the prose in all three documents to the sentence and paragraph bounds the Rule's own heuristics name, and re-run the scorecard against the shipped file. | (a) Rewrite only the passages the review quoted. (b) Re-run the scorecard without rewriting. (c) Leave the scorecard as section 40.8 recorded it. | Section 41 appended prose and left the 40.8 scorecard reporting measurements taken before it, so the file graded itself on a version that no longer existed. Rewriting the quoted passages alone would leave the rest of that growth unmeasured. The bar applied is the Rule's own: no sentence over 45 words, no body paragraph over five sentences, and the 30-to-45-word band recorded as a soft violation rather than reported closed. | A scorecard inside the file it grades goes stale on the next edit, which is why it carries a date and names the tool that produced it. The residual band is real and recorded rather than closed. |
| **DL-395** — Restate the coverage claim as measured rather than asserted, in all three documents. | (a) Keep "100% coverage" and "full coverage" wording. (b) Drop the claim. | Rule 1 asks for 100 per cent coverage with no gaps, and the artifacts claimed it while carrying three asymmetric edges and a two-path arithmetic error. A claim a reader can falsify in one pass costs more than the claim buys. The wording now names what was compared, in which directions, and what the comparison found. Option (b) would leave the Rule's requirement unaddressed. | The claim is now conditional on a comparison a reader can re-run, which is stronger and less quotable. Both documents name the tool and the counts. |

### 42.5 The choices the review found unkeyed

Each row below carries a choice that sections 40.5 and 40.6 stated as a subject bullet with no key of
its own. Every bullet in both lists now names the row that governs it, and these six are the rows that
had to be written for that to be true.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-397** — Bound an accepted `sub` claim by the range the `INTEGER` primary key holds, which PostgreSQL emits as `SERIAL`. **Narrowed out of DL-237, which decided the coercion direction.** | (a) Accept any integer the platform can represent. (b) Bound it by the widest integer the column type could ever hold on any engine. (c) Leave the bound implicit and let the driver refuse an out-of-range value. | A value no key can hold reaches the comparison under options (a) and (c), where the driver refuses it as a server fault instead of the guard refusing it as a 401 — the differential response SEC-08 exists to remove. A narrower bound would refuse a legitimate key. The column's own range is the only bound that refuses exactly what cannot be an identity. | The bound is the range of the shipped column type, so a later migration to a wider key type has to move it as well; the guard case names the boundary values so the coupling is visible. |
| **DL-398** — Count the account attempt before the stored credential is read, rather than after a failed comparison. **Narrowed out of DL-365, which carries the counter's key and constants.** | (a) Count only after the credential comparison fails. (b) Count only in the address-keyed limiter. (c) Count in both places after the comparison. | Counting first is what bounds guessing against one account spread across many client addresses, which the address-keyed limiter cannot see. Option (a) leaves an attacker who rotates addresses unbounded per account. Option (b) is the control OWASP guidance names as insufficient on its own. | A request that fails validation before authentication still spends an account attempt, so a client sending malformed bodies can consume another account's allowance; the window is fifteen minutes and the address limiter bounds the sender independently. |
| **DL-399** — Set the SQLite foreign-key pragma on each connection through a connect listener, not once on the engine. | (a) Set it once after the engine is created. (b) Declare the constraint checks in the model layer instead. (c) Accept that the harness does not enforce the four declared foreign keys. | SQLite ignores a declared foreign key unless the pragma is on for the connection that runs the statement, and the harness holds a connection pool, so option (a) covers the first connection only. Without it a child row referencing no parent inserts cleanly and the harness is weaker than the PostgreSQL engine it stands in for, which would let a required case pass vacuously. Option (b) moves engine behaviour into application code. | The pragma is a harness property, so a production engine's referential integrity is still whatever the deployed database enforces; the listener is registered on the harness engine alone. |
| **DL-400** — Assert the Cloud SQL transport and role declarations by reading `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf` as text, rather than by running a whole-directory validation. | (a) Run `terraform validate` over the directory in the case. (b) Run a targeted plan against a scratch module. (c) Assert nothing about the server end and cover SEC-10 on the client side alone. | The Terraform directory carries pre-existing references to resources it never declares, so the whole-directory command fails for reasons unrelated to transport and option (a) can only ever report that pre-existing failure. Option (b) needs provider credentials and network access a unit run does not have; section 25 records the scratch-module plan taken by hand instead. Reading the declaration is what leaves the server end of SEC-10 covered by an automated case. | A textual assertion passes on a declaration the provider would reject, so it proves the argument is present and not that the resource applies; the by-hand plan in section 25 is the check that closes that gap and it is not re-run by the suite. |
| **DL-401** — Transcribe the frozen setting names into the guard case as literals, rather than reading them from the `Settings` model. | (a) Read the names from the model with introspection. (b) Read them from `.env.example`. (c) Drop the case and rely on the requirements text alone. | Reading the names from `Settings` would make the assertion agree with a rename, which is the change it exists to catch, so option (a) is a case that can never fail. Option (b) has the same defect one file further out, since the template is edited in the same pass as a rename. A literal is the only form that holds the frozen contract independently of the code it guards. | The literals go stale if the requirements ever unfreeze a name, and nothing links them to that document; the case names AAP 0.1.4 in its docstring so the source of the freeze is findable. |
| **DL-402** — Shape-check every suppression identifier in the workflow against the advisory identifier pattern, in addition to comparing the declared set against the reported set. | (a) Compare the sets only. (b) Assert a fixed count of suppressions. (c) Trust the audit tool to reject an unknown identifier. | The audit tool accepts an identifier it does not recognise and silently suppresses nothing, so a typo becomes a hole rather than an error, which option (c) cannot detect. The set comparison catches a typo only while the advisory it was meant to suppress is still reported; a shape check catches it immediately. Option (b) fails on every legitimate change to the register. | A well-formed identifier for an advisory that does not exist still passes the shape check; the staleness comparison DL-205 reinstated is what reports it, and the two cases are deliberately separate. |

### 42.6 Measured evidence for section 42

- **DL-363 through DL-384 and DL-396, comment and docstring residual.** A marker scan over the eleven governed Python files reports **47 comments and 29 docstrings** carrying a marker, and every one of them matches on the causal comma before `so`; the whole marker set raises those to 50 and 30. The four extra units match only on `at no` inside the word sequence "that no", which is a substring artifact rather than a marker. Each of the 76 units was read individually. Every one states observable behaviour — for example, that the guard screens the claim ahead of any comparison, so the answer is the uniform 401 challenge — and none names an option not taken. The bar applied is DL-372's: no comment or docstring justifies a choice against an option not taken, which is not the same as containing a causal `so`. Measured 2026-08-03. The marker set is `because`, `rather than`, `instead of`, `otherwise`, `would`, `why`, `alternative`, `chose`, `chosen`, `so that`, `trade-off`, `could have`, `is what makes`, `the reason`, `which is what`, `which is why`, `not the`, `at no`, and a causal comma or colon before `so`.
- **DL-372, the nine remaining marker lines.** A comment scan over the nine non-Python governed files reports nine lines carrying a marker word: eight in `.env.example` and one in `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`. Each states observable behaviour — for example, that a malformed value fails at the first outbound request rather than at startup — and none names an option not taken. The bar applied is "no comment justifies a choice against an option not taken", not "no comment contains the word *rather*".
- **DL-364 through DL-384 and DL-396, proof the edits changed no behaviour.** For all eleven Python files the parsed syntax tree is identical to the pre-change revision, with docstrings dropped for the nine test modules and kept for the two application modules. For the nine configuration, infrastructure, pipeline and frontend files the comment-stripped text is identical, with one intended exception: the `db_app_password` description, which DL-369 shortened.
- **DL-384, the docstring corpus after the sweep.** Zero sentences over 30 words across `conftest.py` and the eight security modules, down from eleven. Zero body paragraphs over five sentences, with one measurement artifact recorded rather than edited: the fixture reference list in `conftest.py`'s module docstring is a definition list that the paragraph heuristic counts as seven sentences. Module preambles fell from 199 to 120 words in `test_config_guards.py`, 240 to 108 in `test_login_throttle.py` and 222 to 99 in `test_input_validation.py`.
- **DL-385 and DL-387, the register.** Fifteen entries, each carrying its identifier, package and version, its aliases, its fix release and installability verdict, its entry point, its reachability assessment, its acceptance and its date. The guard case parses the block heading and compares the register against the gate in both directions, and asserts the acceptance date on every entry; measured, both comparisons pass and no table in this file carries a shape other than the four decision columns.
- **DL-397 through DL-402, the unkeyed choices.** All 35 subject bullets in sections 40.5 and 40.6 now name the row that governs them. Twenty-nine resolve to rows that already existed, and six choices with no exact row gained one here. Measured: no bullet in either list is without a pointer, every pointer resolves to a row in this file, and the key sequence runs `DL-01` through `DL-402` with no gap and no duplicate across 402 rows.
- **DL-388, the count.** Fifteen appears in the register header, in the review trigger, in `SECURITY.md` and in the shipped gate. The two rows that said ten now say fifteen and name section 2.2.
- **DL-389, the anchors.** Every backticked `path:line` reference in this log and in the matrix was read against the shipped tree. Twenty-five were corrected and none now points past the end of a file or at unrelated text; each pre-fix anchor says so in the sentence that cites it.
- **DL-390 and DL-391, the two directions.** An edge-set comparison extracted from the matrix reports 82 edges in each direction with an empty difference both ways, five declared-asymmetric edges, and 47 unique paths at 20 created, 24 updated and 3 references.
- **DL-394, the prose.** Zero body sentences over 45 words and zero body paragraphs over five sentences in all three documents, measured with fenced code and inline code excluded. The 30-to-45-word band is the recorded soft residual.
- **Suite and style.** The security suite reports **689 passing cases and one pre-existing warning**, unchanged through every step of this pass, and `flake8` reports the same **109** findings with the same per-file distribution.

## 43. Corrections after the serial arithmetic and completeness review

`documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`, `SECURITY.md`,
`documentation/security/decision-log.md`

A serial review read the tree section 42 left behind and raised three minor findings, all in
documentation. All three are closed here: rows are keyed from `DL-403`, each carries one decision, and
the measurements sit in 43.5 rather than inside a cell.

The first is a Direction B row shape that contradicted its own stated invariant. The second is a
stale current-state test count. The third is a follow-on that named a root certificate without naming
the configuration key pointing at it.

### 43.1 The Direction B row shape

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-403** — Split the two grouped Direction B rows into one row per path, and state the row count beside the path count in section 2's preamble and in 3.1. | (a) Keep the grouped rows and reword the invariant to permit grouping by shared control. (b) Drop the invariant and let the edge measurement carry the coverage claim alone. (c) Make grouping the rule, collapsing every row that shares a mode and a finding. | The invariant is what makes coverage checkable by a reader with a text editor: one row per delivered path means an omitted path shows up as a missing row. Section 2 carried 44 rows for 47 paths, so the sentence was false as written even though every edge was present. Option (a) keeps the arithmetic honest but removes the property a reviewer counts, since a grouped cell can hide a fourth path. Option (b) leaves no claim a reader can check without running an extractor. Option (c) collapses rows that carry different contexts: the three ignore files govern three different build contexts, and the two schema files declare different bodies. | Five rows replace two, and the three ignore rows share one exclusion list, so the second and third name the first rather than repeating it. An edit to one therefore has two siblings to keep true. Row and path counts now have to move together: a new delivered path means a new row plus two count statements, and 43.5 measures both directions. |

### 43.2 The current-state test count

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-404** — Correct the current-state count in `SECURITY.md` section 3.3 from 688 to 689, and leave the two 688 figures in section 41.11 of this log standing. | (a) Change all three occurrences to 689. (b) Keep the sentence and add a footnote explaining the difference. (c) Drop the figure and point at the verification commands in section 2. | Two of the three figures measure the tree section 41 describes, and 41.11 says so in its first line, so rewriting them would falsify a dated record rather than repair a stale claim. The third reports what the suite does now, and both invocations measure 689: the security run, and the full run with the collection-error flag. Option (a) erases the evidence that the count moved by one when the harness gained a case. Option (b) spends a paragraph on a one-digit correction. Option (c) removes a figure a reader can check against the command printed above it. | Two counts differing by one now sit in the same repository, and only the heading of 41.11 marks which is historical. A later change to the suite has to move five sites together, three in `SECURITY.md` and two in the matrix baseline table, and 43.5 lists all five. |

### 43.3 The verify-full follow-on

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-405** — Name the configuration mechanism wherever `SECURITY.md` states the `verify-full` follow-on: the mode value, plus the certificate-authority path as `sslrootcert` in the engine connect arguments or `PGSSLROOTCERT` in the environment. | (a) Name the mode and the distributed certificate alone, as before. (b) Thread `sslrootcert` through `connect_args` now, behind a new optional setting. (c) Name only the default location the driver reads. | AAP 0.5.10 requires this document to name `verify-full` with a root-certificate path, and the mode alone is not actionable. The settings domain already admits `verify-full`, so an operator who sets it and stops either fails to connect or trusts whatever sits at the driver's default path. The guard case refuses a compound mode value, so the path has to travel separately. Option (b) adds a setting and a code path for a deferred follow-on, which the Minimal Change Clause keeps out of a documentation correction. Option (c) hides the parameter that makes a rotatable certificate possible, since a rotation job cannot write into every home directory. | The document now names a code site, the `connect_args` call in `backend/app/db/database.py`, so the follow-on and the sentence move together. Certificate distribution and rotation stay out of scope, so SEC-10 remains the partial remediation DL-249 records. |

### 43.4 This log's own measurement

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-406** — Refresh section 40.8's measurement to the count this section leaves behind, and leave every per-pass evidence block at the figure it measured. | (a) Leave 40.8 reporting 402 rows. (b) Date the sentence rather than refreshing it. (c) Refresh every count in every evidence block to the current file. | 40.8 reports the prose verdict for this file in the present tense, and section 42 already refreshed the same sentence from 331 to 402. Leaving it would reproduce the stale current-state figure DL-404 corrects elsewhere. Option (b) makes a reader carry a date to decide whether a figure is live. Option (c) rewrites dated measurements that 43.5 and every earlier evidence block depend on being fixed at the moment they were taken. | Every later pass that adds a row has one sentence to refresh, and forgetting it recreates a stale count. The hedged figures in that paragraph absorb small movement, so only the exact ones need attention. |

### 43.5 Measured evidence for section 43

- **DL-403, the row shape.** Before: 44 Direction B rows for 47 unique paths, with two cells carrying three paths and two paths. After: 47 rows, 47 unique paths and zero multi-path cells, against the same 82 Direction A edges, 82 non-exempt Direction B edges, 5 exempt edges and 20 created, 24 updated and 3 reference modes. The split moved the row count and nothing else, because edges and modes are extracted per path rather than per row.
- **DL-404, the five count sites.** The current-state figures sit at `SECURITY.md:163`, `:176` and `:696`, and in the matrix baseline table at `traceability-matrix.md:342-343`. All five read 689, which both invocations measure: the security run reports 689 passed with one pre-existing warning, and the full run reports 689 passed with the same three collection errors and exit 1. The two historical figures stay at 688, at `decision-log.md:1914` and `:1927`, both under the heading `41.11 Measured evidence for section 41`.
- **DL-405, the mechanism as shipped.** `backend/app/core/config.py` admits `verify-full` in its transport domain and defaults to `require`, while the engine in `backend/app/db/database.py` passes `sslmode` alone, conditionally, for a PostgreSQL URL. The guard case rejects a mode value carrying a second parameter, so the certificate path cannot ride inside the setting. Measured on the shipped driver, `sslrootcert` reaches libpq through both `psycopg2.connect` and engine `connect_args` on psycopg2 2.9.12 with SQLAlchemy 2.0.51: the attempt ends in a refused connection, not a rejected keyword. The vendor documents `~/.postgresql/root.crt` as the default that the parameter and `PGSSLROOTCERT` override, and before this pass neither name appeared anywhere in the repository.
- **DL-406, this file after the pass.** 406 four-column decision rows keyed `DL-01` through `DL-406`, with no gap and no duplicate. Zero rows over 1,800 characters, the longest at 1,796, and the longest single cell at 1,044. The file is about 522,000 characters.
- **The three gates, run as the workflow runs them.** The credential scan reports 21 pattern matches and zero after the reviewed allow-list, the frontend client-secret guard reports zero, and the browser-storage guard reports zero. Neither corrected document carries a credential pattern, and `git diff --check` exits 0.
- **Suite and style.** The security suite reports **689 passing cases and one pre-existing warning**, unchanged through this pass, and `flake8` reports the same **109** findings.

## 44. The twelfth pass — eighteen comment and prose findings (CMT-01 … CMT-18)

`.env.example`, `.github/workflows/ci.yml`, `SECURITY.md`,
`backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/app/main.py`, `backend/app/schema/user.py`,
`backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`, `backend/tests/conftest.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_token_storage.py`,
`documentation/security/decision-log.md`, `documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`,
`frontend/src/services/auth.ts`, `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.frontend`,
`scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`

A review of the comments, docstrings and prose the tree carried raised eighteen findings: seven major
and eleven minor. All eighteen were closed in the pass that shipped them, and every change was
comment, docstring or prose text. That pass amended seven entries in this file in place and opened no
section of its own, so its closure was not recorded here and could not be verified from the tree.

This section is that record, written retrospectively. It carries the eighteen dispositions, names
every entry the pass amended in place, and states the evidence the pass measured.

### 44.1 Recording a pass that amended in place

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-407** — Record the twelfth pass in its own section here, written after the fact and marked as such. | (a) Leave the pass unrecorded, since every finding it closed was a wording correction to an entry that already existed. (b) Extend section 43, which is the nearest neighbour. (c) Record it only in the commit message, where the dispositions already sit. | Rule 1 makes an unrecorded closure unverifiable from the tree, and eighteen findings is not a rounding error: a reader auditing this file could not tell whether `CMT-01` through `CMT-18` were closed, deferred or dropped. Option (a) is the failure the review found. Option (b) would date section 43's measurements to a pass that came after them, which is the falsification DL-404 refuses. Option (c) keeps the record outside the file Rule 1 names as the single source of truth, and a commit message is not where a reviewer looks for a decision. | The record is retrospective, so it reports the pass rather than being written by it. Its dispositions are transcribed from that pass's own commit message and each one was re-verified against the shipped tree; 44.4 states what was checked. |
| **DL-408** — Attribute the seven in-place amendments by naming them in 44.3, and leave the amended entries as that pass left them. | (a) Add a superseding row for each amended entry, the pattern DL-269 and DL-332 use for a decision that changed. (b) Re-date each amended row in place. (c) Revert the amendments and re-apply them as new rows. | In-place amendment is the right mechanism when the decision did not change and only its description was wrong, which is what all seven were: no alternative was re-weighed and no risk was re-accepted. What was missing is the attribution, not the amendment. Option (a) would file seven superseding rows for decisions that never moved, which makes the log harder to read and implies a change of position. Option (b) puts a date inside a cell, which 43 already rejected for measurements. Option (c) is churn with no reader benefit. | An amendment is now attributable only through this section, so a later pass that amends in place has to add its own record or repeat the omission. The convention is stated in 44.3 so it is inheritable. |

### 44.2 The eighteen dispositions

Major findings.

- **CMT-01 — `HttpOnly` described as putting the token beyond script.** The frozen login and register
  bodies still return `access_token` to page code, so six units now separate cookie unreadability from
  response-body visibility: `frontend/src/services/auth.ts`, the module docstring and the
  cookie-attribute comment in `test_token_storage.py`, [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md), DL-361 and
  the SEC-06 matrix row. The same pass corrected "both auth routes" to "the register and login
  routes", since the router carries three.
- **CMT-02 — logout described as ending the session.** It is now described as clearing the session
  cookie so a later cookie-only request is refused, with no revocation implied.
- **CMT-03 — an unqualified timing-equality claim.** Narrowed to hasher invocation count in
  `auth.py`, in the SQ-13 residual and in the SEC-07 matrix row, because the absent-account branch
  skips one query. DL-338 governs.
- **CMT-04 — unqualified once-only replay claims.** Ten units in `paypal_service.py`,
  `test_config_guards.py`, [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md), DL-332 and three matrix rows now carry
  the ledger's retention bound: 4,096 digests per worker, oldest evicted past that, lost on restart.
- **CMT-05 — the secret-file publish described as a move.** Restated as the shipped no-clobber
  `link(2)`, which leaves any existing destination unchanged and aborts the run. The bullet's pointer
  moved from the superseded DL-214 to DL-352 and DL-370.
- **CMT-06 — a blank line splitting a decision table.** The split before DL-294 was deleted, taking
  the count of table splits in this file from one to zero.
- **CMT-07 — a false marker measurement.** The claim of zero marked comments and zero marked
  docstrings was replaced with the measured 47 comments and 29 docstrings, rising to 50 and 30 under
  the whole marker set, with four units matching only the `at no` substring inside "that no". Each of
  the 76 was read against DL-372's bar.

Minor findings.

- **CMT-08** — a stale anchor into `main.py`, pointing at a line the redaction table no
  longer held, was removed.
- **CMT-09** — the password-mirror claim was narrowed to the length and character-class constants, and
  the byte ceiling was marked server-only.
- **CMT-10** — Node 22 was qualified as the lowest platform-permitted line that builds the pinned
  toolchain, in `ci.yml` and `Dockerfile.frontend`.
- **CMT-11** — the `.env.example` header was corrected to four kinds of value and its value-free claim
  narrowed, with matching corrections in `test_config_guards.py` and two matrix rows.
- **CMT-12** — the harness rationale index gained DL-399.
- **CMT-13** — DL-212's emphasis was repaired in the row and in its evidence bullet.
- **CMT-14** — DL-221 was restated as one canonical identifier per advisory, citing DL-373's
  mixed-namespace comparison.
- **CMT-15** — the claim describing a Terraform role assignment that DL-293 had deleted was removed.
- **CMT-16** — DL-269 was marked superseded in part by DL-359 and DL-375, leaving Python 3.9 as the
  only runtime named past end of life.
- **CMT-17** — the `google_sql_user.app` description was corrected to the four arguments the
  declaration carries.
- **CMT-18** — three malformed passages were repaired in `main.py` and `test_config_guards.py`.

### 44.3 The entries that pass amended in place

Seven entries in this file were edited by that pass rather than superseded, and two further prose
units were rewritten. The list is what makes them attributable.

- **DL-212, the row and its evidence bullet.** CMT-13, emphasis repaired in both.
- **DL-221, the row.** CMT-14, restated as one canonical identifier per advisory.
- **DL-269, the row.** CMT-16, marked superseded in part by DL-359 and DL-375.
- **DL-293, the row.** CMT-17, the argument count corrected. The same hunk deleted the CMT-06 table
  split that stood before DL-294.
- **DL-332, the row.** CMT-04, the replay claim qualified by ledger retention.
- **DL-361, the row.** CMT-01 and CMT-02, cookie unreadability separated from response-body
  visibility.
- Section 42.6's `DL-363 through DL-384 and DL-396` measurement bullet — CMT-07, the false zero
  replaced by the measured counts.
- The `google_sql_user` evidence paragraph in section 25 — CMT-15 and CMT-17, the deleted role
  assignment removed and the argument set restated.
- Section 40.5's "Why the run-time file guards exist" bullet — CMT-05, the move restated as the
  no-clobber link.

**The convention this establishes.** In-place amendment stays available for a description that was
wrong about a decision that did not change. The pass making it opens a section, names every entry it
amended, and keys its own rows. A superseding row remains the mechanism when the decision itself
moves, as DL-269 and DL-332 show.

### 44.4 Measured evidence for section 44

- **The pass changed no executable code.** For every changed Python file the docstring-stripped
  syntax tree was identical to the previous revision, and for every changed shell, YAML, TypeScript
  and Dockerfile the comment-stripped text was byte-identical.
- **Its own validation, as that pass reported it.** 689 security cases passing and unchanged, and the
  full suite at 689 passing plus the three pre-existing collection errors. `flake8` at 109 findings,
  the same set once line shifts are normalised. All three repository scans at zero, and the audit gate
  exiting zero with fifteen declared suppressions. Every `SEC-NN` and `DL-NNN` code marker resolving,
  and this file running `DL-01` through `DL-406` with no gap and no orphan.
- **Re-verified for this record, against the shipped tree.** `CMT` appeared zero times in all three
  governed documents before this section was written, which is the finding. The anchor CMT-08
  removed is absent from all three. Table splits inside a decision table are zero. The
  `google_sql_user.app` declaration carries the four arguments CMT-17 names, at
  `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf:93-98`. The nine amended units listed in 44.3 were read against
  that pass's own diff, which touched this file with seventeen insertions and sixteen deletions.
