## 45. The thirteenth pass — twelve final-acceptance findings

`.env.example`, `SECURITY.md`, `backend/app/core/security.py`, `backend/app/main.py`,
`documentation/security/decision-log.md`, `documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`,
`frontend/src/schema/filter.ts`, `infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml`

A final acceptance review found no code defect, no vulnerability and no requirement shortfall, and
raised twelve findings: eleven low and one informational. Eleven sit in documentation accuracy,
residual disclosure and decision logging. One is a single configuration line.

All twelve are closed here. Rows are keyed from `DL-409`, each carries one decision, and the
measurements sit in 45.6 rather than inside a cell. Section 44 is part of the same pass and holds the
retrospective record the first finding asked for.

### 45.1 The configuration line and the two code comments

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-409** — Supply `COOKIE_SECURE` to the Compose backend service as `${COOKIE_SECURE:-false}`, beside the transport exception, and leave the code default at true. | (a) Leave the variable out, as before, and let the operator discover the mismatch. (b) Add an `env_file` entry so the stack reads `.env`. (c) Flip the code default to false and let deployments raise it. (d) Serve the Compose stack over TLS. | A browser discards a `Secure` cookie over plain HTTP, and this stack publishes 80 and 8000. On the shipped default the stack answers a login with 200 and the frozen body while storing no session cookie, and every protected route then answers 401 — a client relying on credentialed requests rather than a bearer header simply cannot hold a session. Option (a) leaves no channel at all: the definition declares no `env_file` and all three build contexts exclude `.env`, so nothing an operator can edit reaches the setting. Option (b) would undo the build-context custody DL-300 established. Option (c) makes the insecure value the default everywhere, which inverts the finding. Option (d) provisions certificates for a development stack. The `:-` default keeps the file self-sufficient, and the shape mirrors the `DB_SSLMODE` exception one line above it. | A local value of `false` could be copied into a deployed environment, which is the same residual DL-245 accepts for the transport exception. Both live in the Compose file rather than in code, so the copy is visible in a diff, and the class default stays true so a deployment that sets nothing is secure. [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) section 3.3 carries it. |
| **DL-410** — Delete the two `HUMAN ASSISTANCE NEEDED` markers that the hardening made false, at the token guard and the origin-policy registration, and retain the other seven. | (a) Delete every marker in every governed file. (b) Correct the two in place, keeping the marker and rewriting the sentence beneath it. (c) Leave both, since neither is executable. | A marker is a request for human attention, so a false one costs a reader the time it takes to establish that nothing is wanted. Both had become false by measurement rather than by opinion: the guard beneath the first is the SEC-02 subject screen with its 401 uniformity, and the registration beneath the second is the SEC-03 allow-list, each covered by its own regression module. The other seven are accurate and each names work this scope excludes — integration tests, the two payment SDK wrappers, the cluster and network configuration, the proxy instance name, the client error-handling shape, and the migration command for a framework this application does not use. Option (a) would delete accurate requests for attention. Option (b) keeps a marker while denying the thing it asks for. | The retention rule now has to be applied by hand whenever hardening lands: a marker survives only while the work it names is still wanted. The seven that remain are the inventory a later pass re-checks. |
| **DL-411** — Reword the browser filter declaration so `zip_codes` reads as a field no code path populates, and state that the form-to-wire mapping drops the collected zip-code value. | (a) Leave the comment, which grouped the field with four the server does populate. (b) Populate `zip_codes` on the create path. (c) Remove the field from the response declaration. | The comment said the server owns five fields, and the server genuinely populates four; nothing anywhere creates a zip-code row, so the fifth always serialises empty. Grouping them reads as a promise the code does not keep. The mapping drop is the same class of silent behaviour: the form collects a comma-separated list, the wire body has no field for it, and the strict create model would refuse the key, so the value is discarded rather than stored. Option (b) adds a write path, which is feature work outside this scope. Option (c) would change a body shape the read path already serves. | The form still presents an input whose value goes nowhere. The comment now says so, which is the whole of what a comment can do; repairing the input is frontend work the brief excludes. |
| **DL-412** — Record the `npm ci` to `npm install --no-audit --no-fund` substitution in the frontend image and the pipeline, and decline `--ignore-scripts`. | (a) Keep `npm ci` and let the step fail. (b) Add `--ignore-scripts` to both invocations. (c) Generate and track a lock file so the strict install runs. | The substitution is functionally forced and was never logged, which is the finding: `npm ci` installs strictly from a lock file and exits non-zero without one, and AAP 0.9.2 excludes generating one, so option (a) is a step that cannot pass and option (c) is excluded work. What the swap costs is real and belongs on the record: a fresh resolution runs lifecycle scripts over a transitive tree nothing pins, which is the vector the March 2026 registry compromise used. Option (b) is the obvious compensating control and is declined for one reason: the bundle does not build today, so the flag could not be verified against a working install, and shipping an unverified change to the build path is worse than naming it. DL-346 chose the resolution source; this row records the installer and the integrity it gives up. | Build integrity rests on the exact direct pin alone. The residual is disclosed in [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) section 3.3 and closes with the lock file in item 2 of section 4, and `--ignore-scripts` stays available to whoever repairs the build. |

### 45.2 Residual disclosure in the operator register

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-413** — Disclose the unbounded public page in [`SECURITY.md`](../../SECURITY.md) and add the follow-on DL-187 recommended, without touching the route. | (a) Narrow `limit` at the route with a bound. (b) Add a bound only above a generous ceiling. (c) Leave the disclosure at DL-187's measured 500 and add nothing. | AAP 0.1.4 freezes the public read path with its `skip` and `limit` parameters, so options (a) and (b) both edit a contract this work is directed to preserve; DL-187 already refused that and named the amendment instead. Option (c) is what the review found: the sanitized 500 holds only above the driver's bindable range, so a reader could take a driver limit for a page limit. One unauthenticated request with a large in-domain value returns every row, and an operator has to know that before the endpoint faces traffic. | The read is still unbounded, and no code change here changes that. The residual and the measurement sit in section 3.3, and the follow-on is item 6 of section 4, where the amendment comes before the code. |
| **DL-414** — Disclose both account-throttle abuse variants in the operator register, keeping the counter's placement unchanged. | (a) Move the reservation after the credential lookup so an unknown address spends nothing. (b) Key the counter on the address alone. (c) Leave the variants in DL-49 and DL-292 only. | The reservation runs before the lookup deliberately: counting only known accounts is itself an enumeration oracle, which is the defect SQ-13 closed on the timing side. So the abuse surface is the price of the control, and option (a) would trade a disclosure for a vulnerability. Option (b) drops the account layer the guidance asks for. Option (c) is the finding: this log carried both variants and the document an operator reads carried neither, and a surface this work created is exactly what a residual register is for. | Both variants stand. The account budget can be spent by anyone who knows an address, and roughly 4,096 distinct addresses can fill the tracking map. Section 3.2 carries them beside the per-worker bound, and item 8 of section 4 closes them. |
| **DL-415** — Disclose the payment ledger's replay window and add a follow-on carrying its own review trigger. | (a) Provision a shared store now. (b) Raise the ledger bound. (c) Persist consumed references in the database. (d) Leave it at DL-163, which decided to keep the ledger. | The register already carried this residual class for the login throttle and, from the same cause, said nothing about a control on a payment authorization. Options (a) and (c) need infrastructure or migration tooling the brief excludes, and option (b) moves the same window to a larger number, which DL-49 rejected for the throttle map. Option (d) leaves the operator unaware that a restart, an eviction or a second worker admits a second charge on one reference. The trigger is named rather than left to a general review, because the first multi-worker deployment is when the window opens by default. | The window stands: a verified reference is spent once inside one worker's retained set and no further. Section 3.3 carries the three states that admit a second charge, and item 9 of section 4 carries the follow-on. |
| **DL-416** — Join the two halves of the live-payment hazard, in the operator register and beside the variable in the template. | (a) Restrict the payment mode to `sandbox` until the schema is repaired. (b) Refuse the charge when the mode is `live`. (c) Leave both facts where they were, each already documented. | Each half is correct alone, which is why neither document flagged it: the domain check exists so an operator can select `live` deliberately, and the subscription route's inability to persist is feature work the brief excludes. Together they are a real charge with no record of it, and option (c) leaves the reader to join them. Options (a) and (b) both narrow a validated setting the requirements prescribe, which would defeat the finding they exist to close and put a code change where a sentence suffices. | The sequence is still reachable by whoever sets the mode. The warning sits in both places an operator looks, and it stops being needed when the subscription schema is repaired. |

### 45.3 Documentation accuracy inside this log and the matrix

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-417** — Point DL-375 at the sections that carry the Node-line departure, and restate section 36's opening as the five it records rather than the five that exist. | (a) Move the Node-line row into section 36. (b) Move the four other labelled departures into section 36. (c) Leave the count and correct only the pointer. | DL-375 claimed section 36 named its departure, and section 36 holds five rows about the credential gate, the template placeholders, the template scope, the README and the scan exit codes — none about the Node line. Two claims were therefore wrong at once: the pointer, and a count that reads as the whole inventory while four labelled departures sit elsewhere. Options (a) and (b) relocate rows whose surrounding context explains them, which costs more than it buys and breaks the pointers into them. Option (c) leaves the count misleading. Naming the four in section 36's opening keeps each row where its work is recorded and still gives a reader one place to start. | The inventory is now distributed by design, so a later departure recorded outside section 36 has to be added to that opening list. The list is short and the section says what it is. |
| **DL-418** — State both entry points for the `python-dotenv` advisory wherever this log and the register group the five entries that sit outside the served application. | (a) Leave the wording, which named audit and test tooling alone. (b) Move that entry into the application group. (c) Drop the grouping and let the fifteen rows stand ungrouped. | Four of the five arrive through the audit and test tooling and the fifth arrives through a direct runtime pin that `pydantic` reads, so "tooling only" was wrong about one row in five, and this log's own register row 15 already said so. Option (b) is worse: the package is read by the application, but the advisory needs a local attacker and a write helper nothing calls, so filing it with the reachable group would overstate it. Option (c) removes the grouping that stops a reader expecting fifteen advisories to map onto the application's own dependency surface. | The grouping now needs two sentences where one used to do. Both this log and section 3.1 of the register carry the same wording, so they have to move together. |
| **DL-419** — Correct the three current-state style counts to the 108 findings this pass leaves, and leave every dated per-pass figure at 109. | (a) Change all figures to 108. (b) Leave the current-state figures at 109 and footnote the difference. (c) Restore the deleted comment line so the count returns to 109. | Deleting a false marker removed a line the style check reported as over-length, so the count moved by one for a reason this pass caused. Two of the three sites report what the check does now and had to move; the per-pass evidence blocks in sections 41, 42 and 43 report what each pass measured, and DL-404 already settled that rewriting those would falsify a dated record. Option (c) is the tail wagging the dog: keeping a false comment to preserve a number. | Counts differing by one now sit in the same repository for the second time, distinguished only by whether their heading names a pass. Three sites moved together — two in the register and one in the matrix baseline — and 45.6 lists them. |
| **DL-420** — Re-sweep every current-state `path:line` anchor in the three governed documents after this pass's insertions, and correct only anchors that point at the wrong shipped line. | (a) Correct the five anchors the review named and stop. (b) Delete line numbers from anchors so nothing can go stale. (c) Automate the sweep as a test case. | Insertions move lines, so correcting five anchors while adding four sections would have shipped a fresh set of stale ones — the same failure the review found, one pass later. Option (b) removes the property that makes an anchor useful, since a reader cannot check a claim against a file without one; DL-389 already set the sweep as the mechanism. Option (c) is attractive and is not free: a test would have to parse prose, distinguish a pre-fix anchor from a current-state one, and would fail on every legitimate documentation edit. Pre-fix anchors stay untouched, because each says in its own sentence which tree it describes. | The sweep is manual and has to run after any insertion into these three files. A pre-fix anchor is exempt only because its sentence qualifies it, so a sentence that loses the qualification silently makes its anchor wrong. |

### 45.4 Findings closed with no change to this log

Two findings needed no decision here, and saying so is part of the record.

- **The stale anchors themselves.** Retargeting an anchor to the line it describes is a correction,
  not a choice; DL-420 governs the sweep and 45.6 lists every anchor that moved.
- **The suite figure the review asked QA to confirm.** The review could not execute the suite inside a
  static boundary and flagged the 689 figure as plausible but unverified across five sites. It was
  executed for this pass and reports 689 passing cases, so all five sites stand as written and none was
  edited.

### 45.5 What this pass deliberately did not change

- **`backend/app/api/endpoints/listings.py`.** The page parameters stay as the brief freezes them.
  DL-187 and DL-413 carry the refusal.
- **The account counter's placement in `backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`.** Moving it would trade a
  disclosed denial-of-service surface for an enumeration oracle. DL-414 carries it.
- **The payment ledger in `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`.** DL-163 keeps it and DL-415
  discloses its window.
- **`infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.frontend` and `.github/workflows/ci.yml`.** The installer stays
  as it is, because no lock file is tracked. DL-412 records the substitution rather than reversing it.
- **The seven accurate `HUMAN ASSISTANCE NEEDED` markers.** Each names work this scope excludes.
  DL-410 carries the retention rule.
- **DL-293's row length.** It has stood over the 1,800-character bound since the twelfth pass amended
  it, and 45.6 reports that rather than rewriting a row whose accuracy that pass had just repaired.

### 45.6 Measured evidence for section 45

- **DL-409, the setting as shipped.** The Compose definition parses, the backend service environment
  carries `COOKIE_SECURE` with the `:-` default, and the `DB_SSLMODE` line the guard case asserts
  verbatim is unchanged. Every variable reference in every service block still carries a `:?` or `:-`
  modifier, and the required-setting comparison is unaffected because the field has a default.
- **DL-410, the marker inventory.** Zero markers remain in `backend/app/core/security.py` and
  `backend/app/main.py`. Seven remain in governed files, one each in `.github/workflows/ci.yml`,
  `infrastructure/terraform/main.tf`, `infrastructure/docker/docker-compose.yml`,
  `frontend/src/services/auth.ts` and `scripts/setup_dev_environment.sh`, and two in
  `backend/app/services/paypal_service.py`. Each of the seven was read against the work it names.
- **DL-411, the field it describes.** The filter create path builds only the name, the criteria
  children and the server-owned timestamp, so no code path creates a zip-code row and the declared
  field always serialises empty.
- **DL-413, the bound as measured.** On PostgreSQL 13.23 a `LIMIT` of 9223372036854775807 is accepted
  and returns every row, and a negative `LIMIT` raises `InvalidRowCountInLimitClause`, which reaches
  the client as the sanitized 500. One past the driver's bindable range never reaches the statement,
  which is DL-187's measurement. The suite already encodes both ends: the widest bindable bound answers
  200 and the unbindable bound answers the uniform envelope.
- **DL-419, the style count.** `flake8` reports 108 findings with one undefined name, against 109
  before this pass and a 129-finding baseline on the pre-work tree. The single removed finding is the
  over-length comment line DL-410 deleted, in `app/main.py`. The category set is unchanged at E302,
  E305, E501, F401, F821, W292 and W293, and the substantive four are unchanged: the undefined
  `datetime` at `app/api/endpoints/subscriptions.py:60` and three unused imports. The three corrected
  sites are `SECURITY.md` section 2.5 and section 3.3, and the matrix baseline table.
- **DL-420, the sweep.** All 129 backticked `path:line` anchors in the three governed documents were
  resolved against the shipped tree, and **eighteen anchors across eleven document lines** were
  retargeted. Nine were already wrong and are the ones the review named: two payment client-secret
  sites, the shipped signing-key declaration, three count sites, the shipped uniform 401, and two
  payment mode reads. Nine more moved because this pass shifted the lines beneath them: three in the
  token guard, two more at the coercion and the filter, one at the origin-policy registration, one at
  the decode clause, and the proxy image line, cited twice.
- **DL-420, what the sweep left.** Every anchor was read at its target rather than checked by
  arithmetic. None points past the end of a file. Eight land on a blank line or a lone delimiter, and
  each of those eight is a pre-fix or dated anchor whose own sentence says which tree it describes, so
  each is correct as written.
- **This file after the pass.** 420 four-column decision rows keyed `DL-01` through `DL-420`, with no
  gap and no duplicate. Zero table splits inside a decision table. One row stands over 1,800
  characters, DL-293 at 1,865, inherited from the twelfth pass and left alone by DL-419's reasoning;
  every other row is under the bound.
- **The three gates, run as the workflow runs them.** The credential scan reports 21 pattern matches
  and zero after the reviewed allow-list, the frontend client-secret guard reports zero, and the
  browser-storage guard reports zero. No file changed by this pass carries a credential pattern, and
  `git diff --check` exits 0.
- **Suite and prose.** The security suite reports 689 passing cases and one pre-existing warning,
  unchanged through every step of this pass. Across the three documents this pass adds no body sentence
  over 45 words and no prose paragraph over four sentences, measured with fenced code, tables and lists
  excluded, so each document's count of sentences over the bound is exactly what it was before: six
  here, two in the register and one in the matrix.

## 46. The fourteenth pass — the final runtime, performance and test-suite review

`backend/app/api/endpoints/auth.py`, `backend/app/api/endpoints/filters.py`,
`backend/app/main.py`, `backend/tests/security/test_config_guards.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_error_handling.py`,
`backend/tests/security/test_input_validation.py`,
`documentation/security/decision-log.md`,
`documentation/security/traceability-matrix.md`,
`frontend/src/components/ListingCard.tsx`, `frontend/src/services/api.ts`,
`frontend/src/services/auth.ts`

The last review of this work exercised the running application rather than its source: the client
boundary in a browser, the read and write paths under load, the diagnostic channel under repetition,
the credential path under timing measurement, and the test suite under reordering and negative
controls. It raised defects in three classes — a client that discarded the server's own error
envelope, a diagnostic channel and a redaction pattern that a client could make expensive, and
coverage gaps behind markers that no case reached.

Rows are keyed from `DL-421`. Two of them record a change **declined**, because the pagination
contract this review twice proposed to narrow is frozen and `DL-187` and `DL-290` already refused it
once each.

| Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risk / residual gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DL-421** — Carry the server's sanitized envelope to the caller through one `toApiError` helper exported from `frontend/src/services/api.ts`, and throw the enriched error from every call site. | (a) Keep `throw new Error('Login failed')`. (b) Rethrow the provider error unchanged. (c) Give each call site its own mapping. | Nine distinct failures rendered byte-identically, so a throttled caller, a rejected credential and an unreachable server were indistinguishable in the interface. The envelope already carries what separates them — status, `detail`, `fields` and the correlation identifier — and discarding it at the boundary threw away the only thing that made a failure actionable. Option (b) is refused on a security ground rather than a style one: the provider error holds the request configuration, and that includes the submitted password. Option (c) repeats the mapping per call site and drifts. | The helper reads an untyped body, so a malformed one yields the fallback message rather than a thrown parse error. `isNetworkError` distinguishes an outage from a rejection, so an outage can never be reported as a bad credential. |
| **DL-422** — Give `logout()` a boolean contract that resolves `true` only when the server confirmed the clear. | (a) Keep `Promise<void>` with no catch. (b) Swallow the failure and resolve. (c) Let the rejection propagate. | Script cannot read an `HttpOnly` cookie, so the server's answer is the caller's only evidence that the session ended. The shipped form produced an unhandled rejection, left the interface loading forever, and left the caller signed in while appearing to have signed out — the failure direction that matters. Option (b) reports a live session as ended, which is worse than an error. Option (c) is the defect. | The result is advisory: a caller that ignores it is no better off than before. The cookie is cleared server-side or not at all, so there is no partial state to reconcile. |
| **DL-423** — Re-use one outstanding login request keyed on the submitted credentials, and export `isLoginPending()`. | (a) Leave the calls unguarded. (b) Disable the control in the component only. (c) Debounce on a timer. | Three clicks inside seven milliseconds issued three logins and spent three of the five attempts the throttle allows, so the interface could exhaust a user's own allowance faster than a user could read the result. The guard is keyed on the credentials, so a corrected password is a new request rather than a re-used one, and it clears in `finally` guarded on promise identity so a slow request cannot clear a newer one. Option (b) leaves every other caller unguarded. Option (c) adds latency to the common case and still admits a burst at the interval. | The key holds the submitted credentials in memory for the life of one request, which is no wider than the request body already is. State is per-module, so two module instances would not share it. |
| **DL-424** — Publish `Retry-After` on both throttle layers and expose that one header across origins. | (a) Publish nothing, as shipped. (b) Publish the `RateLimit-*` family as well. (c) Publish a fixed window rather than the live remainder. | A throttled caller could not tell whether to retry in a second or a quarter of an hour, so the only available strategy was to keep trying — which is what the control exists to stop. The hint is computed from the live window remainder on the address layer and the stored expiry on the account layer, so the two answer alike. Option (b) publishes the attempt threshold, which tells an attacker the budget it has to stay under. Option (c) is either wrong or generous. | The header discloses that a window exists and how much of it is left, which is the disclosure the recovery hint is made of. It rounds up, so a caller that waits it out is admitted rather than refused a second time. No frozen response body changes; the hint is a header. |
| **DL-425** — Bound one diagnostic record and the volume of records: keep the innermost eight frames within a 4,096-byte ceiling, and render the full report at most five times per route and exception chain per sixty seconds. | (a) Leave the channel unbounded. (b) Move the traceback to `DEBUG` and log a compact line at `ERROR`. (c) Sample a fixed fraction. (d) Cap the volume without bounding the record. | A single client that could reach any failing route wrote between six and eleven kilobytes per occurrence with no bound on either size or rate, which turns a reachable failure into a way to fill the log volume the process shares with the host. The outer frames of a request are the same server stack every time, so the innermost frames are the ones worth keeping. Option (b) inverts the two-channel design: an `INFO`-level deployment would then hold no diagnosis at all. Option (c) loses the first occurrence, which is the one a maintainer reads. Option (d) leaves one long exception message deciding a record's size. **Every occurrence is still recorded under its own correlation identifier**, so a reference handed to a caller always resolves — the budget bounds what a record carries, never whether one exists. | A sixth and later occurrence of one failure inside one window is recorded with its type, its count and no traceback, so diagnosing it needs the window to lapse or the count to be read. The budget is per-worker and lost on restart, and the tracking map holds 512 keys and renders nothing new past that rather than evicting a live window. |
| **DL-426** — Anchor the credential-name pattern at the keyword and leave any identifier prefix unmatched. | (a) Keep the leading `[A-Za-z0-9_.\-]*`. (b) Bound the prefix to a fixed length. (c) Drop prefixed names from the pattern. | The unanchored prefix backtracked across an identifier-dense traceback at roughly a millisecond per kilobyte, so redaction cost 7.6 ms of an 11.2 ms response and eight concurrent faults degraded unrelated requests by a factor of forty-five. Output is unchanged: a captured prefix that is re-emitted verbatim and a prefix that is never matched leave the same text, so `signing_key=`, `client_secret=` and `api-token:` still redact and still read. Option (b) keeps the class of defect with a smaller constant. Option (c) stops redacting the names most likely to appear. | The pattern no longer captures the prefix, so a future replacement that dropped group one would change more text than before. A case asserts the prefixed forms redact and the names survive. |
| **DL-427** — Load both declared collections on the filter read with `selectinload`. | (a) Leave the relationships lazy. (b) `joinedload`. (c) Return a projection instead of the mapped rows. | The read cost two statements per row, so one caller's own filter count decided how many round trips the read made — 404 statements at two hundred rows. Both relationships are collections, where `selectinload` issues one statement per relationship rather than multiplying the parent rows as a join would. Option (c) changes the declared response model, which is frozen. | The read now issues a fixed small number of statements at any row count, asserted by comparing two measured counts rather than a transcribed one. The eager load applies to this route only. |
| **DL-428** — Decline to bound or refuse the public read path's `skip` and `limit`, for the third time, and keep the residual disclosed instead. | (a) Refuse a negative bound at the request boundary with `Query(0, ge=0)`. (b) Clamp the window inside the handler at a five-hundred-row ceiling. (c) Do both. | This review proposed (a) and (b) independently, which is itself evidence the question is unsettled rather than obvious. Both narrow what the endpoint serves, and the read path including its `skip` and `limit` pagination is frozen; the file's own change contract permits one line and says in terms that no bound be added to `limit`. `DL-187` refused (a) and `DL-290` withdrew it, each after review. Option (b) is the gentler form and still moves observable behaviour: a request for a million rows would receive five hundred without being told, and a bound past the driver's range would answer a page where it now answers a sanitized fault. The correct order is the amendment first, which is why this stays follow-on item 6 rather than becoming a quiet narrowing. | One unauthenticated request can still read the whole table, and a negative bound still reaches the driver and surfaces as the uniform sanitized 500 — disclosing nothing, but costing an error record. `SECURITY.md` section 3.3 carries the measurement on both engines and section 4 item 6 carries the authorisation this needs. Review trigger: an amendment that authorises a page ceiling. |
| **DL-429** — Keep the suite's assertion that a valid filter body writes a row, and decline to reassert the sanitized 500 the route once answered. | (a) Reassert the fault and its zero row counts. (b) Assert neither. | The route persists: it builds mapped `Criteria` children from the validated allow-list and stamps the server-owned `created_at`, which `DL-296` restored and `DL-298` asserts. A case written against the withdrawn state would fail against the shipped one, and the preservation list requires this route to return its declared response model. Option (b) leaves the one write path that works uncovered. | The zero-row property the withdrawn case pinned is now covered where it still applies: three cases count rows after a 422, and the listing write path keeps its own no-row assertion. |
| **DL-430** — Bind the listing card to the field names the read path publishes, and gate its outbound link on an `http` or `https` scheme. | (a) Leave the component alone as outside the file map. (b) Rename the fields and leave the link ungated. (c) Rename the published API fields to match the component. | Every card rendered an empty heading and announced the literal `Apartment at undefined`, because the component read four names the API does not publish. The link is the reason the two halves are one change rather than two: the value reached no `href` only because the same drift kept it from arriving, so repairing the binding alone would have bound a stored `javascript:` value live. Option (b) ships that trap. Option (c) breaks a frozen response contract. The image element is removed rather than repaired because no image column exists to source it and adding one needs migration tooling. | The component sits outside the planned file map and is recorded as a delivered departure in the matrix. It carries inline style values because the repository ships no stylesheet; a stylesheet is frontend redesign. Escaping still comes from the framework, not from this change — the guard covers the `href` sink alone. |
| **DL-431** — Extend the credential scan's reviewed allow-list to three new test lines rather than reshape the pattern. | (a) Narrow the pattern so a backend name, a policy bound and a planted fixture stop matching. (b) Exclude the test package from the scan. | The three lines are a lower-case backend name, an integer policy bound and a deliberately planted fake credential inside a redaction case. None is a secret, and each matches because the pattern is deliberately broad and excludes no file. Option (a) weakens a gate that guards tracked content for the convenience of three test lines. Option (b) is worse: the test package is exactly where a planted credential is most likely to be committed by accident. The marker is the mechanism the gate documents for a reviewed line. | Three more lines are exempt by marker, so the gate's residual now rests on seven reviewed lines rather than four, a count a case pins. Each names what it is in the same line, and the gate still excludes no file. |
| **DL-432** — Reconcile the traceability map and its accountability pin to the delivered set, at 48 rows and 20/25/3, and read a row's mode out of its emphasis. | (a) Leave the pin's transcribed totals and drop the row. (b) Drop the pin. (c) Stop marking delivered departures in emphasis. | The pin exists so that a map claiming nothing sits outside it fails when something does, and the listing card is exactly that case. Its totals were transcribed from a smaller map, so they had to move with the map rather than pin it in the past. Reading the mode out of its emphasis keeps the map's own convention — a departure is marked where it is declared — without making presentation decide whether a check passes. Option (a) keeps a false completeness claim. Option (b) removes the only mechanism that fails when the documents drift. Option (c) loses the distinction the map is making. | The totals are still transcribed and still have to move with the map; the pin fails loudly when they do not, which is the direction that costs a maintainer nothing. A row whose mode cell is emphasised differently again would need this reader revisited. |
